1 Overview of Infrastructure Management and the DevOps Journey
Join our book community on Discord
https://packt.link/IOzft
The way organizations manage their technical infrastructure has undergone significant changes in recent years, driven by the emergence of cloud computing and the DevOps movement. In this chapter, we will explore the evolution of technical infrastructure, starting with traditional on-premises infrastructure and the challenges it poses for organizations in terms of maintenance, scalability, and agility. We will then look at the limitations of traditional infrastructure management approaches and how the DevOps movement emerged as a response to those challenges. Finally, we will examine the shift toward cloud infrastructure and the role that DevOps engineering has played in this transition.In this chapter, we're going to cover the following main topics:
Technical infrastructure before the public cloud and DevOps
The emergence of DevOps and cloud computing
The shift toward cloud infrastructure
The relationship between cloud computing and DevOps
Standardizing and scaling DevOps
Determining the role of platform engineering in scaling DevOps practices
Technical infrastructure before the public cloud and DevOps
Technical infrastructure
refers to the collection of tools that support the operation and management of an organization's information systems, including—but not limited to—hardware, software, and network resources. It includes physical infrastructure—such as servers, storage devices, and networking equipment—as well as virtual infrastructure—such as virtual servers and virtual storage. Technical infrastructure also includes software components, such as the operating systems, applications, and tools that are used to manage and maintain the hardware and software resources. Technical infrastructure is a critical component of an organization's IT environment as it enables the delivery of services and applications to users and supports the storage and processing of data. It plays a key role in enabling organizations to achieve their business objectives by providing the foundation for information systems and the underlying technology infrastructure. In the software world, technical infrastructure is the collection of tools and applications needed to build, deliver, and maintain software.
Technical infrastructure can be managed in-house, where the organization is responsible for the design, implementation, and maintenance of the infrastructure, or it can be outsourced to a third-party provider. Outsourcing technical infrastructure can provide organizations with access to expertise and resources that they may not have in-house, as well as cost savings through economies of scale. However, outsourcing also carries the risk of vendor lock-in and can be more difficult to customize and adapt to changing business needs.
Evolution of technical infrastructure
The concept of technical infrastructure has evolved over time as the needs and capabilities of organizations have changed. In the early days of computing, technical infrastructure was limited to a few mainframe computers that were used to support a limited number of business functions. These computers were expensive and required specialized skills to operate and maintain, and as a result, they were typically managed in-house by a dedicated team of IT professionals. As computers became more powerful and affordable, organizations began to adopt decentralized computing models, with multiple servers and workstations that were connected through
local area networks
(
LANs
). This decentralized model made it possible for organizations to support a wider range of business functions and enabled users to access data and applications from any location within the organization. In the late 1990s and early 2000s, the rise of the internet and the emergence of web-based applications led to a shift toward more distributed and decentralized technical infrastructure. Web servers and application servers were added to the mix, and networks were expanded to support the growing number of devices and users. This shift toward distributed computing required organizations to adopt new tools and processes for managing and maintaining their technical infrastructure, as well as new approaches to security and data management. In the past couple of decades, the emergence of cloud computing has revolutionized the way organizations manage their technical infrastructure. With the advent of public cloud providers, such as
Amazon Web Services
(
AWS
), Microsoft Azure, and
Google Cloud Platform
(
GCP
), organizations can now
outsource
the management of their hardware, software, and networking resources to a third-party provider. This has made it easier for organizations to scale their technical infrastructure and has enabled them to access a wide range of services and resources on demand, without the need to invest in expensive hardware and software.
Issues with traditional technical infrastructure management
Before the advent of cloud computing and the rise of the DevOps movement, the operations and administration of technical infrastructure were typically labor-intensive processes that involved a variety of manual tasks and processes. These tasks included installing and configuring hardware and software, monitoring and maintaining systems, and troubleshooting issues as they arose. Operations and administration of traditional technical infrastructure was typically the responsibility of a dedicated IT team, which was responsible for managing the hardware, software, and networking resources that supported the organization's information systems. This team would work closely with other teams, such as the development team, to ensure that systems were designed, implemented, and maintained in a way that met the needs of the organization.
Traditional infrastructure management posed several challenges and limitations for organizations, as outlined here:
High maintenance and uptime requirements
: To maintain a high level of availability and uptime, IT teams had to be on call 24/7 to respond to issues and perform regular maintenance and updates. This often led to IT professionals working long hours and being available to troubleshoot issues at any time of the day or night.
Difficulty in scaling
: Scaling infrastructure to meet changing business needs required significant investment in hardware and software, as well as time and effort to install and configure the new resources. This could be a slow and cumbersome process, making it difficult for organizations to keep pace with changing business needs.
Long lead times for making changes
: Manual processes and documentation and the need to follow a structured change management process made it challenging to implement new systems or make changes to existing ones quickly. This could take weeks or even months, making it difficult for organizations to keep up with changing business needs.
Inflexibility
: The inflexibility of traditional systems and processes made it challenging for organizations to respond quickly to new market conditions or take advantage of new opportunities.
Poor visibility into system status
: Lack of real-time monitoring and reliance on manual processes for gathering information about the status of systems made it challenging for IT professionals to troubleshoot issues quickly.
There were several best practices that organizations used to follow in order to manage technical infrastructure before the rise of DevOps. Organizations would maintain detailed documentation of their technical infrastructure, including diagrams of the hardware and software components, as well as instructions for installation and configuration. This documentation was critical for troubleshooting issues and for maintaining the systems over time. Additionally, they would use a variety of tools to monitor the performance and availability of their systems, including system monitoring tools, application performance monitoring tools, and network monitoring tools. These tools would provide alerts when issues arose, and IT professionals could use them to troubleshoot problems and identify the root cause of issues. A very well-structured process for managing changes to their technical infrastructure would be followed, including procedures for testing, staging, and rolling out updates and new releases. This process was designed to minimize the risk of disruption and to ensure that changes were made in a controlled and predictable manner. Organizations would maintain comprehensive backup and recovery procedures to ensure that their systems and data were protected in the event of an outage or a disaster. These procedures would include regular backups of data and systems, as well as processes for restoring systems in the event of an issue. Finally, there was a need to implement a variety of security measures to protect their systems and data from external threats, including firewalls,
intrusion detection systems
(
IDS
), and antivirus software. They would also follow best practices for password management and user access control to ensure that systems were secure and compliant.
The emergence of DevOps and cloud computing
While these best practices helped organizations manage their technical infrastructure effectively, they also had some limitations. Reliance on manual processes and documentation made it difficult to adapt to changing business needs and respond quickly to issues as they arose. The emergence of the
DevOps
movement and the adoption of cloud computing has helped organizations overcome these limitations and improve the speed and reliability of their IT operations. We'll explore this more in the next parts of this chapter. Managing traditional technical infrastructure posed a number of challenges for organizations, particularly in terms of maintenance, scalability, and agility. The DevOps movement promotes a more agile and flexible approach to infrastructure management, with an emphasis on automation, CD and continuous improvement.
DevOps is a set of practices that promotes collaboration and integration between development and operations teams, with the goal of improving the efficiency and effectiveness of software delivery. The DevOps movement has its roots in the agile software development movement, which emerged in the late 1990s and early 2000s. Agile software development emphasized the importance of rapid iteration, continuous delivery, and customer feedback in the software development process. The DevOps movement took this idea a step further by promoting the integration of development and operations teams and by emphasizing the importance of automation and continuous improvement in the software delivery process.
Some major milestones in the birth and growth of DevOps include the following:
2007
: The term
DevOps
is coined by Patrick Debois, a Belgian IT consultant
2009
: The first
DevOpsDays
conference is held in Belgium
2010
: The first version of the
Agile Operations Manifesto
is published
2011
: The book
The Phoenix Project: A Novel About IT, DevOps, and Helping Your Business Win
is published and becomes a key text in the DevOps movement
2012
: The DevOps Institute is founded
Today, DevOps has become an essential part of the software delivery process for many organizations, and it has helped to drive the adoption of agile and flexible approaches to infrastructure management. It has also played a key role in the transition to cloud computing and the adoption of more agile and flexible approaches to IT operations. The DevOps movement emerged as a response to the challenges of traditional infrastructure management, particularly in terms of the difficulty of managing software in on-premises models. There are several differences between traditional infrastructure management and DevOps, the main ones being listed next:
Traditional Infrastructure Management
DevOps
Approach
Siloed approach, where development and operations teams work independently
A collaborative approach, where development and operations teams work together to achieve common goals
Frequency of Releases
Releases are infrequent and take a long time to complete
Frequent and rapid releases, with a focus on continuous delivery
Automation
Limited automation, with many manual processes and tasks
Extensive use of automation to streamline processes and increase efficiency
Monitoring
Reactive monitoring capabilities focused on observing events and system health, with a reliance on manual processes for gathering information
Continuous monitoring and measurement of software delivery processes. Proactive insights into system behavior.
Change Management
Slow and cumbersome change management processes, with a focus on minimizing the risk of disruption
Fast and flexible change management processes, with a focus on continuous incremental improvement
Scalability
Difficult to scale up or down in response to changing business needs
Flexible and scalable, with the ability to quickly adapt to changing business needs
Visibility
Poor visibility into the status of systems, with a reliance on trial and error for troubleshooting issues
Real-time visibility into the status of systems, with the ability to quickly identify and troubleshoot issues
Security
Focus on security, but limited ability to respond to new threats
Comprehensive security measures, with the ability to quickly respond to new threats
Table 1.1 – Differences between traditional infrastructure management and DevOps
Some specific practices and tools commonly used in DevOps include agile development methodologies,
continuous integration and continuous delivery
(
CI/CD
)
tools, configuration management tools, and monitoring and logging tools. Agile development methodologies, such as
Scrum
and
Kanban
, emphasize the importance of rapid iteration, continuous delivery, and customer feedback in the software development process. By breaking down the software development process into smaller, more manageable chunks, Agile methodologies enable teams to deliver working software more quickly and respond more effectively to changing requirements. CI/CD tools automate the software delivery process, including the building, testing, and deployment of code. This helps to reduce the time and effort required to deliver software updates and new features and to improve the reliability of the software delivery process.
With CI/CD, teams can deliver software changes more frequently and with greater confidence. Configuration management tools help to automate the installation and configuration of hardware and software resources, including the provisioning of infrastructure, the configuration of applications, and the management of system updates and patches. By automating these tasks, teams can reduce the risk of errors and inconsistencies and improve the overall efficiency of their infrastructure management processes. Monitoring and logging tools provide real-time visibility into the performance and availability of systems and help to identify and troubleshoot issues as they arise. By proactively monitoring systems and analyzing log data, teams can quickly identify and resolve issues, reducing downtime and improving the overall reliability of their applications and infrastructure. By incorporating these practices and tools into their software delivery process, organizations can improve the speed and reliability of their software delivery, enabling them to respond more quickly to customer needs and gain a competitive advantage in the market.
The rise of DevOps and cloud computing are closely linked, as both are part of a broader shift toward more agile and flexible approaches to IT operations. Cloud computing provides a platform for organizations to host and run their software applications and services in a scalable and cost-effective manner. It also allows for rapid provisioning and deprovisioning of resources, which is a key enabler of DevOps principles such as continuous delivery and automation. The traditional approach to infrastructure management involved managing hardware and software in on-premises models, which were often slow, cumbersome, and expensive. This made it difficult to rapidly provision and deprovision resources and to make changes to the infrastructure the DevOps way.
The rise of cloud computing has transformed this approach, providing organizations with a more flexible and scalable model for managing their IT operations. With cloud computing, organizations can provision and deprovision resources quickly and easily and can scale their infrastructure up or down in response to changing business needs. This has made it possible to implement DevOps principles such as continuous delivery, which involves the frequent and rapid release of new software updates and features. Cloud computing has also provided the tools and infrastructure needed to support automation and collaboration between development and operations teams, which are key components of the DevOps philosophy. By enabling teams to work together more closely and to automate routine tasks, cloud computing has helped to streamline the software delivery process and to improve the speed and reliability of IT operations.
The shift toward cloud infrastructure
Cloud computing has revolutionized the way organizations manage their IT infrastructure, and it has become an essential part of the modern IT landscape. But the concept of cloud computing has a long history, dating back to the early days of mainframe computers.One of the earliest examples of cloud computing was the concept of
mainframe timesharing
, which emerged in the 1960s. Timesharing allowed multiple users to access and share the resources of a single mainframe computer, which was a more efficient and cost-effective way of using computing resources than having each user operate their own computer. The concept of
virtualization
, which involves the abstraction of hardware resources into software-based
virtual
resources, also played a key role in the development of cloud computing. Virtualization made it possible to run multiple
virtual machines
(
VMs
) on a single physical server, which helped to improve the utilization and efficiency of computing resources. In 2011, the
National Institute of Standards and Technology
(
NIST
) published "
The NIST Definition of Cloud Computing
" (NIST Special Publication
800-145
), which described it as "
a model for enabling convenient, on-demand network access to a shared pool of configurable computing resources (for example, networks, servers, storage, applications, and services) that can be rapidly provisioned and released with minimal management effort or service provider interaction.
". Since then, the concept of cloud computing has evolved and matured, and it has become a mainstream way for organizations to access and manage computing resources. Some key milestones in the evolution of cloud computing are described in the following screenshot:
Figure 1.1 – History of cloud computing (SimplyCoding.in)
Public cloud providers played a key role in the evolution and adoption of cloud services. The most important milestones in the growth of the public cloud are noted here:
2006
: AWS launches its first cloud services, including
Amazon Simple Storage Service
(
Amazon S3
) and
Amazon
Elastic Compute Cloud
(
Amazon EC2
). This is the first cloud service offering flexible infrastructure.
2007
: Heroku launches as a platform for a developer to build, run, and scale applications. Although initially only supporting Ruby projects, this is the first cloud service offering a developer and deployment platform.
2008
: Google launches
Google App Engine
(
GAE
), a cloud platform for developing and hosting web applications.
2010
: Microsoft launches
Windows Azure
, a cloud platform for developing, deploying, and managing applications and services.
2011
: IBM launches
SmartCloud
, a cloud platform for managing and deploying IT infrastructure and applications.
2013
: Oracle launches
Oracle Cloud
, a cloud platform for developing, deploying, and managing applications and services.
Today, cloud computing has become an essential part of the IT landscape, and it is used by organizations of all sizes to access and manage a wide range of computing resources, including networks, servers, storage, applications, and services.As the demand for cloud computing continues to grow, more and more companies are entering the market, offering a wide range of cloud services and solutions to meet the needs of organizations of all sizes. The rise of public cloud providers, such as AWS, Microsoft Azure, and GCP, has had a significant impact on the way organizations manage their IT infrastructure. Public cloud providers offer a range of cloud services, including
Infrastructure as a Service
(
IaaS
),
Platform as a Service
(
PaaS
), and
Software as a Service
(
SaaS
), as well as a growing number of emerging cloud services such as
Database as a Service
(
DBaaS
),
Containers as a Service
(
CaaS
), and more. These allow organizations to access and manage a wide range of computing resources on a pay-as-you-go basis.
Benefits and hurdles in the adoption of cloud technologies
The use of public cloud services has a number of benefits for organizations. Public cloud services are generally more cost-effective than on-premises solutions, as they allow organizations to pay only for the resources they consume and to scale up or down as needed.This can help organizations to reduce their IT costs and to better align their IT spend with their business needs. Through highly scalable cloud services, organizations can quickly and easily add or remove resources as needed. This can help organizations to respond more quickly to changing business needs and to support rapid growth.Public cloud providers invest heavily in security and offer a range of security measures and controls to protect their customers' data. This can help organizations to reduce their risk of data breaches and to comply with various security and regulatory requirements. Organizations can stay competitive and innovative gaining access to the latest technologies through public cloud providers, as they continuously invest to bring the latest technologies into the cloud. The use of public cloud services has enabled organizations to become more agile and flexible in the way they manage their IT infrastructure, and it has helped to drive the adoption of DevOps practices and other agile approaches to infrastructure management.While the use of public cloud services has many benefits, there are also some potential challenges and considerations that organizations should be aware of. Depending on the cloud provider and the services that are used, organizations may be subject to vendor lock-in, which can make it difficult to switch to a different provider in the future. This can be a particular concern for organizations that use proprietary services or that have a large investment in a particular cloud platform. Public cloud services may not be compliant with all regulatory and industry requirements, which can be a concern for organizations in highly regulated industries. Organizations should carefully evaluate the compliance of their chosen cloud provider and ensure that their use of cloud services does not violate any regulations. While public cloud providers offer robust security measures and controls, organizations are still responsible for the security of their own data and applications. This means that organizations should implement appropriate security measures and controls, such as encryption and access controls, to protect their data in the cloud. Public cloud services may require organizations to make changes to their existing IT infrastructure and processes, which can be challenging. Organizations should carefully evaluate the impact of moving to the cloud and ensure that they have the necessary resources and expertise to manage the transition. Overall, the use of public cloud services can bring many benefits to organizations, but it is important for organizations to carefully evaluate their options and to ensure that they choose the right provider and services to meet their specific needs.It is also very important for organizations to carefully evaluate their options and to ensure that they choose the right provider and services to meet their specific needs. Public cloud platforms are typically available in multiple regions around the world, which allows organizations to deploy their applications and services in locations that are close to their customers and users.This can help organizations to improve the performance and availability of their applications and to comply with various regulatory requirements. Public cloud platforms offer a range of
disaster recovery
(
DR
) options, including backup and recovery services and the ability to replicate data and applications across multiple regions. This can help organizations to protect their data and applications in the event of a disaster and to ensure
business continuity
(
BC
).
The relationship between cloud computing and DevOps
The transition to cloud infrastructure has been a key driver of the DevOps movement, as it has enabled organizations to become more agile and flexible in the way they manage their IT infrastructure. DevOps engineering plays a critical role in this transition, as it involves the design and management of tools and processes that support the delivery and management of applications at scale. Additionally, DevOps engineers play a crucial role in transitioning to cloud infrastructure. Some of their key responsibilities include designing and implementing automation tools and processes, managing and monitoring cloud environments, collaborating with development and operations teams, implementing security measures, managing vendor relationships, and providing technical guidance and support.One of the primary responsibilities of DevOps engineers is designing and implementing automation tools and processes that streamline the development, testing, and deployment of applications in the cloud. This includes tools for CI/CD, configuration management, and
Infrastructure as Code
(
IaC
). DevOps engineers are also responsible for managing and monitoring cloud environments, ensuring that applications are performing optimally, and identifying and resolving issues as they arise. They collaborate with development and operations teams to design and implement applications effectively and develop tools and processes to support their delivery and management.Another critical responsibility of DevOps engineers is implementing security measures to protect data in the cloud. This includes using encryption, access controls, and security monitoring to safeguard data from unauthorized access or theft. DevOps engineers may also be responsible for managing vendor relationships with cloud providers, negotiating contracts and
service-level agreements
(
SLAs
), and providing technical guidance and support to development and operations teams working with cloud environments. This includes understanding the capabilities and limitations of different cloud providers, negotiating pricing and other terms, and troubleshooting and problem-solving assistance. By fulfilling these responsibilities, DevOps engineers can help organizations successfully transition to cloud infrastructure and realize the many benefits it offers, including greater scalability, flexibility, and cost-effectiveness.The role of DevOps engineering in the transition to cloud infrastructure involves the design and management of tools and processes, as well as the implementation of security measures, vendor management, and technical guidance and support. It is critical in the transition to cloud infrastructure, as it helps organizations design and manage the tools and processes that are needed to deliver and manage applications at scale. The use of public cloud platforms has enabled organizations to adopt a more agile and flexible approach to infrastructure management, with an emphasis on automation, continuous delivery, and continuous improvement. This has been facilitated by the development of a range of DevOps tools and practices that support the delivery and management of applications at scale.Several tools and practices have been developed to support the adoption of DevOps in the cloud. One of the most important practices is
CI/CD
. CI/CD tools automate the process of building, testing, and deploying applications, allowing organizations to rapidly deliver new features and updates to their customers. Another key practice is
IaC
, which allows organizations to define and manage their infrastructure using code. This allows for the automation of the provisioning and management of infrastructure, as well as for the replication and scaling of environments.
Configuration management
is another important practice that enables organizations to automate the management of configurations and dependencies, ensuring that applications and services are consistently deployed and configured across different environments. Finally,
monitoring and logging
are essential tools for tracking the performance and availability of applications and services in the cloud. These tools allow organizations to identify and resolve issues as they arise, ensuring that their systems are reliable and highly available.The shift toward cloud infrastructure has driven the adoption of a more agile and flexible approach to infrastructure management, with an emphasis on automation, continuous delivery, and continuous improvement. This approach is based on the principles of DevOps, which promotes collaboration between development and operations teams to improve the speed and reliability of software delivery. An agile and flexible approach to infrastructure management includes multiple key characteristics.
Automation
is a key principle of DevOps, and it is used to streamline the delivery and management of applications and services in the cloud. Automation tools and processes can be used to build, test, and deploy applications, as well as to manage and monitor cloud environments. Continuous delivery involves building, testing, and releasing code changes frequently and in small increments, rather than in large, infrequent releases. This allows organizations to rapidly deliver new features and updates to their customers and respond more quickly to changing business needs. Continuous improvement is a key characteristic of an agile and flexible approach to infrastructure management. It involves regularly reviewing and analyzing processes and tools, identifying areas for improvement, and implementing changes to improve efficiency and effectiveness.The combination of cloud computing and DevOps provides an agile and flexible approach to infrastructure management, with an emphasis on automation, continuous delivery, and continuous improvement. This allows organizations to adapt more easily to changing business needs and to deliver and manage applications and services more efficiently and effectively in the cloud. As organizations adopt cloud computing and DevOps approaches to infrastructure management, tools and practices tend to be fragmented within a company. An important next step in the process of adoption of DevOps is to standardize and scale these tools and practices.
Standardizing and scaling DevOps
As organizations adopt public cloud platforms and shift toward a more agile and flexible approach to infrastructure management, the importance of standardizing and automating processes becomes increasingly clear. In order to maintain efficiency and realize the full benefits of the cloud, it is important for organizations to have consistent, repeatable processes in place for infrastructure management.
Defining DevOps maturity
There are different levels of DevOps maturity that organizations can progress through as they adopt DevOps practices. At the lowest level, organizations may have a basic understanding of DevOps principles and may have implemented some basic automation processes. As they progress through the different levels of maturity, organizations may implement more advanced automation processes and adopt a more robust culture of collaboration and continuous improvement.One way to understand the progress and effectiveness of an organization's DevOps practices is to use a DevOps maturity model. A DevOps maturity model is a framework that helps organizations to assess their current level of DevOps maturity and to identify areas for improvement. There are many different DevOps maturity models available, but most of them generally include the following levels:
Level 1: Siloed
: At this level, development and operations teams are largely separate and operate in silos, with little collaboration or coordination between them. There may be limited automation and few processes in place to support the delivery and management of applications.
Level 2: Coordinated
: At this level, development and operations teams begin to coordinate their efforts and work more closely together. There may be some automation in place, but processes are often ad hoc and inconsistent.
Level 3: Integrated
: At this level, development and operations teams are fully integrated and work collaboratively to deliver and manage applications. There is a greater focus on automation and on standardizing processes to support the delivery and management of applications.
Level 4: Continuous
: At this level, development and operations teams have fully embraced DevOps principles and practices and are able to continuously deliver and manage applications. There is a strong focus on automation, continuous improvement, and continuous learning, and processes are fully integrated and optimized.
In addition to the general DevOps maturity model just described, there are also several specific maturity models that organizations can use to assess their DevOps practices. The most widespread is the
DevOps Maturity Model
(
DOMM
), which is a framework that was developed to help organizations assess their DevOps practices. The DOMM defines five levels of DevOps maturity, as set out here:
Level 1: Initial
: At this level, organizations have little or no automation and little or no collaboration between development and operations teams.
Level 2: Managed
: At this level, organizations have some automation in place and have established basic processes to support the delivery and management of applications.
Level 3: Defined
: At this level, organizations have fully defined and standardized their DevOps processes and have a strong focus on automation.
Level 4: Measured
: At this level, organizations have fully integrated their DevOps processes and are able to continuously monitor and optimize them.
Level 5: Optimized
: At this level, organizations have fully optimized their DevOps processes and are able to continuously learn and improve.
These levels are shown in
Figure 1.2
:
Figure 1.2 – DOMM
All maturity models, including the DOMM, can help organizations understand their current level of DevOps maturity and identify areas for improvement as they progress through the different levels. They also play a critical role in scaling engineering organizations, to set and maintain standards across different teams.While most maturity models focus on the technical competencies or implementation of DevOps practices, an interesting model is the
Four Stages of DevOps Maturity
, as it highlights the learning curve and organizational changes experienced by companies. As described by Mike Kavis in his
Forbes
article
The Four Stages of DevOps Maturity
, the four stages can be summarized as follows:
DevOps Denial and Misinterpretation
(
Unconscious Incompetence
): In this stage, organizations lack an understanding of what DevOps is and its business benefits. They often misinterpret DevOps as a threat to their existing roles and resist change. This stage is marked by a siloed operating model and skepticism about the value of DevOps.
Automation for the Sake of Automation
(
Conscious Incompetence
): Typically occurring in the first 12 to 18 months of a DevOps journey, organizations focus on automation, often in isolation and without much collaboration between departments. While some value is derived from automating tasks, many of these efforts fail to address the broader goals of DevOps. Common anti-patterns in this stage include creating a separate DevOps silo, developers eliminating the need for operations and rebranding sysadmins as DevOps engineers.
Collaboration and Reorganization
(
Conscious Competence
): Usually taking place between the second and fourth years, organizations begin to understand that DevOps involves improving the entire
software development life cycle
(
SDLC
). They focus on collaboration and process change, including security, legal, and compliance departments. In this stage, platform teams emerge, building a layer of enterprise guardrails and providing self-service capabilities for development teams.
A High-Performing Organization
(
Unconscious Competence
): In this final stage, organizations can deploy multiple times a day with minimal risk, and removing bottlenecks becomes a way of life. Every aspect of the organization is continuously optimized, including shifting responsibilities to
business units
(
BUs
) and transforming them into self-sufficient organizations with full-stack teams.
The Four Stages of DevOps Maturity model provides valuable insights into the learning curve and organizational transformation experienced by companies as they embrace DevOps practices. This model emphasizes the importance of understanding, collaboration, and continuous improvement in achieving a successful DevOps transformation, as shown in
Figure 1.3
:
Figure 1.3 – Burch's learning model: four stages of competence
Some key indicators of a mature DevOps organization include high levels of automation, strong collaboration between teams, and a focus on continuous improvement. These organizations are able to deliver software updates and new features more quickly and reliably, with fewer errors and less downtime. However, scaling DevOps practices in large organizations can be challenging. There may be resistance to change, particularly if there are longstanding silos between development and operations teams. In addition, there may be technical challenges in implementing automation processes and integrating them with existing systems.
Beyond maturity – DevOps at scale
Scaling DevOps in large organizations presents unique challenges that can hinder successful implementation. These challenges include culture, processes, and tools. Establishing a DevOps culture in a large organization requires a significant shift in mindset and behaviors, which can be challenging. Additionally, large organizations may have complex processes and systems in place that can be difficult to change or integrate with DevOps practices, making automation and continuous improvement challenging. Finally, integrating a wide range of tools and technologies can be difficult and can create inconsistencies between teams. To overcome these challenges, organizations can adopt several strategies. One approach is to start small, implementing DevOps practices in a pilot project to build momentum and demonstrate the benefits of DevOps.Engaging leadership is also critical, as it helps to ensure the necessary resources and support are in place to implement DevOps practices. Training and education on new tools and processes, as well as on the principles and practices of DevOps, can also be crucial to the success of DevOps practices in large organizations. In addition to standardizing and automating processes, there are several key indicators of a mature DevOps organization. Such an organization has implemented automation tools and processes to support the delivery and management of applications and services, including automation of build and deployment processes, testing, and infrastructure management. Additionally, the organization has fostered strong collaboration and communication between development and operations teams, as well as other teams such as security, quality assurance, and IT. This collaboration is critical to the success of DevOps practices as it enables teams to work together more effectively. Finally, a strong focus on continuous improvement is essential, regularly reviewing and analyzing processes and tools and implementing changes to improve efficiency and effectiveness. This may involve using techniques such as CI/CD and using metrics to track and measure progress.
The standardization and automation of processes are important aspects of the shift toward a more agile and flexible approach to infrastructure management. By adopting DevOps practices, organizations can improve the speed and reliability of software delivery and achieve higher levels of efficiency and productivity. However, scaling DevOps in large organizations can be challenging, and requires careful planning and a phased approach. A crucial aspect of scaling DevOps in large organizations is the adoption of standards and best practices, in relation to infrastructure management, across different teams.
Platform engineering involves the creation and management of a shared infrastructure platform that provides standardized and automated tools and services for development and operations teams. By leveraging platform engineering, organizations can streamline the deployment and management of software applications, reduce
time to market
(
TTM
), and enhance the overall quality and reliability of software products. Platform engineering is a critical component of the DevOps journey, enabling organizations to scale their DevOps practices in a sustainable and efficient manner. We will further explore the importance of platform engineering in DevOps in the next section.
Determining the role of platform engineering in scaling DevOps practices
As organizations progress through their DevOps journey and mature in their practices, they may reach a point where they need to take the next step in their evolution. This may involve moving beyond the traditional focus on application delivery and management to a more holistic approach that encompasses the entire technology stack, from hardware and infrastructure to applications and services. This is where platform engineering comes into play.Platform engineering involves the design and management of platforms that support the delivery and management of applications and services at scale. This may involve building and maintaining infrastructure and tools that support the deployment, scaling, and management of applications, as well as designing and implementing processes and practices that support the delivery and management of applications and services. Platform engineering can be seen as the next level on the DevOps maturity model, as it involves taking a more comprehensive and strategic approach to the delivery and management of applications and services. This may involve building and maintaining platforms that are flexible, scalable, and highly automated, as well as adopting practices such as CI/CD and IaC to support the delivery and management of applications and services. Platform engineering represents a more comprehensive and strategic approach to DevOps and the delivery and management of applications and services at scale.A well-designed platform is an important component in the successful implementation of DevOps practices for organizations. It can provide a range of benefits, such as improved efficiency, faster delivery times, and reduced risk. By streamlining and optimizing processes, a well-designed platform can help organizations to reduce the time and effort required to deliver and manage applications and services. This includes automating key tasks and processes such as testing, deployment, and infrastructure management. With a well-designed platform, organizations can deliver applications and services more rapidly, allowing them to respond quickly to changing business needs and deliver new features and updates to their customers. Additionally, a well-designed platform can help organizations to reduce risk by automating key tasks and processes, adopting practices such as CI/CD, and using IaC to ensure that systems are consistently configured and compliant. By incorporating these practices into their platform, organizations can achieve greater efficiency, speed, and security in their DevOps processes.Platform engineering plays a critical role in scaling DevOps practices, as it involves the design and management of platforms that support the delivery and management of applications and services at scale. This may involve building and maintaining cloud platforms that support the deployment, scaling, and management of applications, as well as designing and implementing processes and practices that support the delivery and management of applications and services. One of the key benefits of platform engineering is its ability to solve DevOps scaling issues. As organizations grow and their needs evolve, they may face challenges in scaling their DevOps practices to meet changing demands. Platform engineering can help to overcome these challenges by providing the infrastructure and tools needed to support the delivery and management of applications and services at scale. In addition to solving DevOps scaling issues, platform engineering can also drive the adoption of DevOps best practices and standards at scale. By building and maintaining platforms that support the delivery and management of applications and services, organizations can more easily adopt and implement DevOps practices such as CI/CD and IaC. This can help to ensure that organizations are able to deliver and manage applications and services in a consistent and effective manner even as they scale, as shown in the following screenshot:
Figure 1.4 – Platform engineering in the DevOps journey
The role of platform engineering in scaling DevOps practices is critical, as it involves the design and management of platforms that support the delivery and management of applications and services at scale. By solving DevOps scaling issues and driving the adoption of DevOps best practices and standards at scale, platform engineering can help organizations to more effectively deliver and manage their systems and better meet the needs of their customers.

Platform engineering involves designing, building, and managing the underlying infrastructure, services, and tools that enable organizations to deliver and operate their applications at scale.In this chapter, we will explore the world of platform engineering, defining what it is and how it differs from other common operating models such as DevOps and SRE.
