The complexity of delivering SaaS today
Delivering
Software as a Service
(
SaaS
) has become increasingly complex in recent years, driven by the rise of cloud computing, microservices, and other technologies. In this section, we will examine the various factors that contribute to the complexity of delivering
SaaS
today, including the different SaaS delivery architectures and associated challenges.
Architectural complexity of SaaS
There are several SaaS architectures in use today, each with their own unique challenges and considerations. Let’s look at a few options.
Single vs. multi-tenant
A common architectural decision SaaS providers have to make today is choosing between single or multi-tenant architectures.
Single-tenant architectures
involve each customer having their own dedicated instance of the software application, while multi-tenant architectures involve multiple customers sharing the same instance. Single-tenant architectures provide greater control and customization options for customers but can be more complex to manage and scale.
Multi-tenant architectures
, on the other hand, offer greater scalability and efficiency but can be more challenging to customize and secure.
Deciding between single or multi-tenant architectures has a major implication on how an application is deployed and scaled as shown in the following screenshot:
Figure 2.1- Deployment of single vs multi-tenant application
Other challenges associated with single and multi-tenant architectures include:
Managing data isolation and security
: In single-tenant architectures, data is isolated per customer, which can make it easier to manage security and compliance requirements. However, this also means that there are multiple instances of the application to manage and secure. In multi-tenant architectures, backends and databases are shared between customers, which can make it more challenging to ensure data isolation and protect against data breaches.
Providing customizations and upgrades for individual customers
: Single-tenant architectures provide greater flexibility for customizations and upgrades, as each customer has their own dedicated instance. However, this can also lead to a more complex release management process, as each customer's instance needs to be upgraded separately. In multi-tenant architectures, customizations and upgrades need to be applied across all customers, which can be more challenging to manage.
Ensuring scalability and performance across multiple customers and instances
: Multi-tenant architectures provide greater scalability and efficiency, as multiple customers can be served from the same instance. However, this also means that performance and scalability issues can affect multiple customers, which can have a significant impact on the user experience. In single-tenant architectures, each customer has their own dedicated instance, which can make it easier to isolate performance and scalability issues.
Microservices
Microservices architectures involve breaking down software applications into smaller, independent components that can be developed and deployed separately. Containerization technologies such as Docker and Kubernetes enable the packaging and deployment of microservices in a scalable and efficient way. This also has a significant impact on the complexity of a system as shown below.
Figure 2.2- Monolithic vs Microservice architecture
Challenges associated with microservices, and containerization include:
Managing the complexity of distributed systems
: Microservices architectures can be more complex to manage than monolithic architectures, as there are multiple components to coordinate and manage. Containerization adds an additional layer of complexity, as containers need to be orchestrated and managed across multiple hosts and environments.
Ensuring reliability and availability across multiple services and instances
: In microservices architectures, a failure in one service can affect the availability and reliability of other services. This can make it more challenging to ensure high availability and reliability across the entire application. Containerization can help mitigate this by enabling automatic failover and scaling of containers.
Managing orchestration and coordination between services and containers
: Microservices architectures require effective orchestration and coordination between services and containers. This includes managing service discovery, load balancing, and communication between services and containers
Cloud-native
Cloud-native technologies such as serverless computing, functions-as-a-service, and event-driven architectures provide new ways of building and delivering software applications as services. These technologies enable greater scalability, agility, and efficiency, but also introduce new challenges.Challenges associated with cloud-native technologies include:
Managing statelessness and data persistence in serverless environments
: Serverless computing environments are designed to be stateless, which can make it challenging to manage data persistence and ensure consistency across multiple invocations. This requires careful consideration of data management and persistence strategies.
Ensuring security and compliance in distributed systems
: Cloud-native architectures are often distributed across multiple services and environments, which can make it more challenging to ensure security and compliance. This requires effective access control, data encryption, and monitoring across all services and environments.
Managing the complexity of event-driven architectures and event processing
: Event-driven architectures are designed to be loosely coupled and event-driven, which can make them more scalable and flexible. However, this also adds complexity to the application, as events need to be processed and coordinated across multiple services and environments. Effective event processing and coordination is essential to ensure the reliability and performance of the application.
SaaS providers face a range of challenges when designing, building, and delivering software applications as services. These challenges span multiple areas, including scalability, security, performance, and compliance.
Scalability
is a key challenge for SaaS providers, as they need to be able to accommodate a rapidly growing number of users and customers while maintaining high levels of performance and availability. Scalability challenges can arise at multiple levels, including infrastructure, application design, and database architecture. Infrastructure scalability requires the ability to scale up or down compute and storage resources quickly and efficiently. This can be achieved through cloud-based infrastructure and services, such as auto-scaling groups, load balancers, and managed databases. Application scalability requires designing applications that can handle a large number of users and requests in a scalable and efficient way. This can be achieved through techniques such as horizontal scaling, caching, and sharding. Database scalability requires designing databases that can handle a large amount of data and requests in a scalable and efficient way. This can be achieved through techniques such as database partitioning, replication, and caching.
Security
is another key challenge for SaaS providers, as they need to ensure that their applications and data are protected from unauthorized access, data breaches, and other security threats. Security challenges can arise at multiple levels, including infrastructure, application design, and data management. Infrastructure security requires ensuring that cloud-based infrastructure and services are secure and compliant with relevant standards and regulations. This includes implementing access control, data encryption, and monitoring across all services and environments. Application security requires designing applications that are secure by design, with effective access control, authentication, and authorization mechanisms in place. Data management security requires ensuring that data is secure and compliant with relevant standards and regulations. This includes implementing data encryption, access control, and monitoring across all data stores and environments.
Performance
is a critical challenge for SaaS providers, as users expect fast and responsive applications that can handle large amounts of data and requests. Performance challenges can arise at multiple levels, including infrastructure, application design, and database architecture. Performance requires ensuring that cloud-based infrastructure and services are optimized for performance, with low latency, high throughput, and fast response times. This includes optimizing network and storage performance, as well as selecting appropriate instance types and configurations. Application performance requires designing applications that are optimized for performance, with efficient algorithms, caching, and other techniques in place. Database performance requires designing databases that are optimized for performance, with appropriate indexing, partitioning, and caching strategies in place.
Compliance
is a critical challenge for SaaS providers, as they need to ensure that their applications and data are compliant with relevant laws, regulations, and standards. Compliance challenges can arise at multiple levels, including infrastructure, application design, and data management. Infrastructure compliance includes implementing data encryption, access control, and monitoring across all services and environments, as well as ensuring compliance with relevant certifications such as SOC 2, PCI DSS, and HIPAA.Application compliance requires designing applications that are compliant with relevant laws, regulations, and standards, with effective data privacy, data protection, and data retention policies in place. Data management compliance requires ensuring that data is compliant with relevant laws, regulations, and standards, including data privacy and data protection regulations such as GDPR and CCPA. This includes implementing appropriate data encryption, access control, and monitoring across all data stores and environments, as well as ensuring compliance with relevant data protection and retention policies.
The role of cloud computing
Cloud computing
has transformed the way software is developed, delivered, and consumed, providing SaaS providers with a range of benefits, including agility, scalability, availability, performance, and cost-efficiency.Cloud computing provides SaaS providers with a range of benefits, including:
Agility
: Cloud computing enables SaaS providers to rapidly develop, test, and deploy software applications, reducing time-to-market and improving responsiveness to user and customer needs.
Scalability
: Cloud computing enables SaaS providers to rapidly scale their infrastructure and applications up or down in response to changing demand, improving availability and reducing costs.
Availability
: Cloud computing provides SaaS providers with high levels of availability and fault tolerance, reducing the risk of service disruptions and improving user and customer satisfaction.
Performance
: Cloud computing provides SaaS providers with high levels of performance and low latency, improving the user and customer experience.
Cost-efficiency
: Cloud computing enables SaaS providers to reduce infrastructure and operational costs by only paying for what they use, reducing capital expenditure and increasing operational efficiency.
This move from traditional on-premises deployment models to cloud based services, plays a significant role in transferring responsibility of different aspects of an application and its underlying infrastructure from the customer to the vendor as in
Figure 2.3
:
Figure 2.3- Responsibility model for each layer of an application from on-prem to SaaS
Moving from a traditional on-premises software model to a SaaS approach, all the underlying system management tasks, become the vendor’s responsibility. As a result, cloud-based SaaS solutions need to be scalable, available, and performant to meet user and customer needs and expectations. Scalability enables SaaS providers to accommodate growing user and customer bases, while availability and performance ensure that users and customers can access and use the application reliably and efficiently.Cloud infrastructure can help SaaS providers reduce costs and improve efficiency by:
Reducing capital expenditure
: Cloud infrastructure enables SaaS providers to avoid the need for expensive hardware and software investments, reducing upfront capital expenditure.
Paying only for what they use
: Cloud infrastructure enables SaaS providers to pay only for the resources they use, reducing operational costs and improving efficiency.
Automating infrastructure management
: Cloud infrastructure provides automation tools and services that enable SaaS providers to manage their infrastructure more efficiently and effectively, reducing operational costs and improving reliability.
Cloud security and compliance are critical for SaaS delivery, as SaaS providers need to ensure that their applications and data are secure and compliant with relevant laws, regulations, and standards. Cloud infrastructure providers offer a range of security and compliance tools and services, enabling SaaS providers to implement effective security and compliance policies and practices. SaaS providers need to ensure that they have effective security and compliance policies and practices in place, and that they work closely with their cloud infrastructure providers to ensure that their applications and data are secure and compliant.
What does a SaaS product look like?
SaaS delivery today requires a cross-functional organization that can deliver value to customers through continuous innovation and improvement. Multiple layers are the basis of the SaaS delivery model, and a cross-functional organization that can manage these layers effectively can deliver high-quality SaaS products as shown in the following screenshot:
Figure 2.4 - Different layers of Software-as-a-Service
Layer 0 – Cloud Infrastructure
The cloud infrastructure layer provides the foundation for all other layers. This layer consists of physical servers, storage, and networking resources that are used to build and deploy SaaS applications. The infrastructure layer is responsible for managing the underlying hardware and software components that enable SaaS applications to run efficiently and securely.Components of cloud infrastructure include:
Compute
: This includes
virtual machines
(
VMs
), containers, and serverless compute services that are used to run the SaaS application.
Storage
: This includes object storage, block storage, and file storage that are used to store data and files associated with the SaaS application.
Networking
: This includes
virtual private cloud
(
VPC
) and
software-defined networking
(
SDN
) services that are used to manage the network connections between the SaaS application and other services.
Tools used include (
Figure 2.5
):
Public and private cloud tools and infrastructure
, such as AWS, Azure, GCP, Openstack, VMWare and more.
Infrastructure as code
(
IaC
) tools such as Terraform and CloudFormation, which allow teams to define, manage and provision infrastructure as code.
Configuration management tools
such as Ansible and Chef, which enable teams to automate infrastructure configuration.
Figure 2.5 – Tools and platforms used for Cloud Infrastructure
Layer 1 – Cloud Tools, Runtime, CI/CD
The cloud tools layer provides a set of software tools and services that developers use to build, test, and deploy SaaS applications. The tools layer includes runtime environments, programming languages, frameworks, and libraries that allow developers to build applications quickly and efficiently. The
CI/CD
(
Continuous Integration/Continuous Deployment
) pipeline ensures that code changes are tested and deployed to production in a repeatable and automated way.Components and tools include:
Infrastructure as code
(
IaC
) tools such as Terraform and CloudFormation, which allow teams to define, manage and provision infrastructure as code.
Configuration management tools
such as Ansible and Chef, which enable teams to automate infrastructure configuration.
Runtime environments
: This includes different runtimes such Kubernetes, VMs, Functions, Heroku etc.
CI/CD tools
: This includes tools for continuous integration and depoloyment, such as Jenkins, Gitlab, ArgoCD and more. Here we also see code quality and code coverage tools such as SonarQube.
Observability
: Tools for monitoring, logging, and application insights. Examples are Prometheus, Elastic, Splunk, Datadog, Grafana and more.
This layer contains most of the traditional DevOps tech stack as shown in
Figure 2.6
:
Figure 2.6- Tools and platforms used in Layer 1
Layer 2 – Onboarding, Billing, Metering
The onboarding, billing, and metering layer is responsible for managing the customer lifecycle, from the initial sign-up process to billing and customer support. This layer includes tools for customer onboarding, subscription management, and metering and billing services. In this layer we usually see in-house built tools, or third-party platform like ChargeBee, DealHub, Zoho, Stripe, and more.Platform engineering is critical for achieving consistent results and minimizing rework by ensuring repeatability across all layers of SaaS delivery. A unified platform developed by platform engineers decreases variability, empowering organizations to scale more efficiently.To accomplish repeatability, a standardized approach to SaaS delivery is necessary. This includes adopting common tools, processes, and practices such as version control systems, configuration management tools, and automated testing frameworks. Furthermore, organizations must guarantee that all product teams utilize the same cloud infrastructure, tools, and runtime environments.Implementing a shared platform offering a consistent toolset and services across SaaS delivery layers is key. Platform engineers collaborate with product teams to establish and apply best practices for building and deploying applications. These practices include automated testing,
Continuous Integration and Continuous Delivery
(
CI/CD
) pipelines, and
service level objectives
(
SLOs
).By establishing a unified platform and ensuring all products are built upon it, organizations can enhance the consistency and efficiency of their SaaS delivery. This not only reduces the time and effort needed to create and deploy new products but also boosts their quality and reliability.In the next section, we will discuss the scope of platform engineering and how it differs.
Where does the platform end and the product begin?
Teams that start exploring Platform Engineering start with a common question. What is scope of a platform. Looking into the different layers of a SaaS service, a platform can span all or any of the areas that could be replicated across different teams or applications as in
Figure 2.7
. Although this is not a definitive answer, its usually the deciding factor for most companies on where the platform efforts should start and what the platform boundaries should be:
Figure 2.7 - Defining the scope of Platform Engineering
Layer 0
Layer 0
of SaaS delivery includes the cloud infrastructure on which the platform and products are built. This layer includes everything from physical data centers and servers to the virtualized resources provided by cloud service providers. The infrastructure layer provides the foundation for the rest of the layers, and any issues or inefficiencies at this layer can have a significant impact on the entire SaaS delivery process.Platform engineers must unify Layer 0 by providing a consistent and reliable infrastructure layer that meets the needs of all products built on top of it. This includes ensuring that the infrastructure is scalable, secure, and highly available. It also includes providing a set of standard services and tools that can be used by all product teams.In the context of platform engineering, cloud infrastructure teams are responsible for unifying Layer 0 of the platform, which is the cloud infrastructure layer. This includes managing and maintaining the underlying infrastructure that supports the platform, such as the compute, storage, and networking resources, as well as the cloud services and tools used to deploy and manage these resources.Cloud infrastructure teams typically work closely with other teams such as development and operations teams to ensure that the platform is built with scalability, availability, and security in mind. They also ensure that the platform can easily integrate with the cloud services and tools used by other teams in the organization. Additionally, cloud infrastructure teams may be responsible for implementing and managing automation and monitoring tools to ensure that the platform is operating efficiently and effectively
Layer 1
In addition, platform engineers must develop best practices for configuring and managing the infrastructure layer, including the use of automation tools and monitoring systems. This includes setting up auto-scaling policies, ensuring that backups and disaster recovery plans are in place, and monitoring system health and performance.Unifying
Layer 1
of the platform is the responsibility of the internal developer platform team. This layer includes the tools and services that developers use to build and deploy applications on the platform. The goal of this layer is to provide a seamless development experience for developers, so they can focus on building high-quality applications instead of worrying about infrastructure.The internal developer platform team is responsible for providing a set of tools and services that allow developers to build, test, and deploy applications quickly and easily. This includes providing a standard development environment, with tools such as integrated development environments (IDEs) and version control systems (VCSs), as well as build and deployment automation tools, such as continuous integration and continuous deployment (CI/CD) pipelines.Additionally, the internal developer platform team may also provide a set of libraries and frameworks that developers can use to build their applications more quickly and easily. These may include pre-built modules for common functionality, such as authentication and authorization, as well as tools for monitoring and logging applications.The internal developer platform team also plays a critical role in ensuring that the platform is secure, by providing secure development practices and tools for developers. This includes implementing security policies and standards, as well as providing tools for vulnerability scanning and threat detection.
Layer 2
The SaaS Engineering layer is the layer that enables software to be delivered as a cloud service. This is the layer where Platform Engineering is different from product development because the logic in this layer is shareable across different applications within a company.The team responsible for this layer is the SaaS Engineering team, which consists of software engineers who build the business logic that drives the SaaS application. The team's responsibilities include implementing features such as authentication, authorization, billing, metering, and customer on-boarding.One of the key challenges of SaaS Engineering is balancing the need for flexibility with the need for standardization. On the one hand, the team needs to be able to build custom features to meet the needs of individual customers. On the other hand, they must ensure that the code is maintainable and scalable across multiple applications. Achieving this balance requires careful planning and collaboration with other teams, such as the IDP and cloud infrastructure teams.
Authentication
is a critical component of SaaS Engineering because it provides the necessary security controls to protect the application and its data. The SaaS Engineering team must design and implement an authentication and authorization system that can handle a variety of use cases, including multi-factor authentication, social login, and role-based access control.
Billing and metering
are also important components of SaaS Engineering, as they enable the company to monetize the application and provide insights into how the application is being used. The team must design and implement a billing system that can handle different pricing models, such as per user, per feature, or per usage. They must also develop a metering system that can accurately track usage and provide usage reports to customers.
Customer on-boarding
is another key function of SaaS Engineering, as it provides the necessary tools and documentation for customers to get started with the application. The SaaS Engineering team must design and implement a customer on-boarding process that is easy to use and provides the necessary guidance for customers to get up and running quickly.
Defining platform boundaries
When building SaaS products, it is important to understand the distinction between platform and product. The platform provides the foundation on which multiple products can be built, while products are the specific applications or services that are delivered to customers. In this section, we will explore the relationship between platform and product, platform-product boundaries and dependencies, separation of concerns, and API-driven integration of platform and product
Platform and product
The relationship between platform and product is symbiotic. The platform provides the underlying infrastructure and services that products can leverage to deliver value to customers, while the products themselves drive the need for new features and capabilities in the platform. The platform is like the engine of a car, while the product is like the body. The engine is what powers the car, but the body is what people see and interact with.For example, a company may build a platform that provides authentication, billing, metering, and customer onboarding services. Products built on this platform can leverage these services to quickly and easily implement these features without having to build them from scratch. As products evolve and new features are needed, the platform can be extended to provide additional services and functionality.
Platform-product boundaries and dependencies
To ensure the success of both the platform and products, it is important to define clear boundaries and dependencies between the two. The platform should provide a standardized set of services and APIs that products can use to implement their specific functionality. At the same time, the platform should not be tightly coupled to any specific product, as this can limit the flexibility and scalability of the platform.Products should also be designed to minimize their dependencies on the platform. While products may rely on certain platform services, they should be able to function independently if those services are not available. This allows products to be built and tested independently of the platform, which can help speed up development and reduce complexity.
Separation of concerns
One of the key principles in platform engineering is the separation of concerns between the platform and product teams. The platform team is responsible for creating and maintaining the tools and services that the product teams use to build and deploy their applications. The product teams, on the other hand, are responsible for using these tools and services to create the applications that provide value to customers.By separating these responsibilities, the platform team can focus on providing a high-quality, scalable, and secure set of tools and services that can be used by all product teams, while the product teams can focus on creating the applications that differentiate the company and provide value to customers.This separation of concerns also allows the platform team to make changes and updates to the platform without disrupting the work of the product teams. By using APIs and other integration points, the platform team can provide new features and services to the product teams without requiring them to make significant changes to their applicationsAPI-driven integration is a key aspect of platform engineering. By exposing APIs and other integration points, the platform team can provide the product teams with the tools and services they need to build and deploy their applications.
API-driven integration
APIs allow the product teams to integrate their applications with the platform services without having to understand the underlying implementation details. This allows the product teams to focus on building their applications and providing value to customers, while the platform team can focus on providing a scalable, secure, and reliable set of services.API-driven integration also allows the platform team to make changes and updates to the platform services without requiring the product teams to make significant changes to their applications. By providing backwards-compatible APIs, the platform team can ensure that existing applications continue to work while new features and services are added to the platform.In conclusion, platform engineering is a critical aspect of SaaS delivery. By providing a set of scalable, secure, and reliable tools and services that can be used by all product teams, platform engineering enables companies to build and deliver high-quality SaaS applications. By defining the scope of the platform, separating concerns between the platform and product teams, and using API-driven integration, platform engineering teams can ensure that the platform is flexible, adaptable, and able to support the needs of the product teams as they evolve over time.As we define the boundaries and tooling associated with platforms, many similarities with existing models become evident. The most common comparison companies see is these between Platform Engineering with DevOps and
Site Reliability Engineering
(
SRE
).
Platform engineering vs DevOps vs SRE
In this section we’ll to explain the commonalities and differences between Platform Engineering, DevOps, and SRE. Most importantly we’ll try to define how these models interact and how an organization can be structured for scale leveraging the best of all.
Comparing platform engineering with DevOps
Platform engineering and DevOps share some similarities in terms of objectives and principles, but they also have distinct differences in scope and responsibilities.Both Platform Engineering and DevOps aim to achieve fast, reliable, and scalable software delivery through automation, collaboration, and continuous improvement. Both approaches require a strong focus on automation of development and deployment pipelines, and both encourage cross-functional collaboration and communication to enable a more streamlined and efficient software delivery process.However, while DevOps focuses on the entire software development and delivery lifecycle, Platform Engineering is a more specialized approach that focuses specifically on building and maintaining internal platforms and tools to support development and delivery processes.To further illustrate the difference between Platform Engineering and DevOps, we can use the analogy of
Agile and Scaled Agile
(
SAFe
). Agile is a set of principles and practices for software development that emphasizes flexibility, collaboration, and continuous improvement. Similarly, DevOps is a set of principles and practices that emphasizes communication, automation, and collaboration across development and operations teams to enable fast and reliable software delivery.SAFe, on the other hand, is a framework that helps organizations scale Agile principles and practices across the enterprise. Similarly, platform engineering can be seen as a way to scale DevOps principles and practices across the organization by building and maintaining internal platforms and tools that support fast and efficient software delivery.Collaboration between platform engineering and DevOps is critical to achieving fast, reliable, and scalable software delivery. Platform engineering teams build and maintain the internal platforms and tools that enable DevOps teams to deliver software more efficiently and effectively.DevOps teams rely on platform engineering teams to provide the necessary infrastructure and tools to support their work, and platform engineering teams rely on DevOps teams to provide feedback and insights into how their platforms and tools are being used in practice.Effective collaboration between platform engineering and DevOps teams requires clear communication, shared objectives, and a willingness to work together to achieve common goals. This collaboration can take many forms, from joint planning and design sessions to regular check-ins and retrospectives to ensure that the development and delivery process is streamlined and efficient.Overall, while there are similarities between platform engineering and DevOps, the differences in scope and responsibilities make them distinct approaches that require different skills and expertise. Collaboration between the two is critical to achieving fast, reliable, and scalable software delivery in today's complex software development landscape.While platform engineering and DevOps have different focuses and objectives, they share a common goal of delivering high-quality software efficiently and reliably. Collaboration between these two teams is essential for achieving this goal.One area where platform engineering and DevOps can collaborate is in the deployment process. Platform engineering teams can provide standardized deployment scripts and tools to DevOps teams, enabling them to deploy their applications quickly and consistently. They can also provide best practices for monitoring and scaling applications in production, which can help DevOps teams to maintain the reliability of their applications.Another area where platform engineering and DevOps can collaborate is in the development process. Platform engineering teams can work with DevOps teams to define standards and best practices for building and deploying applications. This can include things like code quality standards, version control practices, and automated testing frameworks. By working together to define these standards, both teams can ensure that applications are built and deployed in a consistent, reliable manner.Overall, collaboration between platform engineering and DevOps is essential for delivering high-quality software efficiently and reliably. By working together, these teams can ensure that applications are built, deployed, and maintained in a consistent, reliable manner, ultimately leading to better outcomes for customers and the business.To elaborate further, platform engineering and DevOps have different focuses and objectives. While DevOps is focused on creating a culture of collaboration and agility across development and operations teams, platform engineering is primarily focused on building and maintaining the internal platform that enables developers to deliver software more efficiently.DevOps is often associated with practices such as continuous integration and delivery, automated testing, and infrastructure automation, which aim to accelerate the software development lifecycle and improve the quality of the code. Platform engineering, on the other hand, focuses on building and maintaining the underlying infrastructure, tools, and processes that enable DevOps practices to function effectively.One way to think about the relationship between the two is to see DevOps as the goal, while platform engineering is the means to achieve that goal. In other words, DevOps represents the end result of a well-functioning software delivery process, while Platform Engineering is the set of tools, processes, and best practices that enable that process to happen.Despite their different focuses, platform engineering and DevOps can collaborate and work together towards a common goal. For example, platform engineering can provide the necessary infrastructure and tools to enable DevOps practices, such as automated testing and deployment. DevOps teams can also provide valuable feedback to Platform Engineering on the usability and effectiveness of their tools and processes.In summary, while platform engineering and DevOps have different focuses and objectives, they can work together collaboratively towards a common goal of delivering high-quality software efficiently and effectively.
Comparing platform engineering with SRE
While platform engineering focuses on building internal tools and products to enable software development teams, SRE, on the other hand, is about ensuring the reliability and uptime of the production environment. Both roles are crucial to the success of any modern software organization. However, the approach and responsibilities of each differ significantly.Platform engineering aims to create a unified, standardized set of internal tools and services that can be used across multiple products and teams. By doing so, platform engineers can help ensure that development teams have the necessary tools and infrastructure to build, test, and deploy software more efficiently and effectively. In contrast, SREs are responsible for maintaining the reliability and uptime of production services. This includes monitoring, debugging, and fixing issues that arise, as well as implementing strategies to prevent them from occurring in the first place.Platform engineering and
Site Reliability Engineering
(
SRE
) have many similarities, but also some key differences. Both focus on ensuring the reliability, scalability, and efficiency of a company's systems, and both involve the use of automation and monitoring tools. However, while platform engineering focuses on building and maintaining internal tools and products that can be reused across different applications, SRE focuses on implementing necessary tools for the reliability of a specific product. SRE also places a stronger emphasis on incident response and post-mortem analysis than platform engineering.While platform engineering and SRE have different goals and responsibilities, collaboration between the two is essential for ensuring that software delivery teams can build and deploy reliable, scalable applications. Platform engineering teams can provide SREs with the internal tools and infrastructure needed to maintain and improve the reliability of production services. This can include automation tools, testing frameworks, and monitoring services. SREs, in turn, can provide platform engineers with feedback on the reliability and scalability of the platform's internal services, helping to identify areas for improvement and optimization.An example of how platform engineering and SRE can collaborate is through the use of a shared observability platform. A platform engineering team might build an internal observability platform that provides developers with real-time insights into the performance and behavior of their applications. This platform might include features such as distributed tracing, log aggregation, and performance monitoring.SREs can leverage this platform to help ensure the reliability and uptime of production services. They can use the platform to quickly identify and diagnose issues that arise, as well as to proactively monitor and prevent potential issues from occurring. By working closely with platform engineering teams, SREs can provide feedback on the effectiveness and efficiency of the observability platform, helping to identify areas for improvement and optimization.Overall, while platform engineering and SRE have different goals and responsibilities, collaboration between the two is essential for building and deploying reliable, scalable software applications. By working together, these teams can ensure that software delivery teams have the tools and infrastructure needed to build and maintain high-quality applications.An example of how platform engineering and SRE can work together is as follows: the platform engineering team builds an internal tool for distributed tracing that can be used by all applications within the company. The SRE team then uses this tool to monitor and troubleshoot performance issues in their products. The SRE team can provide feedback to the platform engineering team on how to improve the tool, which can then be used to benefit all applications in the company. In this way, the SRE team is the
customer
of the platform engineering team, and the platform engineering team is responsible for meeting their needs to ensure the reliability and efficiency of the products they support.
Choosing the right approach for your organization
In order to choose the right approach for your organization, it is important to consider a number of factors. These factors will depend on the specific needs and goals of your organization, as well as the resources that are available to you.Some factors to consider when choosing the right approach for your organization include:
Organizational structure
: The structure of your organization can have a significant impact on the approach that you choose. For example, if you have a centralized IT team, it may be more appropriate to adopt a platform engineering approach. On the other hand, if your organization is highly decentralized (each product team has full autonomy to build and run their services how they want), an embedded SRE approach may be more effective.
Size and complexity of your organization
: The size and complexity of your organization will also play a role in determining the approach that you take. For example, if you have a small organization with a limited number of applications, a shared platform approach may be the most appropriate. However, if you have a large, complex organization with many different applications, an embedded SRE approach may be more effective.
Budget and resources
: The resources that are available to your organization will also be a factor in determining the approach that you take. For example, if you have limited resources, a shared platform approach may be more feasible. However, if you have more resources available, an embedded SRE approach may provide greater benefits.
Goals and objectives
: Finally, your organization's goals and objectives will also play a role in determining the approach that you take. For example, if you are focused on improving agility and speed to market, a platform engineering approach may be the best fit. However, if you are focused on improving reliability and uptime, an embedded SRE approach may be more effective.
It is also important to tailor the approach that you choose to the specific needs of your organization. This may involve adapting existing methodologies or practices, or even developing new ones.For example, if you decide to adopt a platform engineering approach, you may need to customize your internal developer platform to meet the specific needs of your organization. Similarly, if you choose an embedded SRE approach, you may need to tailor your SRE team's responsibilities and processes to fit within your existing organizational structure.One common approach that many organizations are adopting is a combination of shared platform and embedded SREs. This approach involves building a shared internal developer platform that can be used by all development teams within the organization, while also embedding SREs within each development team to ensure that reliability is built into each application from the start.This approach has a number of benefits, including improved collaboration between development and operations teams, increased reliability and uptime, and faster time to market for new applications and features.Ultimately, the approach that you choose will depend on a variety of factors, including the needs and goals of your organization, the resources that are available to you, and the structure of your organization. By carefully considering these factors, and tailoring your approach to meet your specific needs, you can ensure that your organization is able to deliver reliable, scalable, and high-quality cloud-based applications and service.
Summary
Throughout this chapter, we've explored the intricacies of delivering SaaS today. We delved into the different layers involved in SaaS delivery and how they each contribute to the success of a SaaS product as well as how these can overlap between different product engineering teams. With that in mind, we were able to define Platform Engineering and the usual boundaries that it might span.We then explored the fine line between a platform and a product, and how to define and identify the boundaries between the two. We also discussed the differences between platform engineering, DevOps, and SRE, and the factors to consider when choosing the right approach for your organization.The world of SaaS delivery is complex, but we hope this chapter has shed some light on the different layers and components involved. We've provided insights into how these layers can be unified into a cohesive structure, and form a platform to be leveraged for SaaS delivery success.In the next chapter, we'll dive deeper into the world of platform engineering and try to understand the
why
behind platform engineering.

3 Understanding the Business Value of Platform engineering
Join our book community on Discord
https://packt.link/IOzft
In today's rapidly evolving technology landscape, businesses are increasingly adopting cloud platforms to deliver applications and services at scale. platform engineering has emerged as a critical discipline to address the challenges and complexities associated with managing cloud infrastructure. In this chapter, we will explore the business value of platform engineering and delve into the four key pillars that drive platforms today: improving developer experience, increasing productivity, ensuring security and compliance, and promoting best practices and standards.Understanding the business value of platform engineering is essential for organizations to prioritize investments, align technology initiatives with strategic goals, and drive digital transformations.In this chapter we will cover the following topics:
The business case for platform engineering-
Pillar 1: Improving developer experience
Pillar 2: Enhancing developer productivity
Pillar 3: Ensuring security and compliance
Pillar 4: Promoting best practices
Strategic aspects of platform engineering
This chapter will help you have a deeper understanding of the business value of platform engineering and be equipped to leverage its benefits in your organizations. We will start by discussing the business case for platform engineering, followed by a detailed examination of each of the four pillars, and conclude with an overview of the strategic aspects of platform engineering.
The business case for platform engineering
platform engineering is a rapidly growing discipline that focuses on the planning, design, and management of cloud platforms, helping organizations deliver applications and services at scale.
Why platform engineering matters?
As organizations adopt cloud technologies and scale their operations, the complexity of managing their infrastructure increases exponentially. This complexity can result in higher operational costs, reduced efficiency, and an increased risk of errors and downtime. Platform engineering helps address these challenges by providing a systematic approach to designing, implementing, and managing cloud infrastructure. By focusing on optimizing resource utilization, simplifying management tasks, and streamlining processes, platform engineering enables organizations to manage their infrastructure more efficiently and cost-effectively, ultimately improving their overall performance:
Standardization and automation
: Platform engineering achieves consistency and predictability by standardizing infrastructure components and processes. Through automation, it minimizes manual intervention and accelerates routine tasks, freeing up valuable time and resources.
Driving innovation
: By providing a robust foundation for application development, platform engineering fosters a culture of continuous improvement and innovation. It promotes cross-functional collaboration and knowledge sharing, paving the way for the discovery of new ideas.
Reducing operational overhead
: Platform engineering reduces operational overhead by streamlining processes and improving resource utilization. By simplifying infrastructure management, it enables organizations to allocate more resources to strategic initiatives that drive growth and innovation.
Enabling agility and adaptability
: With a well-designed platform, organizations can adapt their infrastructure to meet evolving requirements. Platform engineering promotes a culture of continuous learning and improvement, ensuring agility and adaptability in the face of rapidly changing technology landscapes.
By focusing on standardization, automation, and continuous improvement, platform engineering enables organizations to optimize their resources, enhance their overall performance, and ultimately, deliver more value to their customers.
Aligning platform engineering with business objectives
For platform engineering to be effective, it is crucial to align its objectives and strategies with the broader business goals of the organization. This ensures that platform engineering efforts contribute to the overall success of the organization and deliver tangible value.The following are the steps to align platform engineering with business objectives:
Identify business goals
: Understand and identify key business goals and KPIs. This will allow platform engineering teams to prioritize projects that directly contribute to the business's success.
Stakeholder collaboration
: Collaborate with stakeholders across the organization, including product managers, developers, and business leaders. Their input can help platform engineering teams better understand the organization's priorities and challenges.
Technical solution alignment
: Ensure that all technical solutions meet stakeholder requirements and align with business needs. This involves balancing between short-term needs and long-term strategic goals.
Strategy evaluation and adjustment
: With the constant evolution of the business environment and technology landscape, it's crucial to continually evaluate and adjust platform engineering strategies. This will help in staying aligned with the changing business objectives.
Effective communication
: Communicate the value of platform engineering to decision-makers within the organization, showcasing its benefits and impacts on innovation and operational efficiency.
By effectively articulating the value of platform engineering, teams can gain the support and buy-in of business leaders, ensuring that platform engineering remains a strategic priority for the organization.
Key stakeholders and their roles
In order to effectively implement platform engineering and achieve the desired business outcomes, it is crucial to involve key stakeholders from across the organization. In this section, we will discuss the following key stakeholders involved in platform engineering and their respective roles:
Executive sponsors, such as CTOs, CIOs, or other senior business leaders
: They play a pivotal role in ensuring that platform engineering efforts align with the organization's overall strategic objectives. They are responsible for providing strategic direction and guidance, setting priorities, and ensuring that platform engineering initiatives receive the necessary support and resources. Executive sponsors also serve as champions for platform engineering within the organization, helping to communicate the value and benefits of the initiative to other stakeholders and decision-makers.
Platform engineering teams
: They are responsible for the design, implementation, and management of the cloud platform. Their primary goal is to provide a stable, scalable, and flexible infrastructure that enables application development teams to build and deploy solutions quickly and efficiently. Platform engineering teams are responsible for tasks such as selecting the appropriate technologies and tools, designing, and implementing platform services, and ensuring that the platform adheres to security and compliance requirements. They also work closely with other stakeholders, such as developers, IT operations, and security teams, to ensure that the platform meets the organization's needs and supports its strategic objectives.
Developers
: They are the primary consumers of the platform and play a crucial role in the platform engineering process. As the end-users of the platform services, developers provide valuable feedback and insights into the platform's functionality, performance, and usability. This feedback helps the platform engineering teams identify areas for improvement and prioritize new features and enhancements. Developers also play a key role in driving the adoption of the platform within the organization by building and deploying applications on the platform and showcasing its benefits to other teams and stakeholders.
IT operations teams
: They are responsible for ensuring the stability, reliability, and performance of the organization's infrastructure. In the context of platform engineering, IT operations teams collaborate closely with platform engineering teams to manage and monitor the platform, troubleshoot issues, and maintain service levels. They also work together to develop and implement processes and practices that promote operational efficiency, such as automation, monitoring, and incident management. By collaborating with platform engineering teams, IT operations teams can help ensure that the platform meets the organization's operational requirements and delivers a high-quality experience for developers and end-users alike.
Security and compliance teams
: They play a critical role in ensuring that the platform adheres to the organization's security and compliance requirements. They collaborate with platform engineering teams to define and implement security controls, such as access controls, encryption, and vulnerability management. They also work together to develop and enforce policies and processes that promote security best practices and maintain compliance with industry regulations and standards. By working closely with platform engineering teams, security and compliance teams can help ensure that the platform is secure, resilient, and compliant with the organization's requirements.
Each stakeholder group plays a unique role in the platform engineering process and contributes to the overall success of the initiative.
Platform engineering and the four pillars
Platform engineering plays a crucial role in addressing the key business drivers that organizations face today. These drivers can be categorized into four main pillars:
Enhancing the developer experience
: Platform engineering improves the developer experience by providing a robust, scalable platform. Simplifying tasks and automating processes allow developers to work more efficiently, fostering innovation, and accelerating delivery of high-quality applications.
Increasing productivity and efficiency
: Standardizing infrastructure components and automating routine tasks allows platform engineering to reduce operational overhead. This results in faster time to market, improved resource utilization, and higher overall efficiency.
Ensuring a secure and compliant environment
: Platform engineering embeds security controls and best practices into the platform design, ensuring compliance with industry regulations and standards. Working closely with security and compliance teams allows platform engineering to minimize risk and protect sensitive data and systems.
Promoting best practices and standards
: By establishing consistent processes and methodologies, platform engineering ensures adherence to the organization's requirements and industry standards. This optimization enhances overall performance and delivers more value to customers.
The following sections will delve deeper into each of these pillars, providing insights into how platform engineering can be leveraged to maximize value and drive business success.
Pillar 1: Improving developer experience
Developer experience
refers to how developers perceive and interact with the development processes, infrastructure, and tools that are provided to them. It encompasses a range of factors that contribute to their overall satisfaction, productivity, and engagement with the platform, including how they feel about their work, how they value their contributions, and whether they trust the product and its benefits. Additionally, it includes the platform's flexibility and extensibility to accommodate evolving requirements and changing business needs. Developer experience can be expressed in many ways, some include:
Importance of a well-designed platform for developer experience
: A seamless, intuitive interface that simplifies and automates common tasks enhances the developer experience, allowing them to easily navigate the platform's features and functionality.
Empowering developers through a positive developer experience
: A high-quality platform empowers developers with the autonomy and resources they need for their roles. This enables them to focus on writing code and creating innovative solutions.
Enhancing job satisfaction and productivity through developer experience
: A platform that minimizes operational overhead leads to higher job satisfaction, increased engagement, and greater productivity among developers.
Fostering pride and accomplishment in developer work
: By providing necessary tools, resources, and support, platform engineering teams can help developers see the value of their contributions, fostering a sense of ownership and purpose.
Building trust in the platform for a positive developer experience
: Trust can be built by delivering reliable and performant services, providing transparent communication about the platform's capabilities and limitations, and actively addressing developers' concerns.
A platform that is flexible and extensible will enable developers to easily accommodate new requirements, integrate with emerging technologies, and scale their applications to meet growing demands. By prioritizing flexibility and extensibility, platform engineering teams can ensure that their platform remains relevant and valuable to developers, even as the organization's needs and objectives change over time.
Measuring developer experience
A critical aspect of enhancing
Developer Experience
(
DevX
) is the ability to measure and continuously improve it. By identifying and tracking
Key Performance Indicators
(
KPIs
), gathering and analyzing user feedback, implementing continuous improvement processes, and regularly reviewing and adjusting platform offerings, organizations can optimize the developer experience and ensure their platform remains relevant, efficient, and valuable to its users. This section will explore various techniques and best practices for measuring developer experience and fostering a culture of continuous improvement.To effectively measure and improve developer experience, organizations must first identify the KPIs that are most relevant to their specific goals and objectives. It's also important to make a distinction between leading and lagging indicators, as they make a difference on their impact on DevX. Some commonly used DevX KPIs include:
Time to First Hello World
(
TTFHW
): The time it takes for a developer to start using a new platform and produce their first working output (example, a simple "
Hello World !"
application). TTFHW is a leading indicator of how accessible and user-friendly a platform is, as well as the effectiveness of onboarding materials.
Measurement
: Track the time from when a developer first accesses the platform to the moment they successfully run a
"Hello World!"
application.
Onboarding Completion Rate
: The percentage of developers who successfully complete the onboarding process. A high completion rate is a lagging indicator that the onboarding process is effective and provides a smooth transition for developers to start using the platform.
Measurement
: Monitor the number of developers who complete the onboarding process and compare it to the total number of new users.
Developer Satisfaction Score
(
DSS
): The overall satisfaction of developers with the platform, tools, and resources provided. High developer satisfaction is a lagging indicator that leads to increased productivity and a better likelihood of adopting best practices.
Measurement
: Conduct regular developer surveys to gather feedback on their experience with the platform, tools, and resources. Calculate the satisfaction score based on survey results.
IDE Integration
: The compatibility and ease of integration of the platform with popular
Integrated Development Environments
(
IDEs
). IDE integration is a leading indicator of developer productivity and satisfaction.
Measurement
: Assess the availability of platform-specific plugins and extensions for popular IDEs,and gather feedback from developers on their integration experience.
Automation
: The level of automation in internal engineering processes for repetitive tasks. Automation is a leading indicator of developer productivity and satisfaction.
Measurement
: Evaluate the number and quality of automated workflows, tools, and services offered by the platform.
Code Readability, Reusability, and Maintainability
: The quality of the codebase, including its readability, reusability, and maintainability. These metrics are lagging indicators of a strong developer experience, as they impact the ease and efficiency with which developers can work with the codebase.
Measurement
: Conduct regular code reviews, track code complexity metrics, and gather feedback from developers on the code quality.
Maintaining and enhancing the developer experience is a vital part of successful platform engineering. By establishing relevant KPIs, analyzing user feedback, implementing continuous improvement processes, adjusting platform services, and communicating success stories, organizations can optimize the developer experience, ensuring their platform remains efficient and valuable. A data-driven approach allows for informed decisions and resource allocation, leading to improved developer satisfaction and productivity, increased innovation, faster time-to-market, and organizational success. A commitment to ongoing improvement and focus on the developer experience empowers developers to deliver high-quality applications, benefiting not just the developers but also contributing to the overall growth and success of the organization.
Strategies for enhancing developer experience
A few areas that can contribute to improving developer experience include:
Onboarding and documentation for seamless integration
: Create intuitive onboarding processes and thorough, up-to-date documentation to help developers understand the platform effectively. Organize resources logically for easy discovery and encourage user feedback for continual improvements.
Streamlining workflows with tooling and automation
: Identify repetitive tasks for automation to increase efficiency and reduce error. Standardize toolsets and integrate popular tools that developers are familiar with for consistency and improved productivity. Provide clear guidance on effective tool usage.
Establishing effective communication and support channels
: Implement accessible feedback mechanisms such as surveys or forums to gather insights and identify improvement areas. Respond promptly to user concerns, highlighting a commitment to fulfilling their needs and enhancing the developer experience.
Driving developer engagement and satisfaction
: Involve developers in platform decision-making processes to foster ownership and investment. Cultivate a collaborative community to increase engagement, satisfaction, and willingness to contribute to the platform's continual improvement.
By focusing on these key areas, organizations can create a platform that empowers developers, fosters collaboration, and ultimately drives innovation and success.
Importance of developer experience for platform adoption and success
A positive developer experience is crucial for the successful adoption and growth of any platform. Focusing on enhancing the developer experience can lead to a culture of innovation, reduce the learning curve for new developers, improve overall satisfaction and productivity, encourage the adoption of best practices, and facilitate collaboration between teams and disciplines. Encouraging Innovation and Experimentation
Fosters a culture of creativity by providing user-friendly environment, clear documentation, and powerful tools.

Comprehensive onboarding materials and examples shorten the time to productivity, leading to faster development cycles.
Increasing Productivity
A positive developer experience keeps developers engaged and motivated, resulting in higher quality output and faster development cycles.

Tools and resources supporting industry-standard methodologies lead to robust, secure, and maintainable applications, enhancing platform reliability and organization reputation.

Platforms that support communication, version control, and issue tracking foster efficient problem-solving, knowledge sharing, and team cohesion.
