Pillar 2: Enhancing developer productivity
Improving productivity is a primary goal for any organization, especially in the context of platform engineering. Measuring productivity can be challenging because it encompasses various factors that contribute to the successful delivery of projects. This section will discuss several key metrics and frameworks that organizations can use to measure and optimize productivity, including the SPACE framework, DORA metrics, OKRs, resource utilization, and innovation rate.
Key metrics to measure productivity
The
SPACE framework
is a comprehensive model designed to measure developer productivity.
SPACE
is an acronym that stands for
Satisfaction, Performance, Activity, Communication, and Efficiency
. These five dimensions capture the essential aspects of a developer's work experience and can help organizations identify areas where they can improve productivity. By regularly assessing these dimensions, organizations can ensure that their platform engineering efforts are focused on providing a better developer experience, leading to increased productivity. The five dimensions of SPACE can pay an important role on many different aspects of the business, such as alignment, people management, execution. They are shown in
Figure 3.1
:
Figure 3.1 - The SPACE framework and different aspects of the business
The SPACE framework is a comprehensive model for measuring developer productivity, encompassing five dimensions.
Satisfaction
: Developer satisfaction is crucial to retaining top talent and ensuring that they stay motivated and engaged in their work. Satisfaction can be measured through surveys and regular feedback sessions, focusing on factors such as workload, work-life balance, recognition, and opportunities for personal growth. High satisfaction levels can lead to increased productivity, as happy developers are more likely to be committed to their work and collaborate effectively with their team members.
Performance
: Performance refers to the quality and quantity of work produced by developers. This dimension can be measured by tracking metrics like code quality, defect rates, feature completion, and adherence to timelines. Monitoring performance allows organizations to identify areas where developers may be struggling, enabling targeted interventions like training, mentorship, or process improvements to enhance productivity.
Activity
: Activity encompasses the tasks that developers perform daily, such as coding, testing, debugging, and reviewing code. By tracking and analyzing activity data, organizations can identify patterns and trends that can impact productivity, such as bottlenecks in the development process, underutilized skills, or inefficient workflows. Addressing these issues can lead to a more streamlined development process and higher productivity levels.
Communication
: Effective communication is vital for collaboration and knowledge sharing among developers and other stakeholders. Measuring communication can involve evaluating the frequency and quality of interactions, such as team meetings, code reviews, and knowledge sharing sessions. Strong communication can lead to better alignment among team members, resulting in more efficient development processes and improved productivity.
Efficiency
: Efficiency refers to the optimal use of resources, including time, effort, and tools, to accomplish tasks. Tracking efficiency metrics like the time spent on specific tasks, tool usage, and resource allocation can help organizations identify areas for improvement and optimize their development processes. Increased efficiency can lead to higher productivity, as developers can complete tasks more quickly and with fewer resources
The
DevOps Research and Assessment
(
DORA
) metrics are a set of standardized indicators that measure the effectiveness of DevOps practices. By tracking these metrics, organizations can gain insights into their development and operational processes and identify areas for improvement. These metrics can also help organizations understand the impact of platform engineering efforts on their overall productivity and align their goals with industry best practices. The four DORA metrics include:
Deployment Frequency
: This metric reflects the number of times code is deployed to production within a specific period. Higher deployment frequencies can indicate increased productivity, as teams are able to deliver new features and updates more rapidly.
Lead Time for Changes
: Lead time measures the duration from the moment a code change is committed to when it is successfully deployed in production. Shorter lead times are indicative of a more efficient development process, which can lead to higher productivity.
Time to Restore Service
: This metric measures the time it takes to restore a service to normal operation after a failure or incident. Faster restoration times can suggest that teams are more adept at identifying and resolving issues, contributing to increased productivity.
Change Failure Rate
: Change failure rate represents the percentage of deployments that result in a failure, requiring rollback or hotfix. Lower change failure rates are indicative of a more stable and reliable development process, leading to higher productivity.
High performing DevOps organizations have shown significantly greater numbers for DORA metrics, according to the state of DevOps report.
Figure 3.2 - Impact of DORA metrics
Objectives and Key Results
(
OKRs
) are a goal-setting framework that can be used to measure the successful implementation of projects. OKRs consist of a high-level objective and several quantifiable key results that define the achievement of the objective. By using OKRs, organizations can align their platform engineering goals with broader business objectives and ensure that their efforts contribute to the overall success of the company.Resource utilization and cost efficiency are essential metrics to measure when evaluating productivity in platform engineering. These metrics include factors such as infrastructure usage, cost per workload, and the allocation of resources to different projects. By monitoring resource utilization and cost efficiency, organizations can optimize their platform engineering processes to reduce waste, allocate resources more effectively, and ensure that they are getting the most value from their investments. This can lead to increased productivity, as teams can deliver projects on time and within budget while minimizing overhead costs.Innovation rate and time to market are critical metrics that reflect an organization's ability to quickly deliver new features and products. A higher innovation rate indicates that a team is consistently developing and deploying new ideas, while a shorter time to market means that these ideas are reaching customers more quickly. By tracking these metrics, organizations can assess the effectiveness of their platform engineering efforts in driving innovation and delivering value to customers. Improving these metrics can lead to increased productivity, as teams can iterate more quickly, learn from customer feedback, and adapt their strategies to meet changing market demands.
The connection between platform engineering and productivity
Platform engineering plays a critical role in enhancing productivity by creating an environment that supports developers in their work and allows them to focus on their core tasks. This is achieved through standardization, automation, collaboration, and continuous feedback.In this section, we will explore how these elements contribute to increased productivity:
Standardizing processes and toolsets
: platform engineering helps establish standardized processes and toolsets across teams, ensuring consistency and reducing friction in the development process. By implementing uniform coding practices, libraries, and frameworks, developers can work more efficiently, as they don't need to spend time learning different tools or adapting to various methodologies. This standardization also makes it easier to onboard new team members, as they can quickly familiarize themselves with the established processes and tools.
Streamlining workflows and reducing manual tasks
: Platform engineering focuses on optimizing workflows to minimize manual tasks and eliminate unnecessary steps. This can be achieved by integrating tools and systems, simplifying processes, and providing clear guidelines for developers to follow. Streamlined workflows allow developers to work more efficiently and complete tasks faster, thereby increasing their overall productivity.
Automating repetitive tasks and error-prone processes
: One of the key goals of platform engineering is to automate repetitive and error-prone tasks, freeing developers to focus on more valuable and creative work. Examples of automation include automated testing, continuous integration and deployment, and automated monitoring and alerting. By removing the burden of manual tasks, developers can dedicate more time to designing, coding, and optimizing their applications, leading to increased productivity.
Encouraging collaboration and knowledge sharing
: Platform engineering fosters a culture of collaboration and knowledge sharing among team members, breaking down silos and encouraging cross-functional communication. This can be facilitated through shared repositories, code reviews, pair programming, and regular team meetings or stand-ups. By promoting collaboration, platform engineering helps to ensure that developers have access to the knowledge and expertise they need to work more efficiently and effectively, contributing to increased productivity.
Enabling faster feedback loops and iteration
: Platform engineering aims to create an environment where developers can receive quick feedback on their work and iterate on their solutions more rapidly. This can be achieved through continuous integration, automated testing, and deployment pipelines that provide immediate feedback on code quality and performance. Faster feedback loops enable developers to identify and address issues more quickly, reducing the time spent on debugging and allowing them to focus on improving their applications
Developer productivity is a key driver for Platform Engineering, but how could we leverage Platforms to boost productivity?
Boosting productivity through platform engineering
To maximize productivity through platform engineering, organizations must focus on reducing time to market, streamlining operations, and encouraging collaboration. These strategies help drive efficiency, innovation, and overall business success. In this section, we will discuss each of these strategies in detail.Reducing time to market is essential for organizations to stay competitive and respond rapidly to changing market demands. platform engineering can help achieve this goal by:
Implementing continuous integration and continuous delivery
(
CI/CD
): CI/CD pipelines automate the process of building, testing, and deploying applications, allowing developers to catch and fix issues earlier, reduce manual intervention, and accelerate the release of new features.
Encouraging experimentation and fast iteration
: Platform engineering enables developers to quickly prototype and iterate on their ideas, fostering a culture of innovation and experimentation. This agility allows teams to adapt to changing requirements and release new features or updates rapidly.
Streamlining release processes and reducing bottlenecks
: Platform engineering simplifies and standardizes release processes, reducing bottlenecks that can slow down the deployment of new features. By automating and streamlining these processes, organizations can accelerate time to market.
Leveraging cloud-native technologies and services
: Cloud-native technologies, such as containerization and microservices, enable organizations to develop, deploy, and scale applications more quickly and efficiently. By adopting these technologies, organizations can reduce time to market and better respond to changing market conditions.
Ensuring a stable and scalable infrastructure
: Platform engineering provides a reliable and scalable foundation for applications, allowing organizations to rapidly scale their offerings to meet growing demand without sacrificing performance or stability.
Streamlining software operations is critical for increasing productivity and reducing operational overhead. Platform engineering can help organizations achieve this by:
Identifying and removing operational inefficiencies
: Platform engineering teams can analyze existing processes and workflows, identify areas of inefficiency, and implement improvements to streamline operations.
Standardizing and automating common tasks
: By standardizing processes and automating repetitive tasks, platform engineering reduces the manual effort required by developers and operations teams, freeing them to focus on more valuable work.
Consolidating and simplifying infrastructure management
: Platform engineering enables organizations to consolidate and centralize infrastructure management, reducing complexity and making it easier to maintain and scale systems.
Empowering teams with self-service capabilities
: By providing self-service tools and resources, platform engineering allows developers and operations teams to manage their workloads more efficiently, reducing dependencies and delays.
Aligning IT operations with business goals
: Platform engineering ensures that IT operations are aligned with business objectives, helping organizations make more informed decisions and prioritize resources effectively.
Fostering collaboration across teams and disciplines is crucial for improving productivity and driving innovation. Platform engineering can facilitate collaboration by:
Fostering a culture of open communication and knowledge sharing
: Encouraging open communication and knowledge sharing helps teams work more efficiently and effectively by reducing duplication of effort and promoting cross-functional learning.
Implementing collaborative tools and platforms
: Platform engineering provides developers with tools and platforms that enable real-time collaboration, code sharing, and issue tracking, helping teams work more cohesively and efficiently.
Breaking down organizational silos and barriers
: Platform engineering promotes cross-functional collaboration by breaking down silos and encouraging teams to work together toward shared goals.
Incentivizing cross-functional collaboration
: By providing incentives for collaboration, such as recognition or rewards, organizations can encourage teams to work together and share knowledge more effectively.
Conducting regular team retrospectives and reviews
: Regular retrospectives and reviews help teams identify areas for improvement, share best practices, and celebrate successes. These sessions foster a culture of continuous learning and improvement, driving productivity and collaboration across the organization.
By implementing these strategies for enhancing developer experience, streamlining operations, and encouraging collaboration, platform engineering can significantly boost productivity within an organization. This not only improves the efficiency of development and operations teams but also contributes to the overall success and competitiveness of the business. By embracing platform engineering principles and practices, organizations can create a more agile, innovative, and efficient environment that supports rapid growth and adaptation to changing market conditions.
Pillar 3: Ensuring security and compliance
The adoption of cloud computing brings many benefits to organizations, such as increased scalability, cost savings, and reduced operational overhead. However, it also presents unique challenges related to security and compliance.In this section, we will discuss some of the key challenges associated with securing cloud environments and ensuring compliance with relevant regulations:
Shared responsibility model for cloud security
: One of the fundamental concepts in cloud security is the shared responsibility model, which outlines the security responsibilities of both the cloud provider and the customer. Cloud providers are responsible for securing the underlying infrastructure, while customers are responsible for securing the data and applications running on that infrastructure. Understanding and managing these shared responsibilities can be complex, and organizations must establish clear guidelines for the division of security tasks between their internal teams and cloud providers.
Compliance with industry-specific regulations
: Organizations operating in regulated industries, such as healthcare, finance, or government, must adhere to strict regulatory requirements regarding data protection and privacy. Compliance in the cloud can be challenging, as organizations must ensure that their cloud provider meets the necessary standards and that they have the right controls in place to maintain compliance.
Protecting sensitive data and maintaining privacy
: Cloud environments often store and process vast amounts of sensitive data, making them attractive targets for cybercriminals. Organizations must implement robust data protection measures, such as encryption and tokenization, to secure sensitive information both at rest and in transit. Additionally, organizations need to manage privacy concerns by ensuring that they have appropriate policies and procedures in place for handling personal information, as well as complying with data protection regulations like the
General Data Protection Regulation
(
GDPR
).
Managing access control and authentication
: In cloud environments, managing access to resources and applications becomes more complex due to the distributed nature of the infrastructure. Organizations must establish strong access control policies and implement
Multi-Factor Authentication
(
MFA
) to ensure that only authorized users can access sensitive resources. This also involves managing the permissions and privileges of different user roles, such as developers, administrators, and end-users, to minimize the risk of unauthorized access or accidental data exposure.
Ensuring continuous monitoring and threat detection
: Cloud environments are dynamic, with resources being provisioned and deprovisioned rapidly. This requires organizations to adopt a proactive approach to security monitoring and threat detection, as traditional perimeter-based security measures are often insufficient. By implementing continuous monitoring tools and leveraging cloud-native security services, organizations can detect and respond to threats more effectively, reducing the risk of security breaches and data loss.
Securing cloud environments and ensuring compliance with relevant regulations can be challenging, but it is a fundamental building block of platform engineering, as shown in the following figure:
Figure 3.3 – Security as a foundation of Platform Engineering
Organizations must develop a comprehensive security strategy that addresses the unique risks associated with cloud computing while maintaining the agility and flexibility that the cloud offers. By doing so, they can create a secure foundation for their cloud platforms, ensuring the protection of sensitive data and the overall integrity of their infrastructure.
Platform engineering's role in addressing security and compliance
Platform engineering plays a crucial role in addressing security and compliance challenges in cloud environments. By implementing best practices and leveraging the right tools and processes, platform engineering teams can help organizations create a secure and compliant foundation for their applications and services.In this section, we will discuss the key ways in which platform engineering contributes to addressing security and compliance challenges:
Implementing security by design principles
: Platform engineering teams should adopt a security by design approach, which means incorporating security considerations into every stage of the development and deployment process. This includes integrating security best practices into the design and architecture of the platform, as well as the development, testing, and deployment processes. By doing so, platform engineering teams can minimize vulnerabilities and ensure that security is an integral part of the platform's foundation.
Streamlining and automating compliance processes
: Compliance in cloud environments can be complex, with organizations often needing to adhere to multiple regulations and standards. platform engineering teams can help streamline and automate compliance processes by leveraging cloud-native tools and services, such
as Infrastructure as Code
(
IaC
) and
Policy as Code
(
PaC
). These tools enable the codification and automation of security and compliance policies, ensuring that they are consistently applied across the entire platform and reducing the likelihood of human error.
Providing consistent and reliable security configurations
: Ensuring that security configurations are consistent and reliable across the entire platform is a key aspect of maintaining a secure environment. platform engineering teams should develop and maintain standard security configurations for various components of the platform, such as compute instances, storage, and networking. By doing so, they can reduce the risk of misconfigurations and ensure that all resources are secured according to best practices.
Enabling real-time visibility into security and compliance posture
: Continuous monitoring and visibility into the security and compliance posture of the platform is essential for identifying and addressing potential issues before they become critical. platform engineering teams can leverage cloud-native monitoring and logging services to gain real-time insights into the platform's security status. This includes collecting and analyzing log data, setting up alerts for potential security incidents, and integrating with
Security Information and Event Management
(
SIEM
) solutions to gain a holistic view of the platform's security posture.
Encouraging collaboration between security, compliance, and engineering teams
: Addressing security and compliance challenges in the cloud requires close collaboration between security, compliance, and engineering teams. platform engineering teams should work closely with security and compliance experts to ensure that their platforms meet the necessary requirements and that they are aware of any new or emerging threats. This includes regular communication and knowledge sharing, as well as the integration of security and compliance processes into the platform engineering workflow.
Embedding security into the development lifecycle
: This is crucial for ensuring a secure and compliant platform. This involves implementing secure coding practices and standards, conducting regular security reviews and assessments, automating security testing and validation, and ensuring ongoing security training and awareness. By integrating security into every stage of the development process, platform engineering teams can minimize risks and build a robust and secure foundation for their applications and services.
Implementing secure coding practices and standards
: This helps ensure that developers write code that adheres to security best practices, reducing vulnerabilities and potential attack vectors. These practices can include input validation, proper error handling, and secure session management, among others.
Regular security reviews and assessments
:These are essential for identifying and addressing potential security issues early in the development process. This can include code reviews, threat modeling, and penetration testing, which help uncover vulnerabilities and ensure that security controls are working effectively.
Automating security testing and validation
: This helps identify and remediate security issues faster and more efficiently. By integrating security testing tools into the development process and CI/CD pipelines, platform engineering teams can continuously check for vulnerabilities and ensure that new code does not introduce security risks.
Ongoing security training and awareness
: This is critical for keeping developers informed about the latest security threats and best practices. This can include regular security training sessions, workshops, and access to up-to-date security resources and documentation.
Developing and maintaining a compliance policy library
: This helps teams manage and enforce compliance requirements across their platforms. This library should include policies and guidelines that align with relevant industry standards and regulations.
Implementing policy-as-code frameworks
: This enables teams to codify compliance policies, making it easier to automate compliance checks and enforce consistency across environments. This also allows teams to continuously validate compliance, ensuring that their platforms remain compliant even as changes are introduced.
Integrating compliance checks into CI/CD pipelines
: This enables real-time compliance validation throughout the development and deployment process. This ensures that any non-compliant code or infrastructure changes are detected and addressed before they reach production environments.
Generating compliance reports and audit trails
: This is essential for demonstrating compliance to internal and external auditors, as well as maintaining visibility into the platform's compliance status. These reports can provide insights into policy adherence, compliance violations, and remediation efforts.
Implementing real-time monitoring and alerting
: This helps teams detect and respond to security incidents and compliance violations quickly. This can include monitoring for unusual or suspicious activity, such as unauthorized access attempts or unexpected data transfers.
Leveraging cloud-native security tools and services, such as AWS Security Hub or Azure Security Center
: This can provide comprehensive security monitoring and management capabilities, simplifying the process of securing cloud environments and maintaining compliance.
Establishing a centralized security logging and analysis system
: This allows teams to collect, analyze, and correlate security data from across the platform. This can help identify potential security risks and provide insights into the platform's overall security posture.
Regularly reviewing and updating security policies
: This ensures that the platform remains secure and compliant as new threats and vulnerabilities emerge. This can include updating security configurations, patching vulnerabilities, and updating compliance policies to align with evolving regulations and industry standards.
Conducting periodic security audits and assessments
: This helps teams identify potential security gaps and areas for improvement. This can involve internal audits, third-party assessments, or regulatory audits, depending on the organization's requirements and industry-specific regulations.
Assuring compliance and security requirements are met through platform engineering can reduce manual processes and risk significantly. Additionally, platforms can be used to further promote best practices and coding standards across the whole SDLC.
Pillar 4: Promoting best practices and standards
Establishing consistency and predictability is crucial for the success of platform engineering initiatives. Adhering to best practices and standards helps teams develop a common understanding of how to build, maintain, and evolve their platforms. This consistency reduces risk, technical debt, and promotes a culture of continuous improvement.Best practices and standards foster collaboration and knowledge sharing among teams. When everyone adheres to the same set of guidelines, it becomes easier to share knowledge and ideas, leading to improved platform stability and reliability.
Identifying and implementing best practices and standards
Conducting research and gathering industry insights is essential for identifying best practices that are suitable for an organization's specific needs. This research can involve reviewing relevant industry publications, attending conferences, and engaging with peer organizations.Evaluating practices based on organizational needs ensures that the chosen best practices align with the organization's goals and objectives. This evaluation process may involve stakeholder consultations, pilot projects, or proof-of-concept implementations.Defining and documenting standards and guidelines is essential for maintaining consistency across the organization. Clear, comprehensive documentation helps teams understand the rationale behind the chosen practices and how to implement them effectively.Implementing best practices within platform services involves integrating these practices into the development process, infrastructure management, and monitoring solutions. It's essential to continuously refine practices based on feedback and results, ensuring that they remain relevant and effective over time.
Platform engineering as a tool for enforcing best practices and standards
There are multiple ways that platforms can be used in order to assure best practices and standards are met by development teams and services that are released. These include:
Policy as Code
:
Defining policies as code artifacts enables teams to automate policy enforcement and ensure consistent implementation. Integrating policy enforcement into CI/CD pipelines helps maintain compliance with organizational and regulatory requirements throughout the development and deployment process.
Automating policy compliance checks
: This streamlines compliance management, reducing manual effort and minimizing the risk of human error. Versioning policies and maintaining an auditable record of changes is essential for transparency and accountability.
Encouraging collaboration between teams for policy development
: This fosters a shared understanding of the organization's requirements and promotes knowledge sharing.
Templates and blueprints
:
Creating reusable templates for common tasks simplifies and standardizes the development process, reducing the potential for errors and inconsistencies. Developing blueprints for architectural patterns ensures that teams follow established guidelines and best practices when designing their solutions.
Ensuring templates and blueprints are easily accessible helps teams quickly adopt and implement best practices. Regularly reviewing and updating these resources ensures they remain relevant and reflect the organization's evolving needs.
Encouraging feedback and contributions from platform users helps refine and improve templates and blueprints, ensuring they continue to provide value and support for developers.
Continuous Improvement and learning
:
Fostering a culture of learning and experimentation promotes continuous improvement and innovation. Conducting regular team retrospectives and reviews allows teams to identify areas for improvement and develop action plans to address them.
Encouraging cross-functional knowledge sharing helps teams learn from each other's experiences and expertise, fostering collaboration and synergies between different disciplines.
Investing in ongoing training and development ensures that team members remain up to date with the latest industry trends, technologies, and best practices. This investment in continuous learning helps maintain a high level of expertise within the organization, driving platform engineering success.
Many tools and processes can help enforce standard in the organization but enforcing those is always a friction point and a constant area of pushback. There are many ways to implement those and reduce this friction.
Implementing standards across the organization
Leadership commitment and support play a crucial role in implementing best practices and standards across the organization. Leaders should emphasize the importance of adhering to standards, allocate resources for their implementation, and encourage a culture of continuous improvement. By actively promoting the adoption of best practices, leaders can drive organizational change and ensure that platform engineering initiatives align with broader organizational goals. Some key steps to implementing standards across an engineering organization include:
Importance of communication and feedback
: Maintain open channels to share feedback and suggestions, enabling continuous refinement of standards and practices.
Training and mentorship for implementation
: Implement structured training programs and mentorship initiatives to build necessary skills and foster a culture of continuous improvement.
Monitoring performance metrics
: Track progress and measure the impact of platform engineering initiatives through relevant performance metrics.
Collaboration with external partners and communities
: Participate in industry forums and workshops, and share insights with other organizations to discover new best practices.
Recognition and rewards for adherence
: Motivate teams through rewards and recognition that celebrate adherence to best practices and standards.
Scaling best practices across the enterprise
: Systematically scale best practices and standards across the organization through ongoing communication, training, and support.
Overcoming resistance and addressing concerns
: Address the concerns of individuals hesitant to adopt new ways of working through open communication and demonstration of benefits.
Education and ownership of best practices
: Alleviate concerns by educating team members about the rationale behind best practices, providing necessary resources, and fostering a sense of ownership.
Regular review and adjustment
: Ensure relevance and effectiveness of best practices and standards through regular reviews and adjustments based on feedback and real-world experiences.
Promoting best practices and standards
: Emphasize on continuous improvement, collaboration, and adherence to established guidelines to fully leverage platform engineering initiatives.

Strategic aspects of platform engineering
Aligning platform engineering with an organization's strategic goals and objectives is crucial for ensuring its long-term success. This alignment involves a comprehensive understanding of the organization's priorities, challenges, and opportunities and developing a platform engineering roadmap that addresses these needs.To achieve this alignment, it is essential to engage with stakeholders across the organization to gain insights into their specific needs and concerns. This collaboration allows platform engineering teams to create a platform that is not only technically sound but also supports the business's strategic direction.Developing a roadmap for platform evolution involves identifying critical milestones, setting clear objectives, and outlining the steps required to achieve these goals. This roadmap should be flexible enough to adapt to changing business needs and market conditions while providing a clear direction for platform engineering efforts.Continuously monitoring progress and adjusting the strategy as needed ensures that the platform remains relevant and effective in supporting the organization's goals. Regular reviews of the platform's performance, user feedback, and market trends enable platform engineering teams to identify areas for improvement and make necessary adjustments.To sum up, aligning platform engineering with organizational strategy is a critical aspect of ensuring that the platform delivers the desired value to the organization. By understanding strategic goals, identifying challenges and opportunities, collaborating with stakeholders, and continuously monitoring progress, platform engineering teams can create a platform that drives the organization's success.

Fostering a culture of experimentation and collaboration
: Create an environment that encourages the exploration of new ideas and technologies.
Encouraging cross-functional collaboration
: Facilitate seamless communication and collaboration between different teams and departments.
Driving innovation through idea sharing
: Enable the sharing of insights, ideas, and solutions across teams to spur innovation.

: Stay competitive by integrating emerging technologies and practices, such as cloud-native architectures, containerization, and DevOps.
Staying ahead through adoption and exploration
: Continuously explore, evaluate, and adopt cutting-edge technologies and methodologies.
Streamlining idea validation and prototyping
: Provide a conducive environment for rapid testing and iteration of ideas, accelerating the development of new products and services.
Tracking business outcomes to refine innovation strategies
: Measure the impact of innovation on key business outcomes and use these insights to refine your innovation strategies.
As platforms play a critical role to improve the early stages of the SDLC and as a result help teams with faster innovation cycles and time to market for new ideas, similarly they play a vital role in production environments. Platforms are a crucial part of scaling services in a reliable and secure way.
Platform engineering's role in scaling and global expansion
Platform engineering plays a critical role in enabling organizations to scale their operations and expand into new markets. By building a scalable and extensible platform foundation, addressing regional and global compliance requirements, and ensuring platform services can support diverse workloads and use cases, platform engineering teams can help organizations navigate the challenges of growth and globalization.Building a scalable and extensible platform foundation is essential for supporting the organization's growth. As the organization expands, the platform must be able to handle increased workloads, users, and complexity. By designing the platform with scalability in mind, platform engineering teams can ensure that it can adapt to the organization's evolving needs without compromising performance or reliability.Addressing regional and global compliance requirements is another crucial aspect of scaling and global expansion. As organizations enter new markets, they must navigate a complex landscape of data protection regulations, privacy laws, and industry-specific requirements. platform engineering teams can help address these challenges by implementing compliance controls, automating compliance checks, and providing real-time visibility into compliance status.Ensuring platform services can support diverse workloads and use cases is vital for organizations operating in multiple markets. platform engineering teams must develop a deep understanding of the unique requirements of each market, designing platform services that cater to these specific needs. This might involve adapting platform services to support different languages, currencies, or regulatory environments.Optimizing resource utilization and cost management is essential for organizations scaling their operations. platform engineering teams can help achieve this by implementing resource monitoring and optimization tools, automating resource allocation, and providing insights into cost trends and inefficiencies.Facilitating seamless collaboration across geographically dispersed teams is another key aspect of scaling and global expansion. platform engineering teams can support this by providing tools and processes that enable remote teams to work effectively and efficiently, fostering a collaborative and inclusive work environment.
Summary
In this chapter, we have explored the four key pillars of platform engineering: enhancing developer experience, increasing productivity, ensuring security and compliance, and promoting best practices and standards. We have discussed the importance of each pillar and provided strategies for their implementation to drive platform engineering success.Platform engineering significantly impacts several aspects of an organization. It fosters a culture of innovation and collaboration, enhancing the developer experience and productivity by standardizing processes and automating tasks. It also plays a critical role in addressing cloud security and compliance challenges, fostering collaboration among different teams, and implementing security by design principles. Moreover, platform engineering promotes consistency and predictability by enforcing best practices and standards, thereby reducing risk and technical debt.By the end of this chapter, you will be able to comprehend the key business drivers of platform engineering and the role it plays in modern organizations. You will be able to gain insights into the four main pillars of platform engineering and their impact on architectural decisions, such as self-service onboarding and extensibility. You will earn how platform engineering can be used to promote best practices and enforce standards without introducing toil. You will be able to understand the strategic aspects of platform engineering and how it can fuel digital transformations and drive innovation.As we move forward to
Chapter 4
, we will build upon the foundations established in
Chapter 3
by examining the growth into platform engineering. We will explore the tipping point for when an organization should consider building a platform team and we will discuss change management strategies for decommissioning tools and services, ensuring a smooth transition into the world of platform engineering.

4 Growing into Platform Engineering
Join our book community on Discord
https://packt.link/IOzft
As organizations evolve and grow, their needs and requirements transform. These changes can often stretch traditional DevOps approaches and trigger the need for more efficient and scalable solutions. One such solution is transitioning to a platform engineering model. In this chapter, we delve into this journey, guiding you through the signs that signify this transition, strategies for successful change, and the role of change management in decommissioning tools and services.Transitioning to platform engineering is a vital step toward increasing the productivity, scalability, and efficiency of your software development life cycle. However, it's not a change that can or should be made in haste. The transition must be carefully planned and executed, taking into account the existing DevOps practices, the organization's needs, and the potential impact on teams and workflows. This chapter equips you with the knowledge and skills needed to make this transition smoothly and effectively.By the end of this chapter, you will be able to identify the "tipping point" when the transition to platform engineering becomes necessary. You will understand how to use value stream mapping to visualize your organization's processes and identify areas for improvement. You'll also learn how to leverage existing DevOps practices and tools in your new platform and manage the changes effectively to avoid disruptions and maximize benefits.Understanding these concepts and implementing them in your organization will enable you to improve collaboration, reduce bottlenecks, increase the speed and quality of software delivery, and create a more efficient and enjoyable developer experience. This transition is not just about using new tools or adopting new practices; it's about creating a cultural shift toward a more streamlined, efficient, and scalable way of building software.In this chapter, we're going to cover the following main topics:
The tipping point
A lean approach to visualizing value – VSM
From tools to platform – Building on previous success
Change management – How to decommission tools and services
The cultural transition toward platform engineering
Let us dive in!
The tipping point
The journey from DevOps maturity to platform engineering is an evolutionary process, where an organization gradually expands its capabilities and adapts its structures to meet new demands and scale efficiently. This journey is typically not a linear progression but is punctuated by a key moment we will call the
tipping point
. This is the moment when the need for a transition to platform engineering becomes inevitable and urgent. This section delves into what constitutes a tipping point in the context of platform engineering, the signs indicating its arrival, and the benefits of recognizing it early.
Outgrowing traditional embedded DevOps models
In the context of platform engineering, the tipping point can be defined as a critical juncture where the existing DevOps practices no longer sufficiently support the scalability, efficiency, and agility needs of an organization. It signifies a moment of strategic realignment, where the benefits of shifting to a platform-oriented approach significantly outweigh the cost of maintaining the status quo.This tipping point can be triggered by various factors, including escalating operational complexities, increasing demands for speed and agility, the need for better standardization, and the quest for superior developer experience. Recognizing this tipping point is vital as it informs the organization's decision to pivot toward a more sustainable, scalable, and efficient model of software delivery and operation – platform engineering.To better understand this timing, we only need to imagine a scaling organization that consistently onboards new engineering members and deploys new services. As shown in the following figure, when we deploy a new service, there are usually many aspects involved besides coding, such as automations, infrastructure provisioning, and more. On the other hand, in an ideal platform world, the only thing a development team would have to do besides coding would be to integrate with the platform APIs, in order to deploy their new service.
Figure 4.1 – The cost of deploying a new service, with and without platforms
This additional cost can come in many shapes and forms. There are several indicators that an organization has reached the tipping point toward platform engineering:
Rising operational complexities
: As an organization grows, so do its operational intricacies. If these complexities start overwhelming the existing DevOps practices and teams, it's a clear sign that a shift toward platform engineering is due.
Increasing deployment bottlenecks
: If software deployment becomes a constant bottleneck, hampering delivery speed and productivity, it's an indication that a more efficient platform approach is needed.
Scaling challenges
: The inability of current DevOps practices to effectively scale in line with the organization's growth often serves as a tipping point. This can be seen in increasing system latencies, rising infrastructure costs, or difficulties in managing larger teams and workflows.
Standardization needs
: If there's a growing demand for better standardization across teams and processes to improve consistency, repeatability, and governance, it's a clear sign that a shift to platform engineering is beneficial.
Demand for superior developer experience
: A rising need for improved developer experience, such as better tooling, smoother workflows, and enhanced productivity, also signals a tipping point.
Need for specialized skills
: As companies grow, their tooling complexity also grows and requires a set of advanced skills. This comes with a higher cost of recruiting and retaining specialized talent, as well as an increased effort needed to continuously stay at the edge of technological advancements.
If we could use an "equation" to translate the preceding to a simple mental model to follow and locate the right time, it would be the one in the following figure. The total effort/cost/complexity of running each team independently with their own DevOps efforts is larger than the total platform development effort/cost/complexity and the incremental effort of integrating each service with the platform.
Figure 4.2 – The tipping point equation
Recognizing the tipping point early offers several benefits:
Firstly, it allows for proactive planning and a smoother transition to platform engineering, reducing potential disruptions. It facilitates a more organized evolution of DevOps practices into a platform-oriented model, enabling a gradual and structured change rather than a rushed reaction to escalating issues.
Secondly, early recognition helps align stakeholders toward the new strategic direction. This alignment is vital for securing the necessary support and resources for the transition.
Finally, it prevents the unnecessary accumulation of technical debt. If the tipping point is not recognized in time, organizations may continue to build upon an increasingly shaky foundation, leading to unsustainable technical debt. Early recognition allows organizations to switch courses before this debt escalates too far. In this way, it promotes more sustainable and scalable growth.
Signs that your organization is ready for a platform team
Increasing complexity in DevOps processes is a major indicator that your organization is ready for a transition to a platform team. The complexity here refers not just to technical intricacy but also to organizational and process-related complications.From a technical standpoint, as your product or service offerings grow, so do the dependencies and interactions among various software components. This can lead to escalating complexities in managing and operating these components cohesively. For instance, as microservices architecture becomes more prevalent, managing individual services, their interactions, and their dependencies can quickly grow complex.On the process front, as your development and operations teams expand, coordinating their efforts becomes increasingly challenging. The sheer number of tasks, ranging from code integration and testing to deployment and monitoring, can become overwhelming.From an organizational perspective, as more teams and roles are introduced into the DevOps life cycle, the coordination overhead escalates. The necessity of communication and collaboration across diverse roles – from developers, testers, and operations staff to security and compliance teams – adds another layer of complexity.Recognizing these signs of escalating complexity and responding with the introduction of a platform team can bring significant benefits. The platform team can help streamline these complexities by providing a unified platform that simplifies workflows, enhances tooling, and standardizes processes, thus improving efficiency and productivity across the board.A growing need for standardization and efficiency in DevOps processes is a sign that your organization is ripe for a platform team.Standardization brings consistency and predictability to the development life cycle, reducing errors and inefficiencies. It involves defining common practices, guidelines, and tools for tasks such as coding, testing, deployment, and monitoring. However, as the scale and complexity of operations grow, maintaining this standardization can become challenging.Similarly, efficiency in DevOps is crucial for accelerating software delivery and improving resource utilization. However, without a dedicated focus on improving workflows, tooling, and processes, efficiency gains can be hard to achieve and sustain.A platform team can directly address these needs. It can work toward standardizing tools, practices, and processes across the organization, thereby reducing variability and improving predictability. Moreover, by focusing on optimizing workflows and automating routine tasks, it can significantly enhance the overall efficiency of the DevOps life cycle.
Steps to take after identifying the tipping point
Once you've recognized the tipping point, you can follow these steps:
Build a strong business case for a platform team
: This involves articulating the potential benefits, costs, and risks associated with the transition:
Start by emphasizing the value proposition of a platform team. Highlight how it can help manage increasing complexity, alleviate bottlenecks, and promote standardization and efficiency in your DevOps processes. Use quantitative metrics wherever possible. For example, illustrate how a platform team can speed up software delivery times, reduce operational errors, or improve resource utilization.
Next, outline the expected costs of setting up a platform team. This includes not just the direct costs, such as hiring or training personnel, but also indirect costs, such as the effort required to redefine processes and workflows or to integrate existing tools and systems into the new platform.
Also, acknowledge the potential risks and challenges associated with the transition. These could range from resistance to change among existing teams to potential disruptions in workflows during the transition phase.
Finally, provide a roadmap outlining how the platform team would be set up and integrated into the organization's existing structure. This should include tentative timelines, key milestones, and success criteria, thereby providing a clear and realistic path forward.
Identify initial platform engineering projects
: This is the next step after establishing the case for a platform team. These projects should be chosen strategically to demonstrate the value of the platform team quickly and tangibly:
Start by identifying areas where the platform team can make a significant impact. These could be areas where bottlenecks are most prevalent, or where standardization and efficiency gains could bring substantial benefits. Examples could include automating deployment processes, standardizing testing procedures, or setting up a self-service portal for environment provisioning.
Consider the complexity and duration of potential projects. Initial projects should ideally be manageable in scope and achievable within a short timeframe to quickly demonstrate value. However, they should also be significant enough to bring noticeable improvements to DevOps processes.
Align the initial projects with the organization's broader strategic goals
: Whether it's accelerating time-to-market, improving software quality, or enhancing customer experience, make sure that the projects contribute to these overarching objectives.
Set up the platform engineering team structure
: This involves defining roles, responsibilities, and workflows for the new team:
Firstly, identify the key roles required in the platform team. These could include platform engineers who will build and maintain the platform, product managers who will define the platform's vision and roadmap, and possibly DevOps engineers who will help integrate the platform into existing DevOps processes.
Define clear responsibilities for each role. This helps ensure that everyone on the team understands their duties and the expectations placed upon them. It also helps to prevent overlaps or gaps in responsibilities, which could lead to confusion or inefficiencies down the line.
Outline the workflows and processes the platform team will follow
: This should include how they will collaborate with other teams, how they will handle requests and feedback, and how they will track and report on their progress. This structure helps ensure that the platform team operates smoothly and integrates effectively into the larger organization.
A great way to visualize the development process, helping locate bottlenecks and including the value delivered to your customers, is value stream mapping.
A lean approach to visualizing value – VSM
In the journey toward platform engineering, one must clearly understand and articulate the value that the platform will provide. This can be achieved through a lean approach known as
Value Stream Mapping
(
VSM
). This tool offers a visual representation of a process or system from start to finish, highlighting the value added at each step and identifying potential areas of waste or inefficiency. In the context of transitioning from DevOps to platform engineering, VSM can play a pivotal role in identifying high-value opportunities for the platform team.
Introduction to VSM
VSM is a lean management technique used for analyzing and designing the flow of materials and information required to bring a product or service from its inception to the customer. It graphically illustrates, step-by-step, the activities involved in delivering the product or service, allowing organizations to see where delays are occurring, where value is added, and where waste can be identified and eliminated.In software development, VSM can be used to visualize the whole life cycle from the feature request from the user until a new feature is finally deployed in production and the value is realized by the customer. In a company's DevOps journey, this can be used to identify development bottlenecks and inefficiencies, as shown in the following figure.
Figure 4.3 – VSM example in the SDLC
In the context of platform engineering, the
product
is the platform itself, and the
customer
is usually the internal development teams that use the platform to build and deploy applications.The principles of VSM in platform engineering are essentially the same as in other contexts: it's about visualizing the entire life cycle of platform service delivery, identifying value-adding activities, and finding ways to minimize waste and inefficiency.VSM can offer the following significant benefits when transitioning to platform engineering:
It provides a clear visual representation of current DevOps processes. This helps identify areas of inefficiency, redundancies, or bottlenecks that could be addressed through platform engineering solutions.
VSM helps in defining the scope of platform engineering projects. Highlighting the value-adding activities and waste in the current processes can guide the platform team in deciding which services or capabilities to focus on.
VSM promotes a customer-centric approach. It helps the platform team understand the needs of their internal
customers
(that is, the development teams) better, fostering a culture of continuous improvement and user-centric design in the platform.
VSM aids in communication and collaboration. It provides a common language and framework for discussing processes and workflows, facilitating better collaboration between the platform team and other stakeholders.
To illustrate the power of VSM, let's consider an organization transitioning from DevOps to platform engineering. The organization decided to apply VSM to its software release process, which was causing delays and hindering the pace of innovation.They started by mapping out the entire release process from code check-in to production deployment. The map highlighted several areas of inefficiency, including manual testing processes, approval delays, and resource provisioning bottlenecks.These insights guided the formation of their platform engineering strategy. They decided to focus their initial platform projects on automating testing processes, streamlining approval workflows, and setting up a self-service environment provisioning system. These initiatives were able to significantly reduce the lead time for software releases, thereby demonstrating the value of their platform team and setting a solid foundation for future platform engineering initiatives.
Implementing VSM
Creating a value stream map involves several steps:
Identifying the product or service
: In the context of platform engineering, this would typically be a specific service or capability provided by the platform.
Defining the start and end points
: This constitutes the boundaries of the process being mapped. It could be from the point of a developer's request to the delivery of a platform service.
Gathering information
: This involves collecting detailed data about every step in the process, including the time taken, resources used, people involved, and so on.
Drawing the current state map
: This is a visual representation of the process as it currently exists, including all the steps, flows, and delays.
Analyzing the current state map
: This involves looking for waste, bottlenecks, or other opportunities for improvement.
Developing a future state map
: This is a vision of what the process could look like with the identified improvements implemented.
Creating an action plan
: This includes specific steps, responsibilities, and timelines to achieve the future state.
In the value stream map,
waste
is any step that does not add value to the end product or service from the customer's perspective. In platform engineering, these might be manual tasks, delays in approval workflows, or complexities in deployment processes.Once waste is identified, strategies should be devised to eliminate or reduce it. This could involve automating tasks, streamlining processes, or implementing better coordination mechanisms.VSM can be applied to improve each of the four pillars of platform engineering:
Developer experience
: VSM can identify areas in the developer workflow that cause friction or inefficiency. These insights can guide efforts to improve tooling, automation, or documentation, enhancing the overall developer experience.
Scalability and performance
: VSM can highlight bottlenecks or constraints in the platform that hinder scaling or performance. This can inform initiatives to optimize resource usage, improve load balancing, or design more scalable architecture.
Security and compliance
: VSM can uncover security or compliance risks in the platform delivery process. This can drive efforts to strengthen security controls, automate compliance checks, or enhance security monitoring.
Promoting best practices and standards
: VSM can reveal inconsistencies or deviations in adhering to best practices or standards. This can spur actions to standardize processes, reinforce best practices, or implement policy-as-code mechanisms.
By aligning improvements identified through VSM with these four pillars, organizations can ensure a holistic and balanced approach to their platform engineering initiatives.
Continuous improvement through VSM
VSM is not a one-off activity but, rather, a continuous process that evolves with the organization. Stakeholder feedback plays a crucial role in this iterative process. It helps identify unmet needs, overlooked waste, and opportunities for further optimization. After gathering this feedback, the value stream map should be updated to reflect changes in processes, policies, or tools. For example, the implementation of a new automated CI/CD pipeline would be a significant modification to the map that could significantly reduce lead times and eliminate manual processes.As we improve our value stream (our SDLC), we keep on measuring our efficiency (translated to DevX) and effectiveness (translated to DevProd). Both can be measured by tracking our
DevOps Research and Assessment (DORA)
metrics, measuring how often and how fast we can deliver a new feature, as well as how the user experience is affected, by the number of failures and time to recover from those (see the following figure).
Figure 4.4 – Measuring the efficiency and effectiveness of a value stream
Keeping the value stream map up to date is essential for its ongoing relevance. As the platform engineering team introduces changes, such as new tools or refined workflows, the map should be updated accordingly. This ensures it continues to accurately reflect the current state of the platform's processes. Regular review sessions can be held with all relevant stakeholders to verify the map's accuracy and make necessary updates.The ultimate goal of VSM is to drive improvements in efficiency, quality, and speed. Therefore, it's critical to measure the impact of the changes implemented. KPIs such as lead time, process time, and error rates can provide quantifiable measures of success. Comparing these metrics before and after the implementation of changes can highlight the improvements achieved, validate the effectiveness of the modifications, and identify areas that may require further focus. Identifying focus areas through VSM is a good starting point toward moving to a platform approach, but a very important first step for the transition is to build upon existing DevOps tooling, replicating one team's success to others.
From tools to platform – Building on previous success
Transitioning from DevOps to platform engineering doesn't mean starting from scratch. It's a journey that leverages the work already done, the skills already learned, and the tools already in use. The goal is to evolve these assets from serving specific projects or teams to providing a platform that benefits the entire organization. This section explores how to conduct a thorough inventory of existing DevOps practices and tools, evaluate them for their suitability for the platform, and manage the transition process efficiently.
Leveraging existing DevOps practices and tools
The first step in leveraging existing DevOps practices and tools for the platform is conducting a comprehensive inventory. An
internal service catalog
can serve as a structured repository for this inventory. It can catalog everything from infrastructure provisioning scripts to CI/CD pipelines, configuration management tools, testing frameworks, and monitoring solutions. This service catalog not only provides visibility into the tools and practices currently in use but also details their use cases, owners, users, and known issues. It's essential to involve all relevant stakeholders (such as development teams, operations, and security) in this inventory process to ensure a holistic view of the existing DevOps environment.After compiling a thorough inventory, the next step is to evaluate these tools and practices for their suitability for the platform. Criteria for evaluation might include scalability, maintainability, integration capabilities, and the tool or practice's alignment with the platform's goals and the organization's overall strategy. For example, a CI/CD tool that works well for a single team but struggles to scale across multiple teams might not be suitable for the platform. Other considerations could include the tool's support for infrastructure as code, its ability to provide self-service capabilities, and its compatibility with the organization's technology stack.Transitioning selected DevOps tools and practices to the platform involves careful planning and execution. It's crucial to ensure a smooth transition with minimal disruption to current workflows. This might involve setting up dedicated migration teams, conducting pilot migrations, and providing comprehensive training and support for users. An iterative approach is often beneficial, starting with less critical applications and gradually increasing the complexity of the applications migrated. Regular feedback loops with users can help identify any issues early and make necessary adjustments. The transition process is also an opportunity to standardize practices, optimize processes, and improve documentation, leading to a more robust and efficient platform.
Strategies for a successful transition to platform engineering
Planning the transition from DevOps to platform engineering is a strategic effort that involves significant forethought and careful decision-making. A well-crafted plan addresses questions such as which practices and tools will transition first, how the organizational structure will change, and how current workflows will be affected. It's essential to align the transition plan with business goals and ensure it gets buy-in from all relevant stakeholders. This might involve presentations, workshops, or other forms of communication that highlight the benefits of the transition. Furthermore, any potential risks associated with the transition should be identified, and appropriate mitigation strategies should be put in place. It's crucial to remember that the plan should be flexible enough to adjust to unexpected changes or challenges along the way.Executing the transition is the point where planning meets action. To ensure a smooth execution, a phased approach is often the most effective. Begin with low-risk areas, and gradually move on to more complex tools and practices. This approach enables the organization to learn from each phase, making necessary adjustments before the next. Communication during this phase is crucial to ensure that all stakeholders are aware of the progress and have an avenue to raise any concerns. It's also vital to provide the necessary training and resources to help teams adjust to the new platform-centric approach. Finally, it's crucial to celebrate wins along the way, even small ones, to maintain momentum and keep morale high.Once the transition is in progress, it's important to regularly review and improve the process. This involves analyzing the successes and challenges of each phase of the transition and determining what can be learned from them. For instance, if a specific tool's transition resulted in significant workflow disruption, it's essential to understand why this happened and how to avoid it in the future. Regular reviews also allow for ongoing risk management, enabling the organization to proactively address potential issues before they become problematic. Improving the transition process is a continuous endeavor, which underscores the need for a culture of openness, feedback, and constant learning. It's this culture that ultimately paves the way for a successful transition to platform engineering.
Sustaining success in platform engineering
Continuous improvement is the lifeblood of any successful platform engineering endeavor. In the ever-evolving landscape of software development, static practices can quickly become obsolete. Hence, it's vital to adopt a mindset of continuous improvement and iteration. This involves regular reviews of the platform's performance, efficacy, and impact on the organization's broader goals.In addition, it's essential to keep an eye on industry trends and developments. This helps to identify new tools, practices, and ideas that could further enhance your platform engineering capabilities. Furthermore, the platform engineering team should foster a culture of openness and feedback where all members can contribute ideas for improvement. This might involve iterative development practices, regular team retrospectives, or other feedback mechanisms.The goal of continuous improvement is not just about making the platform better. It's about creating a culture where the team is always striving for excellence, where learning and growth are celebrated, and where innovation is not just encouraged but expected.Just as in any other business endeavor, measuring success is crucial in platform engineering. This requires defining clear metrics that align with your organization's goals and objectives. For instance, if one of the goals of your platform engineering initiative is to reduce the time developers spend on routine tasks, a relevant metric might be the average time saved per developer per week.These metrics should be tracked regularly, with results shared with all relevant stakeholders. Regular measurement not only allows you to assess the impact of your platform engineering efforts but also helps identify areas where further improvements can be made.However, while quantitative metrics are important, don't overlook qualitative ones. For instance, developer satisfaction can be a powerful indicator of platform success. Regular surveys or informal feedback sessions can provide valuable insights into how developers perceive the platform and its impact on their work.Ultimately, tracking and measuring success is about ensuring that your platform engineering efforts are delivering real, tangible benefits to your organization. It's about proving that platform engineering isn't just a buzzword but a powerful approach that can drive efficiency, innovation, and growth. As with most change projects, decommissioning tools and introducing new ones is an important step in this journey, as it plays a key role in the acceptance of a new way of working.
Change management – How to decommission tools and services
Change is the only constant in the tech world, and platform engineering is no exception. As your platform evolves and matures, there will be a need to decommission certain tools and services. The process of decommissioning is a significant change that can disrupt operations if not managed properly. This section discusses the importance of effective
change management
in platform engineering, with a focus on decommissioning tools and services.
The importance of effective change management
Change management is the structured approach for transitioning individuals, teams, and organizations from a current state to a desired future state. It is a critical component of any platform engineering transformation, particularly when decommissioning tools and services.According to John Kotter's
change management theory
, successful change requires eight steps, including "
establishing a sense of urgency, creating a guiding coalition, developing a vision and strategy, communicating the change vision, empowering broad-based action, generating short-term wins, consolidating gains and producing more change, and anchoring new approaches in the culture"
(Kotter, 1996).In the context of platform engineering,
decommissioning
a tool or service involves more than just shutting it down. It affects users, workflows, and processes that depend on these tools and services. Effective change management ensures that these changes are communicated and managed properly, thereby minimizing disruption and resistance while maximizing acceptance and engagement.Decommissioning tools and services come with their unique challenges. One of the most common is resistance to change. Tools and services become ingrained in people's workflows and routines. Changing them can be unsettling, and there is often resistance, even when the change is objectively beneficial.Another challenge is managing dependencies. Some tools and services have dependencies on others. Decommissioning a tool or service without understanding and addressing these dependencies can lead to unexpected problems.Communication is another significant challenge. It is crucial to communicate changes effectively to all affected parties, explaining why the change is necessary, what the impact will be, and how the change will be managed. Failure to communicate effectively can lead to confusion, resistance, and disruption.Poor change management can have several negative impacts. It can lead to confusion, resistance, and lower productivity. It can disrupt workflows and lead to errors and delays. In extreme cases, it can even result in the failure of the decommissioning project.On an organizational level, poor change management can lead to a decrease in morale and engagement. It can create a culture of fear and resistance to change, which can hinder future innovation and growth.On the other hand, effective change management can help ensure a smooth transition, with minimal disruption and maximum acceptance. It can help create a culture of adaptability and resilience, where change is not feared but embraced as an opportunity for improvement and innovation.
Strategies for effective change management
Planning for change is the first step in effective change management. This involves understanding the scope of the change, identifying the affected parties, and outlining the steps necessary for a successful transition:
To start, a comprehensive inventory of all tools and services, as well as their dependencies, should be created. This allows for a complete view of the ecosystem and aids in identifying the potential impact of decommissioning a tool or service.
Next, identify all the stakeholders, both internal and external, who will be affected by the decommissioning. This might include development teams, operations teams, product management, customers, and more.
Once these factors have been identified, a detailed plan can be crafted. This plan should outline the steps required for the decommissioning process, including timelines, responsibilities, contingency plans, and KPIs to measure the success of the change.
Effective communication is crucial in managing change. Clear, consistent, and continuous communication helps to ensure that everyone understands what is happening, why it's happening, and how it will affect them.Communication should begin as soon as the decision to decommission a tool or service has been made. The reasons for the change, the benefits it will bring, and how it will be managed should be clearly explained. Regular updates should be provided throughout the process to keep everyone informed of progress and any changes to the plan. Even in cases of little or no progress, it's better to proactively communicate and keep awareness going.A variety of communication methods should be used to ensure that the message reaches everyone. This might include email updates, team meetings, town hall meetings, intranet posts, and more.Resistance to change is natural and should be expected. Managing this resistance is key to ensuring a smooth transition.Firstly, it's important to understand the source of the resistance. Is it due to fear of the unknown? A lack of understanding about the change? Or perhaps it's due to perceived negative impacts on individuals' roles and responsibilities? Another very common source of resistance is the "not invented here" syndrome, where engineers are reluctant to use tooling they haven't built and don't know their internals.Once the source of resistance is understood, strategies can be put in place to address it. This might include additional communication, training, support, and involvement in the change process.To ensure adoption, it can be helpful to identify and work with champions of the change within the organization. These individuals can help to promote the benefits of the change, assist with training and support, and provide feedback on how the change is being received.Finally, patience and persistence are key. Change takes time, and there may be setbacks along the way. However, with careful planning, effective communication, and proactive management of resistance, the transition can be successful.
A guide to decommissioning tools and services
The transition from DevOps to platform engineering often involves decommissioning some existing tools and services. Kurt Lewin describes the change process in three steps, where you start with the status quo, a piece of ice that is hard to change. Initially, you must
unfreeze
, by creating the right environment for the change to happen. The second step is implementing the change. The third and final step is
refreezing
, where a system and processes should be put in place to make the change stick in the long term.
Figure 4.5 – The Lewin three-step change model: Unfreeze-Change-Refreeze
Identifying which ones to decommission requires careful evaluation. Here are some steps to follow to
unfreeze
:
Creating an inventory
: Start by creating a service catalog of all the existing tools and services used in different teams for their DevOps practices. This includes everything from development tools and CI/CD pipelines to monitoring systems and communication platforms.
Analyzing usage and value
: Understand for each team what DevOps issues they're trying to solve and processes to optimize. For each tool and service, analyze its usability across different teams and projects. Evaluate the value it delivers in terms of improving efficiency, reducing errors, facilitating collaboration, and so on.
