Tools and services that are infrequently used or do not provide significant value are potential candidates for decommissioning.
Assessing compatibility
: Evaluate the compatibility of each tool and service with the principles of platform engineering. Does it support self-service, automation, and scalability? If not, it might need some extra work to become generic rather than being replaced.
Considering dependencies
: Understand the dependencies that each tool and service has on others. Decommissioning a tool or service might have a domino effect, impacting other tools and services that depend on it.
Getting feedback
: Seek feedback from the teams that use the tools and services. Their insights can be invaluable in identifying the strengths and weaknesses of each tool and service and understanding the impact of decommissioning them.
Making the change
Once the tools and services to be used or decommissioned have been identified, it's time to start the transition or adoption process. Here's a step-by-step guide:
Notifying stakeholders
: Communicate the decision to decommission the tool or service to all stakeholders, providing the rationale, the expected benefits, and the timeline for decommissioning.
Planning the decommissioning
: Develop a detailed decommissioning plan. This should include tasks, timelines, responsibilities, and contingencies. Make sure you have a rollback plan in case of unexpected issues.
Migrating to alternatives
: If the tool or service is being replaced, migrate the users, data, and functionality to the new tool or service. Provide support and training to help users adapt to the new tool or service.
Testing the changes
: Before fully decommissioning the tool or service, test the changes to ensure everything works as expected.
Decommissioning the tool or service
: Once everything is ready, decommission the tool or service. This may involve shutting down servers, deleting data, or terminating subscriptions.
Refreeze
After the tool or service has been decommissioned, there are some post-decommissioning steps to ensure the transition is smooth:
Monitoring the impact
: Keep a close eye on the impact of the decommissioning. This includes monitoring performance, tracking issues, and observing user adoption and satisfaction.
Gathering feedback
: Solicit feedback from users on their experience with the decommissioning process and the new tool or service (if any). Use this feedback to make improvements.
Documenting the process
: Document the decommissioning process and its outcomes. This will be useful for future decommissioning processes and for sharing learnings across the organization.
Celebrating the success
: Decommissioning a tool or service is a significant achievement, especially when it contributes to the transition to platform engineering. Celebrate the success with your team and the organization.
Transitioning tools and helping teams adopt new ones is often the easiest part of a platform transition. A new organizational structure, coming with a new way of working, will always create a sense of uncertainty, and given the transition of part of each team's responsibilities (deployments, automations, operations, monitoring, and more), there is a mindset and a cultural shift that has to happen as well.
The cultural transition toward platform engineering
As we navigate the exciting journey of transitioning from DevOps to platform engineering, it becomes clear that this transition is not just about technological transformation. The journey toward platform engineering is fundamentally a cultural shift that permeates the entire organization. This cultural transformation plays a pivotal role in defining how successfully an organization can leverage platform engineering to optimize its operations and innovate at scale.In this section, we will delve deep into the criticality of cultural aspects within the framework of platform engineering. We'll explore how culture impacts the overall success of the platform engineering model and identify the specific elements that distinguish an organizational culture from a platform culture. Through real-world case studies, we'll elucidate the profound influence of cultural factors on platform engineering outcomes.We'll then guide you through strategies to build a culture that embodies collaboration, shared responsibility, and transparency – essential attributes for a thriving platform engineering team. We will also shed light on how to foster a continuous learning environment within your teams, crucial for staying abreast with rapid technological changes and for nurturing an experimental mindset that views failures as opportunities to learn and improve.This deep dive into the cultural transition toward platform engineering will equip you with valuable insights and practical strategies to nurture a culture that not only supports but accelerates your transition to platform engineering. The cultural transformation may be an intricate process, but it is an indispensable part of your journey to platform engineering, and this section is designed to guide you every step of the way.
The importance of culture in platform engineering
Just as the DevOps movement underscored the significance of collaboration and shared responsibility between development and operations teams, the advent of platform engineering brings to the fore the criticality of fostering a culture that empowers this new paradigm.Culture is the undercurrent that informs how a team or organization operates, communicates, and makes decisions. In the context of platform engineering, culture shapes how the platform team interacts with the rest of the organization, how they prioritize tasks, and how they approach problem-solving.A positive, supportive culture can accelerate the adoption of platform engineering, facilitate smooth collaboration between teams, and promote a high degree of innovation and efficiency. On the other hand, a negative or unsupportive culture can hinder progress, cause friction between teams, and ultimately compromise the success of the platform engineering initiative.The culture of an organization refers to the shared values, beliefs, and behaviors that define how work gets done. On the other hand, a platform culture is the subset of the organizational culture that specifically pertains to how the platform team operates. While the platform culture should align with the broader organizational culture, it also has unique aspects that support the specific goals of platform engineering, such as promoting collaboration, embracing automation, and fostering innovation.It's essential to understand the existing organizational culture and identify areas where it aligns with or diverges from the desired platform culture. This understanding can guide the strategies to nurture a supportive platform culture within the broader organizational context.Let's consider the case of a large software development company that decided to transition from a traditional DevOps model to platform engineering. While they invested significantly in technology and training, they overlooked the importance of cultural transition. The existing organizational culture was highly siloed, with teams often operating independently and rarely collaborating. This lack of collaboration resulted in misunderstandings and conflicts when the platform team started integrating services. The lack of a supportive culture led to slow progress, frequent bottlenecks, and reduced effectiveness of the platform engineering initiative. In contrast, another company with a collaborative and transparent culture found the transition to platform engineering relatively smooth. Teams were already accustomed to working closely, sharing knowledge, and learning from each other. When the platform team was formed, they were quickly able to align with other teams and drive the platform engineering initiative effectively.The culture of a platform engineering team should inherently support
Developer Experience
(
DX
), operational excellence, business value, and culture itself.A culture focused on DX prioritizes empathy, usability, and continuous feedback. Operational excellence requires a culture of transparency, learning from failures, and continuous improvement. Driving business value calls for a culture of alignment with organizational goals, measuring success by business outcomes, and balancing speed with stability. Lastly, the
culture pillar
emphasizes collaboration, shared responsibility, and continuous learning.Creating such a culture requires conscious effort, commitment from leadership, and the involvement of all stakeholders. But the payoff – a highly effective platform engineering team that accelerates innovation and drives business value – is well worth the effort.
Strategies for building a collaborative culture
Building a collaborative culture is not just about implementing new processes or tools – it's about shifting mindsets and behaviors toward collective success. Here, we will delve into strategies that can help build a collaborative culture, crucial for successful platform engineering:
Shared responsibilities and mutual accountability
: These are foundational to a collaborative culture. In platform engineering, this could mean involving all stakeholders in decision-making processes, having clear roles and responsibilities, and ensuring everyone is accountable for the outcomes. It also means that each component needs a shared effort to be implemented properly – for example, the platform team will build observability tools and platforms, but the development team is responsible for implementing those the right way, shipping the right metrics at the right place.
Figure 4.6 – Shared responsibility model in platform engineering
One way to implement shared responsibilities is through the use of a
RACI matrix
(RACI stands for
Responsible, Accountable, Consulted, Informed
), which helps define roles and responsibilities clearly. With clear expectations, teams can work more cohesively, fostering a sense of ownership and shared purpose.Mutual accountability, on the other hand, fosters a culture of collective ownership where successes are celebrated together and challenges are addressed collectively. Regular feedback and retrospectives can aid in maintaining accountability and identifying areas for improvement.
Transparent communication
: This builds trust and aids collaboration. In the context of platform engineering, transparency might mean openly discussing project plans, progress, blockers, and decision-making processes. Tools such as shared project boards, regular team updates, and open forums for discussion can aid in fostering transparent communication.
Transparency should extend to all areas, including successes, failures, and changes. Celebrating wins together, openly discussing failures and lessons learned, and clearly communicating any changes in direction or strategy, all contribute to an environment of trust and mutual respect.
Trust
: This is the bedrock of a collaborative culture. Without it, teams may become siloed, communication can break down, and productivity can decrease. Trust is built through consistent actions, transparent communication, and respect for each team member's role and contribution.
Building trust takes time and effort. It involves respecting each other's expertise, acknowledging good work, and being supportive in the face of mistakes or failures. Regular team-building exercises can also help foster personal relationships and mutual understanding within the team.
Knowledge sharing
: This is vital for innovation and efficiency in platform engineering. Encouraging a culture where team members freely share their knowledge, insights, and ideas can lead to improved solutions and collective learning. Regular knowledge-sharing sessions, documentation of best practices, and encouraging questions and curiosity can all foster knowledge-sharing.
Collaborative problem-solving
: This involves leveraging the diverse skills and perspectives within the team to address challenges. Encouraging a culture where everyone's input is valued and considered can lead to innovative solutions and a sense of shared accomplishment. Collaborative problem-solving can be encouraged through regular brainstorming sessions, collaborative workshops, and fostering a psychologically safe environment where everyone feels comfortable voicing their ideas and concerns.
Summary
Throughout this chapter, our focus has been on equipping you with the necessary skills to transition smoothly from DevOps to platform engineering. We've seen how to identify the "tipping point," a crucial juncture when an organization's DevOps complexities reach a level that calls for the formation of a dedicated platform team, thereby ensuring optimized workflows and a standardized development environment. We also delved into the application of VSM. This tool not only aids in visualizing the steps involved in product creation but also plays a pivotal role in waste identification, process enhancement, and informed decision-making regarding the integration of existing tools into the new platform.Furthermore, we explored how to strategically plan and execute the shift from a toolkit-based DevOps approach to a platform-centric model. We emphasized the importance of leveraging existing tools and transforming them seamlessly into platform capabilities. Change management, especially in the realm of decommissioning tools and services, was another vital area of exploration. Mastering this ensures that transitions are smooth, resistance is mitigated, and the adoption of new tools and processes is streamlined.Lastly, we highlighted the immense significance of nurturing a transition-friendly culture. A culture that's collaborative, emphasizes continuous learning, and is pivotal for the success of significant organizational changes, such as the shift to platform engineering.In the next chapter, we'll dive deeper into the nuts and bolts of platform engineering. You'll learn about the components of a platform, how they interact, and how to effectively build and manage an
Internal Developer Platform
(
IDP
). This will help you put into practice all that you have learned in this chapter, taking your platform engineering journey to the next level.

5 Understanding Platform Architecture and Internal Developer Platforms (IDPs)
Join our book community on Discord
https://packt.link/IOzft
The DevOps tools and approaches organizations use as they scale their DevOps practices play a pivotal role in determining efficiency, productivity, and innovation potential. Central to this is understanding platform architectures, particularly the emergence and adoption of
internal developer platforms
(
IDPs
). These platforms, tailored for internal use by developers, present a new DevOps paradigm that focuses on automating processes, facilitating collaboration, and streamlining the software delivery cycle.By the end of this chapter, you'll have a clear understanding of what IDPs are, the advantages they bring to the table, and how to approach the adoption or transition toward a platform-centric environment.In this chapter, we're going to cover the following main topics:
Capabilities of an IDP
Can I just find an IDP as a service?
How opinionated should the platform be?
Building golden paths
Transitioning to platform
Let's dive in!
Capabilities of an IDP
The
Cloud Native Computing Foundation
(
CNCF
) hosts a vast number of projects with more than 130k contributors and 2.8 million GitHub stars. To better govern and provide technical guidance to all the projects,
technical advisory groups
(
TAGs
) are formed to provide guidance across projects in a specific domain. TAG App Delivery gathers feedback and produces guidance for projects and initiatives related to delivering cloud-native applications, including building, packaging, deploying, managing, and operating them. Within this TAG, a
working group
(
WG
) has been formed that focuses on platforms specifically. A significant work that the WG platforms have produced is the CNCF Platforms white paper.The CNCF Platforms white paper outlines key capabilities and interfaces that are essential for cloud-native platforms (
Figure 6.1
). These platforms integrate services from various providers and facilitate application developers in achieving security, performance, cost, governance, and a consistent experience:
Figure 6.1 – Capabilities and interface of internal developer platforms (source: CNCF platforms white paper)
In the context of cloud-native platforms, platform capabilities typically include a variety of functionalities and services that support the development, deployment, and management of applications in cloud environments. These capabilities often cover areas such as infrastructure automation, application development environments, observability (including monitoring and logging), data and storage services, network and security management, and integration with various tools and APIs. They are designed to simplify and streamline operations in cloud-native ecosystems, enabling efficient management of applications and resources.Platform interfaces in cloud-native platforms refer to the means through which users, particularly developers, interact with the platform's capabilities. These interfaces can include web portals,
application programming interfaces
(
APIs
), and
command-line interfaces
(
CLIs
). They enable users to observe, provision, and manage the platform's resources and services. Effective platform interfaces are designed to be user-friendly, facilitating easy access to platform capabilities and ensuring that developers can efficiently use the platform's features for application development and management.At its core, an IDP is a composite structure that's woven together by different but interrelated components. These components, while differing in function and design, aim for a singular goal: to accelerate, simplify, and enhance the software development process. Let's look at some of the core aspects of architecting an IDP:
Centralized architecture
: IDPs often employ a centralized architecture that ensures that all the tools and resources developers need are accessible from a single, unified interface. This reduces the overhead of tool management, enhances consistency, and improves developer productivity by decreasing the time spent on setup and integration.
Core capabilities
:
Compute
: Serving as the foundational layer, Kubernetes enables orchestration, scaling, and management of applications. This includes the environment in which applications operate post-deployment, ensuring that they have the resources and conditions required to function effectively.
Pipelines
: Pipelines facilitate the automation of tasks related to deployment, infrastructure setup, and code integration, ensuring that processes are repeatable and less prone to errors.
Observability and reliability
: This includes tools and practices that offer insights into application performance, helping teams identify, debug, and rectify issues swiftly.
Data services
: These encompass a range of services that support the data storage and retrieval needs of applications, such as databases, caches/state stores, and backup mechanisms.
Built-in security
: This includes practices and tools to ensure that applications are free from vulnerabilities, manage secrets efficiently, and ensure compliance with standards.
In subsequent sections, we'll dive deeper into each of these capabilities, elucidating their characteristics and their collective contributions to creating an optimized environment for developers. What becomes evident is that an IDP is not just a toolset; it's a strategic enabler that fundamentally transforms how software development is approached and executed.In recent years, Kubernetes has emerged as a pivotal component in the world of software development, earning its moniker as the
platform of platforms
. At its heart, Kubernetes is an open source system that automates how we can deploy, scale, and manage different workloads. The CNCF estimates that more than 70% of IDPs are built on top of Kubernetes.The following are some of the reasons Kubernetes has become the core foundation of most IDPs:
Unified orchestration
: Kubernetes offers a standardized way of orchestrating containers, regardless of where they're deployed, be it on-premises, in a public cloud, or in a hybrid environment. This universality ensures consistency and portability across diverse infrastructures.
Scalability and reliability
: One of the significant challenges in modern application development is ensuring that applications can scale with demand. Kubernetes effortlessly scales applications out or in based on the demand, ensuring optimal resource utilization. It also ensures high availability, meaning that if a container or even an entire node fails, it will automatically reschedule the affected containers.
Extensible and modular
: Kubernetes supports a plug-and-play architecture, where numerous extensions and modules can be added to cater to specific needs, from networking to storage to security, enhancing its capability as a versatile platform.
Ecosystem and community
: As an open source platform, Kubernetes enjoys robust community support. This vast ecosystem results in continual improvements, a plethora of tools, and extensive documentation, all of which benefit developers immensely. Additionally, many engineers are investing in Kubernetes learnings, and skills and training are becoming standardized with the existing certifications, which helps build and grow platform teams more easily.
In essence, by placing Kubernetes at its core, an IDP taps into a world consisting of standardization, extensibility, and a vibrant ecosystem that constantly evolves to meet the ever-changing demands of modern software development.The runtime environment of an application determines how it operates post-deployment. With Kubernetes serving as the backbone of the IDP, the runtime environment also leans heavily on Kubernetes' features and characteristics:
Resource management
: Kubernetes-based runtime environments can automatically allocate resources such as CPU, memory, and storage to applications based on predefined policies or real-time demands. This dynamic allocation ensures optimal resource utilization and efficient application performance.
Self-healing
: One of the standout features of a Kubernetes-based runtime is its self-healing capability. If an application crashes, Kubernetes can automatically restart the failed containers, ensuring uninterrupted service.
Service discovery and load balancing
: In a microservices architecture, different service components of an application need to communicate seamlessly. Kubernetes facilitates this through built-in service discovery and load balancing, ensuring that application components can find and communicate with each other efficiently and that incoming traffic is distributed optimally among containers.
In the context of an IDP, the term
pipeline
encapsulates a series of automated processes that shepherd code from its source repository to a runtime environment where it becomes a live application (as shown in
Figure 5.2
). As the IDP emphasizes automating as much of the software life cycle as possible, pipelines play a critical role in the platform's operation:
Figure 5.2 – CI/CD pipeline as part of an IDP
Let's delve deeper into the significance of pipelines in the spheres of application deployment, automation, infrastructure automation, and
Infrastructure as Code
(
IaC
):
Application deployment
: Deployment pipelines are often the first thing that comes to mind when discussing automation in the context of software delivery. These pipelines are designed to take code from repositories, build it, test it, and deploy it to production or other specified environments. This automation ensures consistency, reduces manual intervention (and thereby human error), and accelerates the time from code commit to code running in production.
Automation
: Automation pipelines go beyond just deployment. They can include processes for code linting, security scans, and even more complex workflows, such as blue-green or canary deployments. These automated procedures guarantee that code meets organizational standards, is secure, and can be deployed in a manner that minimizes risks to end users.
Infrastructure automation
: Previously, setting up the infrastructure was a manual, time-consuming task that often resulted in
snowflake
environments – each slightly different from the other. With infrastructure automation pipelines, infrastructure provisioning and configuration are automated, ensuring that environments are consistent, reproducible, and scalable. These pipelines can automatically handle tasks such as spinning up virtual machines, configuring network settings, and setting up databases.
IaC
: IaC is a method wherein infrastructure configuration is defined and managed using code and software development techniques. When integrated into pipelines, IaC allows developers to automatically provision and manage infrastructure using the same versioning, CI/CD, and collaboration practices they use for application code, as well as the same practices on pull requests, such as code reviews. Tools such as Terraform, Ansible, and Kubernetes' own declarative configurations facilitate IaC, ensuring that infrastructure provisioning is agile, consistent, and reproducible in terms of application deployment.
By integrating these pipelines into the IDP, organizations can ensure that both their application and infrastructure life cycles are smooth, efficient, and error-free. These pipelines not only increase the speed and agility of software delivery but also introduce a level of standardization and repeatability that significantly reduces risks associated with deployment and infrastructure provisioning.
Observability
is the deeper, more insightful sibling of monitoring. It's about understanding the state of your system from the outside by observing its outputs. It's about predicting failures and being proactive, rather than being reactive. This concept becomes paramount in the context of cloud-native tools and multi-service products, where various components and services coalesce to facilitate seamless software development and delivery.
MELT – The pillars of observability
Observability is typically described in terms of four main pillars, encapsulated by the acronym MELT:
Metrics
: These are numerical values that represent the current state of a system. Examples include the number of requests per second a service is handling or the memory usage of a particular component.
Events
: Events are discrete occurrences in a system that can be significant for various reasons. For instance, a sudden surge in traffic or a service going down are examples of events. A new service being deployed or upgraded is an event. Integrating those in our observability pipeline gives us contextual intelligence.
Logs
: Logs are detailed textual records that are produced by software components. They often contain information about the operations a system is performing, errors it's encountering, or any other pertinent information.
Traces
: Traces provide a pathway through the system. They show the journey of a request as it traverses various services and components, highlighting where delays or failures might occur.
One of the challenges of setting up observability for an IDP is the inherent diversity of monitoring standards across different teams. Different teams often have varied priorities, preferences for tools, or distinct benchmarks for what they consider
healthy
. This variance can make it difficult to establish a unified observability protocol for the entire platform.As IDPs become the backbone of development operations, the stakes of any downtime or inefficiency are amplified. A small issue can impact numerous developers or even halt the entire development pipeline. Observability provides the following:
Better insights
: With the MELT approach, teams can gain a granular understanding of the IDP's performance, allowing for data-informed decisions
Faster recovery
: With deep insights, teams can pinpoint the root causes of issues quickly, dramatically reducing the
mean time to recovery
(
MTTR
)
To standardize the expectations from different services, many organizations adopt
service-level objectives
(
SLOs
) and SLO standards across different services. These are specific, measurable goals regarding the reliability and performance of a service. SLOs ensure that all teams align on the expected quality of service, regardless of their monitoring practices, as well as define a similar way of reporting service availability and performance.The
Watermelon problem
describes a situation where everything seems green (good) on the surface, but inside, there are numerous red (bad) indicators, as shown in
Figure 5.3
:
Figure 5.3 – The watermelon problem – Teams report SLOs in different ways to show a green result, but the service availability is impacted for customers
Just as a watermelon is green on the outside but red inside, systems might outwardly seem fine, with all their metrics within acceptable ranges, when different teams have different ways of reporting health. However, underneath, several smaller issues could be festering, potentially leading to significant problems. The comprehensive nature of observability, especially with MELT, helps mitigate this risk by providing an in-depth view, ensuring that all is well not just on the surface, but deep down too.As the complexities of modern applications grow, so does their reliance on a myriad of data services. These services provide the foundational layers upon which applications are built, ensuring data is stored, retrieved, and managed efficiently. In the context of an IDP, these services are often abstracted and offered
as-a-service
, empowering developers to leverage them without diving into the intricacies of their deployment and management.At the heart of many applications lies a database, a structured collection of data that can be easily accessed, managed, and updated. With
Database-as-a-Service
(
DBaaS
), developers can
outsource
the process of setting up, managing, and scaling databases to platform teams, allowing them to simply use them as a service. This not only simplifies the process of database management but also ensures that databases are optimized, backed up, and updated by experts. As applications become more distributed, maintaining a consistent state across services or microservices becomes vital. State stores, or distributed state management services, ensure that state data is consistently maintained across different parts of an application. In an IDP, developers can leverage pre-configured state stores, ensuring that the complexities of distributed state management are abstracted away. With the rise of microservices and third-party integrations, data flows in and out of multiple services and platforms. Data integration services in an IDP ensure that there's a seamless flow of data between these entities, enabling applications to function as cohesive units. They often come with tools for data transformation, validation, and orchestration. One of the paramount concerns when dealing with data is ensuring its durability and safety. In the context of an IDP, backup, restore, and recovery are offered as standardized services. This means that data across all applications is regularly backed up, can be quickly restored if needed, and there are robust recovery mechanisms in place in case of failures. Therefore, developers can have peace of mind knowing that their application's data is safeguarded against unforeseen issues.As cyber threats become more advanced and frequent, organizations must ensure that their platforms are built with security ingrained in their very fabric. An IDP holds the responsibility for numerous applications, making its security paramount. By embedding security and compliance directly into the platform, we ensure that every application built atop benefits from this robust shield. Routinely scanning code bases and dependencies – even running applications – is the first line of defense against potential vulnerabilities. In the context of an IDP, automated scanning tools can be integrated into the development and deployment pipelines. This ensures that any anomalies or potential security threats are identified early in the life cycle, allowing developers to address them before they escalate. Beyond just identifying vulnerabilities, an effective IDP provides tools and processes to manage and mitigate them. This might include automated patching, notifications to developers, and even integration with issue-tracking systems to ensure that vulnerabilities are tracked, addressed, and documented. Understanding what's inside an application is crucial for security. A
software bill of materials
(
SBOM
) provides a detailed list of packages, libraries, and dependencies used within an application. Within an IDP, SBOMs can be automatically generated, ensuring transparency and allowing teams to pinpoint and update dependencies that might be outdated or vulnerable.Almost every application requires certain secrets or certificates for its operation, be it database credentials, API keys, or SSL certificates. In the wrong hands, these can become potent tools for malicious activities. An IDP provides centralized secrets and certificate management solutions. These tools ensure that secrets are stored securely, accessed only by authorized entities, and rotated regularly to minimize risks. Additionally, by centralizing certificate management, organizations can ensure timely renewals, preventing potential downtimes or security lapses due to expired certificates. Multiple tools can be used to centrally manage secrets and certificates, such as Hashicorp Vault, as shown in
Figure 5.4
:
Figure 5.4 – Hashicorp Vault as a central secret management service
An IDP is not merely a juxtaposition of distinct capabilities, but rather an intricate ecosystem where each element has a pivotal role and interacts harmoniously with others. Like an orchestra where every instrument contributes to the melody, each component of the IDP collaborates to create a seamless developer experience.Each capability within the IDP, while being integral to platform functionality, has been designed with a clear focus: to facilitate and empower developers:
Kubernetes
: By abstracting away the complexities of infrastructure management, developers can focus purely on coding without worrying about where and how their code will run.
Pipelines
: They automate repetitive tasks, from code testing to deployment, ensuring that developers can commit code and see it in action swiftly.
Observability tools
: By offering real-time insights into application performance, they enable developers to debug efficiently and ensure optimal application health.
Data services
: By managing databases, caches, and other data-related functionalities, they free developers from the intricacies of data management, letting them concentrate on data utilization.
Security
: With automatic vulnerability scans, secrets management, and compliance tools, developers can be assured of the security of their applications without diving deep into security protocols themselves.
Multiple components put together in the right way comprise the IDP. Each platform needs significant effort to be designed, built, and operated at scale. Naturally, many companies initially look for off-the-shelf solutions.
Can I just find an IDP as a service?
In a world accustomed to plug-and-play solutions, where
Software-as-a-Service
(
SaaS
) solutions dominate a significant portion of the business technology landscape, a pressing question arises when contemplating IDPs: Why isn't there a one-size-fits-all, off-the-shelf IDP available?There are multiple reasons off-the-shelf solutions might seem like a reasonable starting point, but there's no fully fledged solution:
Diversity in organizational needs and processes
: Every organization is unique, not just in terms of its business goals but also in terms of its development methodologies, processes, and workflows. This translates to a diverse set of requirements that a standard IDP might not be able to cater to comprehensively. For instance, the way a fintech startup uses microservices could differ drastically from a multinational e-commerce conglomerate.
Varying tech stacks and legacy systems
: Companies have diverse technological backgrounds. Some might be using cutting-edge tech stacks, while others might be deeply entrenched in legacy systems. An off-the-shelf IDP would struggle to cater uniformly to both these extremes and everything in between.
The importance of flexibility and customization
: An IDP is not just about tools; it's about providing an environment that boosts developer productivity and efficiency. This demands a high level of flexibility and customization, which, by definition, is challenging to provide in a standardized product. An IDP tailored to an organization can better align with the developers' workflows and needs, something an off-the-shelf solution might fall short of.
The evolving nature of development practices
: The realm of software development is constantly evolving, with new methodologies, tools, and best practices emerging rapidly. This dynamic nature makes it hard for a standardized IDP solution to stay relevant and updated without frequent and sometimes drastic overhauls.
Security and compliance concerns
: A significant facet of IDPs is ensuring security and compliance. Different organizations operate under various regulatory environments and have unique security requirements. It's almost impossible for an off-the-shelf solution to cater to this vast spectrum of security and compliance needs without extensive customization.
There are many commercial solutions available to help you get started with building an IDP. The most popular ones include Qovery, Humanitec, Port, Cortex, and OpsLevel. Some popular open source tools include Backstage, Crossplane, and Krateo.
Backstage – An open platform for building developer portals
In the realm of IDPs,
Backstage
stands out as a compelling choice for many organizations. Originating from Spotify, Backstage was conceived to address the complexity faced by large-scale tech companies with multiple autonomous teams, a plethora of services, and varied tooling. Today, as an open source project, it has garnered significant attention and adoption across the industry. This section explores Backstage's core offerings and how it serves as an open platform for constructing developer portals.Backstage is an open source developer portal that streamlines the developer experience by unifying infrastructure tooling, services, and documentation into a single UI. Instead of developers navigating a maze of tools and services, Backstage offers a centralized dashboard where everything is accessible, from CI/CD pipelines to monitoring metrics, as shown in the following screenshot:
Figure 5.5 – Backstage service catalog
The core features of Backstage are as follows:
Service catalog
: At the heart of Backstage is its service catalog, which provides a holistic view of all services, their ownership, status, APIs, and related documentation. This enables developers to discover and understand services within the organization effortlessly.
Extensible plugin architecture
: Backstage can be thought of as a platform for plugins. This architecture allows teams to create, maintain, and deploy plugins that cater to their specific needs, ensuring that the developer portal remains adaptable and extensible.
Standardized developer experience
: By consolidating tools and services into a single interface, Backstage offers a consistent developer experience. This standardization fosters efficiency and collaboration and reduces the learning curve for new team members.
Software templates
: Backstage provides a mechanism for defining and using templates for common tasks, such as creating a new microservice or library, ensuring best practices, and reducing boilerplate.
Built-in documentation
: With its integrated tech docs solution, documentation lives alongside code, ensuring that it remains relevant, up-to-date, and accessible to developers when they need it.
Being open source, Backstage has fostered a vibrant community that continually contributes with plugins, features, and improvements. This community-driven approach ensures that Backstage remains at the cutting edge of developer needs and industry trends.
Humanitec – A platform orchestrator
In the diverse landscape of IDPs,
Humanitec
emerges as a notable player, distinguishing itself as a
platform orchestrator
. While many tools and platforms focus on specific aspects of the development life cycle, Humanitec aims to weave these various threads into a cohesive tapestry, as shown in
Figure 5.6
. This section delves into the essence of Humanitec, highlighting its functionalities and the unique value proposition it offers to organizations:
Figure 5.6 – Humanitec as a platform orchestrator
Humanitec is a platform orchestrator, which means it's designed to accelerate application delivery by streamlining the deployment process. It functions as a central hub, allowing teams to manage resources, configurations, and deployments across different environments seamlessly.Let's take a look at its key features:
Environment management
: Humanitec allows developers to spin up, manage, and tear down application environments with ease. By automating environment provisioning, Humanitec fosters rapid testing and iteration.
Resource orchestration
: With its robust resource management capabilities, Humanitec simplifies the task of connecting applications to databases, message queues, and other services, eliminating manual configuration overhead.
Integration with existing toolchains
: Humanitec is not prescriptive about tool choices. Instead, it integrates with existing CI pipelines, container registries, and infrastructure providers, ensuring teams can leverage their preferred tools without disruption.
Configuration management
: Handling configurations across multiple environments can be daunting. Humanitec centralizes configuration management, making it effortless to apply, modify, and track configurations throughout the application life cycle.
Developer autonomy
: By providing a self-service interface, Humanitec empowers developers to manage deployments and resources independently, reducing dependencies on Ops teams and accelerating delivery.
Humanitec's philosophy resonates with modern development paradigms such as DevOps and GitOps. By bridging the gap between development and operations, it fosters collaboration, automation, and rapid feedback loops.
How opinionated should the platform be?
The design and implementation of an IDP are often influenced by a fundamental question: "
How opinionated should the platform be?
" The degree to which an IDP dictates certain workflows, technologies, or practices can significantly impact developer experience, the adaptability of the platform, and the overall efficacy of the system. Striking a balance between a highly opinionated platform (essentially a product) and a flexible system that allows for ad hoc solutions is crucial. Understanding this balance can come from understanding loosely defined flexible paths to rigidly defined ones:
Defined pathways
: A highly opinionated IDP offers clear, defined pathways for developers to follow. Such an approach brings uniformity, predictability, and, often, efficiency. Developers spend less time making decisions about tools or processes and more time focusing on coding. This can expedite onboarding, simplify training, and ensure that best practices are applied consistently.
However, the rigidity of defined pathways might stifle innovation. Developers may find it challenging to introduce new tools or practices, even if they're more suitable for specific tasks. Moreover, a one-size-fits-all approach may not cater to the nuanced needs of diverse projects.
Flexible pathways
: On the other hand, a flexible IDP offers teams the liberty to devise their workflows and integrate tools of their choice. Such flexibility can foster innovation and allow teams to adopt emerging technologies rapidly and tailor workflows to project-specific requirements.
However, excessive flexibility can lead to a fragmented tech ecosystem within an organization. It can introduce inconsistencies in how different teams operate, making collaboration and resource sharing more complicated. Additionally, managing and supporting a wide array of tools can strain the operations team and dilute the very purpose of an IDP.
Striking a balance
: The key is to find a middle ground – perhaps a semi-opinionated platform that offers defaults but allows overrides. This approach provides developers with guidance but doesn't box them into rigid pathways.
Before deciding how opinionated an IDP should be, it's essential to evaluate the current technological landscape of the company. The following are some guiding questions:
Diversity of projects
: Are the projects within the company diverse in nature, requiring different tools and workflows? Or is there a predominant type of project that can benefit from standardized processes?
Existing tech stack
: What technologies and tools are currently in use? Are they modern and efficient, or are there legacy systems that the IDP needs to accommodate or replace?
Developer preferences
: What are the preferences and expertise of the development teams? Are they more comfortable with a certain tech stack? Can the IDP offer them the best of both worlds?
Operational challenges
: Are there existing operational bottlenecks or challenges that a more opinionated (or flexible) IDP can address?
Future goals
: Where does the company see itself in the coming years in terms of tech adoption? Is there an inclination toward certain technologies or practices? What's the future roadmap of those tools like and how well does it align with the company's direction?
Striking a balance between flexibility and control
In the quest for the optimal IDP, organizations often grapple with the dilemma of flexibility versus control. While it's tempting to cater to every possible need, it's equally vital to ensure that the platform remains manageable, secure, and consistent. Understanding where to draw the line is critical to achieving an IDP that offers the right mix of adaptability and governance.Every organization is a mosaic of diverse teams and departments, each with its unique needs, tools, and workflows. Ideally, an IDP should accommodate these variations without becoming overly convoluted. To do so, continuous feedback from the internal users is key:
Survey and understand
: Regularly surveying development teams to understand their requirements, pain points, and preferences can provide valuable insights. These insights can guide the platform's evolution.
Cross-departmental collaboration
: Encourage dialog between departments. For instance, what the development team views as flexibility, the security team might see as a potential risk. Strive for solutions that address concerns from multiple vantage points.
Modular approach
: Consider designing the platform in a modular fashion. This way, different teams can plug in the tools and features they need, without cluttering the environment for others.
Flexibility is a double-edged sword. While it caters to diverse needs, excessive flexibility can introduce complications:
Tech fragmentation
: When teams can choose any tool they want, it can lead to a plethora of technologies being in use, making integrations and collaborations cumbersome.
Increased operational overheads
: Supporting a wide array of tools can strain operational teams, leading to longer response times and potential downtimes.
Inconsistent practices
: With every team doing things their way, it becomes challenging to enforce best practices, potentially affecting product quality and security.
Analysis paralysis
: Too many choices can be overwhelming. Instead of speeding up development, it might hinder decision-making.
On the other end of the spectrum, a highly opinionated platform can be stifling:
Resistance to adoption
: Developers might resist using a platform that doesn't accommodate their preferred tools or methods.
Stagnation
: An overly rigid platform can deter innovation. Teams might find it hard to experiment with new technologies or practices.
One-size-fits-none
: While trying to be universally applicable, the platform might end up not being perfectly suited for any particular team or project.
Dependence on platform updates
: Any change or addition might require a full platform update, leading to delays and potential disruptions.
The impact on developer experience and platform adoption
An IDP, regardless of how feature-rich or innovative, will only prove its worth if developers embrace and actively use it. The intersection of flexibility, control, and overall user experience greatly influences platform adoption rates and developers' satisfaction. Let's delve into how to measure this impact and the nuances of choice in platform adoption:
Surveys and questionnaires
: Regularly solicit feedback from developers using structured surveys. This allows for quantitative measurements of satisfaction levels, pain points, and desired features. Tools such as
Net Promoter Score
(
NPS
) can be employed to gauge the likelihood of developers recommending the platform to peers.
Feedback sessions
: Organize open sessions where developers can voice concerns, provide feedback, or even brainstorm about potential improvements. Such interactive platforms not only collect feedback but can also foster a sense of community and involvement.
Platform usage metrics
: Analyze metrics such as frequency of platform usage, duration of sessions, and features most often used. Such data provides insights into which aspects of the platform are most valuable to developers and which might be causing friction.
Incident and support request analysis
: A significant increase in support requests or reported issues can indicate areas of dissatisfaction or confusion. It's essential to continually monitor and address these to enhance the developer experience.
Correlating flexibility with adoption
A common misconception while starting a platform team is that the more flexibility the platform offers, the easier the platform's adoption will be. In most cases, this leads to ad hoc DevOps work, which makes platform teams fall back into their old, fragmented DevOps way of working. To better understand this, we should understand the following concepts:
The paradox of choice
: Contrary to intuition, more choices can sometimes lead to analysis paralysis, where developers are overwhelmed by options. While it might seem that offering numerous tools and pathways would cater to every developer's needs, it can instead cause confusion or inertia, slowing down the decision-making process.
Standardization versus innovation
: While providing flexibility can spur innovation, there's also value in standardization, especially when onboarding new team members or ensuring consistent delivery across teams. A harmonized toolset and process can significantly speed up ramp-up times and lead to predictable results.
Tailoring to the majority
: By analyzing the preferences and needs of the majority of developers, organizations can strike a balance in their IDP offerings. While it's essential to cater to niche requirements, optimizing the platform for the majority can drive higher overall adoption rates.
Feedback loops
: Regularly reassess the correlation between platform flexibility and adoption. By maintaining open channels of communication with developers and monitoring platform usage trends, organizations can make iterative adjustments to better align with developers' evolving needs.
Crafting reusable solutions for flexibility over time
The technology landscape is ever-evolving, and what works today might not necessarily be the best fit tomorrow. Moreover, as an organization grows and matures, its technological requirements will inevitably shift. Therefore, while an IDP's initial setup is crucial, its ability to adapt and improve over time is just as – if not more – important. This section delves into the nuances of evolving an IDP to maintain its relevance and effectiveness. To successfully grow into platform engineering but also grow and evolve your platform practices, you should have the following:
Feedback channels
: Regular feedback mechanisms can be invaluable in understanding the changing needs of the developer community. These channels can be formal, such as periodic surveys or focus groups, or informal, such as open forums or chat groups dedicated to platform feedback.
Performance metrics
: Monitor platform usage metrics, latency, error rates, and other KPIs that can provide insights into areas that might need improvements.
External indicators
: Stay abreast of industry trends, emerging technologies, and best practices. These can act as indicators for potential areas of improvement or incorporation into the platform.
Product life cycle evaluation
: As the platform matures, certain components might become obsolete or less relevant. Regularly evaluate the life cycle of each component while considering factors such as vendor support, community engagement, and alignment with the organization's strategic goals.
A very important concept for building reusable tools is that of Golden Paths or Paved Roads.
Building golden paths
Guiding developers through a labyrinth of choices and ensuring consistency, speed, and quality is a challenge. One solution that has gained traction among leading tech companies is the concept of
golden paths
. These paths provide developers with a set of best practices, tools, and frameworks optimized for the specific environment of an organization.
Understanding golden paths
Golden paths
, as conceived by companies such as Red Hat and VMware, represent the convergence of experience and optimization. For these organizations, the golden paths are essentially the most efficient, secure, and scalable ways to achieve specific tasks or outcomes. They are the result of rigorous testing, feedback, and iteration, and they encapsulate the collective knowledge of the organization.Spotify takes a slightly different approach but with the same core idea. They term it
paved roads
. For Spotify, these are the paths of least resistance and are where developers find everything they need to get their job done quickly and efficiently. When a developer follows a paved road, they're supported by the platform, and everything
just works
. But, if they decide to go off the paved road, they might face more challenges and get less support.The establishment of golden paths has several key impacts on development:
Consistency and standardization
: Golden paths offer a standardized approach to common challenges. When developers follow these paths, the resulting solutions are consistent in design, architecture, and even coding patterns, which simplifies maintenance and troubleshooting.
Accelerated development
: Since golden paths provide proven solutions to common problems, developers don't have to
reinvent the wheel
every time. This can considerably cut down the time spent on R&D, testing, and troubleshooting.
Knowledge transfer
: For new team members, golden paths act as a primer for the organization's best practices. They can quickly come up to speed by following the laid-out paths and understanding both the
how
and the
why
behind certain decisions.
Golden paths, or paved roads, are powerful tools for organizations aiming to harmonize their development processes. While they come with undeniable benefits, organizations must strike a balance, ensuring that while processes are streamlined, innovation and adaptability are not stifled.
The Golden Path Maturity Model
As with any concept in organizational development and software engineering, the adoption of golden paths isn't a binary process. It's more apt to envision it as a maturity model, where organizations progress through various stages, refining and perfecting their approach to standardization and best practices over time. The
Golden Path Maturity Model
serves as a framework to evaluate the current state of an organization's adoption and provides a roadmap for progression.The Golden Path Maturity Model is designed to categorize organizations based on how they've adopted and integrated the concept of golden paths into their workflows. By determining where they currently stand, organizations can better identify gaps, create actionable plans, and achieve a more streamlined, efficient, and consistent development process.The following are the three different levels of maturity:
No golden path
(
Teams have their own DevOps practices
):
At this initial stage, there is little to no standardization across teams. Each team operates in silos, developing and deploying using their own methodologies and toolsets.
Pros
: Maximum flexibility; teams can choose technologies and processes tailored to their unique needs.
Cons
: Results in duplicated effort, increased complexity in collaboration, and a higher potential for errors.
Clone and forget
(
Centrally provided tools, but not a reusable platform yet
):
This is a transitional phase. Here, central teams provide solutions and tools that can be copied and adapted by individual teams. Templates to promote best practices or small automation tools are usually found at this stage.
Pros
: Introduces a level of consistency, reduces the diversity of tools, and begins to establish best practices.
Cons
: While tools and solutions are available, they are often used inconsistently. There may also be a lack of holistic integration as these solutions are not yet unified into a cohesive platform.
Golden paths as products
(
This is your IDP
):
This is the pinnacle of the maturity model. Golden paths are no longer just guidelines but are integrated as first-class entities within an IDP.
Pros
: Maximum efficiency, consistency, and scalability. Best practices are not only suggested but baked into the platform itself, streamlining development significantly.
Cons
: Requires a more considerable upfront investment in setting up, refining, and maintaining the platform.
Throughout the DevOps journey, the maturity of golden paths should progress in parallel. As teams become more agile, integrated, and automated, the golden paths should adapt to provide the most value, driving efficiency, consistency, and quality in the software development life cycle.Golden paths, when approached with the understanding of their maturity model, can serve as invaluable tools for organizations looking to drive excellence in their software development processes. By knowing where they stand and where they need to go, organizations can chart a clear course toward development efficiency and consistency.
Implementing golden paths
Golden paths represent the intersection of best practices and efficiency, guiding development teams toward optimal solutions while maintaining a level of flexibility. This section explores the systematic implementation of golden paths within an organization.Let's consider the development of a golden path for each maturity level:
No Golden Path level
:
Focus
: Start by identifying glaring inconsistencies and redundancies across teams.
Implementation
: Design a baseline golden path that introduces minimal standardization, targeting the most common tasks or tools. At this stage, you focus on the
why
, describing what outcome or goal you want to achieve with a solution or automation. With this, each team can work on creating the tools to achieve that on their own.
Clone and forget level
:
Focus
: At this level, the emphasis should be on expanding the golden path concepts to encompass more workflows and tools, but with flexibility.
Implementation
: Develop centrally-provided tools or templates that can be cloned and adapted by teams. Introduce basic integration mechanisms to ensure smoother workflows.
Golden paths as products level
:
Focus
: The focus here is on deep integration, where golden paths are fully incorporated into the IDP.
Implementation
: Design holistic solutions that unify disparate tools and processes under the umbrella of the IDP. Golden paths should be seamlessly integrated, providing an out-of-the-box solution for the most frequent development tasks. At this point, a platform is provided that covers the key capabilities needed, and a self-service onboarding method is in place to reduce friction.
Examples of golden paths
Golden paths, as best practice guides, can cover a myriad of aspects within the development ecosystem. Here, we'll delve into specific examples encompassing pipelines, observability, and deploying new applications, and how each can evolve across the maturity model.
Pipelines (CI/CD)
A common starting point for many platform teams is standardizing build and release pipelines. There are a few steps until a platform team can reach a Pipeline-as-as-Product approach:
No golden path level
: At this stage, teams might have different CI/CD processes or even lack some entirely. Pipelines could be manual, or inconsistently automated, leading to potential delays and errors.
Clone and forget level
: A central CI/CD template is provided for teams to clone and adapt. The pipeline would have stages for building, testing, and deploying, but specifics would be left for individual teams to customize based on their needs.
Golden paths as products level
: The CI/CD pipeline is a well-integrated, fully featured solution. It offers advanced features such as parallel builds, canary deployments, and rollback mechanisms. Standardized yet customizable stages ensure uniformity while allowing for unique project needs.
Figure 5.7
shows how a golden path as a product is designed for a pipeline.
Figure 5.7 – Reusable golden pipeline with Helm and ArgoCD
More details on how to build this can be found in
Chapter 6
, where you'll learn how to implement cloud-native tools such as ArgoCD and Helm.
Observability
Observability is also one of the engineering functions where we see the most diversity in terms of tools and implementation across different teams, and naturally, it is one of the usual early starting points of IDPs:
No golden path level
: Teams may use different tools and standards for logging, monitoring, and alerting. Some critical metrics or logs might be missed, and there could be redundancies in others.
Clone and forget level
: A basic observability template provides guidelines on what metrics to monitor, how to log effectively, and when to trigger alerts. However, teams might still choose different tools or slightly modify these guidelines based on their preferences.
Golden paths as products level
: A unified observability platform is provided, with integrations to all major tools and services used within the organization. This platform will prescribe best practices, such as setting up dashboards, monitoring key performance indicators, and setting alerts based on SLOs. The data collected is consistent across teams, facilitating easier cross-team troubleshooting and analysis.
Deploying new applications
Platform teams are designed to help organizations scale, so besides modernizing existing tools and services, IDPs should enable easier development and deployment of new greenfield projects:
No golden path level
: Deploying a new application could be a tedious process, with manual configurations, unchecked dependencies, and potential for errors. There might not be a standardized way to handle environment variables, secrets, or database migrations.
Clone and forget level
: A central repository or wiki provides templates and guidelines for deploying new applications. It would cover aspects such as containerization, environment setup, and database initialization. Teams can take these guidelines and adapt them based on the specifics of their application or the infrastructure they're deploying to.
Golden paths as products level
: The IDP provides a seamless creation experience. The platform provides a template repository, scaffolding features, and integration with different tools. Pre-configured templates for various types of applications (web applications, microservices, and batch jobs) ensure that applications are deployed using best practices consistently.
By progressing through these stages, organizations gradually eliminate inefficiencies and uncertainties. The result is a streamlined, predictable, and efficient development and deployment process that reaps the benefits in terms of time, cost, and product quality.
Transitioning to platform
The shift to a platform-centric model from traditional development approaches or scattered, tool-based environments is both strategic and intricate. The underlying motivation for such a transition often springs from a desire for streamlined processes, enhanced collaboration, and an aspiration to be at par with the best in the industry. However, convincing stakeholders, addressing concerns, and navigating challenges are crucial to ensure a smooth transition. One of the very important early decisions of a platform team is what to build and what to use as a third-party procured service.
Understanding the platform transition
One of the main drivers behind the shift to a platform model is scalability. Organizations, as they burgeon, often find that linearly scaling their infrastructure and development processes is not just cumbersome but also inefficient. A platform provides a more holistic solution, offering an avenue for seamlessly onboarding new teams or projects. This comes hand in hand with consistency. By adopting a platform-centric approach, companies can ensure that there are standardized tools, processes, and guidelines in place. This not only ensures consistent development and deployment practices across various teams but also reduces the chances of errors. Additionally, the inherent automation capabilities of platforms can markedly accelerate product development cycles by reducing manual touchpoints. And beyond just the technical advantages, platforms can be a catalyst for fostering a more collaborative organizational culture. This unity in approach and being able to share resources and best practices can break down the silos that often hinder innovation.
Convincing leadership
Transitioning to a platform is not a minor undertaking and getting leadership on board is pivotal. When presenting the case to the upper echelons of management, it's beneficial to underscore the long-term cost savings. This can be attributed to reduced overhead, the potential for tool consolidation, and the ability to bring products to market at a faster clip. Moreover, in today's hyper-competitive business landscape, having a platform can offer a tangible strategic advantage. It not only facilitates rapid adaptability to market changes but can also position the company as a vanguard in its domain. On the risk front, the consistency that platforms bring to the table can be a significant risk mitigator. With standardized environments and processes, outcomes become more predictable. Plus, in the ever-evolving technological landscape, being platform-centric ensures the organization remains agile and future-ready.
Convincing teams
While leadership buy-in is crucial, the teams – the people who will be working with the platform day in, day out – need to be convinced too. It's essential to illustrate the tangible benefits they stand to gain. This could be in the form of superior integrated tooling that the platform offers, or the potential reduction in repetitive tasks thanks to automation. By removing mundane tasks from their plate, teams can pivot their focus more toward innovation and core development activities. Furthermore, platforms often come embedded with industry best practices. This means that teams have a perpetual opportunity to work with state-of-the-art technologies and methodologies, enhancing their own skill sets in the process. And finally, the promise of a more collaborative work environment can be a significant draw. Platforms inherently promote collective problem-solving by eroding the barriers that exist in more siloed work setups.Of course, the path to platform transition is not without its challenges. It's common to encounter resistance, especially from teams that are deeply entrenched in a certain way of working. They may view this shift as a disruption. Technical challenges can also crop up, especially when trying to integrate existing tools or processes into the new platform. In some cases, there may be a discernible skill gap, necessitating either hiring new talent or upskilling current employees. And, from an economic standpoint, the initial phases of the transition – encompassing setup, migration, and training – can bear significant cost implications.
Managing the transition process
The crux of any technological transition doesn't rest solely on the shoulders of the technology itself, but rather on the individuals who utilize it daily. Legacy tools and systems often come with a deep sense of ownership and familiarity for teams. Convincing these teams to move away requires a delicate balance of rational argument, empathetic understanding, and tangible incentives:
Recognizing emotional attachments
: Legacy tools are more than just code or interfaces; they are repositories of memories, hard work, and countless hours of debugging. Acknowledging this emotional tie is the first step toward initiating a dialog about transition.
Showcasing tangible benefits
: A theoretical argument about efficiency may not resonate as powerfully as a hands-on demonstration. Arrange for workshops or pilot programs where teams can see the advantages of platform solutions in terms of speed, ease, and potential outcomes firsthand.
Incentivizing the transition
: Offering training programs, certifications, or even gamified challenges related to the platform can turn the transition into an engaging and rewarding journey. This not only helps in skill-building but also fosters a sense of achievement among members.
Open feedback channels
: An open-door policy, where teams can voice their concerns, reservations, or even nostalgic recounts of their experiences with older tools, can be invaluable. It builds trust and paves the way for constructive discussions.
Collaboration is the bedrock upon which successful platform transitions are built. As the move toward a platform-centric approach gains momentum, the nature of collaboration changes. It's not just about working alongside but working intertwined, with clear goals and collective responsibilities.The structure of the platform team becomes crucial in this environment. While detailed insights on this are reserved for
Chapter 7
, it's important to highlight a few critical aspects here:
Diverse skill sets
: A platform team shouldn't be monolithic in its expertise. Ideally, it should comprise members with varied skill sets, from infrastructure experts to UI/UX designers, ensuring a holistic approach to platform design and development.
Clearly defined roles
: As collaboration intensifies, role clarity ensures there's no overlap or ambiguity. Whether it's the platform architect, the security expert, or the integration specialist, each role should have a clear set of responsibilities and deliverables.
Promoting inter-team dialogs
: Encourage regular interactions between the platform team and other teams. Such interactions are an opportunity to exchange feedback, share success stories, and jointly troubleshoot challenges.
Iterative development approach
: Platforms evolve, and so should the team's approach. Adopting an iterative mindset, where feedback from internal users (other teams) is actively sought and incorporated, ensures the platform remains relevant and user-centric.
Building a platform is a transformative endeavor, not just in terms of technology but also in organizational culture. It demands a departure from siloed operations and a move toward a more cohesive, collaborative future. With the right team structure and motivation strategies, this journey can lead to unparalleled efficiency and innovation.
Summary
Throughout this chapter, we embarked on a comprehensive journey to demystify the architecture and nuances of IDPs. Early on, we recognized the significance of the
tipping point
, that pivotal moment when an organization realizes its DevOps processes have evolved beyond their initial capabilities. It becomes evident that the complexity they've encountered requires a more specialized platform engineering team. Recognizing this transition early on brings a plethora of advantages, from enhanced efficiency to a more standardized development environment free from unnecessary bottlenecks.Our exploration continued with VSM, a lean management tool that visualizes the steps involved in taking a product from its inception to its delivery to the customer. Emphasizing its importance, we underscored how VSM serves as a pivotal tool during the transition to platform engineering. It offers unique insights, helping organizations identify inefficiencies, refine processes, and make well-informed decisions about integrating tools and practices from their DevOps landscape into their budding platform.As we navigated the shift from a toolkit-centric DevOps approach to a platform-centric engineering model, the necessity to build upon past successes became evident. This journey requires meticulous planning, execution, and continuous reflection and improvement. It's not just about introducing new tools; it's about leveraging and transforming existing capabilities into platform competencies.However, such transitions aren't without their challenges. Effective change management emerged as a key theme, especially when decommissioning tools and services. To aid in this challenging process, we provided a step-by-step guide, emphasizing the importance of mitigating resistance, ensuring widespread adoption, and facilitating a smooth transition to innovative tools and processes.Concluding this chapter, we touched upon the cultural transformation that accompanies a shift to platform engineering. It's more than just tools and processes; it's about fostering a culture of collaboration, learning, and support. And in this cultural realm lies the true difference between a successful and a lackluster transition to platform engineering.In the next chapter, we'll build upon the theoretical architectures described here so that we can leverage cloud-native tools to build our platform.

6 Building an IDP with Cloud-native Tools – Core Platform
Join our book community on Discord
https://packt.link/IOzft
In the world of platform engineering, efficiency and flexibility are king. To that end, we'll start by laying out our
Internal Developer Platform
(
IDP
) architecture. The goal is to build a composable, flexible, and scalable platform that empowers developers to manage applications with minimal operational overhead. By the end of this chapter, you'll have a high-level understanding of various cloud-native tools and how to integrate them to build a multi-cloud Kubernetes setup using a hub-spoke pattern for cluster management. We will leverage infrastructure as code based on YAML and Kubernetes objects managed through a GitOps approach and set up an observability platform for a comprehensive view of our applications' performance.In this chapter, we're going to cover the following main topics:
Approaches to platform architecture
Platform capability 1: compute
Platform capability 2: Infrastructure as Code
Approaches to platform architecture
There are multiple ongoing efforts to build standards for architecting IDPs. Due to the nature of IDPs, most approaches provide a high-level guide or the basic building blocks. Firstly,
CNOE (Cloud Native Operational Excellence)
, aims to provide a broad framework of different platform capabilities and how those integrate, as well as what tools can be used to build those. On the other hand, BACK stack is a more prescriptive approach to building a Minimum Viable Platform, using four well know cloud-native tools.
Introduction to the B.A.C.K. stack
The
BACK
stack, comprising
Backstage, ArgoCD, Crossplane, and Kyverno
, forms the foundation of our IDP, utilizing four CNCF projects. These projects interact in different ways to build core platform capabilities (
Figure 6.1
).
Figure 6.1 – Architecture of the BACK stack
Each of these components brings unique strengths to the platform, creating an ecosystem that is both developer-friendly and robust in managing and deploying cloud-native applications. The four components of the BACK stack are:
Backstage
(
B
):
The Developer Empowerment Portal
– Backstage, as described in
Chapter 5
, stands out as a self-service portal that significantly simplifies developers' interaction with the platform's infrastructure and services. It centralizes all the tools, resources, and documentation developers need into a single, intuitive interface. This consolidation transforms the developer experience from navigating through a complex set of tools to a streamlined, efficient workflow. Backstage enables teams to manage microservices, understand service dependencies, and track resource usage, all from a unified dashboard. This visibility not only boosts productivity but also fosters a culture of transparency and collaboration within the development ecosystem.
ArgoCD
(
A
):
Streamlining Software Delivery with GitOps
- ArgoCD epitomizes the GitOps methodology by automating the
continuous delivery
(
CD
) process in Kubernetes. It synchronizes applications with their desired states defined in Git repositories, ensuring that the deployed applications are up-to-date with the version-controlled source. This approach minimizes deployment errors and accelerates the delivery cycle, making it an ideal tool for teams adopting DevOps practices. ArgoCD's declarative setup fosters a transparent, auditable, and reproducible deployment process, crucial for maintaining high standards of software quality and reliability.
Crossplane
(
C
):
Universal Control for Infrastructure Provisioning
- Crossplane extends Kubernetes to manage and compose infrastructure resources from multiple providers in a Kubernetes-native way. It abstracts the complexity of underlying infrastructure into a set of declarative APIs, enabling developers to provision and manage cloud resources as easily as they manage Kubernetes resources. This abstraction is key to accelerating development cycles, as it allows developers to focus on building applications without being bogged down by infrastructure intricacies. Crossplane's approach to
Infrastructure as Code
(
IaC
) not only enhances productivity but also ensures consistency and compliance across the cloud environment.
Kyverno (K
):
Enforcing Policies Elegantly
- Kyverno is a
Policy as Code
(
PaC
) tool designed specifically for Kubernetes, enabling organizations to validate, mutate, and generate Kubernetes resources through policies. This tool is crucial for ensuring that clusters and workloads comply with company policies, security standards, and best practices. Kyverno simplifies the enforcement of complex organizational rules without writing custom code, making it easier to maintain a secure and compliant Kubernetes environment.
