Its ability to proactively detect and prevent configuration drift and potential misconfigurations before they become issues is invaluable in maintaining operational integrity in dynamic cloud-native environments.
Each component of the BACK stack plays a pivotal role in shaping our IDP. Backstage simplifies and organizes the developer experience, ArgoCD automates and secures the deployment process, Crossplane democratizes infrastructure management, and Kyverno ensures compliance and security are never afterthoughts. Together, they form the foundation of a composable, scalable, and efficient IDP.
Introduction to Cloud Native Operational Excellence
Cloud Native Operational Excellence
(
CNOE
) represents a significant shift in how enterprises approach their cloud platform strategies, especially when adopting
Open Source Software
(
OSS
) as the foundation. This approach is designed to address the complexities and challenges associated with selecting and implementing technologies in a cloud-native landscape, where the choices made can have far-reaching implications for the next 3-5 years.CNOE is not just about creating a unified control plane or a set of CI/CD tools; it's a broader framework for integrating various components and capabilities that enhance application integration and delivery. It's not about introducing new technologies or managed services but providing a methodology to interact and integrate existing tools effectively. CNOE is fully open-source, encouraging customization and broad usability, and it's not responsible for operationalizing the toolchain, meaning enterprises still need to fund and manage their open-source tools within the IDP.Enterprises face the daunting task of choosing technologies that will not only support their current operational needs but also remain relevant and efficient in the future. The CNOE framework addresses this by bringing together enterprises operating at similar scales to collaboratively navigate their operational technology decisions, thereby de-risking their tooling investments and coordinating contributions.Key challenges that CNOE addresses include the following:
The Paralysis of Choice
: The vast CNCF landscape and the proliferation of tools often leave enterprises overwhelmed, leading to a lack of consensus and maintainability challenges.
Evolving Definitions of CI/CD
: Traditional systems are becoming outdated, making way for more modern tools like ArgoCD, Flux, and Tekton that embrace paradigms like GitOps.
Declarative, Centralized Control Planes
: The rise of Kubernetes has created an ecosystem that is fundamentally different from previous generations, necessitating a shift in tooling and practices.
Developer Workflow Discontinuity
: Despite technological advancements, core developer workflows have remained largely unchanged, leading to a gap between development and delivery.
Fragmentation in Software Delivery
: The multitude of languages, IaC platforms, and packaging systems has led to non-portable software components, making it challenging to consolidate into a unified application.
Key tenants
CNOE operates based on the following key tenets:
Open Source First
: Prioritizing open-source technology for technology verticals to ensure alignment and collaborative freedom.
Community-Driven
: Direction and decisions are driven by the community and its governing body, focusing on technology selection and contribution levels.
Tools, Not Practices
: CNOE suggests tools and configurations, leaving practices up to the individual companies.
Powered by Kubernetes, But Not Limited
: While Kubernetes is the primary environment, CNOE doesn't limit orchestration to only Kubernetes environments.
Standardized Infrastructure, Customizable Development
: Balancing platform requirements enforced by security and infrastructure operators with usability guaranteed by platform operators and developers.
Built to Be Shared
: CNOE's deliverables, including reference architecture and deployment packages, are developed collaboratively and made available to the larger open-source community.
CNOE's approach to reference implementations emphasizes collaboration and open development. It involves creating architecture and deployment packages in a transparent manner, engaging all participating members. This approach ensures that the solutions are not only practical and robust but also adaptable and shareable within the broader open-source community. CNOE offers a comprehensive framework for enterprises to navigate the complex landscape of cloud-native technologies. By focusing on collaboration, open source, and community-driven decisions, CNOE aims to create an environment where enterprises can de-risk their technology bets, coordinate contributions, and effectively implement cloud-native tools and practices that align with their long-term business outcomes.A high-level overview of CNOE and its different capabilities and patterns can be seen in
Figure 6.2
:
Figure 6.2 – CNOE Capabilities
After seeing different frameworks describing common architectures and patterns to build platforms, the next step is to start building our platform's core capabilities.
Platform capability 1: compute
According to the CNCF, more than 70% of IDPs are built on top of Kubernetes and usually platform runtime capabilities are built on top of Kubernetes. Although spinning up a single Kubernetes cluster has become very easy, managing Kubernetes at scale, using best practices and applying an Infrastructure as Code mode, is fairly complicated.
Kubernetes the easy way
Kubernetes the Hard Way
, by Kelsey Hightower, is a well-known guide to setting up a Kubernetes cluster manually. It's aimed at individuals who want to understand every detail of bootstrapping a Kubernetes cluster, providing a deep dive into the configuration and interconnection of various Kubernetes components. However, this method, while highly educational, is complex and time-consuming, as well as needing significant additional work to make a cluster production-ready and handle day 2 operations.Nowadays, the landscape has evolved with various tools and services that simplify Kubernetes deployment. These solutions abstract much of the complexity involved in setting up and managing a Kubernetes environment. They range from open-source tools to managed services provided by cloud vendors, offering a spectrum of options catering to different levels of control and ease of use. This evolution marks a significant shift from the manual, intricate process described in
Kubernetes the Hard Way
to more streamlined, automated approaches, making Kubernetes accessible to a broader range of users and scenarios.Deploying Kubernetes can be approached in several ways, each suited to different organizational needs and operational preferences. Understanding these options is key to choosing the right path for your organization's Kubernetes journey.Open-source Kubernetes solutions like the following offer flexibility and community support. They are ideal for organizations looking for customization and control over their Kubernetes environment:
Rancher
: An open-source multi-cluster management platform, Rancher simplifies the deployment and operation of Kubernetes. It provides a user-friendly interface for managing clusters across various environments, making it a popular choice for organizations seeking an accessible yet powerful Kubernetes management tool.
Gardener
: Developed by SAP, Gardener manages Kubernetes clusters across different cloud providers or on-premises. Its design as a Kubernetes extension makes it a natural fit for organizations already invested in the Kubernetes ecosystem.
Kubermatic KubeOne
: This is a lifecycle management tool for highly available Kubernetes clusters. KubeOne automates cluster operations across multiple cloud providers, making it a robust choice for multi-cloud Kubernetes deployments.
For enterprises requiring additional support, security, and integration with existing systems, enterprise Kubernetes solutions are a preferred choice:
Openshift by Red Hat
: An enterprise Kubernetes platform, Openshift provides additional security, integrated development tools, and a comprehensive ecosystem. It's suitable for organizations seeking an enterprise-grade, secure, and fully supported Kubernetes environment.
VMware Tanzu
: This suite of products and services is designed to build, run, and manage Kubernetes-controlled container-based applications. Tanzu integrates with VMware's well-established virtualization infrastructure, offering a seamless experience for existing VMware customers.
Managed Kubernetes services like the following offer ease of use, scalability, and reliability without the overhead of internal management:
Fully Managed by Hyperscalers
: Services like
Google Kubernetes Engine
(
GKE
),
Amazon Elastic Kubernetes Service
(
EKS
), and
Azure Kubernetes Service
(
AKS
) manage the Kubernetes infrastructure, letting you focus on deploying and managing your applications. They provide seamless integration with their respective cloud ecosystems, offering a hassle-free Kubernetes experience.
Emerging Solutions
: Platforms like Rafay and Platform9 focus on providing managed control planes, offering flexibility in managing Kubernetes across multiple environments. These solutions are gaining popularity for their ability to simplify Kubernetes operations while providing granular control.
Each method offers unique benefits, and understanding these can guide you to the best choice for your organization's specific needs.
Kubeadm - a project to simplify Kubernetes cluster management
kubeadm
is a tool developed to simplify the process of setting up and managing a Kubernetes cluster. It's designed as part of the Kubernetes project and provides a straightforward way to get a secure and up-to-date cluster up and running in a more manageable way compared to manual setup.The Kubeadm process involves the following steps:
Install Prerequisites on All Nodes
: This includes setting up a container runtime and ensuring network configurations are correct. Prerequisites also involve installing
kubeadm
,
kubelet
, and
kubectl
on each node.
Initialize the Master Node with kubeadm init
: This command sets up the Kubernetes control plane on the master node. Under the hood, it runs several tasks such as generating necessary certificates for secure communication, setting up the Kubernetes API server, and configuring the
etcd
datastore.
A single command to setup the master node:
kubeadm init --pod-network-cidr=10.244.0.0/16
Set Up Worker Nodes with kubeadm join
: Once the master is initialized, worker nodes can be added to the cluster using the
kubeadm join
command. This command requires a token and CA certificate hash provided by the initialization output of the master node.
A single command to setup and join the worker nodes:
kubeadm join [API_SERVER_IP]:6443 --token [TOKEN] --discovery-token-ca-cert-hash sha256:[HASH]
Running those commands includes multiple tasks for each. What happens under the Hood?:
kubeadm init
: This command starts by running pre-flight checks to ensure the node can run Kubernetes. It then creates a unique token for node registration, sets up the necessary certificates, and configures the control plane components.
kubeadm join
: It communicates with the specified master, verifies the provided token, and configures the node to join the cluster as a worker, setting up necessary Kubernetes components.
kubeadm greatly streamlines the Kubernetes deployment process, making it accessible to a wider range of users, from beginners to experienced system administrators. By abstracting complex tasks into simple commands, it reduces the barrier to entry for managing Kubernetes clusters.
How many clusters do I need?
In Kubernetes' early adoption phase, many organizations opted for a single, large cluster. This approach stemmed from the desire to simplify management and maximize resource utilization. However, several challenges and limitations soon became apparent:
Scalability and Performance Issues
: Large clusters often faced difficulties in scaling efficiently, especially as the number of nodes and pods increased. This led to challenges in scheduling, network traffic management, and overall cluster performance.
Risk Concentration
: A single cluster meant that all applications shared the same underlying infrastructure. This created a single point of failure, where issues in one part of the cluster could potentially impact the entire system, increasing the risk of widespread outages.
Complexity in Security and Compliance
: Managing security and compliance in a large, monolithic cluster was challenging. Implementing and enforcing varied security policies across different applications within the same cluster was not only complex but also posed significant risks.
Resource Contention
: In a single-cluster setup, different applications often competed for the same resources, leading to contention and inefficient resource utilization. This made it difficult to guarantee the performance and availability of critical applications.
To address these challenges, the trend shifted towards deploying multiple smaller clusters. This approach aligns better with the principles of cloud-native architecture. The decision between one cluster per application versus one cluster with all applications per environment hinges on several factors:
Isolation
: One cluster per application offers maximum isolation, reducing the risk of cross-application interference and improving security. However, it can increase management overhead and complexity.
Cost Efficiency
: While multiple clusters provide better isolation and resource allocation, they can also increase operational costs. Balancing cost with operational needs is crucial in deciding the right approach.
Resource Management
: For organizations with diverse application portfolios, multiple clusters can offer more flexible and efficient resource management, tailored to the specific needs of each application.
Security and Compliance
: Different applications may have different security and compliance requirements. Multiple clusters allow for more tailored security management, aligning with specific regulatory and organizational standards.
Technical considerations, such as network latency, cluster capacity, and operational complexity, play a significant role in deciding the number of clusters.
Supporting multiple workload types
In complex organizations, the diversity of deployment types, including VMs, containers, and functions, necessitates a versatile approach to workload management. Kubernetes, traditionally known for container orchestration, can also support these varied workloads through tools like the following:
Knative for Serverless Functions
: Knative extends Kubernetes to manage serverless functions, providing features like scaling to zero, event-driven architecture, and quick deployments. It simplifies building and deploying applications in a serverless manner, allowing developers to focus on writing code without worrying about underlying infrastructure.
KubeVirt for Virtual Machines
: KubeVirt enables Kubernetes to run and manage virtual machines alongside containers. This integration provides a unified platform for managing all workloads, making it easier to migrate VM-based applications to container environments and vice versa. KubeVirt creates one pod per VM that is designed to manage that VM, as shown in
Figure 6.3
.
Figure 6.3 – KubeVirt Architecture
Both Knative and KubeVirt represent Kubernetes' expanding capabilities, adapting to the diverse needs of modern, complex IT environments. They exemplify the evolution of Kubernetes from a container-centric platform to a more comprehensive orchestration solution.
Patterns of multi-cluster Kubernetes
Kubernetes plays a pivotal role in enabling multi-cloud strategies for organizations. It acts as a bridge between different cloud providers, providing a consistent layer of abstraction over diverse cloud infrastructures. This abstraction allows development teams to focus on building and deploying applications without needing to worry about the underlying cloud-specific details.In a multi-cloud environment, Kubernetes facilitates seamless migration and management of applications. It offers a unified set of APIs and tools across clouds, which simplifies the process of deploying and managing applications in a heterogeneous cloud environment.Effective strategies for multi-cloud deployment with Kubernetes include using cluster federation to manage multiple clusters across different clouds, implementing centralized logging and monitoring for visibility across clouds, using consistent CI/CD pipelines for deployment automation, and leveraging centralized authentication and authorization to incorporate enterprise identity management.Exploring multi-cluster Kubernetes architectures, particularly cluster federation, involves understanding how to manage multiple Kubernetes clusters as a single entity. Cluster federation allows for the synchronization of configurations, policies, and deployments across various clusters, regardless of their location. This architecture is especially useful in scenarios requiring high availability, disaster recovery, and global load balancing. By federating clusters, organizations can spread their workloads across different regions or cloud providers, enhancing resilience and optimizing resource usage. The federation also simplifies the management of common resources and policies across these clusters, making it an efficient strategy for handling complex, multi-cluster environments.The hub-spoke architecture in multi-cluster Kubernetes environments is a strategic approach to managing multiple clusters. In this model, a central
hub
cluster oversees multiple
spoke
clusters. The hub serves as the administrative control point, from where global policies, configurations, and monitoring are managed. Each spoke cluster operates as an independent entity but is linked to the hub for centralized governance and oversight. This architecture simplifies the management of multi-cluster environments, allowing for consistent policy enforcement and a unified view of cluster health and performance across different cloud providers or regions. The hub-spoke model enhances operational efficiency and is especially effective in scenarios requiring centralized control with decentralized operation.Cluster replication in multi-cluster Kubernetes architectures refers to a strategy where one or more clusters are replicated across different environments, usually for the purposes of high availability, disaster recovery, or geographic distribution. This approach involves synchronizing the state and configuration of one cluster (the primary) to other clusters (replicas). Replication can be used to ensure that if one cluster fails or becomes unreachable, its workload can be rapidly shifted to or taken over by another cluster. This model enhances resilience and ensures continuous operation, which is vital for mission-critical applications. Cluster replication is particularly useful in scenarios where consistent performance across geographically distributed regions is required.
Cluster connectivity
Intra-cluster connectivity within Kubernetes, particularly involving service meshes, is a critical component for managing communication between services. A service mesh like Istio or Linkerd provides a dedicated infrastructure layer for handling service-to-service communication in a microservices architecture. This layer facilitates complex operational requirements such as service discovery, load balancing, encryption, observability, and traceability. By implementing a service mesh, Kubernetes can offer enhanced networking features, more granular control over traffic, and improved security, all of which are essential for efficiently managing microservices-based applications within a Kubernetes cluster.Skupper is a layer 7 service interconnect designed for Kubernetes, enabling secure communication across multiple clusters without the need for VPNs or special firewall rules (
Figure 6.4
). It facilitates multi-cloud strategies by allowing applications to span across various cloud providers, data centers, and regions.
Figure 6.4 – Skupper Architecture of L7 networking
Skupper is simple to set up, requiring no changes to existing applications and no administrator privileges. It supports transparent communication for various protocols and is secure by design, offering communication across clusters without exposing service ports on the internet. Additionally, Skupper provides multicloud, hybrid cloud, and edge-to-edge connectivity, along with dynamic load balancing and cost- and locality-aware traffic forwarding.To get started with Skupper, you can set it up via Helm, and then connect two services located in different clusters. Skupper simplifies the process of connecting Kubernetes services across different environments without requiring significant changes to your existing setup. Getting started with Skupper includes:
Installing Skupper via Helm
:
Add the Skupper Helm Repository
: First, you need to add the Skupper Helm repository to your Helm client:
helm repo add skupper https://skupper.io/charts
Update Your Helm Repositories
: Update your Helm repositories to ensure you have the latest Skupper chart:
helm repo update
Install Skupper
: Install Skupper in your Kubernetes cluster. This can be done in the namespace where you want Skupper to manage communication:
helm install skupper skupper/skupper \
--namespace <your-namespace>
Setting Up Skupper
:
Install Skupper
: On Linux or Mac, you can use the install script to download and extract the cli:
curl https://skupper.io/install.sh | sh
Initialize Skupper
: Use the Skupper CLI to initialize Skupper in your Kubernetes clusters:
skupper init
Check Skupper Status
: Verify the status of Skupper in your cluster:
skupper status
Connecting Two Services in Different Clusters
:
Creating a Skupper Token
: In the first cluster, generate a Skupper token that will be used to authorize the second cluster to connect:
skupper token create skupper-token.yaml
Connecting the Second Cluster
: In the second cluster, use the generated token to establish a connection to the first cluster:
skupper token connect skupper-token.yaml
Exposing Services
: To make a service in one cluster available to the other, use the
skupper expose
command. For example, to expose a service named
my-service
on port
8080
:
skupper expose deployment my-service --port 8080
Skupper provides a lightweight way to connect services across multiple Kubernetes clusters, enabling secure and seamless communication across different cloud environments. Compared to other service mesh solutions, Skupper focuses on application connectivity and can be deployed on a per-namespace basis instead of cluster-wide.We've now built an application runtime layer in our platform. Our compute capability can address needs of containerized applications, or running in VMs, or even applications designed to run serverless.Moving forward our platform aims to optimize infrastructure management and building an IaC capability will help us do that.
Platform capability 2: Infrastructure as Code
IaC plays a critical role in the organizations DevOps path. IaC has reached a very mature state in recent years, with a multitude of tools and practices available. Although this can benefit organizations, it also poses challenges, increasing the number of tools involved and maintained in order to deploy applications, manage infrastructure etc.
Extending Kubernetes
Kubernetes, as a robust and flexible platform, offers extensive capabilities for managing containerized applications. However, to tailor Kubernetes to meet specific organizational requirements, understanding and leveraging its extensibility is crucial. This involves a deep dive into the Kubernetes Controller Manager, the control loop mechanism, and the implementation of
Custom Resource Definitions
(
CRDs
) and custom controllers.At the core of Kubernetes' ability to manage and maintain the desired state of applications and resources is the Kubernetes Controller Manager. This component runs various controllers, each responsible for specific aspects of cluster management.The fundamental operation of these controllers is based on a
control loop mechanism
. This loop continually watches the state of your cluster through the Kubernetes API, compares the current state with the desired state, and takes action to reconcile any differences. The process involves three primary steps:
Observe
: The controller observes the current state of the cluster or a particular resource.
Analyze
: It compares this observed state to the desired state defined by the user or default system settings.
Act
: If discrepancies are found, the controller takes action to bring the current state in line with the desired state.
This continuous monitoring and automatic correction mechanism is what grants Kubernetes its self-healing and auto-scaling capabilities.To extend Kubernetes beyond its built-in resource types, CRDs and custom controllers are used. These tools allow users to define and manage new, custom resources that are native to Kubernetes.
Implementing Custom Resource Definitions
CRDs allow you to define new types of resources that behave like built-in Kubernetes objects. This includes defining the name, version, and schema of the new resources. A CRD defines how these new objects will look and what features they will have. Once defined, these custom resources can be used like any standard Kubernetes object. You can create, update, and delete these resources using kubectl or other Kubernetes API clients.Alongside CRDs, custom controllers are necessary to imbue these new resources with functionality. These controllers use the same control loop mechanism as built-in controllers to watch the custom resources and ensure the correct state. Creating a custom controller in Kubernetes can be an intricate task, but tools like Kubebuilder simplify the process. Kubebuilder is an SDK that facilitates building Kubernetes APIs and controllers using CRDs.The following prerequisites are required for creating a custom controller using Kubebuilder:
Go
: Install the latest version of Go (1.13+ is recommended).
kubectl
: Ensure kubectl is installed and configured to communicate with your Kubernetes cluster.
Kubebuilder
: Install Kubebuilder from the official release page (
https://kubebuilder.io/
)
Here's a step-by-step guide:
Initialize Your Project
:
Create a Project Directory
: Create and navigate to a new directory for your project:
mkdir my-controller cd my-controller
Initialize the Project
: Use Kubebuilder to initialize a new project:
kubebuilder init --domain mydomain.com --repo \
my-controller
Replace
mydomain.com
with your domain and
my-controller
with your project name.
Create an API and Controller
: Run the following command to create a new API and controller:
kubebuilder create api --group webapp --version v1 --kind Guestbook
This command creates a new CRD
Guestbook
in the API group
webapp
with version
v1
. Confirm the creation of the controller and resource.
Define the API Spec and Status
:
Edit API Types
: Modify the
api/v1/guestbook_types.go
file to define the spec and status of your CRD. For example:
type GuestbookSpec struct {
  // Add fields here
}
type GuestbookStatus struct {
  // Add fields here
}
Update the Generated Code
: After modifying the API types, run the following command to update the generated code for your CRD:
make generate
Implement the Controller Logic
: Implement the reconciliation logic in the
controllers/guestbook_controller.go
file. This involves defining how your controller should handle changes to your resources.
Generate CRD Manifests
: Run the following command to generate the CRD manifests:
make manifests
Install the CRD into the Cluster
: Use
kubectl
to install your CRD into the cluster:
make install
Run the Controller
: You can run your controller locally against your Kubernetes cluster:
make run
Deploy the Controller
:
Build and Push Your Image
: Build and push the container image for your controller.
Deploy the Controller
: Update the deployment manifests in the
config/
directory and deploy your controller to the cluster.
Kubernetes Operators represent a paradigm shift in the way applications are managed in Kubernetes environments. They extend Kubernetes' capabilities, allowing for more automated and efficient management of complex, stateful applications. This introduction to Kubernetes Operators will explore what they are, how they work, and their significance in modern cloud-native application management.According to RedHat
"Operators are a method of packaging, deploying, and managing a Kubernetes application. A Kubernetes application is an application that is both deployed on Kubernetes and managed using the Kubernetes APIs and kubectl tooling."
The key idea behind Operators is to encode the operational knowledge of experienced administrators into software. This allows for automating complex tasks that would traditionally require manual intervention. In simple terms, an operator is designed to detect changes in a custom resource, and use the Kubernetes API to adjust the current state to meet the desired state (
Figure 6.5
)
Figure 6.5 – How does an operator work
Operators are implemented as a set of application-specific controllers deployed on Kubernetes. These controllers extend the Kubernetes API and manage the lifecycle of specific resources related to the application. They watch for changes to objects related to their application, then take the necessary actions to reach the desired state.The core components of an Operator include:
CRDs
: CRDs extend the Kubernetes API to introduce new resource types. These custom resources represent the application and its components.
Custom controllers
: These are the brains of the Operator. They watch for changes to the custom resources and other Kubernetes objects and take actions to align the current state of the system with the desired state.
Operational Logic
: This is encoded into the Operator and represents the human knowledge required to manage the application. It includes handling updates, backups, failovers, scaling, and other operational tasks.
Operators offer several key benefits in managing applications within Kubernetes:
Automated Application Management
: They automate complex operational tasks, reducing the need for manual intervention and enabling more consistent and reliable operations.
Deeper Insights and Control
: Operators provide detailed insights into the application's state and behavior and allow for more granular control over its lifecycle.
Easier Deployment and Scalability
: Operators make deploying and scaling applications more straightforward and consistent across different environments.
Customization and Extensibility
: Operators can be tailored to the specific needs of an application, providing custom functionality and workflows.
Operators are particularly beneficial in scenarios where applications require complex setup, configuration, and maintenance. Common use cases include:
Stateful Applications
: Managing databases, message brokers, and other stateful services that require persistent storage and complex lifecycle management
Complex Deployments
: Applications that require complex installation and update processes can benefit from the automation provided by Operators
Multi-Tenant Systems
: Operators can manage resources and isolation in multi-tenant environments effectively
Auto-Remediation
: Automatically handling failovers, backups, and recovery processes
Kubernetes Operators are a powerful tool in the arsenal of cloud-native application management. They bring an advanced level of automation and intelligence to Kubernetes, enabling organizations to manage their applications more effectively and efficiently. As Kubernetes continues to evolve as the backbone of cloud-native environments, Operators play a crucial role in simplifying and enhancing the management of complex applications.Building an Operator using the Operator Framework involves several steps, from setting up your development environment to deploying your operator in a Kubernetes cluster.The following are the prerequisites for the process of building an Operator:
Install Go
: Ensure you have Go installed on your system (version 1.13+).
Install Docker
: You'll need Docker to build and push your operator's container image.
Kubernetes Cluster
: A local cluster (like minikube or kind) or access to a remote cluster.
Install Operator SDK
: Download and install the Operator SDK CLI tool.
Here's a detailed guide:
Create a New Operator Project
: Create a new directory and initialize it with the Operator SDK:
mkdir my-operator
cd my-operator
operator-sdk init --domain example.com --repo github.com/example/my-operator
Create a New API and Controller
: Define a new CRD and generate the corresponding controller:
operator-sdk create api --group webapp --version v1 --kind AppService --resource --controller
Define the API
:
Edit API Types
: Modify the
api/v1/appservice_types.go
file to define your CRD's spec and status.
Generate CRD manifests
: After defining your CRD, generate the CRD manifests:
make generate
make manifests
Implement the Controller
:
Controller Logic
: Write the reconciliation logic in the controller file located at
controllers/appservice_controller.go
.
Handle Resources
: Implement logic to handle the creation, update, and deletion of resources as per the spec of your Custom Resource.
Build and Push the Operator Image
:
Build Image
: Build your operator's Docker image:
make docker-build docker-push IMG="myrepo/my-operator:v0.1"
Push Image
: Push the image to a container registry:
docker push myrepo/my-operator:v0.1
Deploy the Operator
:
Install CRDs
: Install your CRDs into the cluster:
make install
Deploy Operator
: Deploy your Operator into the cluster:
make deploy IMG="myrepo/my-operator:v0.1"
Create a Sample Custom Resource
: Apply a sample custom resource to see your Operator in action:
kubectl apply -f config/samples/webapp_v1_appservice.yaml
Verify the Operator
:
Check Resources
: Verify that the Operator has created/managed the resources as defined in your CR.
Observe Logs
: Check the Operator logs for any errors or important information:
kubectl logs deployment.apps/my-operator-controller-manager -n my-operator-system -c manager
The Operator SDK provides the tools to scaffold, build, and test operators, simplifying the development process and allowing you to focus on the unique operational logic of your application, significantly simplifying operator development.
Managing infrastructure in a Kubernetes-native way
As Kubernetes evolves, so does its ability to manage resources beyond just containers. The concept of treating everything as Kubernetes resources, extended to external resources through Kubernetes Operators, has gained significant traction. This approach offers a Kubernetes-native way of handling a wide range of infrastructure components, from databases and storage systems to complete cloud service environments.The core idea here is to manage external resources using the same Kubernetes API and tooling that developers are already familiar with. This is achieved through Kubernetes Operators that are specifically designed to handle external resources.Kubernetes Operators extend the Kubernetes API to create, configure, and manage instances of complex stateful applications on behalf of a Kubernetes user. They encapsulate operational knowledge and can automate the management of external resources just as they do for in-cluster resources.For example, the AWS Service Operator allows you to create AWS resources directly from Kubernetes. This means you can define an Amazon S3 bucket or an Amazon RDS instance as a Kubernetes resource, and the operator will handle the provisioning and management of these resources in AWS, mirroring the state defined in your Kubernetes manifests.The following are the advantages of this approach:
Unified Management Interface
: Managing external resources through Kubernetes Operators allows for a consistent and unified management interface. This reduces the complexity of dealing with multiple APIs and interfaces.
Declarative Configuration
: Like other Kubernetes resources, external resources are managed declaratively. This means the desired state of these resources is defined in YAML manifests, which the operator works to achieve and maintain.
Improved Developer Productivity
: Developers can provision and manage necessary infrastructure directly from Kubernetes without needing deep expertise in the underlying platforms.
Automation and Orchestration
: Operators automate provisioning, updates, and management tasks, which can significantly reduce the operational overhead and potential for human error.
Seamless Integration
: This approach allows for seamless integration of cloud services and other external resources with the Kubernetes ecosystem, enhancing the overall cloud-native experience.
Similarly to the AWS Service Operator, more and more operators are designed to manage external resources in a Kubernetes-native way. Crossplane is a great project, designed to manage external resources in this way.
Managing cloud resources with Crossplane
Managing cloud resources with Crossplane involves a series of steps to set up Crossplane in your Kubernetes cluster, configure it to interact with your cloud provider, and then deploy and manage resources.The following are the prerequisites for setting up Crossplane:
Kubernetes Cluster
: Ensure you have access to a Kubernetes cluster
kubectl
: Installed and configured to communicate with your cluster
Here's a step-by-step guide:
Install Crossplane
:
Add the Crossplane Helm repository:
helm repo add crossplane-stable \
https://charts.crossplane.io/stable
helm repo update
Install Crossplane via Helm:
helm install crossplane \
--namespace crossplane-system \
--create-namespace \
crossplane-stable/crossplane
Install Crossplane CLI
: Follow the instructions on the Crossplane documentation to install kubectl crossplane (https://docs.crossplane.io/latest/).
Configure Provider
:
Install a Provider Package
: For example, to install the AWS provider:
kubectl crossplane install provider \
crossplane/provider-aws:v0.19.1
Configure the Provider
: Create a
ProviderConfig
resource to configure credentials that Crossplane will use to manage resources in your cloud provider.
Note
For AWS, you'll need to specify your AWS credentials in a Kubernetes secret and reference it in your
ProviderConfig
.
Define and Manage Resources
:
Define Your Resources
: Create YAML manifests for the cloud resources you want to manage (example, databases, storage buckets). These manifests will use the CRDs installed by the provider packages.
Deploy Resources
: Apply the manifests to your cluster.
Creating a
my-resource.yaml
file for deploying an RDS database using Crossplane involves defining a custom resource that specifies the configuration for the RDS instance.
apiVersion: database.aws.crossplane.io/v1beta1
kind: RDSInstance
metadata:
 name: example-rds-instance
spec:
 forProvider:
   dbInstanceClass: db.t3.micro
   masterUsername: masteruser
   masterUserPasswordSecretRef:
     name: rds-instance-creds
     key: password
   allocatedStorage: 20
   engine: mysql
   engineVersion: "5.7"
   region: us-west-2
 providerConfigRef:
   name: default
In the above yaml file:
API Version and Kind
: Specifies the API version and kind for an RDS instance in AWS
Metadata
: Includes the name of the RDS instance
Spec
: Defines the specifications of the RDS instance:
region
: AWS region where the RDS instance will be created
dbInstanceClass
: The compute and memory capacity of the instance
masterUsername
: Initial username for the database
allocatedStorage
: The size of the database in gigabytes
engine
: Database engine type (example, MySQL, PostgreSQL)
engineVersion
: Version of the database engine
skipFinalSnapshotBeforeDeletion
: Determines whether a final snapshot is created before the database is deleted
applyModificationsImmediately
: Whether changes are applied immediately or during the next maintenance window
dbSubnetGroupNameSelector
: Selects a DB Subnet Group for the instance
vpcSecurityGroupIDSelector
: Specifies the VPC security group
Write Connection Secret To Ref
: Defines where the connection details for the database will be stored as a Kubernetes secret
ProviderConfigRef
: References the provider configuration to use for this resource
Deploy this RDS instance to your Kubernetes cluster with Crossplane installed and configured by running:
kubectl apply -f my-resource.yaml
Manage Resource Dependencies
: Use Crossplane's
Composite Resources
(
XRs
) to define and manage groups of resources that must be provisioned together.
Similar to how we're now managing our external capabilities as Kubernetes resources, we'll now implement a way to manage multiple Kubernetes clusters as such capabilities. Cluster API is a tool that will help us do it.
Deploying and managing multiple Kubernetes clusters
Deploying and managing multiple Kubernetes clusters can be a complex task, especially at scale. However, the use of Operators in Kubernetes has greatly simplified this process. Operators extend Kubernetes' native capabilities to automate and streamline complex tasks, including the management of multiple clusters. In this context, solutions like
Cluster API
(
CAPI
) have emerged as powerful tools for managing Kubernetes clusters at scale.CAPI is a Kubernetes project that brings declarative, Kubernetes-style APIs to cluster creation, configuration, and management. It provides a consistent and repeatable way to manage clusters, including their lifecycle, configuration, and upgrades.The key features of CAPI are the following:
Declarative API for Cluster Management
: Just like managing applications within Kubernetes, CAPI allows you to manage clusters using a declarative API. You define your cluster specification, and Cluster API takes care of the rest.
Multi-Cloud and Hybrid-Cloud Support
: CAPI is designed to work across different cloud providers and on-premises environments, making it a versatile tool for diverse infrastructures.
Automated Lifecycle Management
: It automates the tasks associated with managing cluster lifecycles, such as creation, scaling, upgrading, and deletion.
Extensibility and Customization
: Being part of the Kubernetes ecosystem, it allows for customization and extensibility in line with other Kubernetes resources.
Deploying a Kubernetes cluster using the CAPI involves several steps, starting from installing the CAPI components in your existing Kubernetes cluster (often referred to as the management or hub cluster) to creating and managing workload clusters. Here's a detailed step-by-step guide.The following are the prerequisites for the same:
Management Cluster
: A pre-existing Kubernetes cluster where the Cluster API components will be deployed. This cluster is used to manage workload clusters. This is our Hub or Central cluster
kubectl
: Installed and configured to communicate with your management cluster.
Helm or Kustomize
: For installing Cluster API components.
The following are the steps for the process:
Install Cluster API Components
: Depending on your preference, you can use Helm or Kustomize to install the Cluster API components. If using Helm:
helm repo add cluster-api https://cluster-api.sigs.k8s.io/charts
helm repo update
helm install cluster-api \
cluster-api/cluster-api \
--namespace capi-system \
--create-namespace
Install Infrastructure Provider
: Cluster API uses infrastructure providers to interact with different cloud providers or environments. For instance, to install the AWS infrastructure provider, use:
kubectl apply -f \
https://github.com/kubernetes-sigs/cluster-api-provider-aws/ \
releases/download/v0.6.4/infrastructure-components.yaml
Create Cluster Configuration
: Use
clusterctl
to generate cluster configuration templates. For AWS, set up environment variables for your AWS credentials and then run:
clusterctl generate cluster capi-quickstart \
--flavor development \
--kubernetes-version v1.21.2 \
--control-plane-machine-count=3 \
--worker-machine-count=3 > capi-quickstart.yaml
This generates a YAML file with the configuration for your new cluster.
Deploy Workload Cluster
: Deploy the workload cluster by applying the generated configuration.
kubectl apply -f capi-quickstart.yaml
Access the Workload Cluster
:
Retrieve Kubeconfig
: Once the workload cluster is up, you can get its
kubeconfig
file:
kubectl --namespace=default get secret/capi-quickstart-kubeconfig \
-o jsonpath={.data.value} | base64 --decode \
> ./capi-quickstart.kubeconfig
Access the Cluster
: Use the retrieved
kubeconfig
file to interact with your new cluster:
kubectl --kubeconfig=./capi-quickstart.kubeconfig get nodes
In our
capi-quickstart.yaml
file we define multiple Custom Resources:
Cluster Definition
: This section defines the cluster and its network settings, including CIDR blocks for services and pods.
AWSCluster
: Specifies AWS-specific configuration like the region and SSH key name for cluster instances.
MachineDeployment
: Defines a set of worker nodes, their Kubernetes version, and references to bootstrap and infrastructure templates.
AWSMachineTemplate
: Specifies AWS-specific settings for the worker nodes, such as the instance type and IAM instance profile.
apiVersion: cluster.x-k8s.io/v1alpha4
kind: Cluster
metadata:
  name: capi-quickstart
  namespace: default
spec:
  clusterNetwork:
    services:
      cidrBlocks: ["10.128.0.0/12"]
    pods:
      cidrBlocks: ["192.168.0.0/16"]
    serviceDomain: "cluster.local"
  controlPlaneEndpoint:
    host: "control-plane.example.com"
    port: 6443
  infrastructureRef:
    apiVersion: infrastructure.cluster.x-k8s.io/v1alpha4
    kind: AWSCluster
    name: capi-quickstart
apiVersion: infrastructure.cluster.x-k8s.io/v1alpha4
kind: AWSCluster
metadata:
  name: capi-quickstart
  namespace: default
spec:
  region: us-west-2
  sshKeyName: default
apiVersion: cluster.x-k8s.io/v1alpha4
kind: MachineDeployment
metadata:
  name: capi-quickstart-md-0
  namespace: default
spec:
  clusterName: capi-quickstart
  replicas: 3
  selector:
    matchLabels: {}
  template:
    metadata:
      labels: {}
    spec:
      clusterName: capi-quickstart
      version: v1.21.2
      bootstrap:
        configRef:
          apiVersion: bootstrap.cluster.x-k8s.io/v1alpha4
          kind: KubeadmConfigTemplate
          name: capi-quickstart-md-0
      infrastructureRef:
        apiVersion: infrastructure.cluster.x-k8s.io/v1alpha4
        kind: AWSMachineTemplate
        name: capi-quickstart-md-0
apiVersion: infrastructure.cluster.x-k8s.io/v1alpha4
kind: AWSMachineTemplate
metadata:
  name: capi-quickstart-md-0
  namespace: default
spec:
  template:
    spec:
      instanceType: t3.large
      iamInstanceProfile: "nodes.cluster-api-provider-aws.sigs.k8s.io"
      sshKeyName: default
Under the hood, CAPI uses the kubeadm project to bootstrap cluster nodes.
Summary
Throughout the chapter we delved into the intricacies of creating the foundation of an IDP using cloud-native technologies. We began with an introduction to the essential components of an IDP, including compute resources, pipelines, interfaces, and data services, all crucial for a robust and scalable platform.The journey through this chapter commenced with an exploration of Kubernetes, the cornerstone of cloud-native environments. By examining various deployment methods and discussing the decision-making process behind choosing the cluster architecture, the chapter provided a detailed understanding of Kubernetes' role in IDPs.Finally, we explained the significance of CRDs and Operators, detailing how they enable customization and automation of Kubernetes clusters, as well as how those can be leverage to implement Kubernetes-based IaC. In the next chapter we continue to leverage cloud native tools to expand our platform with further capabilities and better developer experience through a self-service model.

7 Expanding the IDP with CI/CD, Observability, Data Services, and Developer Portals
Join our book community on Discord
https://packt.link/IOzft
When it comes to building IDPs, there is no one size fits all. Platforms are designed and built to as enablers, as such, they should be built to solve the specific issues each organization faces. On the other hand, across different engineering teams we often see similar needs arise.Throughout this chapter, we'll continue to leverage cloud-native tools to expand the platform we started building in the previous chapter. We will build application and infrastructure pipeline in order to automate our SDLC, we will then build our platform interfaces, to enable self-serving of development treating everything in our stack as Kubernetes resources managed through a GitOps approach. Then we'll set up an observability platform for a comprehensive view of our applications' performance and finally building data services for our applications to leverage.In this chapter, we're going to cover the following main topics:
Service and Infrastructure Pipelines
Building Observability capabilities and leveraging those to build reliable services.
Building Platform Interfaces and enabling self-service
Building data platform capabilities
Let's get started!
Building Pipeline Capabilities in the IDP
Pipelines are a critical component of an IDP, offering great automation and golden paths for delivering software. Here we'll explore how pipelines not only automate the software development lifecycle but also extend to managing infrastructure as code, leveraging tools like GitHub Actions and ArgoCD to streamline both application and infrastructure deployments.
Figure 7.1 – Build & Release pipeline based on Github Actions and ArgoCD
These pipelines are foundational for enabling continuous integration, delivery, and deployment, ensuring consistent, reliable, and efficient operations across the platform.
Implementing continuous Integration with Github Actions
GitHub Actions is a powerful automation tool that integrates seamlessly with GitHub repositories. It enables the automation of various workflows, including building, testing, and deploying code changes. With GitHub Actions, you can create custom workflows that are triggered by GitHub events like push or pull requests.
Setting up a basic CI workflow
The first part of our pipelines is to implement a worklow in Github Actions for our CI:
Create a Workflow File
:
Initialize Workflow
: In your GitHub repository, create a new file under the
.github/workflows
directory. You can name it
ci.yml
.
Workflow Syntax
: Write the workflow using YAML syntax. The workflow file defines the actions to be executed when the specified trigger event occurs.
Define Trigger Events
:
Specify Triggers
: In the
ci.yml
file, define the events that will trigger the workflow. Commonly, CI workflows are triggered on push events to specific branches or on pull requests. //
on:
   push:
      branches: [ main ]
   pull_request:
      branches: [ main ]
Define Jobs and Steps
:
Jobs:
A workflow consists of one or more jobs. Each job runs in a fresh instance of the virtual environment specified and can have multiple steps.
Steps
: Within each job, define a series of steps that the job will execute. These can include checking out your repository, setting up a runtime environment, running tests, and compiling code.
Set Up the Environment
:
Environment Setup
: Use actions like
actions/checkout@v2
to check out your repository and
actions/setup-node@v2
for setting up a
Node.js
environment.
jobs:
build:
runs-on: ubuntu-latest
steps:
- uses: actions/checkout@v2
- name: Set up Node.js
  uses: actions/setup-node@v2
  with:
node-version: '14'
Run Tests and Build
:
Execute Tests
: Include steps to install dependencies and run tests. Use the command line for running scripts defined in your
package.json
:
- name: Install dependencies
  run: npm install
- name: Run tests
  run: npm test
Build Process
: If your project requires a build process, include steps to build your application, container images, and push to artifactories.
Additional Automation
:
Deployments and Notifications
: You can extend the workflow to include steps for deployment and sending notifications upon successful completion or failure of the workflow. Adding this at your workflow would send a notification in slack after every build:
notify:
    needs: build
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    if: always()  # Always run this job whether build passes                   # or fails
    steps:
      - name: Send Slack Notification
        uses: rtCamp/action-slack-notify@v2
        env:
          SLACK_WEBHOOK: ${{ secrets.SLACK_WEBHOOK }}
          SLACK_COLOR: ${{ job.status == 'success' && 'good'           || 'danger' }}
          SLACK_MESSAGE: |
            *Build Notification*
            - *Status*: ${{ job.status }}
            - *Repository*: ${{ github.repository }}
            - *Branch*: ${{ github.ref }}
            - *Commit*: ${{ github.sha }}
            - *Event*: ${{ github.event_name }}
Commit and Observe
:
Commit the Workflow File
: Commit the
ci.yml
file to your repository.
Monitor Workflow
: Once committed, monitor the
Actions
tab in your GitHub repository to observe the workflow execution.
Implementing CI with GitHub Actions simplifies the process of integrating changes into your main branch. It ensures that every commit or pull request is automatically tested and built, reducing integration issues and improving code quality. This setup is highly customizable, allowing teams to tailor the workflow to their specific project needs.
Implementing continuous Delivery with ArgoCD
Continuous Delivery with Argo CD involves a GitOps approach to manage and deploy applications in Kubernetes. Argo CD is a declarative, GitOps continuous delivery tool that synchronizes applications defined in a Git repository with Kubernetes clusters. It automates deployment by monitoring the specified repository for changes and ensuring that the state in the Kubernetes cluster matches the configurations defined in Git.The prerequisites are the following:
Argo CD installed in your Kubernetes cluster.
kubectl
and
argocd
CLI tools installed and configured to interact with your cluster and Argo CD.
Creating an Application in Argo CD to deploy your application involves a few key steps:
Define the Application in a YAML File
:
Create a YAML file (example,
guestbook-app.yaml
) that defines the Argo CD application. This file should include all necessary configurations, such as the source repository, path, destination cluster, and namespace. Here's an example:
apiVersion: argoproj.io/v1alpha1
kind: Application
metadata:
  name: guestbook
  namespace: argocd
spec:
  project: default
  source:
    repoURL: https://github.com/argoproj/argocd-example-apps.git
    path: guestbook
    targetRevision: HEAD
  destination:
    server: https://kubernetes.default.svc
    namespace: default
  syncPolicy:
    automated:
      prune: false
      selfHeal: false
In this YAML file:
repoURL
is the Git repository URL containing your Kubernetes manifests.
path
is the directory path within the Git repository.
targetRevision
specifies the branch, tag, or commit to synchronize.
server
is the destination Kubernetes cluster.
namespace
is the namespace in the destination cluster where the application will be deployed.
syncPolicy
can be configured for automatic synchronization when a change is pushed in the repository.
Apply the Application Configuration
:
Apply the configuration to create the application in Argo CD:
kubectl apply -f guestbook-app.yaml
This process sets up continuous delivery for your application in Kubernetes using Argo CD. The application's desired state is declared in the Git repository, and Argo CD ensures the Kubernetes cluster's state matches this desired state. This setup enables automated, reliable, and repeatable deployments, adhering to GitOps principles.The ApplicationSet controller is a powerful extension to Argo CD that simplifies managing applications across various Kubernetes clusters or environments. Here's a code example of how we can use the ApplicationSet controller:
apiVersion: argoproj.io/v1alpha1
kind: ApplicationSet
metadata:
  name: guestbook
spec:
  generators:
  - list:
      elements:
      - cluster: engineering-dev
        url: https://1.2.3.4
      - cluster: engineering-prod
        url: https://2.4.6.8
      - cluster: finance-preprod
        url: https://9.8.7.6
  template:
    metadata:
      name: '{{cluster}}-guestbook'
    spec:
      project: default
      source:
        repoURL: https://github.com/argoproj/argo-cd.git
        targetRevision: HEAD
        path: applicationset/examples/
        list-generator/guestbook/{{cluster}}
      destination:
        server: '{{url}}'
        namespace: guestbook
In this example, the
list
generator is used to specify different clusters where the application will be deployed. The
url
and
cluster
fields are used as parameters in the template, allowing for the creation of separate Argo CD applications for each cluster.The
ApplicationSet
controller in Argo CD simplifies multi-cluster and multi-environment application deployment. By defining a single
ApplicationSet
resource, you can automatically generate and manage multiple Argo CD applications, each tailored to a specific cluster or environment.Build and release pipelines is a very important capability for our IDPs. Next, we'll see how we can start building platform interfaces.
Building platform interfaces and enabling self service
A critical aspect of IDPs is the implementation of self-service mechanisms. These allow developers to autonomously access resources, deploy applications, and manage environments without the constant need for IT operations intervention. This self-service approach is pivotal for organizations striving to adopt DevOps and agile methodologies. Additionally, as organizations grow and scale, particularly in technology-driven environments, the complexity of service management and documentation becomes increasingly challenging. This complexity is marked by issues related to service ownership and documentation visibility, which can significantly impact operational efficiency and clarity.IDPs have multiple users with different needs and each of them would require a different type of interaction with the platform. For example, a developer wanting to deploy a service, would prefer to interact through a GitOps workflow. A manager who wants to see services' health would prefer a portal with scorecard. Let's start by building a developer portal with Backstage.
Building a developer portal with Backstage
Building a developer portal with Backstage involves leveraging its service catalog and documentation capabilities to create a centralized platform for managing services and applications. This process is exemplified in Stash's experience, where they utilized Backstage to develop
Elevate
, their internal developer portal. Elevate centralized the visibility of application health metrics, prioritized cloud cost insights, and reduced context switching for teams understanding service ownership.Key aspects of implementing Backstage include:
Creating a Service Catalog
: Backstage's service catalog provides a central place for registering services and documenting ownership, thereby clarifying accountability and ensuring effective communication.
Customizing Views
: Utilizing Backstage plugins to tailor views for different types of entries, such as libraries or deployed services, thereby focusing on relevant information like incident status or documentation.
Integrating SaaS Products
: Backstage allows for integrations with various SaaS products, providing cohesive monitoring and centralized incident management.
Tracking DORA Metrics
: Implementing dashboards for key performance metrics like deployment frequency and mean time to restore.
Setting Up Backstage in Kubernetes
We can assume we have a running Kubernetes cluster and can simply install Backstage using the official Helm charts. After installing and setting up Backstage, you can start populating the Backstage catalog with your services. Define
catalog-info.yaml
files for each service, specifying metadata, ownership, and documentation links.An example of the
catalog-info.yaml
can look like this:
apiVersion: backstage.io/v1alpha1
kind: Component
metadata:
  name: your-service
  description: "A description of your service"
  annotations:
    backstage.io/kubernetes-namespace: your-service-namespace
spec:
  type: service
  owner: team-name
  lifecycle: production
This YAML file defines a service in the Backstage catalog, including basic metadata and Kubernetes namespace information.Backstage plugins are a crucial aspect of its extensibility and adaptability. They enable customization to meet specific organizational needs, enhancing the developer experience by integrating various tools and services into a cohesive platform. These plugins can range from infrastructure management to monitoring and analytics, offering seamless integration with existing systems. Popular plugins include Documentation, Kubernetes, Templates and more. The flexibility to add or develop custom plugins means Backstage can evolve with your technology stack and processes, ensuring it remains a central, efficient tool for your engineering teams.
GitOps as your IDP interface
Adopting GitOps as the interface for an IDP presents two primary approaches for organizing repositories: a centralized infrastructure repository or decentralized, team-specific repositories. Both structures have distinct benefits, tailored to different organizational needs.
Centralized infrastructure repository
In this model, all IaC resides in a single repository. This approach fosters a unified strategy for infrastructure management, ensuring consistency and standardization across the organization. There are a number of benefits and challenges in this approach:
Advantages
:
Consistency
: A single source for all infrastructure changes promotes uniform standards and practices.
Easier Oversight
: Centralized control makes it easier for platform teams to oversee and approve changes, ensuring compliance and security.
Shared Knowledge
: Teams can learn from each other's configurations and challenges.
Challenges
:
Potential Bottlenecks
: The approval process can become a bottleneck, slowing down development cycles.
Complexity
: As the organization grows, the repository can become unwieldy and difficult to manage.
Decentralized team-specific repositories
Here, each team manages its own repository containing both application code and IaC. This structure empowers teams to operate independently and enables for scale, other benefits include:
Advantages
:
Speed and Autonomy
: Teams can move quickly, making and deploying changes without waiting for centralized approval.
Focused Context
: Teams only deal with the infrastructure relevant to their services, reducing complexity.
Resilience
: Decentralization can lead to more resilient systems as teams are able to respond and adapt quickly to their specific needs.
Challenges
:
Inconsistency Risk
: Without a centralized standard, there's a risk of divergent practices and configurations.
Overhead of Coordination
: Coordination between teams becomes more critical and potentially challenging.
Duplication of Effort
: Teams might independently solve the same problems, leading to duplicated effort.
Choosing between a centralized or decentralized repository structure for GitOps in an IDP context depends on the organization's size, culture, and specific operational needs, as a result, a third option has emerged.Some organizations might find value in a hybrid approach, where a centralized repository sets global standards and manages core infrastructure (like requesting a new cluster) and decentralized repositories allow teams to innovate and move quickly within those guidelines for other capabilities.Another critical component of our platform is Observability. Next, we'll see how we can build this capability as part of our IDP.
Integrating observability capabilities in your IDP
Observability plays a crucial role in multiple aspects of an IDP, as it is the building block for reliability, setting standards, and measuring success. Over the next few paragraphs, we'll explore how to leverage cloud-native tools to build our IDP's observability capability and how this can be easily operationalized at scale.
Collecting metrics with Prometheus and Thanos
Prometheus is a key tool in Kubernetes monitoring that provides a powerful framework for gathering and analyzing metrics. It is particularly well-suited for dynamic, container-based environments like Kubernetes due to its multi-dimensional data model, service discovery capabilities, and modular components. Prometheus is also adaptable, capable of scraping metrics exposed over HTTP and integrating easily with various Kubernetes components.
Installing Prometheus using Helm
To get started, you have to install prometheus in your cluster using Helm. You can use the following command:
helm repo add prometheus-community https://prometheus-community.github.io/helm-charts
helm repo update
helm install prometheus prometheus-community/prometheus
To configure Prometheus to scrape metrics from your Kubernetes application, follow these steps:
Edit Prometheus Config
: Modify the
prometheus.yml
configuration file within Prometheus. This file is part of the Prometheus container, typically found at
/etc/prometheus/prometheus.yml
.
Add Scrape Job
: Define a new scrape job within the
scrape_configs
section of the config file:
scrape_configs:
  - job_name: 'kubernetes-application'
    static_configs:
      - targets: ['<application-service-name>:<port>']
Replace
<application-service-name>
with your application's service name in Kubernetes and
<port>
with the port where your application exposes metrics.This configuration directs Prometheus to periodically scrape metrics from the specified application service and port within your Kubernetes cluster.If your application does not have a scraping endpoint for Prometheus, you have a few options:
Instrumentation
: Modify the application code to expose metrics. Prometheus provides client libraries for several programming languages that you can use to instrument your application.
Use an Exporter
: If modifying the application isn't feasible, use an exporter. Exporters are tools that translate metrics from the application's format into the format understood by Prometheus. There are exporters available for various databases, hardware, and third-party services.
Proxy Metrics
: In some cases, you can proxy metrics through another service (like the OpenTelemetry collector show in the following paragraphs) that is capable of exposing the correct format for Prometheus scraping.
Here's a full example, for scraping both Kubernetes deployments and node metrics using the Node Exporter:
global:
  scrape_interval: 15s
scrape_configs:
  - job_name: 'kubernetes-pods'
    kubernetes_sd_configs:
      - role: pod
    relabel_configs:
      - source_labels: [__meta_kubernetes_pod_annotation_prometheus_io_scrape]
        action: keep
        regex: true
      - source_labels: [__meta_kubernetes_pod_annotation_prometheus_io_path]
        action: replace
        target_label: __metrics_path__
        regex: (.+)
      - source_labels: [__address__, __meta_kubernetes_pod_annotation_prometheus_ io_port]
        action: replace
        regex: ([^:]+)(?::\d+)?;(\d+)
        replacement: $1:$2
        target_label: __address__
  - job_name: 'node-exporter'
    kubernetes_sd_configs:
      - role: node
    relabel_configs:
      - action: labelmap
        regex: __meta_kubernetes_node_label_(.+)
      - target_label: __address__
        replacement: kubernetes.default.svc:443
      - source_labels: [__meta_kubernetes_node_name]
        regex: (.+)
        target_label: __metrics_path__
        replacement: /api/v1/nodes/$1/proxy/metrics
This configuration sets up Prometheus to scrape metrics from Kubernetes pods and nodes. The
kubernetes-pods
job scrapes metrics from pods annotated for Prometheus scraping, while the
node-exporter
job scrapes metrics from nodes using the Node Exporter. Remember to adjust the configuration to match your specific Kubernetes setup and Prometheus deployment.To enable Prometheus scraping for pods, they should be annotated properly. The necessary annotations are:
prometheus.io/scrape
: Set to
true
to enable scraping for the pod.
prometheus.io/path
: If the metrics path is not the default (
/metrics
), specify the path with this annotation.
prometheus.io/port
: Specify the port on which the metrics are exposed.
For example, a pod's metadata annotations might look like:
annotations:
  prometheus.io/scrape: 'true'
  prometheus.io/path: '/custom_metrics'
  prometheus.io/port: '8080'
Prometheus is great for monitoring our cluster and application health in a Kubernetes cluster, but things become more complex when we want to monitoring multiple Kubernetes enviornment at the same time. Thanos, can help us build this.
Scaling Prometheus with Thanos
Thanos is an open-source project that turns your Prometheus setup into a highly available metric system with long-term storage capabilities. It's designed to operate in a cloud-native environment and can scale horizontally in multi-cluster setups. Thanos addresses some of Prometheus's limitations, especially around long-term storage and high availability as discussed in the following points:
High Availability
: Thanos can be configured to duplicate Prometheus data, ensuring high availability. It does so by connecting to multiple Prometheus instances.
Long-term Storage
: Thanos allows for storing historical metric data for long-term analysis, which is not feasible with a vanilla Prometheus setup. This is particularly useful for capacity planning and compliance.
Query Interface
: Thanos provides a global query view across all connected Prometheus instances, enabling a unified view of data, regardless of cluster location.
Scalable and Efficient
: Thanos is designed to be scalable. It can handle large volumes of data efficiently, making it suitable for large-scale Kubernetes environments.
Cost-Effective Storage
: Thanos integrates with various cloud storage solutions (like S3, GCS, Azure Blob Storage) to store metric data cost-effectively.
To implement Thanos in a multi-cluster environment, you typically deploy components like Thanos Sidecar (next to Prometheus), Thanos Store, Thanos Compactor, and Thanos Query, as shown in the image below.
Figure 7.2 – Thanos Architecture
These components work together to collect, store, and make the data queryable. The Sidecar pushes Prometheus data to a centralized storage backend, the Store provides an interface to this storage, the Compactor optimizes data storage, and the Query component serves as the central query layer across all data.
Centralized logging with Loki
Loki, developed by Grafana Labs, is a logging system designed to store and query logs efficiently. It's especially popular in cloud-native environments for its simplicity and compatibility with Prometheus' service discovery and labeling paradigm. The reason Loki integrates so well with Prometheus:
Loki's Architecture
: Loki's design is inspired by Prometheus. It focuses on simplicity and efficiency, storing logs as streams and indexing only minimal metadata for faster queries.
Promtail for Log Collection
: Promtail, a key component of the Loki stack, is responsible for collecting logs from various sources, primarily Kubernetes pods. It tails log files and sends the data to Loki.
Promtail Pipelines
: They process and enhance the collected logs before sending them to Loki. Pipelines can add, remove, or transform log data and labels.
Integration with Grafana
: Loki integrates seamlessly with Grafana, providing a powerful interface for querying and visualizing log data.
Use in Kubernetes
: Loki and Promtail are particularly effective in Kubernetes, where they can leverage Kubernetes labels and service discovery for efficient log aggregation and querying.
To deploy Loki and set up a service for log collection using Loki and Promtail we'll follow these steps:
Install Loki with Helm
:
helm repo add grafana https://grafana.github.io/helm-charts
helm repo update
helm install loki grafana/loki
Install Promtail with Helm
:
helm install promtail grafana/promtail --set "loki.serviceName=loki"
Promtail is setup through yaml config and is similar to Prometheus' scrape configs for metrics. Here is a basic example:
config:
  lokiAddress: http://loki:3100/loki/api/v1/push
  snippets:
    pipelineStages:
      - docker: {}
scrapeConfigs:
  - jobName: kubernetes-pods
    kubernetes_sd_configs:
      - role: pod
    relabel_configs:
      - source_labels: [__meta_kubernetes_pod_annotation_prometheus_io_scrape]
        action: keep
        regex: true
      - source_labels: [__meta_kubernetes_pod_annotation_prometheus_io_path]
        action: replace
        target_label: __metrics_path__
        regex: (.+)
      - source_labels: [__address__, __meta_kubernetes_pod_annotation_prome theus_io_port]
        action: replace
        regex: ([^:]+)(?::\d+)?;(\d+)
        replacement: $1:$2
        target_label: __address__
This configuration sets up Promtail to scrape logs from Kubernetes pods that are annotated for Prometheus scraping. It uses the Loki instance at
http://loki:3100
as the destination for the logs.Looking a bit close into the different attributes of
config
file we can see:
pipelineStages
with
docker
stage, extracts log data formatted by the Docker logging driver.
scrapeConfigs
defines the jobs for scraping logs. The example job
kubernetes-pods
targets Kubernetes pods.
kubernetes_sd_configs
with
role: pod
tells Promtail to discover targets based on pod role in Kubernetes.
relabel_configs
are used to filter and modify the discovered targets based on Kubernetes metadata, like annotations indicating whether a pod should be scraped (
prometheus_io_scrape
), the path for scraping (
prometheus_io_path
), and the port (
prometheus_io_port
).
As the number of your monitored applications or the number of your clusters scale, the complexity of collecting this data becomes harder. OpenTelemetry provides a framework to tackle this problem, simplifying the collection and analysis of these data.
Centralizing M.E.L.T collection with Open Telemetry
The OpenTelemetry Collector serves as a fundamental component for aggregating observability data within Kubernetes clusters. It collects and processes traces, metrics, and logs from various parts of a software system, simplifying the task of gathering and exporting telemetry data in cloud-native environments. This centralized approach to collecting telemetry data is essential for maintaining visibility and operational health of distributed systems.The OpenTelemetry collector has a number of different aspects:
Configuring Data Collection
: The OpenTelemetry Collector's data collection is managed through receivers configured in a YAML file. These receivers act as entry points for data into the collector, responsible for collecting data from various sources.
Processing and Exporting Data
: Once data is collected, it's processed and exported. Processors manipulate, filter, or enhance the data, while exporters define the destination for the data, whether it's an external monitoring system, storage, or another service. This setup allows for customized and efficient handling of telemetry data.
Deployment in Kubernetes
: Deploying the OpenTelemetry Collector in a Kubernetes environment involves creating several manifest files, including ConfigMap, Service Account, Cluster Role, Cluster Role Binding, and Deployment. These Kubernetes resources define how the collector is deployed and managed within the cluster.
Flexible and Scalable
: The collector's flexible configuration and scalability make it ideal for large Kubernetes environments, where it can efficiently collect and manage large volumes of telemetry data from across the cluster.
Vendor-Agnostic
: OpenTelemetry provides a vendor-agnostic approach to telemetry, ensuring that the data collected is standardized and can be used with various back-end systems and visualization tools
The first part of configuring the OTel collector is enabling applications to send data to the collector through receivers:
...
  otel-collector-config: |
    receivers:
      # Make sure to add the otlp receiver.
      # This will open up the receiver on port 4317.
      otlp:
        protocols:
          grpc:
            endpoint: "0.0.0.0:4317"
    processors:
...
In order to then route observability data to the right backends, you need to configure exporters. This is done via a ConfigMap:
---
apiVersion: v1
kind: ConfigMap
metadata:
  name: collector-config
  namespace: sample
data:
  collector.yaml: |
    receivers:
      otlp:
        protocols:
          grpc:
    processors:
      batch:
        send_batch_size: 10000
        timeout: 5s
    exporters:
      prometheus:
        endpoint: ":9090"
      logging:
        loglevel: debug
    service:
      pipelines:
        traces:
          receivers: [otlp]
          processors: [batch]
          exporters: [logging]
        metrics:
          receivers: [otlp]
          processors: [batch]
          exporters: [prometheus, logging]
In the above ConfigMap we see the following:The
receivers
section defines the way in which we want to receive our metrics. There are lots of receivers. I decided to use
OpenTelemetry Protocol (OTLP)
as it allows collection of both metrics and traces.The
processors
process the data which goes through the collector and can be used for changing or aggregating the data in some way. This is out of the scope for a simple deployment like ours. I have just added a batch processor which sends batches of data every 5s for demonstration of how it can be configured.The
exporters
can export the data in many formats of which we are interested in the Prometheus format, for our data to be collected by prometheus. Here we export the data on our port:
9090
as configured. Also, we set the log level to debug to catch any potential errors (we can check them on our
opentelemetrycollector
pod logs).The
service
section finally configures all that was in the receivers, processors, and exporters and enables them. If a component is configured in the above sections but is not defined in the service section, it is not enabled. This also defined individual pipelines for traces and metrics and their receivers, exporters, and processors individually.Finally, this ConfigMap is used to configure the OTel collector as we deploy the service:
---
apiVersion: apps/v1
kind: Deployment
metadata:
  name: opentelemetrycollector
  namespace: sample
spec:
  replicas: 1
  selector:
    matchLabels:
      app.kubernetes.io/name: opentelemetrycollector
  template:
    metadata:
      labels:
        app.kubernetes.io/name: opentelemetrycollector
    spec:
      containers:
      - name: otelcol
        args:
        - --config=/conf/collector.yaml
        image: otel/opentelemetry-collector:0.47.0
        volumeMounts:
        - mountPath: /conf
          name: collector-config
      volumes:
      - configMap:
          items:
          - key: collector.yaml
            path: collector.yaml
          name: collector-config
        name: collector-config
With the addition of the OTel collector, we've created a central point of entry for all of our data in our observability stack.
