This allows for better portability, day-2 management, easier onboarding, and advanced capabilities such as filtering, processing etc.Our observability architecture looks like this:
Figure 7.3 – OpenTelemetry Collector Architecture
With the additonal of OTel Collector, we've created an end to end Observability stack, that can easily help collect and analyze data across Metrics, Logs, and Traces.
Making data platform capabilities part of your IDP
A modern IDP must enable teams to manage not just applications and infrastructure, but also
data services
in a way that is consistent with the platform's operating model. As organizations adopt microservices and shift responsibility "left", the need to enable self-service provisioning of data services—such as databases, caches, or message queues—has become critical.To operationalize this in a cloud-native context, we'll build a
Database-as-a-Service (DBaaS
) capability within our platform, exposed through a GitOps interface. This will allow developers to provision databases using a GitOps workflow, treating database deployments just like any other Kubernetes resource.In this section, we'll demonstrate how to do this using:
MongoDB Atlas
– a fully-managed cloud database service for MongoDB.
Atlas Kubernetes Operator
– a controller that extends Kubernetes to manage Atlas resources as native Kubernetes objects.
ArgoCD
– a GitOps continuous delivery tool to sync database configurations from a Git repository.The goal is to allow any team to request and manage database infrastructure by submitting YAML files to a version-controlled repository. These YAML files will be picked up by ArgoCD, applied to a Kubernetes cluster where the Atlas Kubernetes Operator is running, and ultimately provisioned and managed in Atlas through its Admin API.
Building the DBaaS capability
Step 1: Set up the MongoDB Atlas Kubernetes Operator
To start, you need a running Kubernetes cluster. The Atlas Kubernetes Operator must be installed in this cluster. You can install the operator using Helm. Once installed, it watches for custom resources such as AtlasProject and AtlasDeployment.Make sure you've generated an Atlas Programmatic API Key with sufficient permissions and added the credentials as Kubernetes secrets in your cluster.
kubectl create secret generic mongodb-atlas-key \
  --from-literal=orgId=<ORG_ID> \
  --from-literal=publicKey=<PUBLIC_KEY> \
  --from-literal=privateKey=<PRIVATE_KEY> \
  -n mongodb-atlas-system
Step 2: Define the AtlasProject Resource
The AtlasProject custom resource is the first resource we need to define. This object creates a new project in MongoDB Atlas and allows us to associate deployments under it. Here's a sample atlas-project.yaml:
apiVersion: atlas.mongodb.com/v1
kind: AtlasProject
metadata:
  name: demo-project
spec:
  name: demo-project
  projectIpAccessList:
    - ipAddress: "0.0.0.0/0"
      comment: "Allow all IPs for demo purposes"
This will provision a new project on MongoDB Atlas and configure an IP whitelist.
Step 3: Define the AtlasDeployment Resource
The next step is to define a deployment that will spin up a MongoDB database within the specified project.
apiVersion: atlas.mongodb.com/v1
kind: AtlasDeployment
metadata:
  name: demo-deployment
spec:
  projectRef:
    name: demo-project
  deploymentSpec:
    name: demo-deployment
    cloudProviderSettings:
      providerName: AWS
      regionName: EU_CENTRAL_1
      instanceSize: M10
      backingProviderName: AWS
    providerBackupEnabled: false
    clusterType: REPLICASET
This will create a single-region replica set named demo-deployment in the
EU_CENTRAL_1
region using the
M10
instance size.
Exposing through a GitOps interface
To enable GitOps-based management, we use ArgoCD to monitor a Git repository and sync the Kubernetes resources stored there into the cluster. We assume ArgoCD is already installed in the cluster.Here's an ArgoCD Application manifest to sync the Atlas project and deployment files from a Git repo:
apiVersion: argoproj.io/v1alpha1
kind: Application
metadata:
  name: mongodb-dbaas
  namespace: argocd
spec:
  project: default
  source:
    repoURL: https://github.com/giohan/mongodb-dbaas.git
    targetRevision: HEAD
    path: dbaas
  destination:
    server: https://kubernetes.default.svc
    namespace: mongodb-atlas-system
  syncPolicy:
    automated:
      prune: true
      selfHeal: true
Once applied, ArgoCD will continuously monitor the specified repository in it's specific target revision and ensure that the project and deployment are synced into the destination cluster, triggering the Atlas Kubernetes Operator to provision the actual resources on MongoDB Atlas.
Generalizing the DBaaS example to XaaS (everything-as-a-service)
While we used MongoDB Atlas in this DBaaS example, the same pattern can be applied to other cloud services and platform capabilities. Many providers now offer
Kubernetes operators
to manage their resources declaratively.For example:
AWS ACK (Amazon Controllers for Kubernetes)
allows managing services like RDS, DynamoDB, or S3 from Kubernetes.
Crossplane
lets you define infrastructure from multiple clouds using a Kubernetes-native API.
Google Config Connector
works similarly for Google Cloud resources.
All of these tools enable a consistent GitOps-based experience for infrastructure and data services, reducing the gap between development and operations while increasing autonomy for application teams.
Summary
Throughout the chapter we expanded our platform building common capabilities and interfaces that enable developers to self-serve.We explored how pipelines are not just limited to applications but also encompassed infrastructure as code, highlighting the benefits of GitOps for infrastructure management, while using tools like Github Actions and ArgoCD to implement application and infrastructure pipelines.Observability is a critical component of any robust platform. The section began with an introduction to Prometheus, a leading tool for monitoring Kubernetes environments. The discussion expanded to include centralized monitoring with Thanos, logging with Loki and Promtail, and leveraging the OpenTelemetry Collector for a unified approach to observability.Finally, we explored how data services are a critical capability for IDPs, as we shift towards microservice architectures and modernized applications. Our examples included how Operators can help manage databases in or through (cloud-based) Kubernetes, combined with a self-service model using ArgoCD in order to build a Database-as-a-Service capability in our IDP.In the chapter we'll explore how we can address one of the most important challenges of large scale change projects, such as Platform Engineering, which is how to find and deliver quick wins, how to engage with early adopters, and how you can show early signs of delivering value and gaining trust.

8 Identifying Early Adopters of Platforms and Delivering Quick Wins
Join our book community on Discord
https://packt.link/IOzft
Platform Engineering is a journey of transformation and growth. In this chapter, we delve into the pivotal role of early adopters of internal developer platforms and the significance of delivering quick wins to build momentum and credibility. By engaging early adopters and securing quick wins, platform engineering teams can demonstrate value, foster trust, and drive broader adoption of new technologies and practices.You will learn how to identify and engage early adopters, understand the importance of building relationships and reputation, and employ strategic frameworks to prioritize initiatives.In this chapter we're going to cover the following main topics:
Understand the importance of relationships, reputation, and early adopters in Platform Engineering.
Utilize effort-impact and other prioritization matrices to identify and execute quick wins.
Common first use cases in Platform Engineering, such as:
Observability as code
Infrastructure optimization, and
Application of modernization
Let's get started!
The importance of relationships and reputation in Platform Engineering
Engineers, by nature, are deeply analytical and often skeptical, particularly when it comes to adopting new tools and processes. Their opinions are typically formed through years of experience and rigorous scrutiny of various technologies. This skepticism is beneficial as it fosters a culture of excellence and continuous improvement. However, it can also pose significant challenges when introducing new platform engineering initiatives.Additionally, engineers value autonomy and the ability to choose tools that best fit their specific needs. Platform engineering efforts, perceived as imposing standardization, can be seen as a threat to this autonomy. This perception leads to biases against any new platform that appears to limit their freedom or enforce conformity without clear, substantial benefits.The fear of increased complexity is another significant factor. Engineers often worry that new platforms will add layers of abstraction that obscure the underlying system, making debugging and optimization more challenging. This complexity is particularly concerning if it does not come with proportional benefits in terms of performance, reliability, or ease of use.Understanding these biases is crucial for platform engineering teams. Recognizing the sources of resistance allows teams to address concerns directly and demonstrate how new platforms can align with the engineers' goals of simplicity, efficiency, and autonomy.To overcome the inherent skepticism of engineers, platform engineering teams must exhibit exceptional technical skills. Engineers are more likely to trust and adopt tools and methodologies proposed by a team that clearly understands the technical challenges and can provide effective, efficient solutions.Technical proficiency is not merely a matter of having a good theoretical understanding of technologies. It requires practical, hands-on experience and a proven track record of solving complex problems. Engineers respect teams that can speak their language, understand their pain points, and offer solutions that have been tested and proven in real-world scenarios.The platform engineering team must demonstrate expertise in core technologies, such as Kubernetes, container orchestration, CI/CD pipelines, and cloud-native architectures. This expertise should be showcased through technical presentations, detailed documentation, and hands-on workshops. By doing so, the platform team can build credibility and show that they are not only knowledgeable but also capable of delivering practical, reliable solutions.Moreover, showcasing technical successes is vital. Internal showcases, regular presentations, and demonstrations of improvements brought about by platform engineering efforts help build confidence. Highlighting quantifiable benefits such as reduced deployment times, increased system reliability, and enhanced developer productivity provides tangible proof of the platform team's competence.
Overcoming the challenges
Credibility is the cornerstone of any successful platform engineering initiative. Without it, efforts to introduce new tools and processes can be met with skepticism and resistance, significantly hindering progress. Building credibility requires a deliberate and strategic approach, focusing on demonstrating value, reliability, and technical proficiency.Credibility in platform engineering is about being perceived as reliable, knowledgeable, and trustworthy by the engineering teams. It's the foundation upon which trust and collaboration are built. Engineers are more likely to support and adopt platform initiatives if they believe that the platform engineering team is competent and their efforts will lead to tangible improvements.Reliability is a key aspect of credibility. Consistently delivering on promises and meeting deadlines builds a track record of dependable performance. Engineers value teams that can reliably provide solutions that work as expected and within the promised timeframe.Building credibility is a multi-faceted process that involves several strategic actions:
Deliver Small Wins
: Starting with small, manageable projects that can be completed quickly and demonstrate immediate value is essential. These early successes build confidence and show that the platform engineering team can deliver results. Focusing on making incremental improvements that address specific pain points helps to build positive momentum and gradually earn trust.
Transparent Communication
: Keeping stakeholders informed about project progress, challenges, and milestones is crucial. Regular updates help manage expectations and reduce uncertainty. Encouraging open communication channels where engineers can voice their concerns and provide feedback is also important. Actively listening to their input and addressing their concerns promptly fosters a collaborative environment.
Showcase Technical Expertise
: Conducting technical presentations and workshops to demonstrate the platform engineering team's knowledge and skills is vital. These opportunities educate engineers about the benefits and functionalities of the new tools and processes. Providing hands-on support during the implementation phase also helps. Assisting engineers with real-world problems builds credibility and demonstrates the practical value of the platform engineering efforts.
Engage Key Influencers
: Identifying key influencers within the engineering teams who have the respect and ear of their peers is important. Engaging these influencers early can help sway broader opinion in favor of the platform initiatives. Involving key influencers in the planning and decision-making processes ensures their concerns are addressed and their endorsement can significantly enhance the credibility of the platform engineering team.
Use Data and Metrics
: Using data and metrics to quantify the benefits of the platform engineering initiatives is essential. Demonstrating improvements through concrete numbers helps to make a compelling case. Continuously tracking and reporting progress using predefined metrics shows that the platform engineering team is committed to delivering measurable outcomes
As we saw, delivering small quick wins helps platform teams build a better reputation and credibility, which plays a critical role in later adoption. But how can these quick wins be identified and prioritized?
Identifying quick wins
Change management in any organization, especially within engineering teams, can be challenging. Engineers are often skeptical of new processes and tools, preferring to stick with familiar workflows that have proven reliable. This resistance to change can be mitigated through the strategic achievement of quick wins.Quick wins are small, easily achievable initiatives that deliver immediate results. They are crucial for several reasons:
Building Momentum
: Quick wins help build momentum for broader change initiatives. By demonstrating immediate value, they create a sense of progress and forward movement.
Boosting Morale
: Successfully implementing quick wins boosts team morale. Engineers see tangible benefits from their efforts, increasing their motivation to support further changes.
Gaining Trust
: Quick wins help build trust between the platform engineering team and the broader engineering organization. By delivering results quickly, the platform team demonstrates its capability and reliability.
Reducing Resistance
: Quick wins can reduce resistance to change by showing that new initiatives are beneficial and not overly disruptive. This can make engineers more open to larger, more complex changes in the future.
Providing Proof of Concept
: Quick wins act as proof of concept, demonstrating that the proposed changes are viable and effective. This helps secure buy-in from stakeholders and paves the way for more extensive projects.
Identifying high-impact areas often involves looking for bottlenecks or pain points within the current processes. For example, if deployments are frequently delayed due to manual configuration steps, automating these steps could be a high-impact area. Similarly, if debugging production issues is challenging due to a lack of observability, implementing better monitoring and logging could provide significant benefits.There are multiple tools to use as we try to find and prioritize our first focus areas, most importantly the Effort-Impact matrix, the Eisenhower matrix, and the Pareto principle (or 80-20 rule). Let's see them in more detail.
Effort-impact matrix
The Effort-Impact Matrix is a powerful tool for prioritizing initiatives by scoring their potential impact on the one axis and the effort required to complete the work on the other. Using this, you can focus on high-value projects that can be completed with minimal effort.
Figure 8.1 – Effort-impact Matrix
The matrix is divided into four quadrants:
High Impact, Low Effort
:
Focus Here
: These are the ideal projects to start with. They provide significant benefits with minimal effort, making them perfect candidates for quick wins.
Examples
: Automating repetitive tasks, implementing basic monitoring tools, and optimizing existing scripts.
High Impact, High Effort
:
Plan and Prioritize
: These projects are important but require substantial resources. They should be prioritized but might need to be broken down into smaller, more manageable phases.
Examples
: Overhauling the CI/CD pipeline, migrating to a new infrastructure platform, or redesigning critical system components.
Low Impact, Low Effort
:
Consider If Resources Allow
: These projects can be addressed if resources are available. They are not urgent but can provide incremental improvements.
Examples
: Minor UI improvements, small bug fixes, and documentation updates.
Low Impact, High Effort
:
Avoid
: These projects are not worth pursuing as they require significant effort but provide minimal benefits.
Examples
: Refactoring code that is rarely used, implementing niche features that do not address core business needs.
Using the Effort-Impact Matrix involves plotting potential projects on the matrix based on their estimated effort and impact. This visual representation helps teams quickly identify which projects to focus on and which to deprioritize.
Eisenhower matrix
The Eisenhower Matrix, also known as the Urgent-Important Matrix, is another effective tool for prioritizing tasks. It helps teams focus on what truly matters by categorizing tasks based on their urgency and importance.
Figure 8.2 – The Eisenhower Matrix
The matrix is divided into four quadrants:
Urgent and Important
:
Do Now
: These tasks need immediate attention and are critical to the project's success. They should be tackled first.
Examples
: Fixing critical bugs, addressing security vulnerabilities, and handling production outages.
Not Urgent but Important
:
Schedule
: These tasks are important for long-term success but are not time-sensitive. They should be scheduled and planned for.
Examples
: Planning infrastructure upgrades, conducting performance optimizations, and strategic planning.
Urgent but Not Important
:
Delegate
: These tasks are urgent but do not require the team's direct involvement. They can be delegated to others.
Examples
: Routine administrative tasks, minor bug fixes, and responding to non-critical support requests.
Not Urgent and Not Important
:
Delete
: These tasks provide little to no value and should be eliminated or deprioritized.
Examples
: Unnecessary meetings, redundant documentation updates, and low-value feature requests.
The Eisenhower Matrix helps teams prioritize their workload, ensuring that they focus on tasks that drive the most value while avoiding distractions.
Pareto principle
The Pareto Principle, also known as the 80/20 rule, states that 80% of the effects come from 20% of the causes. In platform engineering, this principle can be applied to identify and prioritize initiatives that will deliver the most significant benefits.Applying the Pareto Principle involves the following steps:
Identify Key Areas
:
Analyze Data
: Analyze data to identify the key areas where the majority of issues or inefficiencies arise. This analysis helps pinpoint the 20% of causes that lead to 80% of the problems.
Focus Efforts
: Focus efforts on addressing these key areas to maximize impact.
Prioritize Initiatives
:
High-Impact Projects
: Prioritize projects that address the identified key areas. These projects are likely to provide the most significant benefits.
Incremental Improvements
: Make incremental improvements in these areas to continuously enhance efficiency and effectiveness.
Monitor and Adjust
:
Continuous Monitoring
: Continuously monitor the impact of the initiatives. Use metrics and KPIs to track progress and adjust strategies as needed.
Iterative Approach
: Adopt an iterative approach, making small adjustments based on feedback and results.
The Pareto Principle helps teams focus on the most impactful areas, ensuring that their efforts yield the maximum possible benefits.
Strategic alignment
Strategic alignment involves ensuring that platform engineering initiatives align with the broader business goals and objectives. This alignment ensures that the efforts support the overall strategic direction of the organization and address the most critical needs.To achieve strategic alignment, the following steps should be taken:
Aligning with Business Goals
:
Understand Business Objectives
: Understand the broader business objectives and how platform engineering can support these goals. This understanding ensures that the initiatives are aligned with the strategic direction of the organization.
Prioritize Strategic Projects
: Prioritize projects that directly support the business objectives. These projects are likely to have the most significant impact and gain the most support from stakeholders.
Anticipating Future Needs
:
Forecasting Needs
: Use forecasting techniques to anticipate the future needs of engineering teams. This forecasting helps in planning and ensures that the platform meets future demands.
Stay Ahead
: Stay 2-4 quarters ahead of the planning to ensure that the platform is ready to support future needs. This proactive approach prevents bottlenecks and ensures smooth transitions.
Strategic Planning
:
Develop a Strategic Plan
: Develop a strategic plan that outlines the long-term vision and goals for platform engineering. This plan should be aligned with the broader business strategy and provide a roadmap for achieving the goals.
Engage Stakeholders
: Engage stakeholders in the planning process to ensure their buy-in and support. Their input helps in identifying critical areas and prioritizing initiatives.
Communication and Collaboration
:
Regular Communication
: Maintain regular communication with stakeholders to keep them informed about progress and challenges. This communication ensures alignment and fosters collaboration.
Collaborative Approach
: Adopt a collaborative approach, involving stakeholders in decision-making and planning. This involvement helps in building trust and ensuring that the initiatives are aligned with their needs
In platform engineering, achieving quick wins is essential for fostering change and gaining momentum. Quick wins provide immediate, tangible benefits, helping to build trust and demonstrate the value of new initiatives.In the following sections, we'll explore common areas platform teams can find quick wins to implement.
Use Case 1: Observability as code
Observability has become a cornerstone of modern software development and operations. As organizations grow, the complexity of their systems increases, making it crucial to have a unified and standardized approach to observability. This journey often begins with individual teams managing observability in their own ways, evolves into the adoption of standardized practices, and culminates in the implementation of observability as code.In this section, we will explore the stages of this evolution and focus on the transition from each development team implementing observability in non-standard ways, to observability as code, providing practical insights and examples.
Initial Stage: Independent Observability Practices
In the early stages of an organization's growth, different teams often manage observability independently. Each team selects tools and practices that best suit their needs and preferences, leading to a diverse but fragmented observability landscape. While this approach allows teams to tailor their observability solutions to their specific requirements, it also creates several challenges.
First, there is a lack of standardization. Each team may use different metrics, logs, and tracing formats, making it difficult to aggregate and analyze data across the organization. This fragmentation can hinder the ability to gain a holistic view of system performance and health. For example, one team might use Prometheus for monitoring, while another uses Datadog, leading to disparate data sources and dashboards.
Second, managing observability independently can lead to inefficiencies. Each team spends time and resources setting up and maintaining their observability stack, which can result in duplicated efforts and inconsistent quality. Moreover, when incidents occur, the lack of a unified observability approach can slow down the diagnosis and resolution process, as engineers have to navigate through different tools and formats.
To address these issues, the first step is to define the goal of observability and unify the outcomes across the organization. This does not mean imposing a single tool or practice on all teams but rather establishing a common framework for what observability should achieve. A key part of this process is defining
Service Level Objectives
(
SLOs
) that are consistent across the organization.For instance, leadership might decide that all teams should present their SLOs in a standardized manner, focusing on metrics like uptime, response time, and error rates. While each team can still choose their tools and implementation methods, the way they report and interpret these metrics becomes unified. This approach ensures that all teams are aligned in their understanding of system performance and reliability, facilitating better decision-making and cross-team collaboration.
Transition Stage: Templates and Observability as Code
Once the goals and outcomes of observability are unified, the next step is to introduce templates that teams can copy and reuse. This stage marks the beginning of treating observability as code, a practice that brings the principles of
Infrastructure as Code
(
IaC
) to observability.Observability as code involves defining and managing observability configurations through version-controlled code. This practice offers several advantages. It promotes consistency across teams, as templates ensure that all observability setups adhere to the same standards. It also enhances reproducibility and scalability, allowing teams to quickly replicate observability configurations across different environments and projects.To implement observability as code, organizations can use tools like Terraform. Terraform allows teams to define their observability infrastructure using declarative configuration files, which can be stored in version control systems like Git. By using templates, teams can ensure that their observability setups are consistent and aligned with organizational standards.For example, a template for setting up monitoring with Prometheus might include configurations for scraping metrics from various services, setting up alerting rules, and integrating with visualization tools like Grafana. Teams can customize these templates to suit their specific needs while ensuring that the core configurations remain consistent.Here's a simple example of a Terraform template for setting up Prometheus:
provider "kubernetes" {
  config_path = "~/.kube/config"
}
resource "kubernetes_namespace" "monitoring" {
  metadata {
    name = "monitoring"
  }
}
resource "helm_release" "prometheus" {
  name       = "prometheus"
  namespace  = kubernetes_namespace.monitoring.metadata[0].name
  repository = "https://prometheus-community.github.io/helm-charts"
  chart      = "kube-prometheus-stack"
  version    = "14.0.0"
}
This template sets up Prometheus in a Kubernetes cluster using Helm, a package manager for Kubernetes. By providing a standardized template, teams can quickly deploy Prometheus with a consistent configuration across different environments.
Final Stage: Building an Observability Platform
The final stage in the evolution of observability practices is the development of a comprehensive observability platform. As described in
Chapter 6
, this platform integrates various observability tools and practices into a cohesive system that provides a unified view of application performance and health.Building an observability platform involves integrating multiple tools such as Prometheus for monitoring, Thanos for scalable metrics storage, OpenSearch for log management, and Grafana for visualization. This integrated approach ensures that all observability data is collected, stored, and analyzed in a centralized and consistent manner.Here's a high-level overview of the components of an observability platform:
Prometheus
: Collects and stores metrics from applications and infrastructure.
Thanos
: Provides scalable, long-term storage for Prometheus metrics and enables cross-cluster aggregation.
OpenSearch
: Manages logs, allowing for advanced search and analytics capabilities.
Grafana
: Visualizes metrics, logs, and traces, providing dashboards and alerting capabilities.
The platform approach not only standardizes observability practices but also enhances the organization's ability to monitor, diagnose, and resolve issues efficiently. It provides a comprehensive view of system performance and health, enabling teams to detect and respond to incidents faster.Implementing an observability platform requires careful planning and coordination. It involves defining the architecture, selecting the right tools, and integrating them in a way that meets the organization's needs. The platform should be designed to scale with the organization's growth, ensuring that it can handle increasing amounts of observability data.The journey to effective observability involves several stages, from independent practices to standardized templates and ultimately to a comprehensive observability platform. Each stage builds on the previous one, promoting consistency, efficiency, and scalability. Observability as code and setting standards across Metrics, Logs, and Traces, can be one of the first use cases a scaling organization's platform team might tackle.
Implementing Observability as Code with Terraform
Even before building the full capabilities in our IDP we can streamline observability across different environments, using
observability as code (OaC)
, which leverages IaC principles to help us have reusable best practices across diferent teams before we have our platform as product. This approach involves managing monitoring configurations through declarative code using tools like Terraform, integrated with GitOps workflows.Using Terraform for observability, you can ensure consistency, versioning, and transparency in your observability configurations across environments. It also helps avoid the manual setup of monitors and dashboards, reducing errors and improving collaboration. GitOps complements Terraform by using Git as a source of truth for configuration. Every change to observability resources can be reviewed through pull requests, and the changes are automatically applied when merged.In our example, we'll be using Datadog as our monitoring tool, but anyone can work in the same way. To implement Observability as code we'll do the following:
1. Setting Up Terraform for Datadog
First, configure the Datadog provider in Terraform (shown in the code snippet below). This allows Terraform to interact with your Datadog account and manage resources such as monitors and dashboards.//
terraform {
  required_providers {
    datadog = {
      source = "datadog/datadog"
      version = "~> 3.0"
    }
  }
}
provider "datadog" {
  api_key = var.datadog_api_key
  app_key = var.datadog_app_key
}
The datadog provider is used to interact with Datadog's API. The
api_key
and
app_key
variables are stored securely in environment variables or a secrets manager, ensuring sensitive credentials are not hard-coded.
2. Defining Monitors as Code
Next, define monitors to alert on specific conditions within your infrastructure or applications. Datadog monitors track various metrics, logs, and events, sending notifications when predefined conditions are met.
Example
: Creating a monitor for CPU usage that triggers an alert if the average usage exceeds 80% over five minutes:
resource "datadog_monitor" "cpu_usage" {
  name       = "High CPU Usage"
  type       = "metric alert"
  query      = "avg(last_5m):avg:system.cpu.user{*} > 80"
  message    = "CPU usage is too high. Investigate the issue immediately!"
  escalation_message = "Critical CPU usage alert!"
  thresholds {
    critical = 80
    warning  = 70
  }
  notify_no_data    = true
  no_data_timeframe = 10
  notify_audit      = true
}
In the above file, the
datadog_monitor
resource creates an alert for high CPU usage. The query specifies the metric to monitor (
system.cpu.user
) and the condition for triggering an alert (average usage over 5 minutes exceeds 80%). The monitor sends an alert message to your configured communication channels, such as Slack or email, when the threshold is breached. The
notify_no_data
option ensures alerts are triggered if no data is received within the specified timeframe.
3. Creating Dashboards for Monitoring
With Terraform, you can define and automate the creation of dashboards with various widgets, such as timeseries graphs or heatmaps.
Example
: Creating a simple dashboard with a timeseries widget to display CPU usage over time:
resource "datadog_dashboard" "cpu_dashboard" {
  title      = "CPU Usage Dashboard"
  layout_type = "ordered"
  widget {
    timeseries_definition {
      request {
        q   = "avg:system.cpu.user{*}"
        display_type = "line"
        style {
          palette = "cool"
        }
      }
      title = "CPU Usage Over Time"
    }
  }
}
Here the
datadog_dashboard
resource defines a dashboard titled "
CPU Usage Dashboard
." The dashboard contains a timeseries widget that displays the
system.cpu.user
metric with a line graph. You can further customize widgets by adjusting the queries, styles, and other visual properties.
4. Managing Alerts and Dashboards via GitOps
You can start here by having the terraform templates in a centralized repository where anyone can clone, edit, and use for their teams.To integrate observability configurations with GitOps, store your Terraform code in a Git repository. By using Git workflows, such as branching, pull requests, and code reviews, you can collaborate on changes to your observability setup. Every change to the monitoring configurations—whether it's a new alert, updated thresholds, or modified dashboards—goes through a version-controlled pipeline.For example, a team member could propose changes to a monitor by creating a new branch:
Clone the repository and create a new branch:
git clone https://github.com/your-repo/observability-configs.git
git checkout -b update-cpu-threshold
Update the monitor in Terraform:
resource "datadog_monitor" "cpu_usage" {
  name  = "High CPU Usage"
  query = "avg(last_5m):avg:system.cpu.user{*} > 85"  # Updated
                                                      # threshold
}
Commit and push the changes:
git add .
git commit -m "Updated CPU usage threshold"
git push origin update-cpu-threshold
Open a pull request for review. Once approved, the changes can be automatically applied using a Continuous Deployment (CD) pipeline that triggers Terraform to apply the updates.
Often platform teams evolve through devops or devprod efforts. Naturally, one of the first things a platform team could work on is optimizing cloud infrastructure through central management and standards.
Use Case 2: Infrastructure Optimization
Infrastructure optimization is a critical focus for platform engineering teams, particularly those aiming to embrace a software engineering approach rather than traditional operations. The goal is to streamline and enhance the efficiency of the underlying infrastructure, ensuring it is cost-effective, scalable, and robust. This transition involves leveraging automation, embracing IaC, and continuously refining processes to meet evolving demands.In modern software development, the lines between development and operations are increasingly blurred. Platform engineering teams are at the forefront of this shift, striving to adopt a software engineering mindset to manage infrastructure. This approach focuses on treating infrastructure the same way as application code: it is version-controlled, tested, and continuously integrated and deployed.The rationale behind this shift is to enhance agility, scalability, and reliability. By adopting software engineering principles, platform teams can automate repetitive tasks, reduce manual errors, and ensure that infrastructure changes are consistent and predictable. This transformation also allows for faster iteration and deployment cycles, aligning with the principles of DevOps and continuous delivery.Embracing a software engineering approach means viewing infrastructure not just as a collection of physical or virtual resources but as a product that evolves and improves over time. This perspective encourages platform teams to apply best practices from software development, such as modularity, reusability, and automated testing, to infrastructure management.
Prioritizing Infrastructure Optimization
To understand why platform engineers should prioritize infrastructure optimization, it is essential to consider core principles and strategies that align with the goals of platform engineering teams.
Embracing Automation
Automation is at the heart of infrastructure optimization. By automating repetitive and time-consuming tasks, platform engineers can free up valuable time to focus on more strategic initiatives. Automation also reduces the risk of human error, ensuring that infrastructure operations are reliable and consistent.For instance, automating the provisioning and scaling of resources can significantly improve efficiency. Tools like Terraform and Ansible allow platform teams to define their infrastructure as code, enabling automated deployments and updates. This approach ensures that the infrastructure is always in a known state and can be easily replicated across different environments.
Leveraging Infrastructure as Code (IaC)
IaC is a fundamental practice for infrastructure optimization. IaC involves managing and provisioning computing resources through machine-readable configuration files, rather than through physical hardware configuration or interactive configuration tools.By treating infrastructure as code, platform engineers can apply the same version control, testing, and
continuous integration/continuous deployment
(
CI/CD
) practices used in software development. This approach allows for greater transparency, collaboration, and traceability of infrastructure changes.For example, using tools like Terraform, platform teams can define their infrastructure in code, version control it in Git, and use CI/CD pipelines to automate the deployment and testing of infrastructure changes. This method ensures that infrastructure is consistent, repeatable, and easily auditable.
Continuous Monitoring and Feedback
Optimizing infrastructure is an ongoing process that requires continuous monitoring and feedback. By implementing robust monitoring and observability practices, platform engineers can gain insights into the performance and health of their infrastructure. This information is crucial for identifying bottlenecks, predicting capacity needs, and making informed decisions about optimizations.Tools like Prometheus, Grafana, and
ELK
Stack (short for
Elasticsearch, Logstash, Kibana
) provide comprehensive monitoring and logging capabilities. These tools enable platform teams to collect and analyze metrics, logs, and traces from their infrastructure, helping them detect issues early and optimize resource utilization.
Cost Management and Efficiency
One of the primary goals of infrastructure optimization is to improve cost efficiency. By optimizing resource allocation and usage, platform engineers can reduce unnecessary expenditures and ensure that the infrastructure is cost-effective.Cost management tools like AWS Cost Explorer, Azure Cost Management, and Google Cloud's Cost Management provide insights into resource usage and spending patterns. These tools help platform teams identify areas where costs can be reduced, such as by rightsizing instances, eliminating unused resources, and taking advantage of reserved instances or spot instances.
Scalability and Flexibility
Optimizing infrastructure also involves ensuring that it is scalable and flexible to meet changing demands. Platform engineers must design their infrastructure to scale horizontally, adding more instances to handle increased load, or vertically, upgrading instances to more powerful configurations.Containerization technologies like Docker and orchestration platforms like Kubernetes play a critical role in achieving scalability. By containerizing applications and managing them with Kubernetes, platform teams can easily scale services up or down based on demand, ensuring that the infrastructure can handle varying workloads efficiently.
Security and Compliance
Security and compliance are paramount in infrastructure optimization. Platform engineers must ensure that their infrastructure follows security best practices and remains compliant with regulations and standards.Implementing automated security checks, encryption, and access controls are essential steps in securing the infrastructure. Tools like HashiCorp Vault for secrets management, AWS IAM for access control, and automated compliance frameworks help ensure that the infrastructure remains secure and compliant.
Collaboration and Culture
Finally, optimizing infrastructure requires a collaborative culture that encourages knowledge sharing and continuous improvement. Platform engineers should work closely with development, operations, and security teams to align on goals and best practices.Fostering a culture of collaboration involves regular meetings, shared documentation, and cross-functional training. By promoting a shared understanding of the infrastructure and its optimization goals, platform teams can drive more effective and efficient outcomes.Infrastructure optimization is a critical focus for newly formed platform engineering teams aiming to deliver value quickly. By prioritizing automation, leveraging Infrastructure as Code, implementing continuous monitoring, managing costs, ensuring scalability, enhancing security, and fostering collaboration, platform engineers can create an optimized, efficient, and resilient infrastructure.The transition from traditional operations to a platform engineering mindset involves viewing infrastructure as a product that evolves and improves over time. This shift enables platform teams to apply best practices from software development to infrastructure management, resulting in more consistent, reliable, and scalable operations.
Use Case 3: Application Modernization
Application modernization is a critical process that enables organizations to update and transform their legacy applications to meet current business needs and leverage modern technology stacks. This process is not just about adopting the latest technology; it is about rethinking and reshaping the way applications are developed, deployed, and managed to align with contemporary business goals and user expectations.
The Need for Application Modernization
Organizations rely heavily on legacy applications, which have been built and maintained over the years to support core business functions. While these applications are often stable and deeply integrated into business processes, they come with several challenges such as the following:
Technological Obsolescence
: Legacy applications are frequently built on outdated technologies that may no longer be supported or compatible with modern systems. This can lead to increased maintenance costs and security vulnerabilities.
Scalability Issues
: Many legacy systems were not designed to scale horizontally, making it difficult to handle increased workloads or adapt to changing business demands.
Operational Inefficiencies
: Older applications often lack automation and integration capabilities, resulting in manual processes and inefficiencies that hinder agility and responsiveness.
High Operational Costs
: Maintaining and operating legacy systems can be expensive due to the need for specialized skills, custom integrations, and frequent patches and updates.
Difficulty in Innovation
: Legacy systems can impede innovation, as they are not designed to easily integrate with modern development practices like DevOps, microservices, and continuous delivery.
The Goals of Application Modernization
The primary goal of application modernization is to transform legacy applications in a way that enhances their performance, scalability, and maintainability while reducing operational costs and complexities. When talking about the different migration paths of application modernization, we refer to the 7Rs model, as shown in the image below.
Figure 8.3 – The 7-Rs of Application Modernization
Out of the 7Rs, "Retain" and "Retire" don't encompass any efforts by engineering, so we'll focus in the remaining ones. As a result, modernization efforts can take one of 5 forms:
Rehosting
: Moving applications to modern infrastructure, such as cloud environments, without significant changes to the application code.
Refactoring
: Rewriting parts of the application to improve its architecture, performance, and scalability while preserving its core functionality.
Rearchitecting
: Redesigning the application to take advantage of modern architectural patterns, such as microservices, which offer better scalability and flexibility.
Rebuilding
: Completely redeveloping the application from scratch using modern development practices and technologies.
Replacing
: Phasing out legacy applications and replacing them with new, off-the-shelf solutions that offer enhanced capabilities.
Each approach has its own set of benefits and trade-offs, and the choice of strategy depends on various factors, including the application's complexity, business requirements, and available resources.
Challenges in Application Modernization
While the benefits of application modernization are compelling, the process is not without its challenges. Organizations must navigate several obstacles to achieve successful modernization:
Complexity of Legacy Systems
: Understanding and disentangling the complexities of legacy systems, which may have been developed over decades with minimal documentation, can be daunting.
Resource Constraints
: Modernization efforts require significant investment in terms of time, money, and skilled personnel. Organizations must balance these demands with ongoing operational requirements.
Risk of Disruption
: Migrating or transforming critical applications carries the risk of operational disruptions, which can impact business continuity and customer satisfaction.
Change Management
: Adapting to new technologies and processes requires a cultural shift within the organization. Effective change management strategies are essential to ensure buy-in from stakeholders and smooth transitions.
Now let's see how we can migrate our existing services to new microservice architectures.
Transitioning Legacy Application to Modern Microservices
Transitioning from legacy applications to a modern microservices architecture is a critical process for organizations looking to enhance scalability, maintainability, and agility. This transition, however, is not without its challenges. It requires a deep understanding of existing systems, a strategic approach to decomposition, and the adoption of modern architectural practices. This section delves into the various facets of this transition, starting with an introduction to
Service-Oriented Architecture
(
SOA
) and its relationship to microservices, followed by
Domain-Driven Design
(
DDD
), the Strangler Fig pattern, and the concept of modular monoliths.
SOA
Service-Oriented Architecture
(
SOA
) is an architectural pattern that has been around for decades. It emphasizes the use of discrete services that communicate over a network to perform various functions. SOA aims to promote reuse, integration, and flexibility by encapsulating business logic within services that can be composed to form complex applications.Microservices architecture is an evolution of SOA, taking the principles of service orientation further. While SOA often involves larger, more coarse-grained services, microservices advocate for smaller, fine-grained services that are independently deployable and scalable. Each microservice focuses on a specific business capability and communicates with other services through lightweight protocols, often HTTP/REST or messaging queues.The relationship between SOA and microservices can be understood through their shared goals but differing implementations. SOA laid the groundwork by emphasizing service reuse and integration, which microservices build upon by advocating for autonomy, scalability, and continuous delivery. The transition from SOA to microservices often involves breaking down large, monolithic services into smaller, more manageable microservices that can be developed, deployed, and scaled independently.
DDD
Domain-Driven Design
(
DDD
) is a strategic approach to software development that focuses on modeling software based on the underlying business domain. DDD encourages close collaboration between developers and domain experts to create a shared understanding of the domain and ensure that the software reflects real-world business processes and rules.DDD is particularly relevant to microservices architecture as it provides a methodology for identifying and defining the boundaries of microservices. By using DDD principles, organizations can decompose their monolithic applications into well-defined microservices that align with business capabilities.Key concepts of DDD that facilitate this transition include:
Bounded Contexts
: These are explicit boundaries within which a particular model is defined and applicable. Each bounded context maps closely to a microservice, ensuring that the service encapsulates a specific domain logic and maintains consistency within its boundaries.
Aggregates
: These are clusters of domain objects that can be treated as a single unit. Aggregates ensure that domain invariants are preserved, and they help define the transactional boundaries of microservices.
Entities and Value Objects
: These are the building blocks of the domain model. Entities have a distinct identity and lifecycle, whereas value objects represent descriptive aspects of the domain without identity.
Repositories
: These provide a way to access and persist aggregates. In the context of microservices, repositories often translate to service endpoints that handle data storage and retrieval.
By applying DDD principles, organizations can design microservices that are aligned with business capabilities, promoting clear boundaries, better modularity, and easier maintainability. Using DDD we can also define existing services and use this as the starting point of our migration, using a technique called Strangling the Monolith.
Strangling the Monolith Technique: Breaking Down Microservices from a Monolith
The Strangler Fig pattern is a popular technique for incrementally transforming a monolithic application into a microservices architecture. Inspired by the strangler fig tree that grows around and eventually replaces its host tree, this pattern involves gradually replacing parts of the monolith with microservices until the monolith is fully decomposed, as shown below
Figure 8.4 – The Strangler Pattern – Carving out services gradually out of the monolith
The Strangler Fig pattern involves the following steps:
Identify a Monolithic Component
: Start by identifying a component or functionality within the monolith that can be isolated and re-implemented as a microservice.
Create a Microservice
: Develop a microservice that replicates the functionality of the identified component. Ensure that the new microservice adheres to modern architectural practices, such as being independently deployable and scalable.
Redirect Traffic
: Implement a mechanism to route traffic from the monolith to the new microservice. This can be achieved using a proxy, API gateway, or other routing techniques.
Decommission the Monolith Component
: Once the microservice is fully functional and all traffic is routed to it, decommission the corresponding component within the monolith.
Iterate
: Repeat the process for other components of the monolith, gradually strangling the entire application until it is fully decomposed into microservices.
This incremental approach reduces the risk of disruption and allows for continuous delivery and deployment of new features and improvements. It also provides opportunities to refactor and optimize each component individually, leading to a more modular and maintainable system.While microservices offer significant advantages in terms of scalability and flexibility, they are not always the best solution for every scenario. In some cases, maintaining a modular monolith can provide a balanced approach, combining the simplicity of a monolithic architecture with the modularity of microservices.
The Power of Modular Monoliths
A modular monolith is a monolithic application that is designed with clear modular boundaries. Each module encapsulates a specific business capability and can be developed, tested, and deployed independently, even though they are part of the same deployment unit.The advantages of modular monoliths include:
Simplicity
: Modular monoliths are simpler to develop and deploy compared to microservices, as they avoid the complexities of distributed systems, such as network latency, data consistency, and inter-service communication.
Performance
: Since all modules run within the same process, there are no network overheads associated with inter-module communication, leading to better performance.
Ease of Transition
: For organizations that are not ready to fully commit to microservices, a modular monolith provides a stepping stone. It allows teams to apply microservices principles, such as bounded contexts and modularity, within a monolithic structure.
Unified Deployment
: A modular monolith simplifies deployment and operations, as it is a single service running in our environment, in contrast with running and maintaining multiple microservices.
Transitioning from legacy applications to a modern microservices architecture is a multifaceted process that requires careful planning, strategic execution, and a deep understanding of both the existing systems and modern architectural practices. By leveraging concepts like SOA, DDD, the Strangler Fig pattern, and modular monoliths, organizations can effectively decompose their monoliths and build scalable, maintainable, and agile systems that meet contemporary business needs. This transition not only enhances technical capabilities but also aligns with the broader goals of digital transformation, enabling organizations to innovate and compete in an ever-evolving technological landscape.
Summary
In this chapter, we explored the critical role of early adopters in platform engineering and the strategic importance of delivering quick wins. Key lessons included understanding the dynamics of relationships and reputation within engineering teams, and the importance of engaging stakeholders early to build credibility. We also discussed techniques for identifying high-impact areas using tools like the Effort-Impact Matrix, Eisenhower Matrix, and Pareto Principle to prioritize initiatives effectively.The chapter detailed practical steps to implement quick wins through specific use cases such as Observability as Code and Application Modernization. You learned how to transition legacy applications to modern microservices using methodologies like
Service-Oriented Architecture
(
SOA
),
Domain-Driven Design
(
DDD
), the Strangler Fig pattern, and modular monoliths. Each use case demonstrated how to achieve tangible improvements in performance, scalability, and security.In the next chapter, we will delve into the financial aspects of platform engineering, exploring the principles of FinOps and how to manage cloud costs effectively. This progression will build on the foundational strategies discussed in Chapter 8, providing a comprehensive understanding of how to align technical and financial objectives to maximize the value of platform engineering efforts.

9 Understanding the Financial Aspects of Platform Engineering
Join our book community on Discord
https://packt.link/IOzft
As organizations scale their infrastructure and adopt cloud technologies, the complexity of managing and optimizing costs increases significantly. In this chapter, we delve into the financial intricacies of platform engineering, a critical yet often overlooked aspect of managing modern cloud-native environments. FinOps, or Financial Operations, emerges as a crucial practice to bridge the gap between financial accountability and technical efficiency in this context.In this chapter we're going to cover the following main topics:
A short introduction and history of FinOps
The FinOps Foundation & its work
Putting a price on your IDP with cost attribution
Implementing FinOps with OpenCost, Prometheus, and Kyverno
By the end of this chapter, you will have a comprehensive understanding of the financial aspects of platform engineering and the practical knowledge to implement FinOps practices within your organization. This will enable you to manage cloud costs more effectively, drive financial accountability, and optimize resource usage to support your business goals.
Introducing FinOps
Before cloud computing, IT financial management was relatively straightforward. Companies owned and maintained physical servers, storage, and networking equipment, with costs primarily consisting of upfront capital expenditures (CapEx) for hardware and software, followed by operational expenses (OpEx) like maintenance and energy consumption. IT budgets were allocated annually, and finance teams worked closely with IT departments to plan and justify large capital investments. Financial management in this environment focused on maximizing the utilization of these fixed assets and ensuring capacity was sufficient to meet business needs without waste. However, this model had its limitations, including long procurement cycles, difficulty in scaling quickly, and challenges in adapting to changing business requirements.The introduction of cloud computing revolutionized this model, shifting IT financial management from a CapEx-heavy approach to a more flexible OpEx model. Instead of purchasing hardware, companies could now rent computing resources on a pay-as-you-go basis, allowing for greater scalability and responsiveness to changing demands. Cloud production environments are often classified under Cost of Goods Sold (COGS), while development environments fall under OpEx. This distinction requires more refined financial models and cost attribution methods to manage cloud expenses effectively.Nonetheless, the ease of provisioning cloud resources has also introduced challenges, such as
cloud sprawl
– uncontrolled spending on unused or underutilized resources. Traditional financial management practices were insufficient to address this complexity, prompting the development of FinOps, a framework designed to bring financial accountability to the dynamic cloud environment. It focuses on collaboration between finance, engineering, and operations teams to optimize cloud costs and drive business value.
The evolution of FinOps
FinOps began as a response to the need for better cloud cost management. Initially, organizations relied on basic cost monitoring tools from cloud service providers to track their expenditures. As cloud usage grew, third-party tools and platforms emerged, offering more sophisticated capabilities for cost allocation, budgeting, forecasting, and optimization.The establishment of the FinOps Foundation was a milestone in formalizing FinOps as a discipline. The foundation brought together industry leaders, practitioners, and vendors to create standardized practices, frameworks, and training programs, promoting the adoption of FinOps across organizations of all sizes. Today, FinOps encompasses a wide range of activities, from cost tracking and optimization to strategic financial planning, with an emphasis on leveraging data and analytics to drive enhanced decision-making and maximize the value of cloud investments.With its focus on data-driven insights and continuous optimization, FinOps has become essential for organizations looking to maintain control over their cloud expenditures. Understanding why FinOps matters is key to appreciating its growing impact on both financial and operational success.
Why FinOps matters
FinOps is essential for aligning financial and technical goals within an organization. In the cloud era, engineering teams have unprecedented control over resource provisioning, but this can lead to a disconnect between technical decisions and financial outcomes. FinOps bridges this gap by fostering collaboration between finance, engineering, and operations teams, ensuring that technical decisions are made with an understanding of their financial implications.
Enhancing Cost Transparency
One of the core principles of FinOps is cost transparency. In a traditional IT environment, costs were often hidden in large, upfront capital expenditures. In the cloud, costs are dynamic and distributed across various services and teams. FinOps practices provide visibility into these costs, enabling organizations to understand where their money is going and why. This transparency is crucial for identifying areas of waste, optimizing resource usage, and making informed financial decisions.
Driving Financial Accountability
FinOps promotes financial accountability by assigning costs to the teams and individuals responsible for consuming resources. This accountability encourages more responsible behavior and cost-conscious decision-making. When teams are aware of the financial implications of their actions, they are more likely to take steps to optimize their usage and avoid unnecessary expenses. This shift in mindset can lead to significant cost savings and more efficient use of cloud resources.
Optimizing Cloud Spend
One of the primary goals of FinOps is to optimize cloud spend. This involves not only reducing costs but also maximizing the value derived from cloud investments. FinOps practices include continuous monitoring and analysis of cloud usage, identifying opportunities for cost savings, and implementing strategies to achieve those savings. Techniques such as rightsizing instances, leveraging reserved instances, and eliminating unused resources are commonly employed to optimize spend.
Improving Budget Forecasting
Accurate budget forecasting is a critical aspect of financial management, and FinOps plays a key role in improving forecasting accuracy. By providing detailed insights into historical cloud spending patterns and trends, FinOps enables organizations to make more accurate predictions about future costs. This improved forecasting capability helps organizations plan their budgets more effectively, allocate resources more efficiently, and avoid unexpected expenses.As cloud computing continues to transform the way organizations operate, FinOps provides the tools and practices needed to manage cloud costs effectively, align financial and technical goals, and support sustainable business growth. By adopting FinOps, organizations can gain better visibility into their cloud expenditures, drive financial accountability, and optimize their cloud investments for maximum value. To address this need, the FinOps Foundation was created.To further support organizations in adopting and implementing these crucial practices, the FinOps Foundation was established, offering a structured framework and community-driven resources to guide cloud financial management.
Introducing the FinOps Foundation
The FinOps Foundation is an organization dedicated to advancing the discipline of cloud financial management through best practices, education, and collaboration. The FinOps Foundation plays a pivotal role in addressing community needs by providing resources, frameworks, and a community for professionals involved in managing cloud costs.The foundation was officially launched in 2019 by J.R. Storment and Mike Fuller, both of whom recognized the need for a dedicated organization to support the FinOps community. Since its inception, the FinOps Foundation has grown rapidly, attracting members from various industries and sectors, all united by a common goal of improving cloud financial management.The FinOps Foundation's mission is to advance the practice of FinOps through education, standardization, and community engagement. It offers a range of resources and programs designed to help organizations implement and refine their FinOps practices. Key offerings include the FinOps Framework, training and certification programs, and a vibrant community of practitioners.A defining feature of the FinOps Foundation is its strong focus on community and collaboration. It fosters a global network of FinOps professionals who regularly share their experiences, challenges, and solutions. This collaborative ethos is reflected in initiatives such as working groups, conferences, and online forums.Working groups are specialized teams that focus on specific aspects of FinOps, such as cost allocation, forecasting, and tooling. These groups provide a platform for members to delve deep into particular topics, develop new best practices, and produce valuable resources for the wider community. The insights generated by these working groups are often incorporated into the FinOps Framework, ensuring that it remains relevant and comprehensive.The FinOps Foundation also hosts conferences and events, such as the annual FinOps X Summit, which brings together practitioners, vendors, and thought leaders to discuss the latest trends and innovations in cloud financial management. These events provide valuable opportunities for networking, learning, and sharing knowledge.Through its comprehensive framework, training programs, and community initiatives, the foundation provides the tools and resources needed to navigate the complexities of managing cloud costs. By fostering collaboration and continuous learning, the FinOps Foundation helps organizations optimize their cloud spend, drive financial accountability, and achieve greater efficiency in their cloud operations. As cloud adoption continues to grow, the importance of FinOps and the role of the FinOps Foundation will only increase, making it an indispensable resource for businesses worldwide.This comprehensive framework, which is central to the FinOps Foundation's mission, serves as a structured guide for organizations to manage cloud financial operations with clarity and precision.
FinOps Framework
The FinOps Framework (shown in Figure 9.1) is a comprehensive structure designed to guide organizations through the complex landscape of cloud financial management. It provides a set of principles, domains, capabilities, personas, phases, and maturity models that collectively help organizations achieve financial accountability and optimization in their cloud operations.
Figure 9.1: The FinOps Framework - The core principles, domains, capabilities, personas, and maturity phases of FinOps
The FinOps Framework is built on six core principles that serve as the foundation for effective cloud financial management:
Teams need to collaborate
: Successful FinOps requires collaboration across different teams, including finance, engineering, and operations. This ensures that everyone is aligned on cost management goals and strategies.
Decisions are driven by the business value of cloud
: Financial decisions should be based on the business value derived from cloud services. This principle emphasizes the importance of understanding the return on investment for cloud expenditures.
Everyone takes ownership of their cloud usage
: Responsibility for cloud costs is distributed across the organization, with each team accountable for their usage. This fosters a culture of accountability and encourages cost-effective behavior.
FinOps reports should be accessible and timely
: Financial reports must be easily accessible and provided in a timely manner to enable informed decision-making. This principle ensures that stakeholders have the information they need when they need it.
A centralized team drives FinOps
: A dedicated FinOps team is crucial for implementing and managing cloud financial practices. This team provides the expertise and oversight needed to ensure effective cost management.
Take advantage of the variable cost model of cloud
: The cloud offers a flexible, pay-as-you-go pricing model that can be leveraged to optimize costs. Organizations should take advantage of this model to scale resources up or down based on demand.
Domains of the FinOps Framework
The FinOps Framework categorizes its capabilities into four key domains, each addressing a different aspect of cloud financial management
Understanding Cloud Usage and Cost
This domain aims to provide a clear understanding of an organization's cloud usage and expenses. It involves collecting data on cost, usage, observability, and sustainability. Additionally, it defines how to categorize and allocate costs, ensuring data accessibility for all FinOps stakeholders.To achieve these objectives, the following key activities are essential for ensuring accurate tracking, allocation, and optimization of cloud costs.
Data Ingestion
: Collecting detailed cloud usage and cost data.
Allocation
: Assigning costs to specific teams, projects, or business units.
Reporting and Analytics
: Generating insights and reports on cloud spending.
Anomaly Management
: Detecting and addressing unusual spending patterns.
Quantifying Business Value
In this domain, organizations connect cloud usage and cost data to the business value generated. The focus is on ensuring transparency in cloud expenditures and aligning them with business goals through budgeting, forecasting, and technical key performance indicators (KPIs).
Planning and Estimating
: Projecting future cloud usage and costs.
Forecasting
: Predicting future spending based on historical data.
Budgeting
: Allocating budgets for cloud resources.
Benchmarking
: Comparing cloud costs against industry standards or internal benchmarks.
Unit Economics
: Analyzing the cost efficiency of cloud services at a granular level.
Optimizing Cloud Usage & Cost
This domain ensures that cloud resources are used efficiently, minimizing costs and maximizing value. Organizations measure efficiency through cost, carbon usage, or other operational metrics, using techniques like optimizing resource types and pricing models. To effectively manage and optimize cloud resource usage, the following capabilities are crucial for driving both cost savings and operational efficiency:
Architecting for Cloud
: Designing cloud architectures for cost efficiency.
Rate Optimization
: Leveraging discounts, reserved instances, and other pricing models.
Workload Optimization
: Adjusting workloads to improve efficiency.
Cloud Sustainability
: Incorporating sustainability considerations into cloud operations.
Licensing & SaaS
: Managing licenses and software-as-a-service costs.
Managing the FinOps Practice
This domain is about continuously improving FinOps practices and aligning the organization for success. It covers day-to-day operations, governance, education, and integrating FinOps with other business functions. To drive continuous improvement and ensure seamless adoption of FinOps across the organization, the following capabilities are essential for fostering collaboration, governance, and operational excellence:
FinOps Practice Operations
: Managing the day-to-day operations of the FinOps practice.
Cloud Policy an Governance
: Implementing policies and governance structures for cloud cost management.
FinOps Assessment
: Evaluating the effectiveness of FinOps practices.
FinOps Tools and Services
: Utilizing tools and services to support FinOps activities.
FinOps Education & Enablement
: Providing training and resources to enable effective FinOps practices.
Invoicing and Chargeback
: Managing billing and chargeback processes.
Onboarding Workloads
: Ensuring new workloads are incorporated into FinOps processes.
Intersecting Disciplines
: Integrating FinOps with other disciplines like security, compliance, and procurement.
The FinOps Framework identifies several key personas involved in cloud financial management:
FinOps Practitioner
: Drives the implementation and management of cloud financial operations, ensuring cost visibility, accountability, and optimization.
Leadership
: Senior leader who champions FinOps within the organization, providing strategic direction and securing resources.
Finance
: Focuses on budgeting, forecasting, and financial reporting, ensuring cloud costs align with organizational financial goals.
Engineering
: Developers and infrastructure engineers responsible for provisioning and managing cloud resources, implementing cost-saving measures.
Product
: Product managers who ensure cloud spending aligns with product goals and delivers value to customers.
Procurement
: Manages vendor relationships and cloud contracts, negotiating pricing and terms to ensure cost-effective services.
The FinOps Framework provides a structured approach to managing cloud costs, promoting collaboration, accountability, and cost optimization across the organization, and outlines a lifecycle consisting of three phases:
Figure 9.2: The FinOps Lifecycle in three phases
As shown in the preceding figure, these phases are explained as follows:
Inform
: The Inform phase is about gaining visibility into cloud costs. This involves collecting and analyzing cost data, providing detailed reports to stakeholders, and allocating costs to the appropriate teams or projects. The goal is to ensure that everyone has a clear understanding of where money is being spent and why.
Optimize
: In the Optimize phase, organizations focus on reducing costs and improving efficiency. This involves identifying and eliminating waste, leveraging discounts and pricing options, and adjusting usage patterns to align with cost-saving opportunities. The goal is to ensure that cloud resources are used as efficiently as possible.
Operate
: The Operate phase involves the ongoing management and governance of cloud costs. This includes implementing policies and controls, automating cost management tasks, providing regular financial reports, and engaging with stakeholders to ensure alignment on cost management goals. The goal is to maintain cost efficiency and drive continuous improvement.
The
FinOps Maturity Model
outlines three stages of growth in cloud financial management, guiding organizations from initial implementation to full optimization. Each stage –crawl, walk, and run– builds on the previous, helping businesses improve visibility, optimize cloud usage, and continuously drive cost efficiency while fostering collaboration across teams:
Crawl
: At the Crawl stage, organizations are just beginning to implement FinOps practices. The focus is on gaining visibility into cloud costs and establishing basic cost management processes. Key activities include setting up cost reporting, assigning costs to teams or projects, and providing initial training to stakeholders.
Walk
: In the Walk stage, organizations have established basic FinOps practices and are working to optimize their cloud spending. The focus is on improving cost efficiency through resource optimization, rate optimization, and usage optimization. Key activities include implementing automation tools, conducting regular cost reviews, and engaging with stakeholders to drive cost-saving initiatives.
Run
: At the Run stage, organizations have fully mature FinOps practices and are realizing the full value of their cloud investments. The focus is on continuous improvement and innovation enablement. Key activities include leveraging advanced analytics to drive cost-saving decisions, integrating FinOps practices into CI/CD pipelines, and measuring the business value generated by cloud investments
The next step after establishing a common understanding of the right practices and processes involved in implementing FinOps, a common language to describe the data related to FinOps is required. That is what FOCUS™ is trying to do.
FinOps Cost and Usage Specification (FOCUS™)
The FinOps Cost and Usage Specification, commonly referred to as FOCUS™, is a crucial component of the FinOps Foundation's efforts to standardize cloud financial management practices. FOCUS™ aims to create a common language and framework for describing cloud cost and usage data, enabling organizations to better manage their cloud expenditures and optimize their financial operations. This specification addresses the growing complexity of cloud environments and the need for a unified approach to handling financial data across various cloud service providers.
Figure 9.3: FOCUS™ used as a central normalization framework for all cloud cost sources
FOCUS™ was developed in response to the challenges organizations face in managing cloud costs efficiently. As cloud adoption grows, so does the complexity of tracking and analyzing expenses. Each cloud provider uses its own billing structures, terminologies, and reporting formats, making it difficult for organizations to gain a unified view of their cloud spend. FOCUS™ provides a standardized format for cloud cost and usage data, enabling organizations to integrate and analyze this information more easily.The primary goal of FOCUS™ is to improve cost visibility and transparency across the organization. By standardizing how cloud cost and usage data are represented, FOCUS™ allows organizations to compare expenditures across different cloud providers, identify cost-saving opportunities, and make informed financial decisions. It also fosters better communication between finance, engineering, and operations teams, ensuring alignment on cloud spending.To further enhance cost management, FOCUS™ introduces cost attribution strategies that enable organizations to assign specific cloud expenses to individual services, teams, or business units, providing deeper insights into their financial impact
Cost attribution in an Internal Developer Platform
Cost attribution refers to the process of assigning costs to the specific departments, projects, or business units responsible for those expenses. In the context of an
Internal Developer Platform
(
IDP
), cost attribution is crucial for understanding and managing the financial implications of running a platform.
