It involves breaking down the total expenses incurred from cloud resources, tools, and infrastructure to the various teams and projects utilizing them.The importance of cost attribution cannot be overstated. In a complex, multi-tenant environment like an IDP, where numerous teams and projects share resources, it is essential to have a clear understanding of who is using what and at what cost. This transparency helps organizations manage their budgets more effectively, encourages responsible usage of resources, and aligns financial accountability with resource consumption. By accurately attributing costs, organizations can ensure that each team or project is aware of their spending, promoting a culture of cost consciousness and optimization.Cost attribution also supports better financial planning and forecasting. When costs are precisely attributed, it becomes easier to predict future expenditures based on current usage patterns. This enables more accurate budget allocations and financial planning, helping organizations avoid unexpected costs and allocate resources more efficiently. Furthermore, cost attribution provides the data needed to make informed decisions about investments in infrastructure and services, ensuring that spending aligns with business goals and priorities.Moreover, cost attribution is fundamental for implementing chargeback or showback models, where costs are either billed back to the respective departments or simply reported for transparency. These models drive accountability and can lead to significant cost savings as teams become more aware of their spending and motivated to optimize their usage.However, implementing effective cost attribution comes with challenges:
Tracking complexity
: Cloud services often have variable pricing models (e.g., on-demand, reserved instances, and spot instances), which fluctuate with usage, making accurate cost attribution difficult.
Data granularity
: Accurate cost attribution requires detailed data on resource usage (CPU, memory, storage, network consumption), which can be resource-intensive to collect and analyze. Ensuring the accuracy and completeness of this data is crucial, as any discrepancies can lead to incorrect cost allocations and financial mismanagement.
Shared resources
: Many cloud components, such as networking, security, and storage, are shared by multiple teams. Allocating these costs accurately requires a methodology like proportional usage or predefined ratios. Developing and maintaining these methodologies can be challenging, especially as usage patterns and organizational structures evolve.
Cultural and organizational resistance
: Shifting to a model of financial accountability can face resistance. Implementing cost attribution requires a shift in mindset towards greater financial accountability and transparency, which can be met with resistance if not managed carefully. Clear communication, training, and leadership support are essential for fostering a cost-awareness culture.
Workflow integration
: Organizations often have established processes for budgeting, reporting, and resource management that may not align seamlessly with new cost attribution practices. Integrating cost attribution into existing budgeting and resource management processes can be complex, requiring careful coordination and planning.
To address these challenges, organizations must adopt clear strategies and methodologies for accurate cost distribution across teams and resources, ensuring financial transparency and alignment with business goals.
Methods for Cost Allocation
Cost allocation is a critical aspect of managing cloud expenditures, especially within an
Internal Developer Platform
(
IDP
) where multiple teams and projects share resources. Effective cost allocation ensures that each team or project is charged accurately for the resources they consume, promoting accountability and enabling more precise financial planning. There are several methods for cost allocation, each with its own advantages and challenges. Selecting the right method depends on various factors, including the organization's structure, the nature of the resources being shared, and the specific financial goals.Shared services cost allocation involves distributing the costs of shared resources, such as infrastructure, networking, and security, across multiple teams or projects. This approach is particularly relevant in cloud environments where services are often used by multiple stakeholders. The goal is to allocate these shared costs in a way that reflects the actual usage and benefits derived by each team.There are several strategies for effectively allocating shared services costs. These strategies include:
Direct Allocation
: Costs are allocated directly based on measurable usage metrics. For example, if a shared database service is used by multiple teams, the cost can be allocated based on the number of queries or transactions performed by each team.
Proportional Allocation
: Costs are allocated based on a predefined ratio or proportion. This method is useful when direct measurement is difficult. For instance, if a shared security service protects multiple applications, the cost can be divided based on the relative size or importance of each application.
Fixed Rate Allocation
: Costs are allocated based on a fixed rate or fee. This method simplifies cost allocation by charging a standard fee for each team or project, regardless of actual usage. It is often used for services that provide consistent benefits to all users, such as baseline network security.
Hybrid Allocation
: A combination of different allocation methods is used to best reflect the cost distribution. For example, a shared storage service might allocate costs based on a fixed fee for baseline capacity and a proportional fee for additional usage.
Common Cost Allocation Methods
Accurate cost allocation is key to ensuring financial accountability and optimizing resource usage in cloud environments. Below are some of the most common approaches, ranging from simple to more tailored strategies:
Activity-Based Costing
(
ABC
): Activity-Based Costing (ABC) allocates costs based on the activities that drive those costs. In an IDP, this might involve identifying the various activities involved in providing cloud services, such as computing, storage, and data transfer, and then allocating costs based on the consumption of these activities by different teams or projects. ABC provides a detailed and accurate way to allocate costs but can be complex to implement due to the need for detailed activity tracking.
Resource-Based Costing
: Resource-Based Costing involves allocating costs based on the specific resources consumed by each team or project. This method is straightforward and directly links costs to usage metrics such as CPU hours, gigabytes of storage, or network bandwidth. It is easy to understand and implement but may not account for the indirect costs associated with providing and managing those resources.
User-Based Costing
: User-Based Costing allocates costs based on the number of users or seats assigned to each team or project. This method is often used for software licensing and subscription-based services. It simplifies cost allocation and is easy to administer, but it may not accurately reflect actual usage if the number of users does not correlate with resource consumption.
Time-Based Costing
: Time-Based Costing allocates costs based on the duration that resources are used. For example, costs might be allocated based on the number of hours that virtual machines are running. This method is useful for resources with time-based pricing models but may not capture the full picture of usage efficiency.
Hybrid Costing
: Hybrid Costing combines elements of different allocation methods to create a customized approach that best fits the organization's needs. For example, an organization might use resource-based costing for computing and storage, user-based costing for software licenses, and activity-based costing for support services. Hybrid costing allows for flexibility and can provide a more comprehensive view of costs but may require more effort to manage and maintain.
Selecting the right cost allocation method involves considering several factors to ensure that the chosen approach aligns with the organization's financial goals and operational realities.
Accuracy and Fairness
: The chosen method should provide an accurate and fair distribution of costs. It should reflect the actual usage and benefits derived by each team or project, promoting accountability and encouraging cost-effective behavior. For example, resource-based costing might be appropriate for a cloud environment where usage can be accurately measured and attributed to specific teams.
Simplicity and Transparency
: The cost allocation method should be simple and transparent, making it easy for stakeholders to understand how costs are distributed and why. A complex method might provide more accuracy but can be difficult to administer and explain. User-based costing, for instance, offers simplicity and transparency, making it easier for teams to understand their charges.
Flexibility and Scalability
: The method should be flexible and scalable to accommodate changes in the organization, such as the addition of new teams or projects, changes in resource usage patterns, or shifts in strategic priorities. Hybrid costing, which combines elements of different methods, offers the flexibility needed to adapt to evolving needs.
Cost of Implementation
: The cost of implementing and maintaining the cost allocation method should be considered. Some methods, like activity-based costing, provide high accuracy but can be costly and time-consuming to implement. Simpler methods, like fixed rate allocation, might be less accurate but are easier and cheaper to administer.
Strategic Alignment
: The chosen method should align with the organization's strategic goals. For example, if the goal is to promote efficient resource usage, a method that accurately reflects usage and encourages optimization, such as resource-based costing, might be appropriate. If the goal is to provide predictability and stability in budgeting, a fixed rate allocation might be more suitable.
Stakeholder Buy-In
: Gaining buy-in from stakeholders is crucial for successful implementation. The chosen method should be communicated clearly, and stakeholders should be involved in the decision-making process. Transparent communication and stakeholder engagement help ensure that the method is accepted and adhered to.
By selecting the appropriate cost allocation method, organizations can promote financial accountability, optimize resource usage, and align spending with strategic goals. The choice of method should balance accuracy, simplicity, flexibility, cost, and strategic alignment to meet the organization's specific needs and objectives.With cost allocation serving as a foundation for effective cloud financial management, the next step is implementing practical tools to support these efforts. In the following section, we will explore how open-source tools can be leveraged to implement FinOps in cloud environments, enhancing visibility, accountability, and optimization.
Implementing FinOps with Open-Source tools
In this section, we will explore the practical implementation of FinOps using three cloud-native tools: OpenCost, Prometheus, and Kyverno. These tools together create a comprehensive framework for monitoring, managing, and optimizing cloud costs. The goal is to guide you in using these tools to achieve financial visibility, accountability, and efficiency in a cloud-native environment.OpenCost is an open-source cost monitoring tool designed to provide visibility into Kubernetes resource costs. It collects and analyzes data on resource usage, helping organizations understand their spending patterns and identify cost-saving opportunities. OpenCost integrates seamlessly with Prometheus, a widely-used monitoring and alerting toolkit, to gather metrics and generate actionable insights.Kyverno is a policy engine designed specifically for Kubernetes. It allows you to manage, validate, and enforce configurations across your Kubernetes clusters. Kyverno can be used to implement policies that ensure compliance with cost management practices, such as resource quotas, cost allocation, and optimization rules. By automating policy enforcement, Kyverno helps maintain financial governance and operational efficiency.When used together, OpenCost, Prometheus, and Kyverno create a robust architecture for implementing FinOps and cost management policies in Kubernetes environments. Building on this architecture, the next step is understanding how OpenCost plays a crucial role in providing real-time cost visibility and transparency in Kubernetes environments.
Introduction to OpenCost
OpenCost is an open-source tool specifically designed to provide detailed visibility into the cost of running workloads on Kubernetes clusters. As organizations increasingly adopt cloud-native technologies, managing and optimizing the cost of these environments becomes a significant challenge. OpenCost addresses this by offering a transparent and granular view of resource consumption and associated costs. This capability enables teams to understand their spending patterns, allocate costs accurately, and implement cost-saving measures effectively.OpenCost integrates seamlessly with Prometheus, leveraging its robust monitoring and alerting capabilities to collect data on resource usage. By providing a unified view of costs across multiple clusters and environments, OpenCost empowers organizations to implement FinOps practices, driving financial accountability and optimization.
Figure 9.4: OpenCost Architecture
OpenCost is designed to provide a comprehensive view of Kubernetes costs, offering insights into how resources are consumed and how expenses are incurred. It collects detailed metrics on CPU, memory, storage, and network usage, mapping these to the associated costs. This granular data helps organizations allocate costs accurately to different teams, projects, or business units, fostering a culture of financial responsibility.The primary objective of OpenCost is to enhance cost visibility and transparency. By making detailed cost information readily available, OpenCost enables organizations to identify inefficiencies, optimize resource usage, and reduce unnecessary expenditures. This transparency is crucial for implementing effective FinOps practices, ensuring that cloud spending aligns with business objectives.Here are the key components of OpenCost:
Data Collection
: OpenCost collects detailed metrics on resource usage from Kubernetes clusters using Prometheus. This includes data on CPU and memory consumption, storage utilization, and network traffic.
Cost Analysis
: OpenCost analyzes the collected data to calculate the costs associated with different resources. It uses predefined pricing models and customizable parameters to provide accurate cost estimates.
Cost Allocation
: OpenCost allocates costs to specific teams, projects, or business units based on resource usage. This enables organizations to track spending accurately and hold teams accountable for their consumption.
Reporting and Visualization
: OpenCost provides detailed reports and visualizations that highlight cost trends, usage patterns, and potential savings. These insights help organizations make informed decisions about resource allocation and optimization.
Integration with Prometheus
: By leveraging Prometheus for data collection, OpenCost integrates seamlessly into existing monitoring and alerting workflows, providing a unified view of performance and costs.
OpenCost offers a range of features designed to enhance cost management in Kubernetes environments. These features, some of which are listed ahead, provide significant benefits, enabling organizations to optimize their spending and improve financial accountability:
Granular Cost Visibility
: OpenCost provides detailed insights into the costs associated with running workloads on Kubernetes clusters. By collecting and analyzing metrics on CPU, memory, storage, and network usage, OpenCost offers a granular view of resource consumption and associated expenses. This visibility is crucial for identifying inefficiencies and optimizing resource usage.
Accurate Cost Allocation
: OpenCost allocates costs accurately to different teams, projects, or business units based on resource usage. This ensures that each team is accountable for its spending, promoting responsible resource consumption. Accurate cost allocation also supports more precise budgeting and financial planning.
Customizable Pricing Models
: OpenCost allows organizations to define and customize pricing models based on their specific needs. This flexibility ensures that cost calculations reflect the actual expenses incurred, providing accurate and reliable cost estimates. Organizations can adjust pricing parameters to align with their cloud provider's billing structure and internal cost management practices.
Integration with Prometheus
: OpenCost integrates seamlessly with Prometheus, leveraging its robust monitoring and alerting capabilities to collect data on resource usage. This integration provides a unified view of performance and costs, enabling organizations to correlate cost data with performance metrics and make more informed decisions.
Detailed Reporting and Visualization
: OpenCost offers comprehensive reporting and visualization capabilities, providing detailed insights into cost trends, usage patterns, and potential savings. These reports help organizations identify cost-saving opportunities, optimize resource allocation, and reduce unnecessary expenditures. Visualizations make it easy to understand and communicate cost information to stakeholders.
Real-Time Cost Monitoring
: OpenCost provides real-time monitoring of costs, allowing organizations to track expenses as they occur. This real-time visibility helps teams stay within budget, avoid unexpected costs, and take immediate action to address any issues. Real-time monitoring also supports proactive cost management, enabling organizations to implement cost-saving measures before expenses escalate.
Automation and Scalability
: OpenCost supports automation and scalability, making it suitable for large and complex Kubernetes environments. Organizations can automate cost allocation, reporting, and optimization processes, reducing manual effort and improving efficiency. Scalability ensures that OpenCost can handle the demands of growing cloud-native environments, providing consistent and reliable cost insights.
Community-Driven Development
: As an open-source tool, OpenCost benefits from community-driven development and collaboration. This ensures that OpenCost evolves to meet the changing needs of its users, incorporating the latest best practices and innovations in cloud cost management. Community contributions also enhance the tool's capabilities, providing a rich and diverse set of features.
OpenCost Specification
defines the standards and guidelines for implementing and using OpenCost in Kubernetes environments. It provides a detailed framework for collecting, analyzing, and reporting cost data, ensuring consistency and accuracy in cost management practices. The specification covers various aspects of OpenCost, including data collection, pricing models, cost allocation, reporting, and integration with other tools.
Integrating OpenCost in your IDP
To begin using OpenCost in your Kubernetes cluster, you'll need to deploy the OpenCost application and configure it to start collecting cost data. The simplest way to get up and running is by using the provided Helm chart maintained by the OpenCost project.
Install OpenCost
First, add the OpenCost Helm chart repository and update your local Helm repo index. Then install OpenCost into your Kubernetes cluster with default settings using the following commands:
helm repo add opencost https://opencost.github.io/opencost-helm-chart
helm repo update
helm install opencost opencost/opencost --namespace opencost \
--create-namespace
This will deploy OpenCost in its own namespace with a default configuration. The installation includes:
The OpenCost metrics collector
The Prometheus exporter endpoint
Configuration files and secrets
Connecting OpenCost with Prometheus
Once deployed, OpenCost exposes a Prometheus-compatible /metrics endpoint and a REST API.
Customize Pricing Configurations (Optional)
OpenCost allows customization of cost models using a ConfigMap. For instance, you might want to define custom CPU and memory prices:
apiVersion: v1
kind: ConfigMap
metadata:
  name: custom-pricing-config
  namespace: opencost
data:
  config.yaml: |
    provider: custom
    description: "Custom pricing"
    CPU: "0.03125"
    RAM: "0.00444"
    spotCPU: "0.0150"
    spotRAM: "0.0025"
Apply the custom config:
kubectl apply -f custom-pricing-config.yaml
Integrating OpenCost with Prometheus
OpenCost exports cost metrics on a Prometheus-compatible /metrics endpoint, making integration with Prometheus seamless. You simply have to modify your prometheus.yml to add OpenCost as a scrape target:
scrape_configs:
  - job_name: 'opencost'
    static_configs:
      - targets: ['opencost.opencost.svc.cluster.local:9003']
After integrating OpenCost with Prometheus, you can visualize the data using Grafana dashboards.
Cost Management Policies with Kyverno
Integrating OpenCost with Kyverno empowers organizations to implement policy-driven cost governance within Kubernetes clusters. This combination enables automated enforcement of cost allocation rules, ensuring that resource usage aligns with organizational budgets and policies.To monitor and control costs at the namespace level, you can define a ConfigMap that specifies the allocated daily cost for each namespace:
apiVersion: v1
kind: ConfigMap
metadata:
  name: namespace-quota-cm
  namespace: opencost
data:
  argocd: "1"
This ConfigMap assigns a daily cost quota of $1 to the argocd namespace.​By leveraging Kyverno's ability to make external API calls (introduced in version 1.10), it's very easy to integrate Kyverno to pull cost data from OpenCost through a simple API call. Next step, is to implement a Kyverno policy that retrieves actual cost data from OpenCost and compares it against the allocated quota.
apiVersion: kyverno.io/v1
kind: ClusterPolicy
metadata:
  creationTimestamp: "2025-04-01T00:11:22Z"
  generation: 1
  name: check-namespace-costs-cm
  annotations:
    policies.kyverno.io/title: Namespace Cost Exceeded
    policies.kyverno.io/minversion: 1.10.0
    policies.kyverno.io/category: Cost Management
    policies.kyverno.io/severity: medium
    policies.kyverno.io/subject: Namespace
    policies.kyverno.io/description: >-
      This policy checks for namespace costs and generates a violation if
      the cost exceeds the allocated cost specified in the namespace-
      quota-cm config map.
spec:
  background: true
  rules:
  - context:
    - apiCall:
        method: GET
        service:
          url:
            http://opencost.opencost:9090/model/allocation/
            compute?window=1d&aggregate=namespace&step=1d&accumulate=false
      name: result
    - name: totalCost
      variable:
        value: '{{ result.data[0].["{{ request.object.metadata.name }}"]
        [0].totalCost || ''0'' }}'
    - name: costConfigMap
      configMap:
        name: namespace-quota-cm
        namespace: opencost
    - name: allocatedCost
      variable:
        value: '{{ costConfigMap.data."{{ request.object.metadata.name }}"        || ''0'' }}'
    match:
      any:
      - resources:
          kinds:
          - Namespace
    name: check-namespace-cost
    validate:
      deny:
        conditions:
          all:
          - key: '{{ allocatedCost }}'
            operator: GreaterThan
            value: '0'
          - key: '{{ totalCost }}'
            operator: GreaterThan
            value: '{{ allocatedCost }}'
      message: namespace {{request.object.metadata.name}} cost {{
      totalCost }} exceeds maximum cost threshold {{ allocatedCost }}
  validationFailureAction: Audit
This policy performs the following actions:
Fetches the actual daily cost for each namespace from OpenCost.
Retrieves the allocated cost from the namespace-quota-cm ConfigMap.
Compares the actual cost against the allocated quota.
Generates a policy violation if the actual cost exceeds the allocated quota.
Implementing such policies facilitates proactive cost management, ensuring that Kubernetes resource usage remains within defined financial boundaries
Summary
In this chapter, we explored the financial aspects of platform engineering, focusing on the principles and practices of FinOps. We began by understanding the history and evolution of FinOps, emphasizing its importance in aligning financial and technical goals, enhancing cost transparency, and driving accountability within organizations. Understanding these foundational elements set the stage for implementing effective cloud financial management practices.We then examined the FinOps Foundation, its framework, and the principles that guide effective financial operations. Key components such as the domains and capabilities, personas involved in FinOps, and the maturity model were discussed in detail, providing a structured approach to managing cloud costs.We covered different methods for cost allocation, such as shared services cost allocation and chargeback models, addressing the challenges and best practices for achieving accurate cost distribution.Finally, we provided a detailed guide on implementing FinOps using OpenCost, Prometheus, and Kyverno. These tools, when integrated effectively, offer a robust framework for monitoring, managing, and optimizing cloud costs, ensuring financial visibility and accountability in cloud-native environments.In the next chapter, we will transition to the human aspect of platform engineering and the third part of our book, Platform Leadership, exploring how to build and manage a successful platform engineering team, that will drive the complex initiatives described in the last four chapters.

10 Building Your Platform Engineering Team, The Right Way

https://packt.link/IOzft
The rise of the cloud-native ecosystem has seen the emergence of the platform engineering field as a key element in modern software delivery. The role of a platform engineering team is to provide the foundations upon which product development teams can build and deliver software quickly, efficiently, and reliably. The value of such a team is enormous but building and maintaining such a team requires careful consideration and strategic planning.In this chapter, we're going to cover the following main topics:
The relationship between Conway's Law and platform engineering
: We'll explore how
Conway's Law
- which posits that the structure of a system reflects the organization that created it - applies to platform engineering. We'll discuss how to use this principle to guide the structure of your platform engineering team.
Creating Fit-for-Purpose teams
: We'll delve into the concept of
Fit-for-Purpose
teams, exploring how to ensure that your team's structure, skills, and objectives align with the organization's needs. We will particularly focus on applying this concept to platform engineering teams, ensuring they are equipped to deliver on their key pillars of developer experience, productivity, security, compliance, and best practices.
Team topologies and platform engineering
: We'll introduce the concept of
team topologies
and their role in shaping effective platform engineering teams. We'll discuss strategies for integrating a platform engineering team into your broader organization and how different team structures can influence effectiveness.
The role of a platform engineer
: We'll provide a comprehensive overview of the role of a platform engineer. This includes the technical skills required, the diverse backgrounds that platform engineers may come from, the potential career paths, and the importance of soft skills in this role.
Conway's Law and Fit-for-Purpose teams
In 1968, Melvin Conway, an early pioneer of software development, made an astute observation about the way organizations develop software. This observation, now known as Conway's Law, posits: "
Any organization that designs a system (defined broadly) will produce a design whose structure is a copy of the organization's communication structure.
"In simpler terms, Conway's Law suggests that the way teams are organized in a company will invariably influence the design of the products they create. This principle has profound implications for software development and platform engineering, as it provides a lens through which we can better understand the often complex relationship between an organization's structure and the systems it produces.Conway's Law has been validated by multiple studies and observed in action in numerous organizations. The law is particularly relevant to the modern world of microservices and distributed systems, where architectural boundaries often mirror team boundaries. For instance, if a company has a team dedicated to user authentication, it is likely that the company's software will have a distinct user authentication service.
Note
You can refer to these studies for more information on Conway's Law:
https://www.hbs.edu/ris/Publication%20Files/08-039_1861e507-1dc1-4602-85b8-90d71559d85b.pdf
https://link.springer.com/chapter/10.1007/978-3-642-38928-3_8
Consider a large online retail company with separate teams handling inventory, user interface, customer transactions, and shipping. In accordance with Conway's Law, it is highly likely that the architecture of the company's software system would mirror this organizational structure, with separate components or services for inventory management, user interface, transaction processing, and shipping.This mirroring can be beneficial, allowing for ownership and expertise within each service. However, it also poses challenges, as the lines of communication that exist organizationally are often echoed in the software, potentially leading to a high degree of coupling between services if not managed properly.Conway's Law can serve as a useful tool for organizations planning their structure and processes, particularly when embarking on a new software project or restructuring existing systems. It can inform decisions about team organization and project management to align with the desired architecture and design. In the context of software engineering, understanding Conway's Law can help organizations to design their teams in a way that naturally fosters the development of robust, efficient products. By carefully structuring and organizing teams, organizations can drive architectural decisions that align with their strategic goals and yield high-quality systems. As a team's structure will influence the team's output, you can start by architecting the desired output, and then derive the desired structure
(figure 7.1)
Figure 7.1 – Applying Conway's Law – Product Design defining team design
In the following sections, we will delve into how Conway's Law can be leveraged when organizing a platform engineering team, and how to ensure that a team is "
Fit-for-Purpose
" in the context of their specific organizational needs and goals.
Organizational design for large scale engineering teams
In the world of microservices and cloud-native technologies, aligning organizational design with technical architecture is crucial. As Conway's Law suggests, the internal structure of our teams greatly impacts the design of our systems, and this is particularly pronounced when we're dealing with large-scale engineering teams.The
Domain-Driven Design
(
DDD
) approach complements Conway's Law by emphasizing the importance of aligning software boundaries with business domains. DDD is a software design philosophy that promotes understanding the business domain, its needs, and its language, and then designing the software to match. This approach is extremely helpful when it comes to microservices architecture, where each microservice is ideally mapped to a specific business capability or sub-domain, also known as a
bounded context
in DDD terminology.When we combine DDD and Conway's Law, we start seeing the structure of our engineering teams mirror these bounded contexts. Product development teams, therefore, map to these bounded contexts, which essentially means that each team is responsible for a particular business sub-domain or functionality. This creates a clear understanding of areas of ownership, encourages loose coupling of teams (and therefore services), and fosters innovation within those areas of responsibility. Designing small agile cross-functional teams, will empower delivery of independent end to end services, which the team can have full ownership over.
(figure 7.2)
Figure 7.2 – Small independent teams, building loosely couple service architectures
For instance, a team focused on the
inventory
business sub-domain would be responsible for the inventory management microservice. The team would have a profound understanding of what
inventory
means to the business, the rules associated with it, and how it interacts with other sub-domains. It would own the full lifecycle of this service - designing it, building it, testing it, deploying it, and maintaining it. This structure promotes loose coupling and high cohesion within the teams and the services they own, which are fundamental characteristics of a well-architected microservices setup.However, it's essential to note that a 'bounded context' does not always mean a 'microservice'. A bounded context represents a boundary within which a particular model is valid and consistent, and a single bounded context could be implemented by one or multiple microservices.By incorporating DDD and Conway's Law in the organizational design, large scale engineering teams can break down complex business problems into manageable sub-problems handled by autonomous teams. This approach facilitates faster innovation, as each team can work independently, iterating on their services without being constrained by the progress or structure of others. This model, sometimes referred to as
teams as products
or
you build it, you run it
approach, is increasingly popular among organizations aiming to maximize their agility and efficiency in the cloud-native world.
Leveraging Conway's Law in organizing a platform engineering team
When it comes to structuring a platform engineering team, Conway's Law can prove to be a potent tool. The relationship between the structure of an organization and its produced designs becomes evident when you examine the output produced by different team arrangements.Consider, for instance, a scenario where each product team has an embedded DevOps engineer or a DevOps team. With this setup, the most likely outcome is a series of automation solutions that are specific to each product. This is because the DevOps efforts are closely aligned with the specific needs and processes of each product team, resulting in highly tailored solutions. Although this model promotes close collaboration and can be highly effective, it risks creating silos and duplicating effort. Each product team could end up solving the same problems independently and in slightly different ways, reducing the potential for shared learnings and efficiencies.In contrast, consider a setup where there is a dedicated platform engineering team. This team's objective is to build the foundation upon which all products are built, a shared platform that serves the needs of multiple product teams. With Conway's Law in mind, the design of this platform would likely reflect the structure and communication patterns of the platform team. The platform would be a coherent, unified product, instead of a collection of disparate solutions. This doesn't mean that the platform can't be flexible and cater to specific product needs, but rather that there's a consistent underlying infrastructure that can be leveraged across the organization.Despite the advantages, leveraging Conway's Law in this way is not without challenges. For one, the platform engineering team must have a broad and deep understanding of the needs and processes of the product teams it serves. This is crucial to ensure the platform delivers value and is aligned with the product teams' needs. The platform team must also strike a balance between creating a platform that's flexible enough to accommodate different product needs, and one that's not too generic or complex to navigate and use.There's also the risk of the platform team becoming a bottleneck in the software delivery process if the platform requires manual of ad-hoc interventions and they can't keep up with the demands of the product teams. To mitigate this risk, some organizations adopt an inner source approach, where product teams can contribute to the platform. This requires a strong culture of collaboration, clear contribution guidelines, and robust testing and integration processes.Another potential solution is to implement a service or product-oriented model for the platform team. This means the team not only builds and maintains the platform but also treats it as a product, focusing on user needs, usability, and continuous improvement. This model encourages the platform team to work closely with the product teams, understand their challenges and needs, and iterate on the platform to better serve its users.
Understanding Fit-for-Purpose in team structures
The concept of
fit-for-purpose
is a vital principle in many fields, from manufacturing to service industries. In essence, it denotes the idea that a product, service, or even a team should adequately meet its intended purpose or the requirements of a particular function.In the context of team structures, especially in platform engineering, the fit-for-purpose principle applies to how a team's composition, skill sets, and objectives align with the organizational needs and strategic goals. Essentially, a team that is fit-for-purpose is capable of efficiently and effectively fulfilling the tasks and responsibilities required of them. This alignment between a team's capabilities and its objectives is critical in ensuring optimal productivity, smooth workflows, and successful outputs.For a platform engineering team, being fit-for-purpose could mean having a mix of skills that encompass both software engineering and operations. It could mean having a team structure that enables efficient collaboration, rapid response to incidents, and continuous delivery of platform features. And crucially, it means having clear objectives that align with the organization's overarching strategic goals.Assessing whether a team is fit-for-purpose is an ongoing process. As an organization grows and evolves, so too will its needs and objectives. Thus, team structures and skills should be regularly evaluated and adjusted as necessary to remain aligned with these changing needs. This process might involve regular skills audits to identify any gaps or areas for development. It could also include assessing team structures and communication workflows to ensure they still facilitate efficient and effective work.Several techniques can be employed to assess and refine a team's fit-for-purpose:
Surveys or feedback sessions can help gather insights into team dynamics, strengths, and areas for improvement
Regular team performance metrics and reviews (like sprint/iteration retros) can highlight issues relating to productivity or quality of output
Training and development programs can help to upskill team members and align their capabilities with the team's objectives
To conclude, understanding and applying the concept of fit-for-purpose in team structures is a dynamic and continuous process. It involves regularly evaluating and refining the team's structure, skills, and objectives to ensure they remain aligned with the changing needs of the organization. It's a principle that lies at the heart of building and sustaining an effective platform engineering team.
Fit-for-Purpose in the context of platform engineering
Platform engineering, as a discipline, serves a unique role in modern organizations. It seeks to bridge the gap between infrastructure, development, operations, and business by providing an abstracted platform that enhances developer experience, increases productivity, ensures security and compliance, and promotes best practices and standards. These four pillars serve as a guiding force in defining the purpose of platform engineering, and subsequently, in designing an organization that is fit to serve this purpose:
Developer experience
: A fit-for-purpose platform engineering team needs to prioritize the developer experience. It must be composed of individuals who not only understand the technical aspects of platform development but are also empathetic towards the developers' needs. The team should be able to design and build a platform that is intuitive, robust, and provides the right abstractions that make developers' tasks more straightforward and efficient.
Increased productivity
: The ultimate goal of platform engineering is to increase productivity across the organization. The team needs to be designed in a way that it can deliver on this goal. This could mean having individuals with skills in automation, performance optimization, and
Continuous Integration/Continuous Deployment
(
CI/CD
). Moreover, the team should be capable of working in close collaboration with development teams, understanding their pain points, and continuously refining the platform to boost efficiency.
Ensuring security and compliance
: A critical part of being fit-for-purpose in platform engineering is having a team that is knowledgeable and capable of implementing security and compliance measures. This could mean having individuals with specific skills and expertise in cybersecurity, data privacy regulations, and industry-specific compliance requirements. Furthermore, the team should have a solid understanding of how to incorporate these security measures into the platform without compromising on the developer experience.
Promoting best practices and standards
: Lastly, a fit-for-purpose platform engineering team should be capable of promoting best practices and standards. The team should include members who stay abreast of the latest trends and standards in the industry, can advocate for their adoption within the platform, and provide guidance and support to other teams in following these standards.
In designing an organization around these four pillars, it becomes crucial to ensure that the team structure, skillset, and the objectives align with these pillars. For example, if developer experience is a significant pillar, the team might need to include roles focused on user experience or developer advocacy. If ensuring security and compliance is critical, roles dedicated to cybersecurity and compliance may be needed.In order to determine Fit-for-Purpose for an organization, we can leverage the Fit-for-Purpose model, as described in
Better Business Better Future
by Elisabet Lagerstedt as shown in
Figure 7.4
:
Figure 7.3 - Fit-for-Purpose model by Elisabet Lagerstedt
WHY
: The reason for the existence of the platform engineering team is to enable the wider organization to deliver high-quality products more efficiently and securely. This is achieved by developing an abstracted platform that simplifies the complexities of infrastructure, reduces repetitive tasks, and provides a reliable and secure environment for application deployment and management.
WHAT
: The platform engineering team creates an
Internal Developer Platform
(
IDP
). This platform abstracts underlying infrastructure complexities, provides tooling and services to automate deployment processes, enforces security and compliance standards, and enhances the overall developer experience by providing a self-service interface for provisioning resources and managing applications.
HOW
: The team builds the IDP using cloud-native and DevOps best practices, coupled with state-of-the-art technologies. They focus on delivering reliable, scalable, and secure services with automation at their core. The team collaborates with other engineering teams to understand their needs, gather feedback, and continuously improve the platform based on this feedback.
WHO
: The primary users of the platform built by the platform engineering team are the internal development teams within the organization. By providing them with a platform that automates much of their routine tasks and enforces best practices, the platform engineering team empowers them to focus more on creating innovative, high-quality software and less on the intricacies of deployment and management. Additionally, by fostering a culture of collaboration and continuous improvement, the platform engineering team indirectly serves the wider organization, stakeholders, and ultimately, the end-users of the software.
In essence, the platform engineering team serves a pivotal role in modern organizations. By defining the
WHY
,
WHAT
,
HOW
, and
WHO
, the purpose of the team becomes clear, and a structure can be designed around these elements to ensure a fit-for-purpose team. This clear understanding of purpose and alignment allows the team to effectively support the wider organization, leading to overall improved efficiency and productivity.
Team topologies
The book "Team topologies" was written in 2019 by Manuel Pais and Matthew Skelton and is probably one of the most impactful writings in understanding Platform teams, why they exist, and how they interact with other teams in an organization. Team topologies examines the arrangement or pattern of teams in an organization, and it analyzes how work is carried out, and how teams communicate and collaborate.The term team topologies provides a well-structured approach to team organization within IT delivery. In their book, Pais and Skelton, propose four fundamental types of teams in software delivery:
Figure 7.4 – Team topologies – the four team types and three interaction models
Stream-aligned teams
: These teams are aligned to a flow of work from a segment of the business domain, usually dedicated to a specific product, service, or feature set. Their goal is to understand and deliver value to the end-user or customer without being obstructed by other teams. They are cross-functional, containing all the necessary skills to achieve their objectives.
Platform teams
: These teams provide a self-service API to other teams. The goal of a platform team is to enable stream-aligned teams to deliver work without needing to understand the underlying complexities. In our context, the platform engineering team would fall into this category.
Enabling teams
: These teams help other teams build up their capabilities and overcome obstacles. They usually have a wide range of skills and act as force multipliers, helping stream-aligned teams to acquire new skills and improve existing ones.
Complicated subsystem teams
: These teams take care of parts of the system that are so intricate or require such specialized knowledge that they can't be effectively handled by a regular stream-aligned team. They are relatively rare in comparison to the other types of teams.
Each of these team topologies plays a unique role, and understanding their individual strengths and purposes is the first step towards organizing a more effective software delivery setup. However, it's important to note that there is no one-size-fits-all answer when it comes to team topology. The most effective setup will depend on the unique requirements, culture, and goals of your organization.Determining the best topology for a platform engineering team involves careful analysis of the organization's needs, culture, and existing technical landscape. The first step is understanding the organization's strategy and objectives. This allows the platform engineering team's purpose and goals to be clearly aligned with the organization's vision.For a platform engineering team, the
platform team
topology often makes a lot of sense. As we've previously discussed, a platform team is characterized by providing a self-service API to other teams. This maps well onto the platform engineering team's mission of enabling faster, more reliable delivery through the provision of an internal developer platform. The team essentially builds and maintains a foundation upon which other teams can work more effectively.However, the dynamic nature of platform engineering often requires it to also fulfill roles typically associated with the
Enabling team
and
Complicated Subsystem team
topologies. As an
Enabling team
, the platform engineering team often acts as a force multiplier, enabling other teams to leverage the platform's capabilities, and guiding them on how to use the platform effectively. They may also provide training or assistance in using new platform features or tools.On the other hand, as a
Complicated Subsystem team
, a platform engineering team may handle complex parts of the infrastructure that are critical to the platform's functionality. These parts are often highly specialized (such as HPC infrastructure for scientific computing, or GPU infrastructure for AI model training) and require specific skills and expertise. By centralizing these skills in the platform engineering team, it can streamline the handling of such complex subsystems.The choice of team topology has a significant impact on communication, workflow, and overall performance. A well-structured team can facilitate clear communication, streamline workflows, and improve the overall productivity and effectiveness of the team. For instance, a platform team can enable more efficient workflows by providing self-service capabilities to other teams, reducing bottlenecks and improving the speed of delivery.It's worth noting that the different roles a platform engineering team can play aren't mutually exclusive. In fact, effective platform engineering teams often blend aspects of the
Platform team
,
Enabling team
, and
Complicated Subsystem team
topologies, shifting their focus as needed to best support their organization's goals. This flexibility allows the platform engineering team to provide maximum value and adapt as the organization evolves.
Platform team placement
Integrating a platform engineering team within the broader organizational structure requires thoughtful strategizing and consideration. The positioning of the platform engineering team can directly impact its effectiveness, influencing factors such as communication, decision-making speed, and resource allocation. Understanding the organization's needs and taking into account its structure, culture, and strategy is crucial in placing the platform team appropriately. In terms of an organization's structure, we should first consider the architectural changes we want to achieve (XXXXX) in conjunction with Conway's law.
Figure 7.5 – Moving from a DevOps Organization to a Platform
With that in mind, the platform engineering team typically finds itself nestled between the infrastructure team and the various product or service teams. This central placement underscores its role as a facilitator and enabler. The platform engineering team needs to maintain close collaboration with both the infrastructure team (to understand the constraints and opportunities of the underlying infrastructure) and the product or service teams (to understand their needs and ensure the platform is serving them effectively).However, positioning the platform team effectively requires more than just understanding the organizational layers. Factors like communication channels, the organization's decision-making processes, and the way resources are allocated can all have a significant impact.To ensure effective communication, the platform team needs to be placed where it can maintain open, regular dialogues with both the teams it serves and the teams it relies on. In terms of decision-making, positioning the platform team close to the locus of power can help to ensure that its needs and contributions are considered when strategic decisions are made.In terms of resource allocation, positioning the platform team such that it is seen as a vital part of the organization can help ensure it gets the resources it needs to perform its function effectively. In this context, the platform team should not be considered as a cost center but as an investment which can significantly enhance the productivity and effectiveness of the entire organization.The effectiveness of a platform engineering team is often gauged by how well it serves the needs of the different teams that use the platform. However, meeting the needs of diverse users within the organization is not without its challenges. Different teams often have varying requirements and priorities, which may even conflict with each other. It is here that the placement of the platform team plays a crucial role in balancing these needs and ensuring optimum service delivery.One primary challenge faced by the platform team is to provide a uniform and seamless platform experience to all user teams, irrespective of their different demands and functional complexities. This involves accommodating the diverse technical requirements of the various teams, often while working within tight resource constraints. The goal here is to empower each team with the tools and services they need without overwhelming them with unnecessary complexity.To accomplish this, the platform team needs to maintain a holistic understanding of the organization's workings. They must know how each team functions, their technology stacks, their workflows, and their specific pain points. This is easier to achieve when the platform team is centrally placed within the organization, allowing for more efficient information exchange, better visibility into team operations, and quicker resolution of issues.Another major aspect that the platform team needs to address is fostering collaboration and promoting best practices across the organization. The platform can serve as a common ground where teams converge, share their learnings, and adopt standard protocols. This not only promotes consistency across the organization but also cultivates a culture of shared learning and continual improvement. A centrally located platform team can effectively drive this, leading by example and influencing other teams through their operations.However, it is important to note that the role of the platform team is not to enforce their decisions on other teams. Rather, they should act as enablers, providing the necessary tools, guidance, and support that empower teams to perform at their best. This involves constant communication with the different teams, understanding their changing needs, and adapting the platform accordingly. This, in turn, requires the platform team to be proactive, flexible, and customer oriented.The placement of the platform team within an organization is a strategic choice that can significantly influence its effectiveness. By being centrally located, the platform team can better understand the diverse needs of its user teams, promote collaboration, and provide the necessary support and tools that help other teams succeed. Thus, a well-placed platform team can not only balance the needs of different teams but also foster an environment of innovation, efficiency, and continual growth.
Defining the platform team API
In the realm of platform engineering, one of the key considerations for ensuring smooth functioning and productive collaboration is the definition of the platform team API. This essentially represents the formalized interaction and collaboration model of the platform team with other teams in the organization. In this section, we delve deeper into defining the type of the team and its areas of ownership.The platform team is generally typified as a cross-functional team, combining a broad range of technical skills and domain knowledge. It usually comprises individuals with diverse backgrounds in software engineering, systems administration, DevOps, security, and more. This blend of varied expertise facilitates the handling of a wide array of tasks related to the building, management, and improvement of the platform.Being cross-functional, the platform team has a strategic role, acting as a bridge between infrastructure and the various product or service teams in the organization. They are the facilitators, the enablers, and sometimes the mediators, smoothing out processes, resolving technical difficulties, and making sure that the different parts of the organization can work in harmony.Defining the areas of ownership for the platform team is crucial for setting clear expectations and responsibilities. It not only delineates what the team is accountable for, but it also spells out what falls outside its purview. The ownership areas of the platform team typically encompass several key aspects of the organization's tech stack:
Platform infrastructure
: This includes the design, implementation, and management of the foundational elements of the platform, such as servers, storage, networking, and the technology stack.
Tooling and automation
: The platform team is responsible for developing and maintaining the tooling and automation that support continuous integration, continuous delivery, and other aspects of the development lifecycle. This could include everything from configuration management tools to monitoring and alerting systems.
Security and compliance
: Given the vital importance of security and compliance in modern software delivery, the platform team plays a crucial role in implementing security practices and ensuring the platform adheres to the necessary compliance standards.
Standards and best practices
: The platform team often serves as the custodian of standards and best practices. They help set coding standards, architectural patterns, and workflow practices that promote consistency, reliability, and efficiency across the organization.
Developer experience
: In addition to infrastructure and tooling, the platform team is also tasked with optimizing the developer experience. They work to minimize friction in the development process, streamline workflows, and provide the support and resources that developers need to be productive.
The platform team can be seen as a fulcrum on which the organization's tech strategy balances. By defining the team type and areas of ownership, the organization can ensure that the platform team is well-equipped and empowered to fulfill its role effectively. It's a strategic decision that involves careful consideration of the organization's goals, processes, and culture. The aim is to foster an environment where the platform team can serve as an effective catalyst, driving innovation, productivity, and quality across the software delivery lifecycle.
Defining interactions and collaboration with other teams
Defining the way of interaction and collaboration between the platform engineering team and other teams in the organization is critical to facilitating productive relationships and maintaining effective workflows. This communication pattern, essentially the platform team API, sets the stage for how other teams can request support, contribute to the platform, and interact with the platform services.To understand this better, let's break down the key aspects of the platform team's interaction and collaboration model:
Channels of communication
: Establishing clear communication channels is fundamental. Whether it's Slack channels, JIRA boards, or weekly meetings, deciding where and how teams communicate reduces ambiguity and ensures that everyone knows how to get the information they need. The channels should be open, encouraging questions, ideas, and feedback. They should also support asynchronous communication to respect different working styles and time zones in distributed teams.
Request and response protocol
: Defining how teams can request support or resources from the platform team is crucial. This might be in the form of a ticketing system, a service catalog, or even a self-service portal. The goal is to make it easy for teams to express their needs and for the platform team to respond effectively. It is also important to set expectations regarding response times and prioritization to manage teams' expectations.
Contribution guidelines
: If your organization encourages contributions to the platform from other teams - a practice known as inner sourcing - it's essential to provide clear guidelines. This can include coding standards, testing requirements, documentation practices, and a clear process for submitting and reviewing contributions.
Change management
: The platform team should clearly communicate how changes to the platform are managed. This includes how updates are rolled out, how teams are informed about changes, and how potential disruptions are minimized. It might also involve defining processes for teams to request changes or new features.
Incident management
: When things go wrong, knowing how to report issues and who to contact can save valuable time. The platform team should provide clear guidelines for incident reporting, as well as expectations for incident response times.
Feedback mechanism
: Lastly, it's crucial to create mechanisms for continuous feedback. Regular check-ins, satisfaction surveys, and open channels for suggestions can help the platform team gauge how well they are meeting the needs of other teams, and where they can improve.
By defining these aspects of the platform team API, organizations can ensure smoother interactions, reduce friction, and foster a collaborative environment. However, it's important to remember that these are not set in stone. They should be reviewed and refined over time, in response to changes in the organization and feedback from teams
Other teams contributing to platform tooling
In many organizations, it's not uncommon for the platform engineering team to be the primary builders and caretakers of the internal platform tooling. However, there's an alternative approach that can be extremely beneficial: encouraging contributions from other teams. This approach, often referred to as
innersourcing
, is akin to an open-source model, but within the boundaries of a single organization. Here, the platform team acts more as a maintainer, overseeing the development and evolution of tools while welcoming contributions from any corner of the organization.Inner sourcing enables a diversity of thought and experience that can enrich the platform tooling in unique ways. Developers working on varied projects have a unique perspective on what tools and features can enhance their work. They can introduce fresh ideas or propose adjustments that the platform team might not have considered.Inner sourcing involves multiple different roles interacting as would in any open-source project. Each role has a different level of responsibility and contribution, as can be seen in
figure 7.6.
Figure 7.6 – Roles and Responsibilities of Inner Sourcing
Here are the key considerations for successfully implementing an inner sourcing approach to platform tooling:
Clear contribution guidelines
: It's vital to establish and communicate clear contribution guidelines. These should outline coding standards, testing protocols, and the process for submitting and reviewing contributions. This helps ensure consistency and quality in the additions made to the platform tooling.
Open communication channels
: Foster a culture of open dialogue around the platform's evolution. Encourage teams to propose ideas, discuss potential features, and share their experiences using the platform tools. This can be facilitated through regular meetings, online forums, or dedicated communication channels.
Mentorship and support
: For many developers, contributing to platform tooling might be a new experience. The platform team should be ready to offer support and mentorship, helping contributors understand the architecture, design principles, and best practices of the platform tooling.
Recognition and incentives
: Recognizing and rewarding contributions can go a long way in encouraging participation. This can be done through public acknowledgment, gamification elements like badges, or tangible rewards.
Effective documentation
: Comprehensive, up-to-date documentation is vital in supporting contributors. Good documentation can help developers understand the platform's existing tools and features, make it easier for them to get started with their contributions, and maintain consistency as the platform evolves.
CI/CD practices
: Implementing robust CI/CD practices helps ensure that contributions can be tested and integrated promptly, reducing the friction and delays in incorporating new changes.
By embracing an inner sourcing approach to platform services, you can tap into the collective intelligence of your entire organization, driving innovation and improving your platform in ways that better serve all its users. This, however, requires careful planning and a commitment to nurturing a culture of openness, collaboration, and shared ownership.
Platform onboarding, support, documentation, outreach, and enablement
The platform engineering team's work doesn't end once they've developed and deployed the internal platform tooling; in fact, one could argue that's when some of the most crucial work begins. Onboarding, support, documentation, outreach, and enablement are all critical aspects of ensuring the platform is effectively adopted and leveraged across the organization. Platform onboarding is the process of introducing and familiarizing new users with the platform's capabilities, usage, and best practices. Effective onboarding involves:
Thorough introduction to the platform's functionalities, purpose, and benefits.
Clear instructions on how to access and use the platform.
Guidance on the platform's best practices and common use-cases.
Opportunities to practice and apply learned skills, ideally through hands-on exercises or simulations.
Offering robust support is key to fostering user trust and ensuring smooth platform operation. This involves:
Providing a clear channel for users to report issues or ask for help.
Delivering timely, effective responses to support requests.
Proactively monitoring the platform for potential issues and resolving them promptly.
Regularly reviewing support cases to identify and address common problems or areas for improvement.
Comprehensive, accurate, and easy-to-understand documentation is a cornerstone of successful platform usage. High-quality documentation includes:
User guides that outline how to use the platform's tools and features.
API references for developers who want to interface with the platform programmatically.
Troubleshooting guides to help users resolve common issues independently.
Regular updates to ensure all documentation remains current as the platform evolves.
Effective outreach ensures that all potential platform users are aware of the tooling available to them, understand its benefits, and feel encouraged to use it. This can involve:
Regular presentations or demos showcasing the platform's capabilities, such as Sprint demos in Scrum or System demos in SAFe
Newsletters or update emails detailing new features or improvements.
Testimonials or case studies illustrating successful platform use.
Forums or community spaces where users can share their experiences, tips, and tricks.
Finally, enablement involves empowering users to leverage the platform to its fullest extent. This requires:
Training sessions or workshops to deepen users' platform skills.
Resources for self-guided learning and exploration.
Opportunities for users to contribute their ideas or enhancements to the platform (as discussed in the innersourcing section).
Encouragement of a culture of innovation and experimentation, allowing users to explore new ways of using the platform to solve their challenges.
All these components, working in harmony, ensure that the platform serves its purpose effectively, acting as an enabler of productivity, innovation, and growth across the organization. Remember, building the platform is just the beginning; nurturing its adoption and evolution is where the real magic happens.
The role and background of a platform engineer
The role of a platform engineer is critical in shaping and driving the evolution of the platform that supports and empowers the wider technology organization. They play a pivotal role in designing, developing, and maintaining the underlying software infrastructure, providing the vital capabilities, and tooling required to expedite product development, improve operational efficiency, and maintain security and compliance.Unlike more traditional engineering roles, a platform engineer isn't solely dedicated to building automation and tools for a specific product or service. Instead, they are responsible for creating a product of their own: the internal developer platform. This responsibility calls for a set of skills and an approach that leans more towards software engineering than traditional DevOps.Platform engineers are stewards of the platform's architecture, ensuring it is robust, scalable, and resilient. They're responsible for creating and optimizing development tools and processes, enabling quick and efficient deployment of services. They maintain the platform's health and availability, ensuring it provides a reliable foundation upon which the other teams build and run their services. Moreover, they also ensure that the platform adheres to the highest security standards and complies with relevant regulations.Given the scope and nature of their work, platform engineers require a broad range of technical skills and knowledge. Deep understanding of cloud technologies and services, distributed systems, and microservices architecture are paramount. They should be adept in various programming languages, particularly those used within their specific organization.Knowledge of automation and CI/CD principles is vital as they automate routine tasks and streamline the software delivery process. Familiarity with
Infrastructure as Code
(
IaC
) tools, containerization technologies, and orchestration systems like Kubernetes is also essential.Beyond these technical skills, platform engineers also need to understand the broader business context, have strong problem-solving capabilities, and excellent communication skills to interact effectively with various stakeholders and understand their needs.The career path for a platform engineer can be diverse and rewarding. From an entry-level platform engineer, one could progress to a senior engineer, leading to roles like platform architect or principal platform engineer. Some may choose to specialize further in a specific area such as security or database architecture. Others might move into leadership roles, heading platform engineering teams or even entire technology departments.While there may be similarities, the role of a platform engineer is distinct from other roles in the tech industry. Unlike application developers, who focus on designing, writing, and modifying software geared towards specific end-user applications, platform engineers work on the underlying platform that supports these applications. While they share common skills with DevOps Engineers, their role is broader, focusing on building an entire platform rather than automating specific operations. Their work impacts all the developers in an organization, unlike other roles which might focus on individual projects or products.The role of a platform engineer is not only complex but also diverse, welcoming individuals from a broad range of backgrounds. This is primarily due to the multifaceted nature of platform engineering, which incorporates elements from various domains within the technology sector. From software development and operations to security and system administration, the role benefits from diverse experiences and skills that these backgrounds bring.Platform engineers come from a variety of backgrounds, and this diversity serves to enhance and enrich the field. Many have roots in software development, bringing with them strong programming skills and an in-depth understanding of software design principles. Others might come from a more operations-focused background, providing them with a profound knowledge of system administration, cloud management, and automation techniques.Each of these diverse backgrounds contributes valuable perspectives and skills to the role of a platform engineer. Software developers, for instance, lend their expertise to build robust, efficient, and maintainable tools and systems. Operations professionals bring their understanding of system stability, automation, and efficiency, crucial for ensuring the platform's reliability and high performance.Security professionals help in maintaining and improving the platform's security posture, a critical aspect given the importance of data protection and regulatory compliance in today's digital world. Those from a database or network background contribute their specialized knowledge to optimize data management and connectivity aspects of the platform.In platform engineering, having a multi-disciplinary background is highly beneficial. It allows engineers to approach problems from different perspectives, encouraging innovative solutions. Such diversity fosters a more comprehensive understanding of the system as a whole, enabling engineers to design and implement more holistic and effective solutions.A multi-disciplinary background also promotes better collaboration within the team and with other teams. It enables engineers to speak the language of developers, operations, security, and other stakeholders, facilitating more effective communication and cooperation.
Soft skills in platform engineering
The field of platform engineering, while technically demanding, also requires a wide range of soft skills. These are the interpersonal attributes that facilitate effective communication, foster strong relationships, and enable individuals to navigate the complexities of their work environments. Key among these are skills like communication, leadership, teamwork, and problem-solving. These skills are critical not only for individual success, but for the success of the team and the organization as a whole.While the technical aspects of platform engineering may be its most visible facet, the soft skills are the underlying backbone that facilitates effective work. A platform engineer frequently interacts with various stakeholders, including software developers, operations teams, managers, and sometimes even clients. Effective communication is essential to understanding needs, articulating complex ideas, and ensuring alignment across the team.Leadership, while often associated with managerial roles, is also essential for platform engineers. It's about making decisions under pressure, driving change, and influencing others - including those not directly under one's authority.Teamwork is another crucial soft skill. platform engineers often work in multidisciplinary teams and need to collaborate with professionals from a range of backgrounds. Understanding how to leverage the strengths of others, share knowledge, and work towards common goals is vital.Problem-solving, a skill often associated with technical tasks, also has a strong interpersonal element. It involves negotiation, understanding different perspectives, and finding creative solutions to challenges that are acceptable to all stakeholders.Like any skills, soft skills can be developed and improved over time. For example, communication skills can be enhanced by practicing public speaking, active listening, and clear writing. Leadership can be improved by taking on new responsibilities, seeking feedback, and learning from mentors. Teamwork can be honed by working on group projects and developing empathy and understanding of others' perspectives. Problem-solving can be developed through activities that require creative thinking, negotiation, and conflict resolution.While technical skills are the cornerstone of platform engineering, soft skills are the mortar that holds everything together. They enable platform engineers to effectively use their technical skills, ensuring they can explain their work, collaborate with others, and make meaningful contributions to their organization's goals. Without soft skills, even the most technically proficient engineer may struggle to make an impact.
Communication in platform engineering
The importance of effective communication in platform engineering cannot be overstated. The nature of the role necessitates interaction with various stakeholders including developers, operations, security teams, and leadership. platform engineers not only need to understand the needs of these different stakeholders but also be able to convey complex technical concepts in a manner that's accessible to each audience.Platform engineering functions as the bridge between different technical teams, and the conduit of interaction between technology and business. As such, communication is integral to the role. Effective communication ensures accurate understanding of needs, alignment of goals, timely resolution of issues, and fosters trust and collaboration.For instance, a platform engineer explaining the benefits of a proposed automation tool to a non-technical stakeholder will need to distil complex concepts into understandable terms. They must also be able to actively listen and understand the needs of different stakeholders, as well as articulate their own requirements and constraints. Effective communication can save time, reduce misunderstandings, and contribute significantly to the successful delivery of projects. Best practices for communication include:
Clarity and conciseness
: Be clear and concise in your communication. Avoid jargon and acronyms where possible, especially when communicating with non-technical stakeholders.
Active listening
: Effective communication is a two-way process. Listen actively to others' inputs and feedback and show understanding by summarizing key points back to them.
Open and regular communication
: Encourage a culture of open and regular communication within the team. Regular status updates, meetings, and one-on-ones can ensure everyone is aligned and informed.
Use of tools
: Utilize communication tools effectively. This might include instant messaging for quick updates, video conferencing for team meetings, and project management tools for tracking progress.
Visual aids
: Where possible, use diagrams, charts, or other visual aids to supplement verbal or written communication. They can be extremely effective in conveying complex technical concepts.
Challenges in communication can arise from a number of factors, such as cultural or language barriers, remote work, and complex technical jargon. However, these challenges can be mitigated.For language or cultural barriers, it's important to promote a culture of patience, respect, and inclusivity. Utilise tools to facilitate clear communication, and encourage team members to seek clarification when needed.In the case of remote work, ensure that communication channels are open, clear, and used consistently.
