Understanding Outputs, Outcomes, Impact
Measuring success in platform engineering efforts is not straightforward. It requires a nuanced understanding of various metrics and their implications. The process begins with differentiating between outputs, outcomes, and impacts—terms often used interchangeably but which hold distinct meanings and significance.A critical aspect of understanding the success of platform engineering is debunking common myths surrounding developer productivity. These myths often mislead organizations and skew their focus away from meaningful metrics.One prevalent myth is that productivity is solely about developer activity. This misconception equates busyness with productivity, ignoring the quality and impact of the work being done. Measuring the number of lines of code written or the number of tasks completed does not necessarily correlate with meaningful progress or value creation.Another myth is that productivity is only about individual performance. In reality, productivity is a collective outcome, heavily influenced by team dynamics, collaboration, and the efficiency of tools and processes in place. Overemphasizing individual metrics can create a competitive rather than collaborative environment, undermining overall team performance.A third myth is that one productivity metric can tell us everything. Relying on a single metric, such as velocity or cycle time, provides a limited view of productivity. Effective measurement requires a combination of metrics that capture different dimensions of productivity, from code quality to delivery speed and user satisfaction.Additionally, there is a belief that productivity measures are useful only for managers. This perspective neglects the value these measures bring to developers themselves. By understanding productivity metrics, developers can identify areas for improvement, streamline their workflows, and achieve greater satisfaction in their work.Lastly, there is the misconception that productivity is only about engineering systems and developer tools. While these elements are crucial, productivity also hinges on organizational culture, communication, and the alignment of technical and business goals.To effectively measure the success of platform engineering, it's essential to understand the progression from output to outcome to impact. This concept, articulated in various management theories, underscores the importance of looking beyond immediate results to long-term value.
Outputs
refer to the direct results of activities, such as the number of features developed or bugs fixed. These are tangible, measurable deliverables produced by the development team. While outputs are necessary for tracking progress, they do not alone indicate whether the work is creating value.
Outcomes
are the changes or benefits resulting from these outputs. They reflect how well the outputs are solving the problems they were intended to address. For example, an outcome might be improved system performance or increased user engagement. Outcomes provide a better measure of effectiveness than outputs, as they consider the real-world application and benefits of the work done.
Impact
goes a step further, evaluating the broader, long-term effects of these outcomes on the business or community. It encompasses the overall value delivered, such as increased revenue, customer satisfaction, or market share. Measuring impact involves assessing how the outcomes contribute to the organization's strategic goals and mission.
Figure 12.1: The relationship between output, outcome, and impact
Understanding this progression helps teams focus on activities that deliver meaningful value. For instance, while it is useful to track the number of deployments (output), it is more valuable to measure the stability and performance of those deployments (outcome) and how they improve customer satisfaction and business metrics (impact).As platform engineering matures, the focus of measurement shifts from outputs to outcomes and ultimately to impact. At the initial stages, measuring success might involve tracking the number of services migrated to the platform or the adoption rate of platform tools (outputs).As the platform matures, the focus should shift to outcomes, such as reducing the time to market for new features, improving deployment frequency, and enhancing system reliability. Ultimately, the goal is to measure the impact, such as increased business agility, higher user satisfaction, and tangible business growth.There is no one-size-fits-all metric or framework for measuring platform engineering success. Each organization's context, goals, and challenges are unique, requiring a tailored approach.However, there are several widely recognized frameworks that provide valuable guidance.
DORA Metrics
, as discussed earlier, focus on software delivery performance, emphasizing metrics such as deployment frequency, lead time for changes, mean time to recovery, and change failure rate. These metrics are useful for assessing the efficiency and reliability of the development and deployment processes.
SPACE Framework
combines leading and lagging indicators to provide a comprehensive view of developer productivity and satisfaction. It includes metrics across five dimensions: Satisfaction and well-being, Performance, Activity, Communication and collaboration, and Efficiency and flow.
DevEx Metrics
focus on developer experience, measuring aspects such as tool usability, workflow efficiency, and the overall satisfaction of developers. These metrics highlight areas that directly impact the daily experiences and productivity of developers.
We've covered DORA and SPACE metrics in previous chapters, as those have been used in various DevOps implementations in the past. DevEx is aa more recently introduced framework, created by the creators of SPACE, let's explore this further.
Understanding DevEx metrics
Developer Experience
(
DevEx
) metrics are crucial for assessing how the platform impacts the day-to-day work of developers. These metrics go beyond traditional productivity measures to capture the overall satisfaction, efficiency, and effectiveness of developers as they interact with the platform. Here are some key areas to consider:
Usability and Tooling
The ease of use and effectiveness of tools provided by the platform are fundamental to a positive developer experience. Metrics in this area might include:
Tool Usability
: Assessing how intuitive and easy-to-use the tools are. This can be measured through developer feedback surveys, usability testing, and tool adoption rates.
Integration Quality
: Evaluating how well different tools and services integrate within the platform. Poor integration can lead to friction and inefficiencies, whereas seamless integration enhances productivity.
Onboarding Time
: Measuring the time it takes for new developers to become productive on the platform. Shorter onboarding times indicate better-designed tools and documentation.
Workflow Efficiency
Effective workflows are essential for maintaining high developer morale and productivity. Metrics for workflow efficiency include:
Cycle Time
: The total time it takes for a task to move from start to finish. This includes coding, testing, and deployment phases.
Lead Time
: The time from when a feature or fix is requested to when it is delivered to production. Short lead times indicate efficient workflows and processes.
Context Switching
: Assessing the frequency and impact of context switching on developers. Frequent interruptions can significantly reduce productivity and satisfaction.
Developer Satisfaction and Well-being
Developer satisfaction is a critical indicator of a healthy platform environment. Metrics in this area include:
Net Promoter Score
(
NPS
): Asking developers how likely they are to recommend the platform to others. A high NPS indicates strong satisfaction and advocacy.
Feedback Scores
: Regularly collecting feedback on specific aspects of the platform, such as tooling, documentation, and support.
Burnout Indicators
: Monitoring signs of burnout, such as extended hours, frequent overtime, and high stress levels. Addressing these issues promptly is crucial for maintaining a healthy work environment.
Collaboration and Communication
Effective collaboration and communication are key to successful platform engineering. Metrics here include:
Collaboration Tools Usage
: Tracking the usage and effectiveness of collaboration tools, such as version control systems, chat applications, and project management software.
Communication Patterns
: Analyzing communication patterns to identify bottlenecks or areas where information flow can be improved. This can involve monitoring meeting frequencies, email volumes, and other communication channels.
Cross-team Interactions
: Measuring the quality and frequency of interactions between different teams. Strong cross-team collaboration often leads to better integration and more innovative solutions.
Measuring and Improving DevEx Metrics
To effectively measure and improve DevEx metrics, consider the following steps:
Establish Baselines
: Before making changes, understand the current state by establishing baseline metrics. This provides a point of comparison for future improvements.
Regular Feedback Loops
: Implement regular feedback loops to gather continuous input from developers. This can be done through surveys, focus groups, and informal check-ins.
Iterative Improvements
: Use the feedback to make iterative improvements to the platform. Small, incremental changes can lead to significant long-term benefits.
Transparent Communication
: Maintain transparency about the metrics being tracked and the improvements being made. Keeping developers informed fosters trust and encourages engagement.
Benchmarking
: Compare your metrics with industry benchmarks to understand how your platform stacks up against others. This can provide valuable insights and identify areas for improvement.
By focusing on these DevEx metrics, organizations can create a more developer-friendly environment that not only enhances productivity but also boosts morale and job satisfaction. This holistic approach to measuring and improving developer experience is essential for the long-term success and sustainability of platform engineering initiatives.
Understanding and measuring Flow
Flow, a concept rooted in lean and agile methodologies, refers to the smooth and uninterrupted progression of work through a system. In platform engineering, flow signifies the seamless movement of tasks from ideation to deployment, ensuring that work progresses efficiently and without unnecessary delays. The goal is to minimize waste and maximize value delivery, enhancing both developer productivity and the overall performance of the platform.Optimizing flow is crucial for several reasons:
Increased Efficiency
: By reducing bottlenecks and delays, teams can deliver features and fixes more quickly. This leads to faster time-to-market and a more responsive development process.
Higher Quality
: Smooth flow often results in fewer errors and rework, as work is completed more systematically and predictably. This contributes to higher-quality outputs and more reliable software.
Enhanced Developer Experience
: Developers are less likely to experience frustration and burnout when they can work in a well-optimized environment. Improved flow means fewer interruptions, smoother handoffs, and a more satisfying work experience.
Greater Predictability
: With optimized flow, it becomes easier to predict delivery timelines and manage expectations. This predictability is essential for planning and aligning with business goals.
Several metrics help assess and improve flow within platform engineering:
Cycle Time
: This measures the total time taken for a task to move from start to finish. A shorter cycle time indicates more efficient processes and quicker delivery.
Lead Time
: This metric tracks the time from when a feature or fix is requested to when it is delivered to production. It encompasses both development and deployment phases, providing a comprehensive view of efficiency.
Throughput
: The number of tasks completed in a given time frame. Higher throughput suggests better capacity and process efficiency.
Work in Progress
(
WIP
): The number of tasks being worked on at any given time. Managing WIP is crucial to prevent overloading the system and causing delays.
Strategies for Improving Flow
To enhance workflow efficiency and reduce delays, consider implementing the following proven strategies:
Value Stream Mapping
: This technique involves visualizing the entire workflow, identifying each step, and pinpointing areas of waste or delay. By mapping the value stream, teams can better understand the current state and design more efficient processes.
Limiting WIP
: Implementing WIP limits helps prevent bottlenecks and ensures that tasks are completed before new ones are started. This maintains a steady flow and avoids overloading team members.
Continuous Integration/Continuous Deployment (CI/CD)
: CI/CD practices automate and streamline the integration and deployment processes, reducing manual intervention and minimizing delays. This ensures that code changes are tested, integrated, and deployed rapidly and reliably.
Kanban Boards
: Using Kanban boards to visualize and manage workflow helps teams track progress, identify bottlenecks, and balance workloads. This visual management tool supports continuous improvement and flow optimization.
Feedback Loops
: Regular feedback loops, such as daily stand-ups and retrospectives, help teams identify issues early, adjust processes, and continuously improve flow. This iterative approach ensures that the team can adapt quickly to changing requirements and challenges.
While optimizing flow is beneficial, it comes with challenges:
Resistance to Change
: Teams may be resistant to adopting new practices and tools, especially if they are used to traditional methods.
Complexity of Workflows
: Complex workflows with multiple dependencies can be difficult to streamline and optimize.
Balancing Speed and Quality
: Striking the right balance between delivering quickly and maintaining high-quality standards can be challenging.
Continuous Monitoring and Adjustment
: Flow optimization is not a one-time effort but requires continuous monitoring, feedback, and adjustment.
Incorporating flow metrics and strategies into platform engineering practices helps create a more efficient, predictable, and satisfying development environment. By focusing on cycle time, lead time, throughput, and WIP, teams can identify areas for improvement and implement changes that enhance the overall performance and value delivery of the platform. Next, we will explore how to measure cloud-native maturity to further optimize these practices.
Measuring Cloud native maturity
The cloud native maturity model provides a framework for organizations to assess and enhance their cloud native capabilities. This model is crucial for understanding how well an organization is leveraging cloud technologies and practices to achieve its goals. It ties closely with the DevOps journey, as
Internal Developer Platforms
(
IDPs
) typically start becoming effective at the "scale" stage of cloud native maturity. Let's delve into each level of the model to understand its significance and the steps involved.
Level 1 - Build: Establishing a Baseline Cloud Native Implementation
At the "
Build
" level, organizations are in the early stages of their cloud native journey. This stage is characterized by the initial implementation of cloud native practices and technologies, primarily in pre-production environments.The key characteristics are:
Containerization
: Organizations begin containerizing their applications using tools like Docker. This provides a standardized way to package applications and their dependencies.
Basic Orchestration
: Introduction of orchestration tools like Kubernetes to manage containerized applications. However, usage is often basic and experimental.
CI/CD Pipelines
: Basic CI/CD pipelines are set up to automate the build and deployment processes. These pipelines are essential for streamlining workflows and ensuring consistency.
Learning and Experimentation
: This stage involves a lot of learning and experimentation. Teams are getting familiar with new tools, processes, and the cloud native mindset.
Here are some of the challenges:
Skills Gap
: Teams may lack the necessary skills and experience with cloud native technologies.
Cultural Shift
: Moving to cloud native practices requires a cultural shift, which can be challenging to initiate.
Initial Investment
: Setting up the initial infrastructure and tooling requires investment in time and resources.
At this level, organizations focus on critical goals:
Establish a Solid Foundation
: The primary goal at this stage is to establish a solid foundation for cloud native practices. This includes setting up the necessary tools and processes, and ensuring the team is adequately trained.
Minimize Technical Debt
: It's crucial to minimize technical debt by adhering to best practices from the beginning.
Level 2 - Operate: Moving to Production
Once the foundational elements are in place, organizations move to the "Operate" level. This stage involves transitioning from pre-production to production environments.Below follow some of the key characteristics of this level:
Production-Ready Infrastructure
: The cloud native infrastructure is now robust enough to support production workloads. This includes setting up monitoring, logging, and alerting systems to ensure the reliability and performance of applications.
Advanced Orchestration
: Organizations start leveraging more advanced features of orchestration tools like Kubernetes. This includes autoscaling, self-healing, and more sophisticated deployment strategies.
Security and Compliance
: Security practices are integrated into the CI/CD pipelines, and compliance requirements are addressed. This includes implementing
role-based access control
(
RBAC
), network policies, and secrets management.
Operational Visibility
: Improved visibility into the operational state of the applications and infrastructure through enhanced monitoring and logging.
At the Operate level, organizations encounter challenges such as:
Operational Complexity
: As the environment becomes more complex, managing it requires advanced skills and knowledge.
Security Concerns
: Ensuring security in a cloud native environment is challenging and requires continuous effort.
Cost Management
: Moving to production can significantly increase costs, necessitating effective cost management strategies.
The primary goals at this stage are to:
Ensure Reliability
: The primary goal is to ensure the reliability and performance of applications in production. This includes setting up comprehensive monitoring and alerting systems.
Enhance Security
: Integrate security practices into the development and deployment processes to protect against vulnerabilities and ensure compliance.
Level 3 - Scale: Defining Processes for Scale
At the "Scale" level, organizations have achieved a certain level of maturity and are now focused on scaling their operations efficiently. At this level we start seeing standardization and cloud costs are driven down both for legacy and cloud. This is where platform engineering start to make a big difference.
Figure 12.2: Cost comparison of cloud native vs legacy systems across maturity levels
Key Characteristics of the Scale level include:
Standardization
: Processes and practices are standardized across teams to ensure consistency and efficiency. This includes standardized CI/CD pipelines, monitoring setups, and deployment strategies.
Cost Optimization
: Organizations implement cost optimization strategies to manage cloud expenses effectively. This includes rightsizing resources, using spot instances, and leveraging cost management tools.
Enhanced Automation
: Automation is extended to more areas of the development and deployment processes. This includes automated testing, security scans, and infrastructure provisioning.
Resilience and Scalability
: Applications and infrastructure are designed for resilience and scalability. This includes implementing multi-region deployments, disaster recovery plans, and scalable architectures.
At the Scale level, organizations face challenges such as:
Managing Complexity
: As operations scale, managing the complexity of the environment becomes more challenging.
Coordination Across Teams
: Ensuring effective coordination and collaboration across multiple teams is crucial for scaling successfully.
Continuous Improvement
: Organizations need to continuously improve their processes and practices to maintain efficiency at scale.
The primary goals of this stage are to:
Achieve Efficiency at Scale
: The primary goal is to achieve efficiency at scale by standardizing processes, optimizing costs, and enhancing automation.
Ensure Resilience and Scalability
: Design applications and infrastructure to be resilient and scalable to handle increased workloads and ensure business continuity.
Level 4 - Improve: Enhancing Security, Policy, and Governance
At the "Improve" level, organizations focus on enhancing security, policy, and governance across their cloud native environment.The key characteristics of the Improve level are:
Advanced Security Practices
: Organizations implement advanced security practices, including threat detection, incident response, and continuous security monitoring.
Policy Enforcement
: Policies are enforced across the environment to ensure compliance with regulatory requirements and internal standards. This includes implementing policy-as-code tools like Open Policy Agent (OPA).
Governance Frameworks
: Governance frameworks are established to ensure that all teams adhere to best practices and organizational standards. This includes setting up governance boards and committees.
Continuous Compliance
: Compliance processes are automated and integrated into the CI/CD pipelines to ensure continuous compliance with regulatory requirements.
The challenges faced at this level are the following:
Balancing Security and Agility
: Balancing the need for security and compliance with the need for agility and speed can be challenging.
Maintaining Compliance
: Ensuring continuous compliance with evolving regulatory requirements requires continuous effort and monitoring.
Policy Management
: Managing and enforcing policies across a large and complex environment can be difficult.
The goals of the Improve level can be summarized as follows:
Enhance Security and Compliance
: The primary goal is to enhance security and ensure continuous compliance with regulatory requirements and internal standards.
Establish Governance
: Establish governance frameworks to ensure that all teams adhere to best practices and organizational standards.
Level 5 - Adapt: Revisiting Decisions and Optimizing
At the "Adapt" level, organizations focus on revisiting decisions made earlier and optimizing their applications and infrastructure for continuous improvement.The Adapt level encompasses the following key characteristics:
Continuous Monitoring and Optimization
: Organizations continuously monitor their applications and infrastructure for optimization opportunities. This includes performance tuning, cost optimization, and security improvements.
Feedback Loops
: Feedback loops are established to gather insights from developers, operations teams, and end-users. This feedback is used to drive continuous improvement.
Innovation and Experimentation
: Organizations encourage innovation and experimentation to find new ways to improve their cloud native practices. This includes adopting new technologies and practices.
Agile and Adaptive
: Organizations become agile and adaptive, capable of responding quickly to changes in the market, technology landscape, and business requirements.
Challenges at this level include:
Keeping Up with Change
: Keeping up with the rapid pace of change in the technology landscape requires continuous learning and adaptation.
Balancing Innovation and Stability
: Balancing the need for innovation with the need for stability and reliability can be challenging.
Managing Complexity
: As the environment becomes more complex, managing it effectively requires advanced skills and knowledge.
The goals of this level are:
Continuous Improvement
: The primary goal is to achieve continuous improvement by revisiting decisions made earlier and optimizing applications and infrastructure.
Encourage Innovation
: Encourage innovation and experimentation to find new ways to improve cloud native practices and drive business value.
Measuring cloud native maturity involves assessing the organization's capabilities across different levels of the maturity model. Each level has its own characteristics, challenges, and goals, and progressing through these levels requires continuous effort and improvement. By understanding and leveraging the cloud native maturity model, organizations can effectively assess their current state, identify areas for improvement, and drive their cloud native journey forward. To support this journey, it is essential to delve into the specific metrics that provide insights into platform performance and health.
Understanding platform metrics
Platform metrics provide insights into the performance, efficiency, and impact of the engineering efforts, enabling teams to make data-driven decisions. Understanding these metrics involves distinguishing between leading and lagging indicators, recognizing the limitations of certain metrics like DORA, and adopting frameworks that offer a comprehensive view, such as SPACE.
Leading indicators
are predictive measures that provide early signs of future performance. These indicators are proactive, helping teams identify potential issues and opportunities for improvement before they impact the end results. An example of a leading indicator can be:
Cycle Time
: The time taken for a task to move from start to finish. Shorter cycle times indicate more efficient processes. Improving Cycle time can be an early indication of improved development lifecycle.
Lagging indicators
, on the other hand, measure the outcomes of past actions. These indicators are reflective, showing the results of previous efforts and providing a historical perspective. In platform engineering, lagging indicators might include:
Mean Time to Recovery (MTTR)
: The average time taken to recover from a failure. Lower MTTR indicates more resilient systems.
Change Failure Rate
: The percentage of changes that result in failures. Lower failure rates indicate higher quality and stability.
Understanding the balance between leading and lagging indicators is crucial for effective performance measurement. While lagging indicators are easier to implement and understand, leading indicators help teams anticipate and prevent issues, driving continuous improvement.
Figure 12.3: Leading vs. Lagging indicators.
The
DevOps Research and Assessment
(
DORA
) metrics are widely used in the industry to measure software delivery performance. These metrics include deployment frequency, lead time for changes, change failure rate, and MTTR. However, there are some inherent challenges and limitations with DORA metrics, particularly because they are primarily lagging indicators.DORA metrics provide a retrospective view of performance, focusing on the outcomes of past actions. While this information is valuable, it does not offer predictive insights or help teams identify issues before they impact performance. To address these limitations, it is essential to complement DORA metrics with other frameworks that provide a more holistic view of platform performance.The SPACE framework combines both leading and lagging indicators, offering a balanced perspective on platform engineering performance.In platform engineering, measuring success requires a nuanced approach that goes beyond traditional metrics. By understanding the differences between leading and lagging indicators, recognizing the limitations of metrics like DORA, and adopting comprehensive frameworks like SPACE, teams can gain deeper insights into their performance and drive continuous improvement.
Going beyond DORA, SPACE, and DevEx
Traditional frameworks like DORA, SPACE, and DevEx provide valuable insights into various aspects of developer productivity and system performance. However, as the field evolves, it becomes crucial to look beyond these established metrics and consider additional
Key Performance Indicators
(
KPIs
) that offer a more comprehensive view of platform engineering success. This section explores advanced KPIs that can help engineering teams achieve a more nuanced understanding of their performance and impact.While DORA focuses on deployment frequency, lead time for changes, change failure rate, and mean time to recovery, and SPACE emphasizes satisfaction, performance, activity, communication and collaboration, and efficiency and flow, there are still aspects of platform engineering that these frameworks do not fully capture. Similarly, DevEx metrics provide insights into developer experience but may not encompass the entire spectrum of platform engineering activities. Therefore, it is essential to incorporate additional KPIs to paint a complete picture.
Advanced KPIs for Platform Engineering
These KPIs provide deeper insights into various dimensions of platform performance, such as cost efficiency, user engagement, incident management, and innovation.
1. Cost Efficiency
Cost efficiency measures how effectively the platform engineering team manages and optimizes the costs associated with running and maintaining the platform. This KPI is crucial for ensuring that the platform delivers value without unnecessary expenditure.
Cost per Deployment
: This metric tracks the average cost associated with each deployment. Lower costs per deployment indicate more efficient resource usage and cost management practices.
Infrastructure Utilization
: This measures the percentage of utilized resources (CPU, memory, storage) against the allocated resources. Higher utilization rates suggest better optimization and cost efficiency.
2. User Adoption and Engagement
User adoption and engagement metrics assess how well the platform is being adopted and utilized by the development teams. These metrics help gauge the platform's relevance and effectiveness in meeting user needs.
Adoption Rate
: This metric tracks the percentage of developers using the platform compared to the total number of developers. Higher adoption rates indicate greater acceptance and reliance on the platform.
Active Users
: This measures the number of active users interacting with the platform within a given period. Consistently high active user counts suggest ongoing engagement and value.
3. Incident Management and Resolution
Metrics related to incident management and resolution provide insights into the platform's reliability and the team's ability to respond to and resolve issues promptly.
Incident Frequency
: This tracks the number of incidents occurring within a specified period. Lower incident frequencies indicate a more stable and reliable platform.
Incident Resolution Time
: This measures the average time taken to resolve incidents. Faster resolution times reflect the team's efficiency in addressing and mitigating issues.
4. Innovation and Experimentation
Innovation and experimentation metrics assess the platform engineering team's ability to drive innovation and support experimentation within the organization.
Experimentation Rate
: This metric tracks the number of experiments or pilot projects initiated on the platform. Higher experimentation rates indicate a culture of innovation and continuous improvement.
Feature Adoption Rate
: This measures the percentage of newly introduced features that are adopted and used by the development teams. Higher adoption rates suggest that the platform effectively meets user needs and supports innovation.
Expanding the scope of platform engineering metrics beyond DORA, SPACE, and DevEx is essential for gaining a comprehensive understanding of performance and impact. By incorporating advanced KPIs such as cost efficiency, user adoption and engagement, incident management and resolution, and innovation and experimentation, platform engineering teams can drive continuous improvement, enhance user satisfaction, and deliver greater value to the organization.Implementing these KPIs involves defining clear objectives, setting baselines and targets, collecting and analyzing data, and regularly reporting progress. Ultimately, the goal is to create a robust and dynamic platform engineering environment that supports innovation, meets user needs, and drives organizational success. By leveraging a comprehensive set of KPIs, platform engineering teams can navigate the complexities of their roles, deliver impactful results, and continuously evolve to meet the challenges of the future.
Summary
In this chapter, we explored the essential aspects of measuring platform engineering success. We began by understanding the differences between outputs, outcomes, and impacts, and debunking common myths about developer productivity. This foundational knowledge is crucial as it shifts the focus from merely completing tasks to delivering business value.We delved into the Cloud Native Maturity Model, detailing the five levels of maturity—Build, Operate, Scale, Improve, and Adapt. This model helps organizations assess their cloud-native journey and align their platform engineering efforts accordingly. Each level provides a roadmap for growth, from establishing a baseline implementation to continuously optimizing and revisiting past decisions.Understanding platform metrics was another critical component. We discussed the importance of distinguishing between leading and lagging indicators, using SPACE to combine both types of metrics for a more comprehensive view. This approach ensures that we not only measure past performance but also anticipate future trends and needs.Implementing platform metrics involved practical steps to incorporate DORA and SPACE metrics into our monitoring systems. We emphasized creating a platform dashboard using tools like Grafana and integrating metrics in Backstage through plugins, providing real-time insights into platform performance and developer productivity.Finally, we looked beyond traditional metrics to advanced KPIs, including cost efficiency, user adoption and engagement, incident management, and innovation. These KPIs offer a broader perspective on platform engineering success, helping teams drive continuous improvement and deliver greater value to their organizations.In the next chapter, Chapter 13, we will explore real-world use cases, drawing lessons from various companies' platform engineering journeys. This will provide practical insights and strategies that can be applied to enhance your platform engineering efforts.

How companies are moving towards platform engineering
The Two Pizza Team Rule, coined by Jeff Bezos at
Amazon
, suggests that teams should be small enough to be fed by two pizzas. This principal advocates for smaller, more manageable teams to enhance collaboration, productivity, and agility. The core idea is that as team size increases, so does the communication overhead, leading to inefficiencies. By keeping teams small, Amazon aims to foster better alignment, quicker decision-making, and increased innovation.Despite its popularity, the Two Pizza Team Rule is often misunderstood and misapplied. Many organizations interpret it as a strict guideline for team size without considering the context and dynamics of their own environments. The focus should not be on the literal amount of pizza but on the underlying principles of team autonomy, responsibility, and communication. It’s crucial to understand that effective implementation requires more than just limiting team size; it involves empowering teams with the right tools, clear goals, and organizational support.In the realm of platform engineering, the Two Pizza Team Rule plays a significant role in structuring teams for maximum efficiency and innovation. Platform engineering teams, tasked with building and maintaining the foundational infrastructure and services that other teams rely on, benefit greatly from being small and agile. Additionally, when designing teams that are designed too be autonomous and move fast, empowering them with the right tools and infrastructure can play a critical role in their long term success.At Amazon, the Two Pizza Team Rule has been instrumental in driving innovation and maintaining agility despite the company’s vast size. By keeping teams small, Amazon ensures that each team is focused on specific goals and can move quickly to adapt to new challenges and opportunities. This approach has been particularly effective in their platform engineering efforts, where small, autonomous teams have developed robust, scalable services that power Amazon’s extensive ecosystem.
