Uber and Netflix – Building Platforms before Platform Engineering
Before platform engineering became a well-known term, companies like
Uber
and
Netflix
were already laying the groundwork with their innovative approaches to infrastructure management and developer productivity. Their pioneering efforts in building robust, scalable, and efficient systems have significantly influenced modern platform engineering practices. This section explores how Uber and Netflix’s strategies and technologies have shaped the evolution of platform engineering.
Uber
Uber
, known for its rapid scaling and complex operational needs, has been a forerunner in developing sophisticated engineering platforms. Their approach to platform engineering has focused on several key areas:
Microservices Architecture
: Uber adopted a microservices architecture early on to handle the high volume of transactions and diverse functionalities required by its platform. This approach allowed Uber to scale different parts of its system independently, improving fault tolerance and enabling rapid development cycles. The key aspects of this implementation include:
Service-Oriented Architecture
(
SOA
): By breaking down its monolithic application into smaller, manageable services, Uber could deploy, monitor, and update services without affecting the entire system. This modularity has been critical in maintaining system stability and performance during rapid growth phases.
Dynamic Configuration Management
: Uber implemented dynamic configuration management to ensure that services could be updated and managed without downtime. This system allowed for real-time changes to service configurations, enhancing flexibility and responsiveness.
Infrastructure as Code (IaC):
Uber leveraged IaC to automate the provisioning and management of its infrastructure. This practice ensured consistency, repeatability, and scalability of their environments, crucial for supporting their global operations.
Developer Experience and Productivity:
Uber’s focus on enhancing developer productivity led to the creation of internal platforms and tools designed to streamline the development process.
Standardized Tooling and Frameworks
: By providing standardized development frameworks and tools, Uber reduced the cognitive load on developers, allowing them to focus on building features rather than managing infrastructure.
Automated CI/CD Pipelines
: Automated
continuous integration and continuous deployment (CI/CD)
pipelines facilitated faster and more reliable code deployments. This automation reduced manual intervention, minimized errors, and accelerated the delivery of new features.
More on Uber’s Platform strategy can be found here:
https://newsletter.pragmaticengineer.com/p/the-platform-and-program-split-at
Netflix
Netflix
, renowned for its culture of innovation and agility, has been a trailblazer in developing robust platform engineering practices. Their approach emphasizes creating a balance between freedom and responsibility, enabling teams to innovate while maintaining high reliability standards.
The “Paved Road” PaaS:
Netflix introduced the concept of a “Paved Road”
Platform as a Service
(
PaaS
), offering a standardized yet customizable platform for developers. This approach ensured consistency while allowing flexibility for innovation.
Standardized Components
: The “Paved Road” PaaS provided pre-assembled, standardized components, such as microservice
remote procedure calls
(
RPC
), service discovery, logging, and monitoring. These components ensured that developers could build and deploy services quickly and reliably.
Customizable Framework
: Despite the standardization, Netflix’s platform allowed for customization, giving developers the freedom to integrate new technologies and approaches. This balance of standardization and flexibility fostered innovation while maintaining system reliability.
Automation and Tooling:
Automation has been a cornerstone of Netflix’s platform engineering strategy, enabling the company to manage its complex infrastructure efficiently.
Continuous Delivery with Spinnaker
: Netflix developed Spinnaker, an open-source continuous delivery platform, to automate the deployment process. Spinnaker allowed for seamless deployments across multiple cloud providers, ensuring high availability and reliability.
Operational Tooling
: Netflix invested in robust operational tooling, such as the Netflix OSS suite, which includes tools like Hystrix for latency and fault tolerance, Eureka for service discovery, and Ribbon for client-side load balancing. These tools enhanced the resilience and scalability of their services.
Observability and Reliability:
Ensuring high reliability and performance has been paramount for Netflix, given its global user base and demanding operational environment.
Centralized Logging and Monitoring
: Netflix implemented centralized logging and monitoring systems, providing real-time insights into system performance and enabling quick issue detection and resolution. Tools like Atlas for metrics collection and Spectator for monitoring played crucial roles in their observability strategy.
Chaos Engineering with Chaos Monkey:
Netflix pioneered chaos engineering to test the resilience of their systems. By intentionally injecting failures into their infrastructure using tools like Chaos Monkey, Netflix ensured that their systems could withstand unexpected disruptions and continue to operate smoothly.
Netflix engineers shared a presentation in Qcon about their Paved Road Paas:
https://www.infoq.com/news/2017/06/paved-paas-netflix
The pioneering efforts of Uber and Netflix have significantly influenced the field of platform engineering. Their innovative approaches to microservices architecture, infrastructure automation, developer productivity, and observability have set the stage for modern platform engineering practices. Here are some key takeaways:
Microservices and Modularity
: The adoption of microservices architecture by both Uber and Netflix has demonstrated the importance of modularity in building scalable and maintainable systems. This approach has become a standard in modern platform engineering, enabling organizations to scale their services independently and improve fault tolerance.
Automation and IaC
: The emphasis on automation and Infrastructure as Code has shown how critical these practices are for managing large-scale infrastructure efficiently. By automating repetitive tasks and ensuring consistent environments, organizations can achieve higher reliability and faster deployment cycles.
Developer Experience
: Enhancing developer productivity through standardized tooling and automated pipelines has proven to be a key factor in accelerating software delivery. By reducing the cognitive load on developers and providing robust internal platforms, companies can foster innovation and improve overall efficiency.
Observability and Reliability
: The focus on observability and reliability has highlighted the need for comprehensive monitoring and proactive issue resolution. Implementing centralized logging, metrics collection, and chaos engineering practices ensures that systems remain resilient and performant under various conditions.
A big question many are trying to answer is when you need to start a platform team. Let’s see how successful implementations of Platforms have approached this.
When to form a platform engineering team
Deciding when to establish a dedicated Platform Engineering team is crucial for optimizing engineering efficiency and maintaining a high level of developer satisfaction. In research conducted by DX (
https://getdx.com/blog/developer-productivity-team-when-to-establish/
) we see valuable insights into the optimal timing for forming such teams. DX’s research involved interviews with leaders from 20 companies, ranging from tech giants to non-tech organizations. The study revealed that the timing of establishing a DevProd team varies significantly across companies, influenced by their specific contexts and needs.
Timing and Organizational Size
The research found that companies establish DevProd teams at different stages of their growth. Some companies, like
Lattice
and
Cash App
, formed their teams when they had around 100 engineers, while others, such as
Disney
and
Thomson Reuters
, waited until they had several thousand engineers. The variance in timing was often due to the company’s ability to handle developer productivity challenges without a dedicated team.
Major Technology Transitions
One of the common triggers for forming a DevProd team is a major technology transition. Companies like
Stem
and
ThoughtSpot
established their teams during significant shifts in their technology stack, such as moving from a monolithic architecture to microservices or transitioning from a private cloud to a hybrid cloud environment. These transitions require focused efforts on engineering enablement, making it an ideal time to introduce a dedicated team.
Need for Standardization and Cost Efficiency
Another key driver for establishing a DevProd team is the need for standardization and cost efficiency. Companies facing redundancy in tooling or seeking to ensure mature development practices benefit from a centralized team responsible for these initiatives. For instance,
Tapad
aimed to reduce tooling redundancies and promote standardized development practices, while
ThoughtSpot’s
DevEx team drove more cost-effective operations by optimizing tool usage.
Infrastructure Scaling with Business Growth
Several companies reported challenges with their infrastructure as their business grew. For example,
Mercury
initially managed its infrastructure through volunteer efforts, but as the company scaled to 50 engineers, it became necessary to have a full-time function dedicated to these problems. Establishing a DevProd team at this stage helped them manage the increasing complexity and scale of their infrastructure.
Best practices
The research also identified best practices for organizations considering the formation of a DevProd team:
Allocate a Fixed Percentage of Engineering Investment
: Companies like
Atlassian
have mandated that a certain percentage of engineering resources be dedicated to improving developer productivity. This ensures that productivity improvements are prioritized alongside feature development.
Provide Teams with Data on Developer Experience
: Offering teams insights into their developer experience, such as pain points and metrics like deployment frequency, can motivate them to address these issues proactively. This data can also help advocate for the establishment of a DevProd team by highlighting the impact of productivity challenges.
Assign Dedicated Internal Champions
: Designating internal champions for specific developer experience areas helps curate insights and best practices, providing visibility to leaders and support for teams facing common challenges.
Start with Local Efforts
: Before establishing a centralized DevProd team, many organizations begin with local efforts to improve developer productivity. These grassroots initiatives can lay the groundwork for a more formalized team and demonstrate the value of focused productivity improvements.
Determining the optimal timing for forming a DevProd team depends on several factors:
During Major Shifts
: Introducing a DevProd team during significant technology transitions can help manage the increased complexity and enable successful adoption of new technologies.
When Standardization is Needed
: If there is a need to standardize development practices or tools across the organization, forming a DevProd team can drive these initiatives more effectively.
As Infrastructure Struggles to Scale
: When the existing infrastructure can no longer support business growth efficiently, a DevProd team can address scaling challenges and ensure continued reliability and performance.
The research by DX highlights that there is no one-size-fits-all answer to when an organization should establish a Developer Productivity team. The decision is influenced by the company’s growth stage, technological transitions, need for standardization, and infrastructure scaling challenges. By considering these factors and implementing best practices, organizations can determine the optimal timing for forming a DevProd team to enhance developer productivity and maintain high operational efficiency.
When did Yelp build their developer productivity
Yelp
, known for its reviews and recommendations platform, has a large-scale engineering operation that requires a keen focus on developer productivity to maintain its rapid development and deployment cycles. The formation of a dedicated
Developer Productivity
(
DevProd
) team at Yelp was a strategic move to enhance engineering efficiency, streamline processes, and improve the overall developer experience.Yelp’s decision to establish a DevProd team was driven by the need to address inefficiencies in the development process and to support the company’s growth and scaling efforts. As the engineering team expanded, so did the complexity of maintaining high productivity levels. Key factors that influenced Yelp’s decision included:
Developer Productivity to Support Growing Technical Complexities
. As Yelp’s user base and feature set grew, so did the demands on its engineering team. The increasing number of services and the complexity of the infrastructure required a more focused approach to ensure that developers could work efficiently and effectively. The need for a dedicated team to address these scaling challenges became evident.
Developer Productivity as a Driver of Change
. Yelp underwent significant technological transitions, including the adoption of microservices and the migration to cloud-based infrastructure. These changes brought about new challenges in terms of service orchestration, deployment pipelines, and overall system reliability. A DevProd team was necessary to manage these transitions smoothly and to optimize the development workflows.
The DevProd team at Yelp undertook several key initiatives to enhance developer productivity. These projects focused on reducing friction in the development process, automating repetitive tasks, and providing better tools and frameworks for developers.As mentioned in previous chapters, Platform Engineering is not just about backend services. Taking a closer look into Netflix’s DevProd implementation, we can explore things like their internal design platform, Hawkins.
Supercharging Developer Productivity at Netflix
Netflix is renowned for its innovative culture and commitment to maintaining high developer productivity. To keep pace with rapid technological advancements and the demands of its extensive user base, Netflix has implemented several strategic initiatives aimed at enhancing developer productivity. This section explores Netflix’s journey in establishing robust developer productivity practices, focusing on the reasons behind building front-end platforms, the development of their internal design system,
Hawkins
, and the creation of a federated platform console to improve the developer experience.Netflix’s approach to developer productivity is rooted in its philosophy of balancing freedom and responsibility. The company’s focus on enabling developers to work efficiently and innovate rapidly has led to the establishment of dedicated teams and tools designed to streamline development workflows.The primary goal of Netflix’s
Developer Productivity Engineering
(
DPE
) team is to create an environment where developers can be highly productive. This involves providing the necessary tools, frameworks, and infrastructure that allow developers to focus on writing code and delivering features without being bogged down by operational complexities.The rise of front-end platforms is driven by the need to standardize and streamline the development of
user interfaces
(
UI
) across different applications. For Netflix, building a front-end platform addresses several key challenges.
Consistency and Reusability
: A standardized front-end platform ensures consistency in the look and feel of applications, which is critical for maintaining a cohesive user experience. By reusing components and design patterns, developers can avoid duplicative efforts and reduce the time spent on building and maintaining UIs.
Component Libraries
: Netflix’s front-end platform includes a comprehensive library of reusable components, allowing developers to quickly assemble UIs without starting from scratch. These components are designed to be configurable and composable, ensuring flexibility and adaptability.
Design Systems
: Implementing a robust design system, such as Netflix’s Hawkins, provides a unified framework for developing UIs. This system ensures that all applications adhere to the same design principles and standards, resulting in a consistent user experience.
Efficiency and Productivity
: By providing a standardized platform, Netflix can significantly enhance developer productivity. Developers spend less time on repetitive tasks and more time on building new features and improving existing ones.
Streamlined Development Workflows
: Standardized workflows and tools reduce the cognitive load on developers, allowing them to focus on writing code and delivering value. This also reduces the learning curve for new developers, enabling them to become productive more quickly.
Automation of Repetitive Tasks
: Automation plays a crucial role in front-end development. Tools that automate testing, deployment, and monitoring of UIs ensure that developers can iterate quickly and maintain high-quality standards.
Building Hawkins - Netflix’s Design System
Hawkins
, Netflix’s internal design system, is a cornerstone of its front-end platform strategy. Designed to provide a consistent user experience across all applications, Hawkins plays a crucial role in enhancing developer productivity and maintaining design standards.The development of Hawkins was driven by the need to address the fragmentation and inconsistencies in Netflix’s UI development. Before Hawkins, different teams used varying design patterns and components, leading to a disjointed user experience and increased maintenance efforts.
Unified Design Language
: Hawkins provides a unified design language that all teams can adhere to, ensuring consistency across applications. This design language includes standardized components, patterns, and guidelines that help developers create cohesive UIs.
Reusable Components
: The system includes a library of reusable components that can be configured and composed to meet different needs. This reduces the effort required to develop new UIs and ensures that all applications maintain a consistent look and feel.
The implementation of Hawkins involved close collaboration between design and development teams. By integrating Hawkins into their workflows, Netflix ensured that all new and existing applications could leverage the design system’s benefits such as:
Improved Developer Efficiency
: Developers can now quickly assemble UIs using Hawkins’ components, significantly reducing development time. The consistent design patterns also make it easier to maintain and update applications.
Enhanced User Experience
: Users benefit from a consistent and intuitive interface across all Netflix applications. This cohesion enhances usability and satisfaction, contributing to Netflix’s overall user experience.
Building a Platform Console to Improve DevEx
To further enhance developer productivity, Netflix developed a federated platform console that unifies the various tools and services used throughout the
software development lifecycle
(
SDLC
). This console addresses several key challenges faced by developers, such as tool fragmentation and context switching.As Netflix’s platform grew, so did the number of tools and services used by developers. Managing these disparate tools became increasingly challenging, leading to inefficiencies and lost productivity.
Tool Fragmentation
: Developers had to navigate multiple tools and interfaces to manage their workflows, leading to context switching and reduced efficiency. The federated platform console aims to consolidate these tools into a single, cohesive interface.
Context Switching
: Constantly switching between different tools can disrupt a developer’s flow and lead to errors. The platform console provides a unified interface, reducing the need for context switching and improving overall productivity.
The federated platform console at Netflix offers several features designed to streamline the developer experience and enhance productivity:
Unified Interface
: The console provides a single interface for accessing all the tools and services required for development. This includes code repositories, CI/CD pipelines, monitoring tools, and more.
GraphQL Federation
: Leveraging GraphQL Federation, the console can aggregate data from various services and present it in a cohesive manner. This allows developers to access relevant information quickly and efficiently.
Hawkins Integration
: By integrating Hawkins, the platform console ensures a consistent user experience across all tools and services. This reduces the learning curve for developers and enhances usability.
Customizable Workflows
: The console allows developers to customize their workflows and integrate additional tools as needed. This flexibility ensures that the platform can adapt to the evolving needs of the development team.
The implementation of the federated platform console involved several phases, including initial concept development, pilot testing, and full-scale rollout. Throughout this process, Netflix gathered feedback from developers to refine and improve the console’s features.
Pilot Testing
: Initial pilot testing with select teams provided valuable insights into the console’s usability and effectiveness. Feedback from these tests was used to make necessary adjustments and enhancements.
Full-Scale Rollout
: Following successful pilot testing, the console was rolled out to the entire development team. Ongoing support and training were provided to ensure smooth adoption and to address any issues that arose.
Netflix’s commitment to enhancing developer productivity is evident in its strategic initiatives and innovative tools. By building a robust front-end platform, developing the Hawkins design system, and creating a federated platform console, Netflix has significantly improved the developer experience.
Adobe’s journey to building an IDP
Adobe
, a leader in digital media and digital marketing solutions, embarked on an ambitious journey to transform its software development processes by building an
Internal Developer Platform
(
IDP
). This platform is designed to streamline development workflows, enhance developer productivity, and ensure consistency across various products and services. Adobe’s journey to building an IDP began with the foundational transformation to cloud-native technologies. This journey, which started in 2015, has evolved significantly, resulting in the robust platform known as Ethos. Ethos has been pivotal in providing high resiliency, reliability, and extensive CI/CD capabilities to Adobe’s diverse product teams.Initially, Adobe’s cloud-native transformation involved migrating various services and applications to the cloud. This shift required a comprehensive platform that could handle the complexity of multi-tenant clusters and container orchestration across multiple regions.
Ethos
was developed as a response to these needs, providing a unified infrastructure that supports over 200 multi-tenant clusters and more than 3 million containers. This infrastructure enables over 200 production deployments each day, showcasing its scalability and efficiency.The IDP built on Ethos integrates several key capabilities, including security, infrastructure provisioning, monitoring, diagnostics, and GitOps-based CI/CD tooling using Argo. These components ensure that developers have a seamless experience when creating, deploying, and managing services. The platform’s design aims to reduce complexity and cognitive load, allowing developers to focus on innovation rather than infrastructure management.Ethos also includes a significant modernization project for Adobe’s Kubernetes (K8s) cluster fleet. This modernization effort is expected to enhance resiliency and result in substantial cost savings annually. By upgrading its K8s infrastructure, Adobe ensures that its platform remains at the cutting edge of technology, providing robust support for its extensive developer community.Building an IDP is not without its challenges. One of the primary difficulties is balancing the need for standardization with the flexibility required by different teams. Adobe addressed this by implementing a set of “golden paths” or best practices that guide developers through the most common workflows. These golden paths help standardize configurations and processes, reducing variability and potential errors.Moreover, the IDP team at Adobe has focused on shielding developers from the underlying complexity of the infrastructure. This approach aligns with the principle of driving productivity by abstracting away the intricate details of the platform, allowing developers to concentrate on delivering value.Measuring productivity within the IDP framework involves tracking various metrics related to deployment frequency, lead time for changes, and mean time to recovery. These metrics provide insights into the efficiency of the development process and highlight areas for improvement. By continuously monitoring these indicators, Adobe ensures that its IDP evolves to meet the changing needs of its developers.The transition to an IDP has also required significant cultural and organizational changes. Adobe has had to foster a culture that embraces platform engineering principles, where developers are encouraged to take ownership of their code in production. This shift necessitates a change in mindset from both developers and operations teams, promoting collaboration and shared responsibility.One of the strategies Adobe employed to facilitate this cultural shift was the establishment of cross-functional teams. These teams bring together developers, operations staff, and platform engineers to work collaboratively on building and maintaining the IDP. This approach enhances communication and cooperation and ensures that the platform meets all stakeholders' needs.Looking ahead, Adobe plans to continue expanding and refining its IDP. This includes integrating more advanced monitoring and diagnostics tools, enhancing security features, and further optimizing infrastructure provisioning. Adobe is also exploring ways to leverage artificial intelligence and machine learning to automate more aspects of the development and deployment process, further reducing the cognitive load on developers.Additionally, Adobe is committed to sharing its learnings and best practices with the broader community. By participating in conferences like PlatformCon and engaging with other industry leaders, Adobe contributes to the ongoing evolution of platform engineering and helps other organizations on their journey to building effective IDPs.Adobe’s journey to building an Internal Developer Platform has been marked by continuous innovation and improvement. From the initial development of Ethos to the current efforts in modernizing its Kubernetes fleet, Adobe has demonstrated a commitment to providing its developers with a robust, reliable, and efficient platform.
More on Adobe’s Ethos platform can be found here:
https://blog.developer.adobe.com/how-ethos-powers-a-cloud-native-transformation-at-adobe-16c1a2e2f67a
Scaling Kubernetes with ArgoCD at Zoominfo
Zoominfo
embarked on a journey to establish a robust pattern for setting up and managing Kubernetes clusters. The project aimed to support multiple cloud environments, maintain reproducible configurations, and utilize Terraform for IaC. Initially, this approach was effective for managing the cluster infrastructure using Cloud APIs and established Terraform providers. However, managing the platform cluster components such as networking, security, and observability through Helm configurations within Terraform began to reveal significant challenges as the number of clusters scaled from dozens to over a hundred.The initial setup involved using the Helm provider within Terraform to manage cluster components. Each cluster was separated into its own repository and Terraform workspace, necessitating individual
pull requests (PRs)
for configuration updates. As the infrastructure grew, this approach became untenable, leading to several key issues:
Effort and Time Consumption
: The process of opening and managing PRs for each cluster became increasingly time-consuming, taking days to weeks to roll out updates across all clusters.
Security Concerns
: The slow update process impeded the rapid deployment of security patches, raising vulnerabilities.
Error Handling
: Helm’s atomic deployments, while useful, provided generic error messages that were difficult to debug.
CI/CD Limitations
: The distributed nature of the Terraform CI/CD system was not well-suited for deploying and monitoring cluster components.
Visibility Issues
: Monitoring the health of applications across numerous clusters required manual checks, making it inefficient and error-prone.
To address these challenges, Zoominfo identified several critical requirements for a new solution:
Centralized Management
: A centralized solution to efficiently roll out configuration changes to one or more clusters.
Support for Configurations
: The ability to support configuration differences between clusters based on unique requirements.
Risk Management
: A balance between the risk of changes and deployment agility.
Scalability
: A scalable solution that can grow with the infrastructure.
Automation
: Automatic registration and bootstrapping of new clusters without requiring engineer intervention.
Visibility
: Enhanced visibility into the health of specific components across multiple clusters.
Given these requirements, Zoominfo turned to ArgoCD. ArgoCD offers a fully-loaded UI, enabling the deployment of workloads as Helm charts or manifests in a declarative manner while providing comprehensive visibility into workload health.
Implementation Details
Let’s dive a bit deeper to see how Zoominfo leverage specific ArgoCD Controllers in order to manage Kubernetes at scale.
ApplicationSet Controller
: The ApplicationSet controller in ArgoCD comprises two main parts - generators and templates. Generators create parameters rendered into the template fields of the ApplicationSet resource. The template portion defines an application for each parameter set. Zoominfo utilized the Cluster Generator and Merge Generator to manage configuration differences and metadata rules for deployment.
Secrets and Labels
: Secrets stored in the cluster define target clusters with unique labels to modify configurations based on the cluster’s specific requirements. This mechanism allowed Zoominfo to efficiently manage and deploy Helm charts across all clusters with configuration overrides for specific clusters.
Example
: A secret might define a cluster with unique labels, while an ApplicationSet manifest uses these secrets to deploy a Helm chart with specific configurations.Transitioning to ArgoCD yielded significant benefits:
Centralized Control
: One central ArgoCD cluster per SDLC environment now manages and pushes components to all clusters within that environment.
Efficient Updates
: Instead of managing hundreds of PRs, Zoominfo can now use a single PR to update all clusters, vastly improving efficiency.
Customization and Validation
: The ability to customize configurations for specific clusters allows Zoominfo to validate upgrades before broader rollouts.
Enhanced Visibility
: Centralized visibility into the health of all deployments and configurations provides better monitoring and management.
The migration from Terraform to ArgoCD for managing Kubernetes cluster components at Zoominfo represents a strategic shift towards more efficient, scalable, and secure operations. By leveraging ArgoCD’s declarative, GitOps-based approach, Zoominfo has significantly streamlined its deployment processes, enhanced security patch management, and improved visibility across its extensive infrastructure. This transition not only addresses the immediate challenges but also positions Zoominfo for greater scalability and adaptability, ensuring robust infrastructure management as the company continues to grow.
How Chick-fil-A is building a platform designed for the Edge
Chick-fil-A, traditionally known for its fast-food prowess, is rapidly becoming a case study in cutting-edge IT infrastructure with its pioneering efforts in edge computing. Leveraging Kubernetes and GitOps, Chick-fil-A is transforming its restaurant operations by deploying small-scale data centers within each location. This approach not only enhances operational efficiency but also ensures high availability and low latency in managing critical business functions. This article delves into Chick-fil-A’s innovative edge computing platform, its implementation strategies, and the resulting operational benefits
https://www.eweek.com/cloud/how-chick-fil-a-uses-kubernetes-and-gitops-at-the-edge/
At the core of Chick-fil-A’s edge computing initiative is the need to handle vast amounts of data generated by IoT devices within its restaurants. These devices include fryers, grills, refrigeration units, and point-of-sale systems, all of which produce significant amounts of telemetry data. By processing this data locally, Chick-fil-A can achieve real-time insights and decision-making capabilities, crucial for maintaining high service standards and optimizing restaurant operations
https://www.appvia.io/blog/chick-fil-a-kubernetes-success-story
Kubernetes and GitOps at the Edge
Chick-fil-A has adopted Kubernetes to manage its edge computing environment. Each restaurant operates its own Kubernetes cluster, consisting of three small, networked computers with limited hardware specifications. This setup, described as a “micro data center,” allows for local processing of data while ensuring that critical business information is also sent to the cloud for broader analysis.The implementation of GitOps further streamlines this process. GitOps utilizes Git repositories as the single source of truth for infrastructure configurations. By storing configuration files in Git, Chick-fil-A can manage updates and deployments across its numerous Kubernetes clusters through automated pull requests. This ensures consistent and secure updates across all locations without the need for manual intervention.One of the remarkable aspects of Chick-fil-A’s edge computing strategy is its scalability. Each restaurant’s Kubernetes cluster is designed to be easily deployable and self-sustaining. The initial setup involves unboxing the hardware, connecting it to the network, and powering it on. The devices then self-bootstrap and cluster automatically, requiring minimal technical expertise for installation. This simplicity enables Chick-fil-A to rapidly scale its edge computing infrastructure as it opens new locations.Additionally, this decentralized approach allows each restaurant to operate independently, tolerating network failures and ensuring continuous operation even during internet outages. This high availability is critical for maintaining uninterrupted service during peak hours and promotional events​.To maintain visibility and control over its distributed edge computing environment, Chick-fil-A has invested heavily in observability tools. These tools provide real-time monitoring of each Kubernetes cluster, enabling the IT team to detect and address issues promptly. By collecting telemetry data from IoT devices and processing it locally, Chick-fil-A can optimize equipment performance, predict maintenance needs, and enhance overall operational efficiency.The benefits of Chick-fil-A’s edge computing platform are manifold:
Firstly, it significantly reduces latency, as data processing occurs locally rather than relying on centralized cloud servers. This immediate data processing capability is vital for real-time decision-making and enhances the customer experience by ensuring faster service and more efficient operations.
Secondly, the platform’s scalability and ease of deployment allow Chick-fil-A to maintain consistent service standards across all locations. Each restaurant operates as a micro private cloud, seamlessly integrating with the broader network while retaining autonomy over local operations. This hybrid approach ensures that critical business functions continue without interruption, even in the face of network failures or high traffic volumes​.
Lastly, the use of Kubernetes and GitOps provides a robust framework for managing and updating the edge infrastructure. Automated deployments and configurations reduce the risk of human error and ensure that all restaurants are operating with the latest software and security patches. This consistency is crucial for maintaining the integrity and reliability of the entire system.
Chick-fil-A’s edge computing platform exemplifies how traditional businesses can leverage advanced IT solutions to enhance operational efficiency and customer satisfaction. By deploying Kubernetes clusters at each restaurant and utilizing GitOps for automated management, Chick-fil-A has created a resilient, scalable, and efficient infrastructure that meets the demands of modern fast-food operations. As edge computing continues to evolve, Chick-fil-A’s innovative approach serves as a model for other enterprises looking to harness the power of local data processing and real-time analytics.
Summary
In this chapter we explored how various leading companies have implemented platform engineering to enhance their operations. We started with Amazon’s “Two Pizza Team Rule” and its impact on platform engineering, followed by insights from Uber and Netflix, the early adopters of platform engineering and DevProd practices. We delved into the specifics of Netflix’s approach to developer productivity, focusing on their Hawkins design system and federated platform console. Adobe’s comprehensive journey to building their IDP was also detailed, highlighting their strategic phases, key components, and best practices.In the next chapter, we will delve into the future outlook of Platform Engineering and how the platform landscape is expected to evolve over the next few years.

14The Road Ahead for Platform Engineering
Join our book community on Discord
https://packt.link/IOzft
Throughout this book, we explored what Platform Engineering is and where does it fit in the DevOps journey. We designed a full-fledged platform using cloud-native tools, and we explored critical aspects of platform leadership. This final chapter delves into the future of platform engineering, exploring the trends, challenges, and opportunities that lie ahead. As organizations continue to embrace digital transformation, the role of platform engineering becomes increasingly critical. In this chapter, we will outline the anticipated developments in platform engineering, providing insights into how teams and technologies will evolve to meet emerging demands.In this chapter, we're going to cover the following main topics:
Defining Platform Engineering
How Platform Engineering Teams Will Evolve
Is Kubernetes the Endgame?
Cloud-Native Tools Will Evolve and Consolidate
By the end of this chapter, readers will have a clear understanding of the trajectory of platform engineering. They will learn about the consolidation of cloud-native tools, the increasing importance of multi-cluster deployments, and the evolving nature of platform engineering teams. This knowledge will equip you with the foresight to adapt their strategies and stay ahead in the competitive landscape.
Agreeing on a Definition of Platform Engineering
The term "platform engineering" has emerged as a pivotal concept in software engineering. However, despite its growing importance, there remains a significant lack of clarity and consensus around its precise definition and scope. This ambiguity can impede the adoption and effective implementation of platform engineering practices across organizations. Therefore, establishing a common understanding of platform engineering over the next one to two years is crucial for several reasons.
Platform engineering can be broadly defined as the practice of designing, building, and maintaining an
internal developer platform
(
IDP
) that serves as a cohesive and integrated set of tools, processes, and services. The primary goal of platform engineering is to enhance the efficiency, productivity, and satisfaction of development teams by providing a seamless and automated environment for software development and deployment.
The lack of a standardized definition of platform engineering leads to inconsistencies in how it is understood and implemented across different organizations. Some companies may focus primarily on infrastructure automation, while others might emphasize developer experience or CI/CD pipelines. This variability can result in fragmented and suboptimal implementations that fail to deliver the full potential of platform engineering.
Moreover, the rapid evolution of the cloud-native ecosystem has introduced a plethora of new tools, frameworks, and methodologies, each with its own set of capabilities and complexities. Organizations often struggle to navigate this landscape, piecing together various components without a clear strategy or roadmap. This can lead to inefficient use of resources, increased operational overhead, and slower time-to-market.
So, establishing a common understanding of platform engineering will provide several key benefits. Some are mentioned here:
Accelerated Adoption
: A clear and standardized definition will help organizations better understand the scope and objectives of platform engineering. This clarity will reduce hesitation and uncertainty, enabling companies to adopt platform engineering practices more quickly and confidently.
Improved Collaboration
: With a unified definition, teams will have a common language and framework to discuss their platform engineering efforts, share insights, and learn from each other's experiences. This collaborative environment can accelerate innovation and drive the development of more robust and effective platforms.
Enhanced Training and Development
: A standardized definition will facilitate the development and dissemination of training programs and educational resources. This will help build a skilled workforce capable of implementing and managing platform engineering practices, further accelerating adoption and improving outcomes.
Better Benchmarking and Best Practices
: A common understanding will enable organizations to benchmark their platform engineering efforts against industry standards and best practices. This can help identify areas for improvement, optimize resource allocation, and drive continuous improvement.
The community agreeing on a common understanding of Platform Engineering will play a crucial role for this field's future. As we agree on what we mean with the term, the team charter swill evolve for Platform team as well.
How Platform Engineering Teams will evolve
Platform engineering is increasingly becoming a vital aspect of modern software development and IT operations. However, many organizations still struggle with fragmented efforts, where platform engineering initiatives are spread across different departments, leading to inefficiencies and silos. As the field continues to mature, we anticipate significant changes in how platform engineering teams are structured and operate. This evolution will focus on breaking down silos, integrating efforts across departments, and making platforms more intuitive and seamless for end users.Currently, many organizations face the challenge of fragmented platform engineering efforts. Different teams and departments often work in isolation, each developing their own tools, processes, and platforms. This siloed approach can lead to several issues:
Duplication of Efforts
: Multiple teams may end up working on similar projects without knowledge of each other's work, leading to redundant efforts and wasted resources.
Inconsistent Standards
: Without a unified approach, different teams may adopt varying standards and practices, resulting in inconsistencies and interoperability issues.
Communication Barriers
: Siloed teams often face communication challenges, hindering collaboration and the sharing of knowledge and best practices.
Fragmented User Experience
: End users, typically developers, may have to navigate different platforms and tools, leading to a fragmented and inefficient experience.
One of the promises of Platform Engineering is to break down silos and improve collaboration using a common set of tools and processes. This is something in most cases that hasn't yet been a reality.
Building Platforms Hasn't Been Breaking Silos
To address the challenges of fragmented platform engineering, organizations need to shift their focus from merely building platforms to breaking down silos and fostering a more integrated approach. This involves several key strategies:
Unified Platform Strategy
: Develop a cohesive platform strategy that aligns with the organization's overall goals and priorities. This strategy should encompass all platform engineering efforts across departments, ensuring a unified direction and vision.
Cross-Functional Teams
: Establish cross-functional teams that bring together experts from different departments to collaborate on platform engineering projects. These teams can leverage diverse skills and perspectives, leading to more innovative and effective solutions.
Centralized Governance
: Implement centralized governance structures to oversee platform engineering efforts. This includes defining standards, best practices, and guidelines to ensure consistency and interoperability across different teams and platforms.
Effective Communication
: Foster open and transparent communication channels to facilitate collaboration and knowledge sharing. Regular meetings, workshops, and collaborative tools can help bridge communication gaps and promote a culture of teamwork.
Integrated Tools and Processes
: Adopt integrated tools and processes that support seamless collaboration and coordination across teams. This includes unified CI/CD pipelines, shared code repositories, and common monitoring and observability tools.
One of the key goals of platform engineering is to make platforms "invisible" to end users. This means creating platforms that are so intuitive and seamless that developers can focus on their core tasks without being bogged down by the underlying infrastructure and processes. Achieving this requires several important steps:
User-Centric Design
: Design platforms with a strong focus on the user experience. Understand the needs and pain points of developers and create solutions that simplify their workflows and enhance productivity.
Self-Service Capabilities
: Provide robust self-service capabilities that allow developers to access and utilize platform resources without needing to go through complex approval processes or relying on other teams. This includes self-service portals, automated provisioning, and easy-to-use APIs.
Automation and Abstraction
: Automate repetitive tasks and abstract away the complexities of the underlying infrastructure. This allows developers to focus on writing code and delivering features, rather than managing infrastructure and configurations.
Seamless Integration
: Ensure that platforms integrate seamlessly with the tools and workflows that developers are already using. This includes integration with popular IDEs, version control systems, CI/CD pipelines, and monitoring tools.
Continuous Improvement
: Continuously gather feedback from developers and iterate on the platform to address their evolving needs and challenges. This involves regularly updating and enhancing the platform based on user feedback and technological advancements.
Documentation
: Provide great documentation and guides of the services the platform provides. This can come in the form of documentation, blueprints, even service catalogs.
As organizations adopt these strategies, platform engineering teams will evolve in several important ways:
Greater Collaboration
: Cross-functional collaboration will become the norm, with teams working together to build and maintain integrated platforms that serve the entire organization.
Enhanced Efficiency
: By breaking down silos and adopting unified strategies, organizations will achieve greater efficiency in their platform engineering efforts, reducing duplication and optimizing resource utilization.
Improved Developer Experience
: Platforms will become more user-friendly and intuitive, enabling developers to focus on their core tasks and improving overall productivity and satisfaction.
Scalability and Flexibility
: Unified and integrated platforms will be more scalable and flexible, allowing organizations to respond quickly to changing business needs and technological advancements.
Innovation and Agility
: By fostering a culture of collaboration and continuous improvement, organizations will drive innovation and agility, staying ahead of the competition in a rapidly evolving technological landscape.
The evolution of platform engineering teams will be marked by a shift from fragmented, siloed efforts to integrated, collaborative approaches that prioritize user experience and efficiency. By adopting unified strategies, fostering cross-functional collaboration, and making platforms invisible to end users, organizations can fully leverage the benefits of platform engineering. This evolution will enhance developer productivity, optimize resource utilization, and drive innovation, positioning organizations for success in the future.Besides the definition of Platforms and the structure of Platform teams, cloud-native technologies should evolve alongside them. Kubernetes is the clear winner as the underlying infrastructure orchestrator for IDPs, but what does the future hold for Kubernetes?
Is Kubernetes the endgame?
Kubernetes has become a cornerstone of modern cloud-native architecture, providing a powerful, flexible, and scalable platform for managing containerized applications. However, as the ecosystem evolves, questions arise about whether Kubernetes is the ultimate solution or if it will give way to new paradigms and tools. This section explores the complexities and potentials of Kubernetes, its role as an API-based workload scheduler, the growing necessity for multi-cluster deployments, and the future of Kubernetes in platform engineering.Kubernetes, while powerful, is often perceived as overly complex, especially by newcomers. This perception can be attributed to its vast array of features, the steep learning curve associated with mastering its components, and the challenges of managing large-scale deployments. However, much of this complexity can be mitigated through proper education, tooling, and abstraction layers.Kubernetes abstracts the underlying infrastructure, providing a consistent and declarative approach to deploying and managing applications. This abstraction is powerful but can be daunting without a clear understanding of its principles and best practices. To address this, many organizations are investing in training and creating simplified interfaces that hide much of the underlying complexity.Efforts such as the Kubernetes learning resources, and community-driven tutorials have made it easier for developers and operators to get started with Kubernetes. Moreover, managed Kubernetes services from cloud providers like
Google Kubernetes Engine
(
GKE
), Amazon's
Elastic Kubernetes Service
(
EKS
), and
Azure Kubernetes Service
(
AKS
) further simplify the operational aspects, allowing teams to focus more on application development and less on infrastructure management.At its core, Kubernetes is nothing more than a declarative API that helps us declaratively describe and manage our workloads. It provides a powerful API that allows developers and operators to declaratively define the desired state of their applications and infrastructure. Kubernetes then takes on the responsibility of ensuring that the actual state matches the desired state, handling tasks such as scaling, healing, and updating applications.The power of Kubernetes lies in its extensibility and flexibility. It is designed to be modular, allowing organizations to extend its capabilities through custom controllers, operators, and Custom Resource Definitions (CRDs). This extensibility enables teams to tailor Kubernetes to their specific needs, integrating it seamlessly with their existing tools and workflows.Despite its perceived complexity, many of the challenges associated with Kubernetes are gradually being addressed. For example, the Kubernetes community has been working on improving the user experience by simplifying the API, enhancing documentation, and developing more intuitive tooling. As a result, many of the complexities that once made Kubernetes daunting are being mitigated, making it more accessible and user-friendly.The recent (in v1.29) removal of over a million lines of Cloud Provider code from Kubernetes is a significant step towards simplifying the platform. This effort, part of the
Kubernetes Enhancement Proposal
(
KEP
) 2395, aims to move vendor-specific code out-of-tree, reducing the out-of-box complexity and making Kubernetes more modular and easier to manage. These changes reflect the ongoing efforts to streamline Kubernetes and make it more adaptable to various use cases.While there is an effort to simplify Kubernetes at its core, there is also the need for managing complex deployments and set ups. As we see companies trying to rationalize their cloud spend and adopt hybrid cloud strategies, the need for multi-cluster, hybrid deployments become very important.
Multi-cluster is becoming a necessity
As organizations increasingly adopt Kubernetes, the need for multi-cluster deployments has become more apparent. Multi-cluster setups provide several advantages, including improved fault tolerance, better resource utilization, and enhanced security through isolation. They also support hybrid and multi-cloud strategies, allowing organizations to deploy applications across different cloud providers and on-premises environments.Managing multiple Kubernetes clusters, however, introduces new challenges. These include the complexity of managing cluster configurations, ensuring consistent security policies, and coordinating deployments across clusters. Tools and frameworks such as Kubernetes Federation, Cluster API, and open-source projects like Rancher and KubeFed are emerging to address these challenges, providing centralized management and orchestration for multi-cluster environments.A notable development in this space is the trend towards centralized add-on management for multi-cluster Kubernetes environments. This approach simplifies the deployment and management of common services, such as monitoring, logging, and security, across multiple clusters. It allows organizations to maintain consistency and streamline operations, making multi-cluster Kubernetes more practical and efficient.Moreover, the increasing adoption of edge computing and IoT is driving the need for multi-cluster deployments. These environments often require applications to be deployed close to the data source or end-users, necessitating distributed and geographically dispersed clusters. Kubernetes, with its robust scheduling and orchestration capabilities, is well-suited to manage these complex, distributed environments.
Kubernetes was not designed to build Platforms
Kubernetes was originally designed to be a container orchestration platform, focusing on scheduling and managing containerized workloads. It provides a robust framework for deploying, scaling, and managing applications in a distributed environment. However, as the needs of the cloud-native ecosystem have evolved, Kubernetes has been extended to support a broader range of use cases, including platform engineering.The core strength of Kubernetes lies in its ability to abstract the underlying infrastructure and provide a consistent interface for managing workloads. This makes it an ideal foundation for building platforms that can support diverse applications and services. However, Kubernetes was not initially designed with platform engineering in mind, and this has led to certain limitations and challenges.One of the key challenges is the complexity of integrating various tools and services into a cohesive platform. While Kubernetes provides the foundational infrastructure, building a complete platform requires additional components for monitoring, logging, security, CI/CD, and more. This often involves stitching together multiple open-source projects and custom solutions, which can be cumbersome and difficult to manage.To address these challenges, the Kubernetes community and the broader cloud-native ecosystem are exploring ways to simplify and streamline platform engineering. This includes the development of opinionated distributions and frameworks that provide integrated solutions out of the box.Looking ahead, it is likely that we will see further evolution and specialization within the Kubernetes ecosystem. This could include the development of forks or specialized distributions that focus specifically on platform engineering needs. These specialized versions of Kubernetes would aim to provide the robust scheduling and orchestration capabilities of Kubernetes while abstracting away much of the complexity and integrating essential platform services.As Kubernetes evolves, we do expect a significant evolution in the whole cloud-native ecosystem.
The evolution of cloud-native tools
The evolution of cloud-native tools is inevitable as the ecosystem matures. Currently, the landscape is vast and complex, with numerous tools and frameworks emerging to address various aspects of cloud-native development and operations. This diversity, while beneficial in fostering innovation, also poses significant challenges. Many organizations struggle to navigate the plethora of available tools, leading to fragmentation and inefficiencies. Over the coming years, we can expect to see significant consolidation and evolution in this space, aimed at simplifying the landscape and providing more comprehensive and user-friendly solutions.The cloud-native ecosystem, largely driven by the
Cloud Native Computing Foundation
(
CNCF
), has grown exponentially over the past few years. With over 100 projects under its umbrella, CNCF provides a rich repository of tools for container orchestration, service meshes, observability, CI/CD, security, and more. While this abundance of tools offers flexibility, it also creates complexity. Organizations often find themselves overwhelmed by the choices and the effort required to integrate these disparate tools into a cohesive platform.For instance, consider the domain of observability. Tools like Prometheus, Grafana, Jaeger, and Loki each provide valuable capabilities, but integrating them into a unified observability stack can be challenging. Similar complexities arise in other areas such as security, CI/CD pipelines, and service meshes.This fragmented approach with multiple solutions addressing problems in the same space can lead to inefficiencies, increased operational overhead, and a steep learning curve for teams.
The need for ready-to-use enterprise platforms
Despite the plethora of tools available, there is still no comprehensive, ready-to-use enterprise platform that addresses all aspects of cloud-native development and operations. Most existing solutions either focus on specific areas (like developer portals or platform orchestrators) or require significant effort to integrate and customize.For instance, platforms like OpenShift provide a robust enterprise-grade Kubernetes platform with integrated tools for CI/CD, monitoring, and security. However, OpenShift is known for being opinionated and difficult to customize, which can be a limitation for organizations with specific needs. While it offers strong defaults, the flexibility to tailor the platform to unique requirements is limited.This gap presents an opportunity for new solutions that offer strong defaults while being flexible enough to accommodate customizations. Such platforms would need to provide a seamless, integrated experience that covers all aspects of the cloud-native lifecycle, from development and testing to deployment, monitoring, and scaling.
The Future: Strong Defaults with Flexible Options
As the cloud-native ecosystem continues to evolve, we can expect to see the emergence of platforms that strike a balance between strong defaults and flexibility. These platforms will aim to reduce complexity by providing integrated solutions out of the box while allowing for customization to meet specific needs.
Integrated Solutions
: Future platforms will bundle essential tools and services into a cohesive package, reducing the effort required to integrate and manage them. These platforms will offer pre-configured environments that follow best practices, making it easier for organizations to get started and achieve quick wins.
Flexibility and Customization
: While strong defaults are essential for reducing complexity, flexibility will be crucial for addressing unique requirements. Future platforms will offer modular architectures that allow organizations to swap out components or add new ones without disrupting the overall system. This will enable teams to tailor the platform to their specific needs while maintaining a seamless user experience.
Standardization and Interoperability
: Efforts to standardize APIs and interfaces will facilitate interoperability between different tools and platforms. This will make it easier for organizations to integrate new tools, switch between solutions, and avoid vendor lock-in. Standards like the Open Application Model (OAM) and the Service Mesh Interface (SMI) are steps in this direction.
Enhanced User Experience
: Future platforms will prioritize user experience, making it easier for developers and operators to interact with the platform. This will involve intuitive user interfaces, comprehensive documentation, and robust support for automation and self-service capabilities.
Currently, most platforms are built on top of CNCF tools, leveraging the rich ecosystem of open-source projects. However, we can expect to see more enterprise solutions emerging that package and distribute these tools in a more user-friendly and integrated manner. Some of those are mentioned here:
Packaging and Distribution
: Enterprises will look for solutions that package CNCF tools with enterprise-grade features such as enhanced security, compliance, and support. This will make it easier for organizations to adopt these tools without the need for extensive customization and integration efforts.
Enterprise-Grade Features
: Future platforms will incorporate features that address enterprise requirements, such as
role-based access control
(
RBAC
), auditing, compliance, and scalability. These features will be built into the platform, reducing the need for organizations to implement them separately.
Support and Maintenance
: Enterprises will seek platforms that come with robust support and maintenance services, ensuring that they can rely on the platform for mission-critical applications. This will include regular updates, security patches, and access to expert support.
In conclusion, the cloud-native tools landscape is poised for significant evolution and consolidation. The current complexity and redundancy present challenges, but they also offer opportunities for innovation. Future platforms will aim to provide strong defaults with flexible options, integrating essential tools into cohesive packages that simplify cloud-native development and operations.As these platforms evolve, they will prioritize user experience, standardization, and enterprise-grade features, making it easier for organizations to adopt and benefit from cloud-native technologies. By striking a balance between simplicity and flexibility, these platforms will enable organizations to build robust, scalable, and efficient cloud-native environments that drive innovation and business value.This consolidation and evolution will ultimately lead to a more streamlined and user-friendly cloud-native ecosystem, reducing the barriers to adoption and enabling organizations to fully leverage the benefits of cloud-native technologies.
Summary
In this chapter, we explored the future trajectory of platform engineering, emphasizing several crucial aspects. Firstly, we discussed the importance of achieving a common understanding of platform engineering. This understanding will facilitate faster adoption and more effective platform strategies across organizations. Over the next few years, standardizing practices will help streamline efforts and promote broader acceptance and implementation.We then examined how platform engineering teams will evolve. The shift from siloed efforts to integrated, cross-functional teams will be a significant trend. Breaking down silos, fostering collaboration, and making platforms intuitive and user-friendly were key strategies highlighted. This evolution will lead to a unified platform strategy, open communication, and centralized governance, ensuring more efficient and effective platform engineering efforts.The role of Kubernetes was another focal point. Kubernetes has revolutionized cloud-native architecture. However, its complexity has posed challenges. We discussed how these complexities are being addressed and how multi-cluster deployments are becoming increasingly necessary. The potential future of Kubernetes includes specialized forks tailored for platform engineering, making it more adaptable and user-friendly.Finally, we addressed the consolidation of cloud-native tools. The current landscape is vast and complex, with numerous tools addressing similar problems. This fragmentation leads to inefficiencies and challenges in integration. The future will see the emergence of integrated, user-friendly platforms that balance strong defaults with flexibility. These platforms will prioritize standardization, enhanced user experience, and enterprise-grade features, making cloud-native development and operations more streamlined and accessible.Understanding these trends and developments equips you with the knowledge to navigate the future of platform engineering. By recognizing the importance of standardization, integrated teams, and user-friendly platforms, organizations can enhance efficiency, collaboration, and innovation in their platform engineering efforts. This knowledge is crucial for adapting strategies and staying ahead in the competitive technological landscape.//Congratulations on reaching the end of this journey through the evolving landscape of platform engineering!
